III. OF TWO GENTLEMEN OF WALES, AND HOW THEY HUNTED WITH THE HOUNDS, AND YET RAN WITH THE DEER

“The hollow oak our palace is,
Our heritage the sea.”

All who have wandered through the enchanting landscape of North Devon will know the little white town of Bideford, nestled along the broad tidal river, its banks lined with golden sands and crossed by an ancient stone bridge where salmon wait for autumn rains. The town climbs gently from the water’s edge toward the rolling uplands to the west. Above it, hills close in, draped in deep oak woods, broken here and there by jagged slate crags crowned with ferns; below, the land softens into rounded knolls and fertile fields of red and green, stretching out until they merge into vast salt marshes and dunes. Here, the rivers Torridge and Taw meet, flowing together toward the open sea, where waves crash against the bar and the endless thunder of the Atlantic rolls in.

Bideford stands there quietly, beneath a sky as soft as Italian art, cooled day and night by the fresh ocean breeze—breeze that keeps winter frosts at bay and summer heat at arm’s length. For perhaps eight hundred years it has stood thus, since the first Grenville, cousin to the Conqueror, returned from conquering South Wales and gathered around him Saxons, Norsemen with their golden curls, and dark Britons from Swansea—all mingling their blood, which still gives the people of the western counties their strength, intelligence, and even today, their distinctive beauty.

But in the year 1575, Bideford was more than just a peaceful market town with a few fishing boats bobbing at its quay. It was one of England’s most vital ports. It contributed seven ships to the fight against the Spanish Armada. A century later, chroniclers noted that “it sent more vessels to the northern trade than any port in England, save London and Topsham.” It was the heart of a thriving coastal culture—small by modern standards, yet how can we scorn the dawn of greatness? It was men like those of Devon—Drakes, Hawkinses, Gilberts, Raleighs, Grenvilles, Oxenhams, and countless others now forgotten—who laid the foundation of England’s naval might and global reach. Had they not first struck hard at Spain’s wealth in the West Indies, then shattered her final fleet at Britain’s Salamis—the glorious battle of 1588—we might now be no more than a Catholic dependency under a tyranny crueler than pagan Rome itself.

It is in honor of these men—their voyages, their battles, their courage, their faith, their lives lived boldly and often cut short—that I write this tale. If my words sometimes soar too high or grow grandly rhetorical, let me be forgiven: this story deserves song rather than speech, and should be proclaimed not as fiction but as epic—a true saga of heroism, like those of Troy or Marathon, meant for every true English heart.

One bright summer afternoon in 1575, a tall, fair-haired boy walked slowly along Bideford’s quay, his scholar’s gown trailing behind him, satchel slung over one shoulder, slate clutched in hand. His eyes were fixed on the ships and sailors, drawn to the sea as if by some invisible pull. Just past the foot of the High Street, he passed a tavern that opened onto the river. In its wide bay window sat merchants and gentlemen, sipping sack and debating the news. Outside, a group of sailors clustered around a man standing in their midst, speaking loudly and passionately.

The boy, eager for any word of adventure, slipped forward, joining the younger lads huddled near the men’s elbows. And so he heard the following account, delivered in a bold voice thick with Devon accent and punctuated with oaths:

“If you don’t believe me, go see for yourself—or stay here and rot with blue mold. I saw it with my own eyes, and so did Salvation Yeo, looking through a window in the lower room. We measured it ourselves—seventy feet long, ten feet wide, twelve feet high—stacked with silver bars, each weighing between thirty and forty pounds. And Captain Drake said: *‘There, my lads of Devon, I’ve brought you to the mouth of the world’s treasure-house. It’s your fault now if you don’t sweep it clean as a stockfish.’*”

“Why didn’t you bring some back, Mr. Oxenham?”

“Why weren’t you there to carry them? We could’ve taken it all—easily. Young Drake and I had already broken down the door, but Captain Drake fainted dead away. When we checked, he had a wound in his leg so deep you could fit three fingers inside. His boots were soaked in blood—had been for an hour or more—but he never knew it until he collapsed. Then his brother and I dragged him to the boats, kicking and screaming, begging us to let him keep fighting. Every step he took left a pool of blood in the sand. And tell me—wasn’t it worth saving him, even if it meant losing the silver? Silver we can get again. There’s more fish in the sea than ever came out of it, and more silver in Nombre de Dios than would pave every street in the west country. But a captain like Franky Drake? Heaven makes only one such man at a time. Lose him, and England’s luck dies with him. Who disagrees? Let him name his weapon—I’m ready.”

The speaker was a tall, broad-shouldered man with a ruddy face and a thick black beard. His eyes were sharp and restless. He leaned against the wall with legs crossed and arms akimbo, seeming to the schoolboy like some noble prince or duke. He wore a crimson velvet suit—outlawed by law, though clearly worn with pride—his side adorned with a long Spanish rapier and two daggers whose hilts sparkled with gold. Rings glittered on his fingers, gold chains hung about his neck, and large earrings dangled from his ears. Behind one ear, a red rose peeked from among his glossy black curls. On his head sat a wide-brimmed Spanish hat, not adorned with a feather, but with a whole Quezal bird—its feathers of iridescent green and gold shimmering like a single jewel.

As he finished, he removed the hat and looked at the bird.

“Look here, boys,” he said. “Have you ever seen such a creature? This is the bird the old kings of Mexico forbade anyone but themselves to wear. So I wear it—John Oxenham of South Tawton—as a sign to every brave lad in Devon: just as the Spaniards rule the Indians, we shall rule the Spaniards.”

A murmur of approval rose from the crowd. One man muttered, “I doubt the Spaniards are too many for us.”

“Too many?” Oxenham laughed. “We took Nombre de Dios with seventy-three men—no more when we left Plymouth Sound. Half were weakened by scurvy before we reached the Spanish Main. Then Captain Rawse of Cowes joined us with thirty more. With fifty-three men total, we cracked open the New World! Who died? Only the trumpeter—he stood braying like a donkey in the square instead of watching his neck. That’s why the Spaniards are cowards—like all bullies. They pray to a woman, those idol-worshipping rogues! No wonder they fight like women.”

“You’re right, captain!” cried a gaunt sailor beside him. “One westcountryman can beat two easterlings, and an easterling can take on three Dons any day. Eh, lads of Devon?”

“For O! it's the herrings and the good brown beef,
And the cider and the cream so white;
O! they are the making of the jolly Devon lads,
For to play, and eke to fight!”

“Come!” said Oxenham. “Who’ll join? Who’ll make his fortune?”

“Oh, who will join, jolly mariners all?
And who will join, says he, O!
To fill his pockets with the good red gold,
By sailing on the sea, O!”

“Who’ll join?” the gaunt man called again. “Now’s your chance! We’ve got forty men already waiting in Plymouth, ready to sail the moment we return. We need a dozen from Bideford—and a few boys. Then we’re off—make our fortunes or die trying.”

“Our bodies in the sea so deep,
Our souls in heaven to rest!
Where valiant seamen, one and all,
Hereafter shall be blest!”

“Now,” Oxenham declared, “you won’t let the Plymouth men say Bideford’s too scared to follow? North Devon versus South Devon—it’s settled. Who’ll join? It’s just a short voyage. Once past Cape Finisterre, the sea’s as calm as a duckpond. I’ll race a Clovelly herring boat there and back for twenty pounds—without taking a bucketful of water. Who’ll join? Don’t think you’re buying a pig in a poke. I know the route—Salvation Yeo here knows it too. He was gunner’s mate. Ask him to show you the chart—he’ll trace it better than Drake himself.”

At that, the gaunt man pulled from under his arm a great white buffalo horn carved with rough maps of lands and seas, islands, animals, and ships. He held it aloft.

“Look, boys,” he said. “Behold the picture of the place—drawn as real as life. I got it from a Portuguese down at the Azores. He marked every place he sailed, every thing he saw. Take it, Simon Evans—hold it in your hands. I’ll wager you’ll know the way in five minutes—better than any shark in the sea.”

The horn passed from hand to hand. Oxenham, seeing the crowd stirred, called through the window for a large tankard of sack, which he passed around after the horn.

The schoolboy, who had watched everything with wide eyes and ears, had managed to inch closer. Now he stood face to face with the man wearing the emerald bird, gazing at the marvel. As sailors turned the horn over and offered to join Oxenham, the boy’s heart burned with longing—to see that horn up close, as magical as Tristram’s horn or Ariosto’s enchanted instruments. When the group began to disperse and Oxenham stepped into the tavern with his recruits, the boy stepped forward boldly.

“Can I see it again?” he asked.

“It’s yours,” Oxenham said, handing it over. “If you want it.”

“I want it,” the boy replied. “But not your soul. That’s a stale sole, maybe. There are plenty of fresh ones in the bay.”

He fumbled in his pocket and pulled out a tester—the only coin he had.

“Will this buy it?”

“That? Not even twenty of them.”

The boy thought for a moment—what would a knight do in such a case?

“I’ll fight you for it,” he said.

“Thank you, sir,” Oxenham chuckled.

“Break the jackanapes’s head for him, Yeo,” he added.

“Call me jackanapes again, and I’ll break yours,” the boy snapped, raising his fist.

Oxenham studied him, smiling. “Tut, tut. Hit someone your own size, if you must. Spare little folk like me.”

“I may be a boy in age,” the lad said, “but I’ve a man’s fist. I turn fifteen this month, and I know how to answer anyone who insults me.”

“Fifteen, eh?” Oxenham said, admiring the boy’s broad shoulders, keen blue eyes, golden curls, and honest face. “You look more like twenty. If I had half a dozen lads like you, I’d make knights of them before I died. What do you say, Yeo?”

“He’ll do,” Yeo said. “In a year or two, he’ll make a fine gamecock—if he dares ruffle up so early at a tough old hen-master like the captain.”

Everyone laughed, including Oxenham, who then turned to the boy.

“So tell me, why are you so eager for that horn?”

“Because,” the boy said, lifting his head proudly, “I want to go to sea. I want to see the Indies. I want to fight the Spaniards. Though I’m a gentleman’s son, I’d far rather be a cabin-boy on your ship.”

“And you shall,” Oxenham declared, swearing fiercely. “You’ll eat galloon and dine on carbonadoed Dons. Who is your father, young gallant?”

“Mr. Leigh of Burrough Court.”

“Bless his soul! I know him as well as I know the Eddystone. Who’s dining with him tonight?”

“Sir Richard Grenville.”

“Dick Grenville? I didn’t know he was in town. Go home and tell your father John Oxenham will come to keep him company. Off with you! I’ll settle things with the good man, and you’ll have your place on my ship. And as for the horn—give it to him, Yeo. I’ll give you a noble for it.”

“No penny, noble captain,” Yeo said. “If the young master wants a poor sailor’s gift, here it is—for the love he shows the sea. May God bless him in it.” And with a sudden burst of generosity, he thrust the horn into the boy’s hands and walked away, avoiding thanks.

“And now,” Oxenham said, turning to his crew, “make up your minds what kind of men you want to be. I don’t want sneaky shore rats who take five pounds from one captain, ten from another, then vanish into tavern cellars or under women’s mufflers. If you’re that sort, better cut yourself up and salt yourself down like pork. If I catch you, seven years hence, I’ll slit your throat in the street—pity if I don’t! But if you’ll stand true to me, I’ll stand true to you—through wreck or prize, storm or calm, salt water or fresh, victuals or none—share and share alike. Here’s my hand on it, for every man and all!”

“Westward ho! with a rumbelow,
And hurra for the Spanish Main, O!”

With that, Oxenham strode into the tavern, followed by his new recruits. The boy walked home, cradling the horn, trembling with hope and fear, blushing with shame at having revealed his deepest dream to a stranger—one he’d hidden even from his parents since he was ten.

This youth, Amyas Leigh, though born of noble blood and raised in the finest circles of Devon, was not what modern readers might call “interesting” or “highly educated.” Beyond a smattering of Latin—drilled into him with blows like a nail—he knew only the Bible, the Prayer Book, Caxton’s *Mort d’Arthur*, and a translation of Las Casas’ *History of the West Indies*, titled *The Cruelties of the Spaniards*. He believed in pixies, thought they swapped babies and danced in mushroom rings, visited the white witch at Northam for warts and burns, and believed the sun moved around the earth and the moon resembled a Cheshire cheese. He thought swallows slept at the bottom of the horse-pond and spoke with a broad Devonshire accent, like Raleigh and Grenville. By today’s standards, he was ignorant—so much so that any schoolboy might laugh at him.

Yet this “ignorant savage,” untouched by modern science, literature, or history books that paint all pre-1688 England as hypocritical fools, had learned things no school today would teach: truthfulness, courage, endurance, and the dignity of being a gentleman—not as a title, but as a way of life. He had been trained to speak truth and draw the bow, to endure pain without complaint, and to value kindness above pride. For a year, he’d cared for a colt and a flock of young hawks from Lundy Isle, learning perseverance and patience. He read the clouds like a sailor, knew every bird, fish, and insect by name, and had become the undisputed champion of his school—fearless in fights, yet always defending the weak. He fought not for cruelty, but justice. He was feared by sailor boys, loved by town children, and felt he hadn’t done his duty unless he’d punished a bully.

He didn’t think about thinking. He didn’t feel about feeling. His ambition? To please his parents, earn red quarrenders and mazard cherries, and go to sea when he was big enough. He wasn’t what we’d call “pious”—he said his prayers, went to church, read Psalms with his mother—but beyond the catechism, he knew nothing of theology. Still, was he untrained in virtue? Was his narrow world devoid of depth?

Perhaps not. Perhaps the ignorance of his age was balanced by the strength of his character—by honesty, courage, loyalty, and a fierce sense of honor. So let us watch him now, climbing the hill with his precious horn, eager to tell his mother all that had happened—though he’d never hidden anything from her, except this sea fever, which he kept secret because he knew it would pain her, and because, wise beyond his years, he knew he wasn’t yet old enough. As he told her that afternoon, “There’s no use hollaing till I’m out of the wood.”

“*If men remain silent, the stars will make you known; the Sun does not forget its comrade.*”
—Old Epigram on Drake.

Five years have passed. It is nine o’clock on a crisp, bright November morning, and yet the bells of Bideford Church still ring out, two hours beyond their usual hour. They do not toll in solemn order, but burst into joyful peals every five minutes, as if they too could not contain their mirth. The streets of Bideford are transformed into a riot of color—garlands strung across lanes, tapestries draped from windows, and crowds of seamen, merchants, and townsfolk dressed in their finest holiday attire. Ships in the harbor fly all their flags, and cannon roar in celebration. Horses crowd every stable, and Sir Richard Grenville’s house thrums with feasting, laughter, and servants running to and fro.

Along the churchyard path, packed with women in bonnets and shawls, moves the noble blood of North Devon—tall men, stately ladies, heirs of ancient houses whose names once echoed through England’s courts and fields. Among them is the Countess of Bath, escorted by Sir Richard himself, her cap in hand (for her husband, Earl Bourchier, is in London at the queen’s court). There are Bassets from Umberleigh, Carys from Clovelly, Fortescues from Wear and Buckland, Coles from Slade, Stukelys from Affton, St. Legers from Annery, Coffins from Portledge, and even Coplestones from Eggesford, thirty miles distant. And last—but not least—comes Sir John Chichester of Ralegh, followed by his eight daughters and three sons in single file, a sight straight from old patriarchal times. One son is absent, fighting in Ireland to avenge his brother’s murder, and who will one day rule there as Lord Deputy and Baron of Belfast. At the gate, he meets his cousin from Arlington, whose own procession stretches behind him—four daughters and nineteen sons, the youngest not yet past the town hall, the eldest already at the Lychgate. They laugh and yield the way to the elder branch, a living tree of noble stock.

Into the church they go, each placed according to rank or near enough to it—though not without sharp glances, whispered complaints, and quiet jostlings among high-born matrons. The churchwardens and sidesmen, overwhelmed by such grand company, grow flustered and red-faced, finally begging Sir Richard himself to sort out the nobility: who is senior, who junior, which family bears eight quarterings, which only four—lest they spark a feud between half the fine ladies of the region. The older men sit safely in the corporation pews; the younger ones care only for a view of the ladies. At last, silence falls. All eyes turn to the door. Then, from afar, music approaches—flutes, hautboys, drums, and trumpets—blaring, shrieking, thundering up to the very threshold. Then, silence. The churchwardens descend with rods in hand. A hush, then a rustle, and sudden tears and blessings from many women—and some men too—as the long-awaited heroes enter. The rector begins not the morning service, but the ancient rite of thanksgiving after a great sea victory.

What has stirred this joy in old Bideford? Why do all eyes fix upon four weather-beaten mariners, adorned with ribbons and knots by loving hands? And especially upon the towering figure before them—a boy without beard, yet built like Hercules, golden hair flowing over his shoulders, rising head and shoulders above the congregation? And why, as they move instinctively toward the altar and kneel before the rails, does Mrs. Leigh of Burrough hide her face in her hands, her hood trembling with sobs?

Because these are Devon men—men of Bideford. Their names are Amyas Leigh of Burrough, John Staveley, Michael Heard, Jonas Marshall, and Thomas Braund of Clovelly. They are the first English sailors ever to sail around the world, having voyaged with Francis Drake. And now they return home—not just to rest, but to give thanks to God.

It is a tale that must be told. To understand how they came to this moment, we must look back, nearly to where we began.

For more than a year after Mr. Oxenham’s departure, young Amyas had lived quietly, as promised, though prone to occasional bursts of temper—common enough in strong-willed boys. His studies did not improve much, but his training at home flourished. He became a skilled archer, rider, and swordsman (in the old style of buckler fencing). But then, while attending the Exeter Assizes on business, his father caught the prison fever common in those days, fell ill in court, and died within a week.

Now Mrs. Leigh was left alone—widowed, with a fierce young son in her care. She had loved her husband deeply and truly. He had been a man of talent—scholar, courtier, soldier—but also one of disappointment. His estate had been ruined by his father’s extravagance and his own youthful ambition, which led him to court, where he spent his heart and purse chasing shadows. Though gifted, he lacked the strength to use them wisely. Shyness, pride, or a delicate honor kept him from seizing success, even as he resented the world that denied him. Finally, he met Miss Foljambe, a fair young lady from Derbyshire at Queen Elizabeth’s court—also weary of court life. They married despite the queen’s disapproval, and fled Whitehall to live quietly at Burrough. In her, he found peace and love—and knew it.

Mrs. Leigh was one of the noble English women of that age—devout, gentle, free from superstition or harshness. Yet she carried a shadow: memories of persecution. Twenty-five years earlier, she had seen Joan Waste burned at Derby, blind and innocent, and Mistress Joyce Lewis, a lady like herself, perish at the stake. Sometimes, in dreams, she heard her mother’s cries—pleading for mercy or strength to endure the fire. Her mother, a Yorkshire gentlewoman and former maid-of-honor to Queen Catherine, had been a close friend of Anne Askew. She had watched, horror-stricken, as Askew, crippled by torture, was carried to Smithfield in a chair, to die beside Lascelles. The heavens themselves seemed to mourn, thunder roaring and rain hissing on the flames.

This sorrow stayed with Mrs. Leigh all her life, deepening under Mary’s reign, when she had to flee for her life through the hills of the Peak District, hidden by loyal tenants and protected by her husband’s bold declaration: no constable, priest, bishop, or cardinal would lay hands on her. She survived—but the sadness remained, a dark mantle trailing behind her. That same sorrow touched her daughter, who, after marriage, gave herself wholly to God.

Yet her devotion was not withdrawn from the world. She served her husband, her children, the poor of Northam Town. She was respected by the gentry—Grenvilles, Fortescues, Chichesters—all who admired her husband’s wit and talents. She practiced self-denial, often drawing gentle rebukes from her husband—but never out of fear of God, nor to earn favor. Her faith was shaped by Luther’s teachings, by the “Alt-Deutsch Theologie,” a mystic theology the Reformer said he valued more than any book save Scripture and Augustine. It guided her by day and night.

Now, barely forty, she was widowed—still beautiful, still radiant with a calm that seemed divine. The dove of peace rested upon her words, her gestures, her presence. It was no wonder Sir Richard and Lady Grenville cherished her. No wonder her children adored her. And no wonder young Amyas, once the storm of grief passed, felt a new life begin. He realized: his mother would no longer act for him alone—he must now act for her.

So, the day after his father’s funeral, instead of returning home, he walked boldly into Sir Richard Grenville’s house and asked to see his godfather.

“You must be my father now, sir,” he said firmly.

Sir Richard looked at the boy’s broad, strong face and swore a mighty oath—by oak, ash, and thorn—that he would be father to him and brother to his mother, for Christ’s sake. Lady Grenville took the boy by the hand and walked home with him to Burrough. There, the two women embraced, weeping—one for loss, the other in foreboding. For Mrs. St. Leger knew well that her husband’s fiery spirit would not leave his body peacefully. Death, she prayed nightly, would find him sword in hand, on the field of duty and fame.

And so they vowed eternal sisterhood—and kept it. Life at Burrough continued as before. Amyas rode, shot, boxed, wandered the quay beside Sir Richard. Mrs. Leigh saw no need to change the training her husband had set for his son. It was enough that her elder son, Frank, had chosen the path she had secretly wished for both.

Frank—God’s gift to her pure love—had earned honor at home and abroad. First at Bideford school, then at Exeter College, where he befriended Sir Philip Sidney and others of promise. In 1572, on his way to Heidelberg, he stopped in Paris with letters to Walsingham at the English Embassy. There, he met Sidney again—and saved his own life, as Sidney did his, during the Massacre of St. Bartholomew’s Day.

At Heidelberg, he stayed two years, gaining respect from princes and scholars alike. He refused Sidney’s urging to go to Italy, choosing instead to become tutor to two young German princes. After living with them at their father’s court, he brought them down to Padua, writing home that he would “perfect them, according to his insufficiency, in all princely studies.”

Sidney returned to England, but Frank made friends wherever he went—carrying recommendations from princes, magnificos, and learned doctors who admired his learning, modesty, and virtue. He soaked in the wonders of Italy: debated poetry with Tasso, history with Sarpi, listened to Galileo’s bold theories, took his pupils to Venice to be painted by Veronese, saw Palladio’s palaces, the Rialto merchants, the Ragusan argosies. He watched Tintoretto paint “tempestuous glories o’er the scene.” By secret intercession, he entered Titian’s sacred studio—the old master, silver-bearded, undimmed-eyed, amidst his creations—where he spoke of Bellini, Raphael, Michelangelo, the building of St. Peter’s, the fire in Venice, the sack of Rome, kings and poets long gone, and showed the brush that Francis I once picked up for him.

He visited Rome—though forbidden to Sidney by Languet—and saw the Pope, whom Sidney called “a right good fellow.” He saw the Vatican frescoes, heard Palestrina conduct his music beneath St. Peter’s dome, and nearly fell in love with the melodies—until he remembered the prayers offered under that same dome for the murdered Protestants of Paris.

Finally, months before his father died, he returned his pupils to Germany, dismissed with rich gifts. Mrs. Leigh’s heart soared—her wanderer would come home. But within a month of her husband’s death, a letter arrived from Frank—describing the Alps, the valleys of the Waldenses, and his meeting with Stephanus Parmenius (Budaeus), a famed scholar. He had visited Geneva with Budaeus, heard the doctors debate, and disliked their harsh judgments, though admiring their subtlety. He wrote in Latin, full of quotations—yet the letter never reached its intended reader. The widow wept over it alone, and wept harder at the end, where Frank confessed he had set out with Budaeus down the Danube to Buda, to experience Hungarian learning before finishing his travels.

After that, he wrote again and again to his father—only to receive no reply for nearly two years. Fearing disaster, he rushed back to England, only to find his mother a widow, and his brother Amyas gone to the South Seas with Captain Drake of Plymouth.

Still, even then, Frank was not allowed to stay. Sir Richard, who abhorred idleness, sent him to Court to serve Lord Hunsdon. There, delicate and fair as Amyas was strong, he quickly gained favor through Carew, Sidney, and Leicester. He entered the queen’s household, basked in royal favor, and became a favorite of Gloriana’s court—surrounded by ladies, chivalry, and the elegance of Elizabethan life. He found Sidney again—now twenty-five, renowned across Europe: patron of scholars, counselor to warriors, confidant of William of Orange, Languut, Plessis du Mornay, and leaders of Protestantism. And he found that the son of a Devon squire was welcomed by the most favored of fortune and nature.

Mrs. Leigh submitted silently. “You took away my mastiff-pup,” she smiled at Sir Richard, “and now you must take my fair greyhound too.”

“Would you have your greyhound grow into a true Cotswold dog—capable of pulling down a ten-point stag—or a smooth-skinned poppet, led by Florence ladies with bells and farthingales?”

She accepted. Months later, she received a letter—through Sir Richard—from Queen Elizabeth herself. The queen thanked her for “the loan of that most delicate and flawless crystal, the soul of your excellent son,” praised him beyond measure, and exalted her above Cornelia: “Cornelia showed her jewels, but kept them. You, madam, have lent both your sons to your country and your queen. I am in your debt, and shall repay it as becomes both me and you.”

The letter, drenched in holy tears, was placed in her cedar chest beside Frank’s diplomas. She read the Latin aloud, though she understood none of it—hoping to find, somewhere, the phrase *excellentissimus Noster Franciscus Leighius Anglus*, the treasure of her heart.

But why did Amyas go to the South Seas?

Two reasons—each one powerful enough to send many a youth to far worse fates: first, a schoolmaster. Second, a beauty.

Vindex Brimblecombe, once a servant of Exeter College, Oxford—called “Sir Vindex” in the fashion of the time—was master of Bideford’s grammar school. He was, at heart, a kind and godly man, but long accustomed to punishing weaker boys. Such power, though useful for discipline, hardened the soul of the teacher. Over time, he grew crueler. When Amyas became fatherless, Sir Vindex felt a new duty—to care for the boy. But the only result was an increase in beatings—from two per week to one per day.

All the while, Amyas had never lost his dream of the sea. When he couldn’t walk the quay or sit on the pebble ridge at Northam, staring at the endless ocean, he drew ships and maps on his slate—imaginary islands, castles, dragons, gallant vessels with lances like porcupine quills, and little round o’s for heads—himself and his friends slaying the dragon, rescuing the princess.

One afternoon, he was deeply absorbed in such a drawing—when the other boys gathered around, laughing. Sir Vindex, as usual after dinner, slept soundly in his chair.

And so, the story goes, the boy’s dream took flight—into the real world.

OF TWO GENTLEMEN OF WALES, AND HOW THEY HUNTED WITH THE HOUNDS, AND YET RAN WITH THE DEER

“I know that Deformed; he has been a vile thief this seven years; he goes up and down like a gentleman: I remember his name.”—*Much Ado About Nothing.*

Amyas slept that night a restless and troubled sleep. His mother and Frank, watching over him, could see the storm of dreams behind his closed eyes. And no wonder. The day had been full of excitement, but it was the memory of John Oxenham—the lost sailor, the vanished adventurer—that clung to his thoughts like a shadow. All evening, seated in the bay-windowed room where he had last seen him, Amyas replayed every glance, every gesture, in his mind. He found himself wondering why he should care so deeply for a man he barely knew, until sleep came—and then the dream began.

He found himself sailing westward, chasing a tiny sail on the horizon—Oxenham’s ship. A terrible dread gripped him: if he did not catch up in time, something dreadful would happen. But the ship refused to move. Around her floated thick beds of sargasso weed, their long, snaking tendrils wrapping around the bow, dragging her down. He struggled, imagining speed, urging the sails forward—but nothing changed. The sun sank, and darkness swallowed the sea. Then the moon rose, cold and bright, and suddenly, Oxenham’s vessel loomed beside him.

Her sails were torn and flapping. Pitch oozed from her timbers. Her railings rotted away. And along the mainyard—dangling in a ghastly row—were dark figures, hanged men. From the yard-arm above, John Oxenham’s corpse stared down with grave, lifeless eyes. It beckoned, pointed—not ahead, but back—toward the course they had come. And when Amyas turned, there it was: the snow-capped peaks of the Andes glittering under the moonlight. He was in the South Seas again, America stretching between him and home.

The corpse still pointed, its lips trembling as though trying to speak, its eyes filled with agony and warning. And then Amyas woke, gasping, shouting into the silence. He lay in his little cove chamber at Burrough, the gray light of autumn morning already creeping through the window.

Feverish and wide-awake, he could not return to sleep. After an hour of tossing, he rose, dressed, and set off for his favorite pebble ridge, where the sea had built a natural breakwater of smooth boulders, two miles long, shaped by time like a work of human hands. There, beneath the crisp salt air, he stripped and plunged into the waves, diving deep, rolling in the foam, swimming with the strength of youth—until a voice called out from shore.

Looking up, he saw Eustace Leigh standing atop the ridge.

Amyas felt a pang of disappointment. He had been thinking only of Rose Salterne, dreaming of her all the way there. He didn’t want company now. Yet, not having seen his cousin in three years, he gave in to manners and scrambled ashore, dressing quickly while Eustace paced the grassy turf beyond.

Eustace was the son of Thomas Leigh, younger brother of Sir Richard’s family, who had severed ties with both kin and country by remaining a Catholic. Though born a Papist, he had once followed the Reformation under Edward VI, then returned to Rome under Mary. But when Elizabeth came to power, he refused to change again—honorable, perhaps, but stubborn. He lived quietly at Chapel House, perched on the Atlantic cliffs near Moorwinstow, far from the eye of the law. The penal statutes never touched him. For one thing, he never plotted rebellion—only practiced faith. Jesuits came and went without notice, celebrating mass in secret chambers, hiding in turrets, pretending martyrdom for sport. When the local parson complained, Sir Richard Grenville merely scolded him for being a “fantastical Puritan” and told him to mind his own business. So the priest, though offended, kept quiet—especially since Mr. Leigh paid his tithes regularly.

But the weight of conscience pressed heavily upon Thomas Leigh. He held lands once belonging to Hartland Abbey, and though he half-believed he was damned for keeping them, he couldn’t bring himself to give them up. Like many Catholics of the time, he compromised: he kept the wealth, secretly supported foreign priests, and sent his children abroad—some to be trained as seminary priests—to serve as sacrifices, offerings to God for his sins. One such child was Eustace.

And now, Eustace had returned from Rheims, where he had been taught not just theology, but the art of persuasion, secrecy, and influence. He was tall and fair, with a high forehead and small mouth, his face always striving to appear cheerful, even when it wasn’t. He smiled often—too often—because he believed joy was a duty. But his smile was stiff, forced, like a mask worn too long. Had he not been handsome, many might have thought him a skeleton dancing in chains.

He had come back in obedience to a royal proclamation requiring all Englishmen abroad to return within four months. He had arrived in Bideford the night before, on private business for the Catholic cause. Now he sat beside Amyas on the pebbles, studying him carefully from the corner of his eyes.

Amyas turned fully toward him, grinning broadly, and offered a firm handshake. Eustace took it eagerly, squeezing hard, his fingers moving violently as if trying to prove his joy. Their greetings were worlds apart.

“Hold on, old lad,” said Amyas, laughing. “You’ll break my arm. Where’ve you been?”

“Going to and fro in the earth, walking up and down in it,” replied Eustace with a faint, knowing smile.

“Like the devil, eh? Well, everyone has their model. How’s Uncle?”

And here, dear reader, lies the truth: Eustace feared Amyas more than any man. Not because he was stronger—though he was—but because Amyas carried himself with a calm, unshakable confidence. Eustace felt himself judged, despised even. They hadn’t met in three years, but Amyas had always treated him as if his beliefs were nonsense—“wind and moonshine,” he’d called them. Eustace knew it. He also knew Amyas was wrong. “He used to undervalue me,” he thought. “Let’s see if he thinks so now.”

And then, in secret, he prayed for mercy—for forgetting himself, for remembering that God’s glory, not his own pride, was the goal.

But let us not dwell too long on souls. Let me simply say this: Eustace was a man trying desperately to be good, following rules, measuring his steps, checking his heart like a weak swimmer testing his muscles. Amyas was different—he did what was right without thinking, guided not by doctrine but by instinct, as a child follows love.

And yet, Eustace was not evil. Only misled. His path had been shaped by Jesuit training, which twisted noble hearts into instruments of intrigue. Had he grown up differently, he might have stood beside his brothers—true Englishmen, loyal to queen and country—as one did in Ireland under St. Leger, or on the Crimean fields. But fate had chosen otherwise.

“Ah, my dearest cousin!” said Eustace. “I was so disappointed to arrive just a day too late to see your triumph! But I came straight to your home, learned from your mother you’d be here, and hurried down to greet you.”

“Well, old lad,” said Amyas, “it feels very natural to see you. I often thought of you pacing the deck at night. How is Uncle? Are the girls well? Is the old pony dead yet? And Dick the smith—how’s Nancy? Grown into a fine girl, I bet. Good grief, it seems like half a lifetime since I left.”

“And you truly thought of your poor cousin?” asked Eustace softly. “Know that I, too, prayed nightly for your safety—surely not without effect—before the saints, whose intercessions, I doubt not, preserved you.”

“Halt there, coz,” said Amyas. “If they’re half as good as we think, they’ll help me without asking.”

“They have helped you, Amyas.”

“Maybe. I’d have done the same for them, had I been in their place.”

“And do you not feel gratitude, then—especially to her, the star of the sea, the compassionate guide of sailors?”

“Humph,” said Amyas. “Here’s Frank. Let him answer.”

At that moment, Frank arrived. After greetings, he sat beside them.

“I say, brother,” said Amyas, “Eustace is already trying to convert me. Says I owe all my luck to the Blessed Virgin.”

“It may be,” said Frank, “but you owe it to the prayers of the purest virgin ever known—the one who commanded your voyage. Her sweet incense has risen daily for you, and for her sake, you were spared from flood and foe, so you might spread the fame of Elizabeth, our queen, champion of truth, justice, and freedom.”

Amyas chuckled—a loyal, hearty sound. Eustace smiled meekly, but replied with quiet venom:

“I am certain I speak the truth when I call my patroness a virgin undefiled.”

Both brothers frowned. Amyas, lying on his back among the stones, looked up at the gulls overhead.

“I wonder what the Frenchman I cut off at the Azores thinks now about all that.”

“Cut off a Frenchman’s head?” asked Frank.

“Yes, faith. I fleshed my sword there. Let me tell you. We were in a tavern—George Drake and I—and there sat this Frenchman, sword on the table, ready for a fight. Later I learned he was a notorious bully. He started shouting insults—about this, about that. But then, stirred by the devil, he launched into a dozen slanders against her majesty’s honor, one after another. I was ashamed to hear them, and ashamed to repeat them.”

“I’ve heard enough of such,” said Frank. “They come mostly from lewd rogues around the French ambassador—men bred in the corruption of the Medici court, where Queen Mary of Scots was schooled. To them, virtue is just a cover for vice. Let them bark. *Honi soit qui mal y pense* is our motto—and shall be forever.”

“But I didn’t let him bark,” said Amyas. “I grabbed him by the ears and dragged him out. He drew his sword. I drew mine. I nearly died that day. He didn’t fight like a Christian—no edge and buckler—but with some newfangled French trick—stabbing, lunging, stamping, tracing at me. I expected to be full of holes before I could close with him.”

“Thank God you’re safe,” said Frank. “I know that style. Dangerous.”

“Of course you do. I didn’t.”

“Well, I’ll teach you, lad—better than Rowland Yorke himself.”

“Rowland Yorke? Who’s he?”

“A reckless fellow in London making a fortune teaching fencing. As likely to die in a brawl as your Frenchman. But how did you escape?”

“He pierced my left arm before I could turn. That made me mad. I leapt on him, caught his wrist, and landed a clean blow. By luck, his head fell onto the table. End of slander.”

“So perish all her enemies!” said Frank.

Eustace, who had pretended not to listen, rose abruptly.

“I trust you do not count me among them?”

“As you speak, I do, coz,” said Frank. “But for your own sake, believe the true reports—those who live under her rule know her virtue as they know the sun rises. Don’t trust the lies of cowardly spies seeking favor with the Guises. Come, let’s say no more. Walk with us to Appledore, then home to breakfast.”

But Eustace declined. He had urgent business in Northam, then Bideford. So he left them to linger on the beach, then walk across the smooth green plain to the little fishing village of Appledore, where the Torridge and Taw rivers meet.

Yet Eustace lied. He told them he was going to Northam—but his real destination was Appledore. After passing the nearest house in Northam, he veered sharply left across the fields, murmuring prayers to the Virgin all the way. He traveled several miles out of his way—just as crooked spirits often do, especially Jesuits, who, history shows, rarely outwit themselves.

Meanwhile, Amyas and Frank walked straight across the turf, arriving at Appledore just in time to see what Eustace had gone to great lengths to hide: four horses standing outside the “Mariner’s Rest” Inn, saddled and ready. Three men were mounting—Eustace among them, and two strangers.

“There’s one lie already this morning,” growled Amyas. “He said he was going to Northam.”

“And we don’t know he hasn’t been,” Frank replied gently.

“You’re as bad a Jesuit as he is, helping him out.”

“He may have changed his mind.”

“Bless your pure imagination, my sweet boy,” said Amyas, mimicking his mother’s tone, laying a hand on Frank’s head. “Let’s step into this shop and buy a pennyworth of whipcord.”

“What do you want with whipcord?”

“To spin my top, of course.”

“Top? How long have you had a top?”

“I’ll buy one. Save my conscience. But I must see the end of this.”

So he pulled Frank into the shop, unseen by the group at the inn.

“What strange creatures has he brought now?” whispered Amyas. “Look at that three-legged fellow trying to mount—clawing the horse’s ribs like a cat scratching a tree!”

The man was tall, meek-faced, draped in flamboyant clothes, a huge feather in his hat, and carrying a sword so long and broad it seemed ill-suited to his frame.

“Young David in Saul’s armor,” said Frank. “He’d better not go in them—he hasn’t proven them.”

“Look—his third leg’s turning into a tail! Why doesn’t someone pull in half a yard of his belt?”

And thus they watched, amused and wary, as the shadows of secrets unfolded beneath the autumn sky.

“I could not love thee, dear, so much, Loved I not honor more.”—LOVELACE.

And what, all this while, has become of the fair one who broke so many hearts, yet whom I have not yet introduced to my readers?

She sat in the little farmhouse beside the mill, nestled deep within the green folds of Combe Valley, halfway between Stow and Chapel. There she brooded—not with anger, but with quiet melancholy—at being shut out from the grand affairs stirring in Bideford, forced into a kind of Martinmas Lent in this remote western glen. So solitary was her life that though she looked upon Eustace Leigh with no small measure of disfavor, and (being a devout Protestant) with considerable suspicion, she found herself unable to resist his company when he came down to the farm and its mill. He made it a point, on some pretext or other, to visit nearly every day.

Her uncle and aunt at first frowned upon these visits; the latter always taking care to be present whenever conversation turned. Yet Mr. Leigh was a gentleman’s son, a neighboring squire, and a good customer. Rose, too, was the daughter of wealth, while they were poor cousins—so quarreling with her would have been unseemly. And besides, the pretty maid, half by willfulness, half by charm, usually managed to get her own way wherever she went. She had even been wise enough to beg her aunt never to leave them alone—"for I could not bear the sight of Mr. Eustace," she said, "only I must have someone to talk with down here."

Her aunt, after pondering, concluded that she was only a simple countrywoman, that townsmen's ways were different, and that people knew their own business best. Thus, matters drifted along as they pleased.

Eustace, meanwhile, well aware that the difference in faith between him and Rose might prove the greatest obstacle to his affection, took great care to keep his private beliefs hidden. Instead of trying to convert the folk at the mill, he daily bought milk and flour from them, then gave it away to the old women of Moorwinstow, who remarked among themselves that, for a Papist, he was a remarkably godly young man. Eventually, after conferring with Campian and Parsons about certain political schemes then brewing, he decided they should all three attend church the following Sunday.

There, Messrs. Evan Morgans and Morgan Evans, having studied the rubrics beforehand, behaved with perfect orthodoxy. Poor Eustace, too, conducted himself so reverently that the pious folk were astonished. He returned home convinced he had fooled parson, clerk, and congregation alike—unaware, in his innocent cunning, that each good wife in the parish whispered to her neighbor: “He turned Protestant? The devil turned monk! He’s only after Mistress Salterne, the young hypocrite.”

Yet if the two Jesuits found it prudent, for the holy cause they served, to outwardly conform to the powers that be, they were no less active in secret, plotting their downfall.

Since April of the previous year, they had wandered across England like fugitives playing hide-and-seek. Now they lay quiet, awaiting news from Ireland to signal their next move—a great uprising in the West to sweep the throne from the stiff-necked, persecuting, excommunicated, illegitimate, profligate usurper who falsely called herself Queen of England.

They had long since convinced themselves—just as they do today, with a real Baconian disdain for sensible experience—that the heart of England still beat for Rome, and that the nation stood on the brink of returning to the Catholic fold, ready to drag Elizabeth in chains before the rightful Lord of Creation, the Old Man of the Seven Hills. This hope, ever just ahead of them, always just out of reach like the rainbow touching earth, they proclaimed in language that terrified old Mr. Leigh.

One day, as Eustace entered his father’s private room after his usual visit to the mill, he heard voices raised in debate—Parsons, as usual, blustering; Mr. Leigh peevishly protesting; and Campian, the gentlest of men, attempting to soothe the storm. Eustace, for the sake of the cause, paused outside and listened.

“My excellent sir,” said Mr. Leigh, “does not your very presence here show how I am affected toward the holy cause of the Catholic faith? But I cannot forget that I am an Englishman.”

“And what is England?” demanded Parsons. “A heretic and schismatic Babylon, whereof it is written, ‘Come out of her, my people, lest you be partaker of her plagues.’ Yea, what is a country? An arbitrary division of territory by princes who are naught and come to naught. They are created by the people’s will; their existence depends on the sanction of Him to whom all power is given in heaven and earth—our Holy Father the Pope. Take away the latter, and what is a king? The people who made him may unmake him.”

“My dear sir,” replied Mr. Leigh, “recollect that I have sworn allegiance to Queen Elizabeth!”

“Yes, sir, you have,” said Parsons. “And as I have shown at length in my writings, you were absolved from that allegiance the moment the bull of Pius the Fifth declared her a heretic and excommunicate, thereby forfeiting all dominion. I tell you, sir, what I thought you already knew—that since 1569, England has had no queen, no magistrates, no laws, no lawful authority whatsoever. To swear fealty to any English magistrate, or plead in an English court, is to disobey the apostolic precept: ‘How dare you go to law before the unbelievers?’ Rebellion is not merely permitted, sir—it is a duty.”

“Take care, sir!” cried Mr. Leigh. “For God’s sake, take care! Right or wrong, I cannot allow such words in my house. For the sake of my wife and children, I cannot!”

“My dear brother Parsons,” interposed Campian, “deal more gently with the flock. Your opinion, though probable in the eyes of most of our order, is hardly safe here. The opposite view is at least safe enough for Mr. Leigh to excuse his conscience. After all, are we not sent hither to proclaim precisely this—to relieve the souls of good Catholics from burdens they find too heavy?”

“Yes,” said Parsons, half-sulkily, “to allow all Balaams who will to sacrifice to Baal, while calling themselves by the name of the Lord.”

“My dear brother,” said Campian, “have I not often reminded you that Naaman was allowed to bow in the house of Rimmon? Can we then complain of the office to which the Holy Father has appointed us—to declare to such as Mr. Leigh his especial grace, whereby the bull of Pius the Fifth (on whose soul God have mercy!) shall bind the queen and heretics, but not the Catholics—at least as long as the present tyranny prevents the pious purposes of the bull?”

“Be it so,” said Parsons. “But observe, Mr. Leigh, that our brother Campian confesses this to be a tyranny. Observe that the bull still binds the so-called queen, and that she and her magistrates remain usurpers, nonentities, shadows of a shade. And observe this: when that which is lawful is excused to the weak, it remains no less lawful to the strong. The seven thousand who did not bow to Baal did not slay his priests; but Elijah did—and won a good reward. If the rest of Israel sinned not in sparing Eglon, yet Ehud’s deed was justified by all laws, human and divine.”

“For Heaven’s sake, do not speak thus!” cried Mr. Leigh. “Or I must leave the room. What have I to do with Ehud and Eglon, with slaughter and tyranny? Our queen is a good queen, if Heaven would grant her repentance and turn her to the true faith. I have never troubled myself about religion, nor anyone else I know in the West Country.”

“You forget Mr. Trudgeon of Launceston, father,” interposed Eustace, slipping in unnoticed. “And poor Father Mayne.”

“Yes,” added Campian softly, “your West of England has also known martyrs, as London has known Story.”

“What, young malapert?” roared poor Leigh, turning sharply on his son, glad to vent his irritation on someone. “Are you against me too, with a murrain on you? And pray, what brought Cuthbert Mayne to the gallows, or turned Mr. Trudgeron—always a foolish hot-head—out of house and home, but just such treasonable talk as Mr. Parsons holds in my house, to make a beggar of me and my children, as he will before he’s done?”

“The Blessed Virgin forbid!” said Campian.

“The Blessed Virgin forbid? But you must help her forbid it, Mr. Campian. We should never have had the law of 1571 against bulls, Agnus Deis, and blessed grains, if the Pope’s bull of 1569 hadn’t made them treason, preventing a poor soul from saving himself in the true Church without risking his neck by denying the queen’s authority.”

“What, sir?” almost roared Parsons. “Do you dare speak ill of the edicts of the Vicar of Christ?”

“I? No. I didn’t. Who says I did? All I meant was—I’m sure, Mr. Campian, you’re a reasonable man. Speak for me.”

“Mr. Leigh only meant,” said Campian, “that the Holy Father’s prudent intentions have been thwarted by the stubbornness and invincible misunderstanding of the heretics. What was meant for the good of oppressed Catholics has been perverted to their harm.”

“And thus, reverend sir,” said Eustace, eager to regain his father’s favor, “my father blames not the Pope—Heaven forbid—but the pravity of his enemies.”

“And it is for this very reason,” said Campian, “that we have brought with us the present merciful explanation of the bull.”

“I’ll tell you what, gentlemen,” said Mr. Leigh, growing bolder as his opponent seemed inclined to peace. “I don’t think the declaration was needed. After the new law of 1571, it wasn’t enforced until Mayne and Trudgeon made fools of themselves—six years later. A few offenders were warned and released. But even that didn’t happen here. It need not happen now—unless you put my son here (though you’ll never put me, I warrant) upon some deed better left undone, bringing us all to shame.”

“Your son, sir, if not publicly vowed to God, has, I hope, a due sense of that inward vocation we’ve seen in him. He reverences his spiritual fathers too well to listen to the temptations of his earthly father.”

“What, sir? Will you teach my son to disobey me?”

“Your son is ours also, sir. This is strange language from one who owes a debt to the Church—charitably believed to be paid through his child.”

These last words struck Mr. Leigh hard. He burst out, swearing fiercely at Parsons, who stood foaming with rage.

“A plague upon you, sir, and a black assizes for you—you’ll hang yet! Do you mean to taunt me in my own house with that Hartland land? You’d better go back and ask those who sent you where the dispensation is—the one they promised years ago, and kept putting off, till they’ve taken my money, my son, my conscience, and now, I vow before all saints, seem to want my head as well. God help me!”—and tears filled his eyes.

Now it was Eustace’s turn to rise. Though he was an Englishman and a gentleman, he spoke kindly but firmly:

“Courage, my dearest father. Remember I am still your son, not a Jesuit yet. Whether I ever become one will depend largely on how you are treated by these reverend gentlemen—for whom I, having brought them here, must stand surety.”

If a powder-barrel had exploded in their faces, they could not have been more stunned. Campian stared at Parsons, and Parsons at Campian. At last, the stouter-hearted recovered:

“Sir! Do you know the curse on those who, after putting their hand to the plough, look back?”

Eustace was one of those impulsive men—lacking moral courage—who could raise the devil but never fight him once roused. He tried to dismiss his words with winks and gestures toward his father, as if to say he was only calming the old man. But Campian was too startled, Parsons too furious, to notice. Eustace had to carry it through.

“All I read, Father Parsons,” he said, “is that such are not fit for the kingdom of God—of which high honor I’ve long felt myself unworthy. I have doubts about my vocation. In the meantime, I have not forgotten I am a citizen of a free country.”

And so saying, he took his father’s arm and walked out.

His final words struck the Jesuits hard. They had awakened the painful truth—daily thrust upon them since then, yet never learned—that all their scholastic cunning, plots, bulls, pardons, indulgences—here, across the Channel—were nothing but an enchanter’s cloud-castle, vanishing at the touch of the constable’s staff. “A citizen of a free country!” That was the rub. They exchanged glances, more perplexed than ever.

At last, Parsons spoke.

“There’s a woman in the wind. I’ll lay my life on it. I saw him blush crimson yesterday when his mother asked if some Rose Salterne was still nearby.”

“A woman? Well, the spirit may be willing, though the flesh be weak. We’ll inquire. The youth may serve us well as a layman. If anything happens to his elder brother—may the saints protect him—he inherits wealth. Meanwhile, perhaps dear brother Parsons sees the need to alter our tactics.”

And so began a long discussion between the two, sent together under the Jesuit rule: “Two are better than one”—so that Campian might temper Parsons’ fervor, and Parsons stir Campian’s gentleness, each complementing the other. And indeed, each often advised nearly the opposite of the other, “without the danger,” as their writers noted, “of seeming changeable or inconsistent.”

The result was that a day or two later—after Mr. Leigh and Eustace had made amends and things ran smoothly—Father Campian asked Father Francis, the household chaplain, as a special favor, to allow him to hear Eustace’s confession the following Friday.

Poor Father Francis dared not refuse. He consented with a sigh, knowing full well the intent: to extract family secrets that would weaken his influence and strengthen the Jesuits’. For throughout England, the regular clergy and the Jesuits lived in armed neutrality, barely short of open war—as it became during James I’s reign, when these gentle missionaries, by their moral torments, literally hunted to death the poor Popish bishop of Hippopotamus (that is, London).

Campion heard Eustace’s confession. Through subtle questions—easy to imagine to those familiar with the confessional—he discovered satisfactorily that Eustace was, as Campian would have said, “in love.” Though I question whether the term fits any facts poor prurient Campian uncovered—or indeed knew how to uncover—since a swine has no eye for pearls. Still, he had learned enough. He smiled, and set to work to discover who the lady was.

Had he simply said, “I understand your pain. If your desires are reasonable, lawful, possible, I will help you with all my heart,” he might have gained the young man’s confidence and saved himself hours of effort. But instinctively, he chose the crooked, suspicious path—assuming the worst, as one trained to see the basest motives as the engine of all human action. He began his moral probing with delicate questions, which Eustace dodged in every way, knowing the good father was too clever for him, and that he would eventually yield.

Yet like a rabbit squealing around the weasel, knowing it must leap into its jaws, Eustace parried, pretended ignorance, surprise, scrupulousness, even anger. Each question—about her marriage, creed, nationality—brought Campian closer to his goal. Finally, when Campian, finding the situation not entirely hopeless, asked about her worldly wealth, Eustace saw a way out and seized it.

“Even if she’s a heretic, she’s heiress to one of Devon’s wealthiest merchants.”

“Ah!” said Campian thoughtfully. “And she is but eighteen, you say?”

“Only eighteen.”

“Ah, well, my son, there is time. She may be reconciled to the Church. Or you may change.”

“I shall die first.”

“Ah, poor lad. Well, she may be reconciled, and her wealth may serve the cause of Heaven.”

“And it shall serve. Only absolve me, and let me be at peace. Let me have her,” he pleaded. “I do not want her wealth—not I! Let me have her, even for one year, one month, one day—and all the rest—money, fame, talents, yea, my life itself—are at the service of Holy Church. I shall glory in showing my devotion through some special sacrifice—some desperate deed. Prove me now, and see what I will not do!”

So Eustace was absolved. Then Campian added:

“This is well, my son. For there is a task now—one that may cost life.”

“Prove me!” Eustace exclaimed impatiently.

“Here is a letter brought me last night—no matter from whence. You can read it better than I. I longed to show it to you, but feared my son had become—”

“You feared wrongly, then, my dear Father Campian.”

So Campian translated the cipher:

“To Evan Morgans, gentleman, at Mr. Leigh’s house in Moorwinstow, Devonshire. News may be had by one who goes to Clovelly shore any evening after the 25th of November, at dead low tide, and watches for a boat rowed by one with a red beard, speaking Portuguese. If asked, 'How many?' answer, 'Eight hundred and one.' Take his letters and read. If the shore is watched, let the man show a light three times in a safe place under the cliff above the town; below is dangerous. Farewell, expect great things!”

“I will go,” said Eustace. “Tomorrow is the 25th. I know a sure and easy path. Your friend knows these shores well.”

“Ah! What is it we do not know?” said Campian, smiling mysteriously. “And now?”

“And now,” said Eustace, “to prove my trust in you, you shall come with me, and see—her, the lady I spoke of. Judge for yourself whether my fault is venial.”

“Ah, my son,” said Campian, “have I not absolved you already? What have I to do with fair faces? Nevertheless, I will come—to show you I trust you, and perhaps to reclaim a heretic, save a lost soul. Who knows?”

So they set out together. As fate ordained, they reached the top of the hill between Chapel and Stow Mill just as Mistress Rose Salterne appeared down the lane, radiant in a new scarlet hood. From beneath it, her large dark eyes flashed soft lightning straight into Eustace’s heart. She tripped up to them on delicate ankles and tiny feet—tall, lithe, graceful, a true West-country lass. As she passed with a blush and a curtsey, even Campian glanced back at the innocent creature, her long dark curls, in the fashion of the time, flowing down past her waist, ensnaring Eustace’s soul in their glossy nets.

“There!” whispered Eustace, trembling. “Can you excuse me now?”

“I had excused you long ago,” said the kindhearted father. “Alas, that so much fair red and white should have been created only as a feast for worms!”

“A feast for gods, you mean!” cried Eustace, struck by the absurdity. Then, as if to escape scolding for his heathenry:

“Will you let me return for a moment? I’ll follow you—let me go!”

Campian saw it was useless to object, and nodded. Eustace darted away, crossed a field, and met Rose at the next bend.

She started, giving a little shriek.

“Mr. Leigh! I thought you had gone on.”

“I came back to speak to you—Rose—Mistress Salterne, I mean.”

“To me?”

“To you I must speak—tell you all—or die!” He pressed close. She shrank back, frightened.

“Do not move! Do not go! I implore you! Rose, only hear me!” He seized her hand fiercely, pouring out his love, showering her with every extravagant compliment he could muster.

Much of what he said she had heard before. But the tremor in his voice, the fire in his eyes, stirred her instinctively.

“Let me go!” she said. “You are too rough, sir!”

“Ay!” he said, now gripping both her hands. “Rougher, perhaps, than the gay gallants of Bideford who serenade you, write sonnets, send posies. Rougher, but more loving, Rose! Do not turn away! I shall die if you look away! Tell me—now, here—this moment—before we part—if I may love you!”

“Go away!” she answered, struggling, bursting into tears. “This is too rude. If I am but a merchant’s daughter, I am God’s child. Remember I am alone. Leave me! Go! Or I will call for help!”

Eustace had read somewhere that such words were mere facons de parler—signs she meant no harm, intended no call for aid. So he gripped her hands tighter, gazed into her face with hungry eyes. But she was serious. A loud shriek made him realize—if he wished to preserve his reputation—he must flee. Yet there was one question for which he would risk his life.

He darted back, catching up with Campian just as the sun dipped behind the hills.

“It was among the ways of good Queen Bess,
Who ruled as well as ever mortal can, sir,
When she was stogg'd, and the country in a mess,
She was wont to send for a Devon man, sir.”

—West Country Song.

The morning dawned bright and clear, yet Amyas Leigh was nowhere to be found. Not that he had fled into despair or wandered off to weep by the Torridge’s banks—he had simply ridden away, Frank discovered, to visit Sir Richard Grenville at Stow. His mother, quick to sense his intent, understood at once: Amyas had gone seeking a commission in the Irish army. Without hesitation, she sent Frank after him, charged with bringing him back and compelling him to reconsider.

Frank mounted his horse and rode ten miles through winding lanes and open moorland. With no inns along the way—fewer even today—he turned down the hill toward Clovelly Court, hoping to find shelter and refreshment under the hospitable roof of Mr. Cary, the local squire, in the manner so common among gentlemen of that time.

Stepping boldly into the long, dim hall of the court—like Menelaus shouting into the gathering—he was met immediately by the towering figure of Amyas, seated at the great oak table, half-buried in a pasty. His face alternated between being lost in the pastry and buried within it, as if sorrow had only sharpened his appetite. Opposite him, young Will Cary knelt on the bench, elbows resting on the table, speaking earnestly and fiercely in a low voice.

“Hillo! lad,” Amyas called out, lifting his head. “Come here and rescue me from this fire-eater! I swear he’ll kill me unless I let him kill someone else first.”

“Ah, Mr. Frank!” said Will, who, like most young men of the region, held Frank in high regard—seeing him as a paragon of fashion, learning, and chivalry. “Welcome! I’ve been longing for you. I wanted your counsel on half a dozen matters. Sit down and eat. There’s ale.”

“None so early, thank you,” replied Frank.

“Ah, no!” Amyas muttered, lowering his head into his tankard before mimicking Frank with exaggerated solemnity. “Avoid strong ale in the morning. It heats the blood, thickens the animal spirits, and clouds the cerebrum with frenzied, lymphatic idols that obscure the pure light of reason. Eh? Young Plato, young Daniel, come hither to judgment! And yet—though I can’t see through the bottom of this tankard already—I can still see plainly enough that Will shall not fight.”

“Shall I not?” Will shot back, eyes flashing. “Who says so? Mr. Frank, I appeal to you—only hear!”

“We are in the judgment-seat,” Frank declared, settling himself beside the pasty. “Proceed, appellant.”

“Well,” Will began, “I told Amyas that Tom Coffin of Portledge—I will not endure him any longer.”

“Let him be,” Amyas said, chewing thoughtfully. “He stood very well on his own feet when I last saw him.”

“Plague on you, keep quiet!” Will snapped. “Does he have the right to look at me the way he does whenever I pass?”

“That depends on how he looks,” Amyas replied. “A cat may look at a king—provided she doesnno take him for a mouse.”

“Oh, I know how he looks—and what he means. He shall stop, or I will stop him. The other day, when I mentioned Rose Salterne—”

“Ate’s apple again!” Frank groaned.

“Never mind what I said,” Will continued. “He burst out laughing in my face. Is that not cause enough for a quarrel? Worse than that—I know he wrote a sonnet and sent it to her by a market woman. What right has he to write poetry when I cannot? It’s not fair play, Mr. Frank—or I am a Jew, a Spaniard, and a Papist all at once—it’s not!” And with that, he slammed his fist on the table, sending plates clattering.

“My dear knight of the burning pestle,” Frank said, smiling, “I have a plan—a device, a resolution, according to the noblest rules of chivalry. Let us fix a day and summon by drumbeat all young gentlemen under thirty living within fifteen miles of the dwelling of that peerless Oriana.”

“And all apprentices too,” Amyas called out from behind his pasty.

“And all apprentices,” Frank agreed. “The bold lads shall fight first with quarterstaves in Bideford Market until every head is broken; then, whoever remains unharmed must pay the penalty for the cowardice of their neck. After that grand tournament—greater than Tottenham’s flea-bite and batrachomyomachy—”

“Confound you and your long words!” Will cried. “I know you’re mocking me.”

“Pazienza, Signor Cavaliere,” Frank said gently. “What follows is no mockery—but bloody, warlike truth. Then, all young gentlemen shall gather in a field—on sand, or better yet, in a bog, where no one can flee if knee-deep in peat. Stripped to our shirts, armed with rapiers of equal length and temper, each shall slay his man, catch who can, and the victors shall fight again—like valiant gamecocks—until all are dead and freed from their sorrows. Then, the last survivor, lamenting before heaven and earth the cruelty of our Fair Oriana and the slaughter her basilisk eyes have caused, shall fall gracefully upon his sword and end the woes of our love-lorn generation. *Placetne Domini?* as they used to say in Oxford’s Senate.”

“Really,” Will said, “this is too much.”

“So is,” Frank added, “your fighting Mr. Coffin with anything longer than a bodkin.”

“Bodkins are too short for such fierce Bobadils,” Amyas interjected. “They’d close in too quickly—we’d end up wrestling after the first blow.”

“Then let them fight with squirts across the market-place,” Frank declared. “For by heaven and the queen’s laws, they shall fight with nothing else.”

“My dear Mr. Cary,” Frank said, suddenly shifting tone to one of gentle sincerity, “do not think I cannot feel your pain, nor that I, too, have not known the sting of love and the sharper sting of jealousy. But oh, Mr. Cary—does it not seem awful to waste the divine wrath—so rightly called by Plato the root of all virtue—upon your own petty quarrel? That wrath, given to you like all else, is meant to be spent in service to her whom all wicked souls fear and all noble ones adore—our peerless queen. Who dares, while she reigns over England, claim sword or courage as his own? Are there no Spaniards to conquer, no wild Irish to free from tyranny, that two gentlemen of Devon must waste their blades in each other’s hearts?”

“By heaven!” Amyas exclaimed. “Frank speaks like a book. And for my part, I believe Christian gentlemen should leave love quarrels to bulls and rams.”

“And the heir of Clovelly,” Frank smiled, “may find nobler examples to follow than the stags in his own deer park.”

“Well,” Will said, humbled, “you are a great scholar, Mr. Frank, and speak like one. But gentlemen must fight sometimes—or where would their honor be?”

“I speak,” Frank replied, a touch proud, “not merely as a scholar, but as a gentleman—one who has fought, and once killed a man (on whose soul may God have mercy). Yet I boast that I have never fought in my own quarrel, nor do I trust that I ever shall. For while there is nothing nobler than fighting for those we love, to fight for oneself is beneath a Christian man—unless refusal means certain death or dishonor. Even then, it may be (though I would not burden any conscience) better not to resist evil, but to overcome it with good.”

“And I can tell you, Will,” Amyas said, “I fear no ghosts. But when I cut off that Frenchman’s head, I said to myself, ‘If he had been slandering me instead of her Majesty, I’d expect to see that head lying on my pillow every night.’”

“God forbid!” Will shuddered. “But what shall I do? Tomorrow, I go to the market—even if it were choked with Coffins, and a ghost in each one.”

“Leave it to me,” Amyas said. “I have my own plan, as does scholar Frank here. If there’s a quarrel tomorrow, see if I don’t—”

“Well, you’re both good fellows,” Will said, rising. “Let’s have another tankard.”

“And drink to Mr. Coffin and all gallant lads of the North,” Frank said. “Now, to business. I must take this runaway youth home to his mother. If he won’t go quietly, I have orders to carry him across my saddle.”

“I hope your horse has a strong back,” Amyas laughed. “But I must go on to see Sir Richard, Frank. All jesting aside, my mind is made up.”

“Stop,” Cary said. “You must stay tonight. First, for fellowship; second, because I need the wisdom of our Phoenix—our oracle, our paragon.” He leaned close. “Mr. Frank, can you decipher this for me? Speak low—here comes my father. You’d best give me the letter again.”

“Eh? Company here?” the old squire called, entering. “Young men, always welcome—especially such as you. Would there were more of your sort in these dirty times! How is your good mother, Frank? Where have I been, Will? To the house-farm, checking the cattle. That sheeted heifer of Prowse’s is all wrong—her coat stares like a hedgehog’s. Tell Jewell to bring her in before night. Then up the forty acres—two coveys sprang, and I took a leash. The Irish hawk flies wild as any haggard and won’t make a bird. Had to hand her to Tom and take the little peregrine. Give me a Clovelly hawk against the world. And—heigh ho—I’m starving! Half-past twelve and dinner not served? What, Master Amyas, spoiling your appetite with strong ale? Better have tried sack, lad. Have some now with me.”

With that, the worthy old man settled onto a bench by the chimney, placed his hawk on a perch above his head, while his cockerels curled near the warm peat ashes. His son began removing his boots, careful not to disturb his father’s corns.

“Come, Master Amyas,” he said, “a pint of white wine and sugar, and a bit of shoeing-horn to start. Some pickled prawns, or a rasher off the coals to whet your appetite?”

“Thank you,” Amyas replied. “But I’ve drunk many foreign liquors in the past three years—better and worse—and none compare to good ale. It needs neither shoeing-horn before nor after. It takes care of itself—and of honest stomachs, I think.”

“You speak like a book, boy,” old Cary chuckled. “And what good comes of these newfangled hot wines and aqua vitae that’ve come since the wars? Only madness and fever in the blood.”

“I fear we haven’t seen the end of it,” Frank said. “My friends in the Netherlands write that our men are falling into a swinish habit of drinking like the Hollanders. Heaven grant they don’t bring the custom home.”

“A man must drink,” Amyas said, “or die of ague in those vile swamps. When they return here, they won’t need it.”

“Heaven grant it,” Frank said. “I’d hate to see Devonshire become a drunken county. Many of our men serve with Mr. Champernoun.”

“Ah,” Cary nodded. “There, as in Ireland, we prove her Majesty’s words true: Devonshire is her right hand, and its young men like arrows in the giant’s hand.”

“They may well be,” his son said, “when some of them are giants themselves—like my tall schoolmate opposite.”

“He’ll be up and doing again soon,” old Cary said.

“And so I shall,” Amyas declared. “I’ve been devising brave deeds—enchanters to bind, dragons to choke, empires to conquer—though not in Holland.”

“You do?” Will asked sharply, sensing more than he heard.

“Yes,” Amyas said, brushing it off. “I go to what Raleigh calls the Land of the Nymphs. Another month, and I hope to be abroad in Ireland.”

“Abroad?” old Cary said. “Call it rather at home. Ireland is full of Devon men from end to end. You’ll find friends everywhere. George Bourchier commands in Munster, Warham St. Leger is marshal, George Carew serves Lord Grey of Wilton—poor Peter Carew fell at Glendalough. After last year’s defeat, when that villain Desmond cut off Herbert and Price, six hundred Devon men filled the ranks, led by Arthur Fortescue. So Devon holds her head high in Ireland, as in the Low Countries and the Spanish Main.”

“And where,” Amyas asked, “is Davils of Marsland? He taught me trout fishing when I stayed at Stow. Is he in Ireland too?”

“Ah, my lad,” Cary said, “that’s a sad tale. I thought all England knew it.”

“You forget, sir,” Amyas said, “I’m a stranger. Surely he’s not dead?”

“Murdered foully, lad! Like a dog—by the man he treated as his son, who pretended to call him father.”

“His blood avenged?” Amyas asked, eyes blazing.

“No, by heaven, not yet! Wait—don’t shout. I’m growing old. Let me tell it my way. Last July—was it not, Will?—Saunders arrived in Ireland, one of those Jesuit foxes, as the Pope’s legate, with money, bulls, and a banner blessed by the Pope. With him, James Fitzmaurice—the same man who swore on his knees to Perrott at Kilmallock to be loyal to Queen Elizabeth, swearing by saints and all his Irish howling, until Perrott told me he had to cover his ears. Well, Fitzmaurice had tried the King of France, got nothing but laughter, so he went to the Most Catholic King, promising to unite Ireland to Spain and restore Popery. And the King, thinking Ireland better in his hands than the Pope’s bastard, outfitted him and sent him off—on an errand like Stukely’s. Though I say, for Devon’s honor, if Stukely lived like a fool, he died like an honest man.”

“Sir Thomas Stukely dead too?” Amyas asked.

“Wait, lad. You’ll hear that tragedy later. Where was I? Ah—Fitzmaurice and the Jesuits landed at Smerwick, three ships, chose a site for a fort, blessed it with holy water and ritual washings to cleanse it of heretic rule. Meanwhile, a Courtenay—of Haccombe, was it? Or Boconnock? Silence, Will—I’ll recall. Yes, Haccombe—was anchored nearby in a warship, seized the three vessels, cutting off the Dons from the sea. John and James Desmond, with a rabble, joined the Spaniards. Earl Desmond refused to join but wouldn’t fight, waiting to side with the winner. Then came poor Davils, sent by the Lord Deputy to command the Desmonds, in the queen’s name, to attack the Spaniards. Some say it was rash—but I say, what better could be done? Everyone knows Davils was the stoutest, shrewdest soldier alive. The young Desmonds looked to him as their father. But he learned what it meant to trust Englishmen turned Irish.

The Desmonds suddenly realized the Spaniards were desperate Paladins—madness to meddle, even at five to one. Poor Davils, seeing no fight in them, returned for help and slept that night at Tralee. Arthur Carter of Bideford, St. Leger’s lieutenant, a soldier as stout as Davils himself, shared his bed. Their lacquey-boy—now serving Sir Richard at Stow—slept at their feet. But in the dead of night, who should enter but James Desmond, sword drawn, with a dozen ruffians behind him, glibs over their faces, skenes in hand. Davils leapt up, asking only, ‘What is the matter, my son?’ The traitor, without letting him pray, struck him naked, crying, ‘Thou shalt be my father no longer, nor I thy son! Thou shalt die!’ Then all attacked. The boy jumped to shield his master, received several skene thrusts, and fell—dead or dying. Captain Carter and Davils were truly dead. God reward them! Then the ruffians slaughtered every Englishman in the house—save the boy, who crawled out wounded through a window. Desmond, covered in blood, returned to the Spaniards, boasting, ‘There! Take that as proof of my faithfulness to you.’ That, my lad, was the end of Henry Davils—and the fate of all who trust the faith of wild savages.”

“I’d ride a hundred miles to see that Desmond hanged!” Amyas said, tears streaming. “Poor Mr. Davils! And now—what of Sir Thomas?”

“Your brother must tell you that, lad. I’m somewhat breathless.”

“And I have a right,” Frank said, smiling, “to tell it. Do you know I nearly became Earl of the Bog of Allen and a peer of the realm to King Buoncompagna, son and heir to Pope Gregory XIII?”

“No, surely!”

“As I am a gentleman. When I was in Rome, I often saw poor Stukely. He offered me this and more, on behalf of the Pope, if I’d only reconcile to the Catholic Church and join the invasion of Ireland.”

“Poor deluded heretic!” Will said. “To lose an earldom for such silly loyalty!”

“It’s no jest,” Frank said. “I saw him often. I couldn’t believe he was in his right mind—so mad with vanity and ambition. Yet in private, he was as honorable a gentleman as any. Still, he sailed for Ireland with eight hundred Spaniards and Italians. And yes—I know the King of Spain paid their expenses. Marquis Vinola—James Buoncompagna—remained in Rome, preferring Stukely conquer Ireland for him while he guarded the family treasures. I saw him off at Civita Vecchia.
