Though younger, I felt compelled, as a gentleman and a Devon man, to warn him about his duty to the queen and the honor of his country. We argued fiercely. God forgive me if I spoke too harshly—I never saw him again.”

“Too harshly to an open traitor, Frank? Why not run him through?”

“Nay,” Frank said. “I had no clean life for Sundays, so I couldn’t waste my weekday one. As for England’s welfare, I knew he’d do little harm—and told him so. At that, he went mad with pride, swearing he’d have fought me then, if the wind hadn’t favored sailing. I said I was ready whenever he wished. He left, saying, ‘It’s a pity, sir, I cannot fight you now. When next we meet, it will be beneath my dignity to measure swords with you.’”

“I suppose he expected to return a prince,” Amyas said. “Heaven knows. I bear him no ill will. He’s paid all debts now.”

“How did he die?”

“On his voyage, he stopped in Portugal. King Sebastian was sailing for Africa with Mohammed, Prince of Fez, to aid King Abdallah. He persuaded Stukely to join him. Some thought both he and the Spaniards didn’t want the Pope’s son ruling Ireland. Others said he thought an island too small for his ambition—must conquer a continent. I know not why, but he went. They faced heavy seas. Upon landing, many soldiers were seasick. Stukely wisely advised waiting two or three days to recover. But Don Sebastian was mad for battle—wanted his veni, vidi, vici. Instead, he got veni, vidi, perii. Sebastian, Abdallah, and his son Mohammed all died in the first battle at Alcasar. Stukely, surrounded and overwhelmed, fought until he could fight no more, then died heroically—wounded only in front. May God have mercy on his soul!”

“Ah,” Amyas said. “We heard of the battle near Lima, but not of poor Stukely.”

“That last,” old Cary said, “was a Popish prayer, Master Frank.”

“Most worshipful sir,” Frank replied, “surely you wouldn’t wish God not to have mercy on his soul?”

“No—eh? Of course not. But it’s settled now—he’s dead, poor fellow.”

“Certainly, my dear sir. And you can’t help feeling fond of him still.”

“Eh? Why, I should be a brute if I weren’t. He and I were schoolfellows—though he was younger. I thrashed him many times. One cannot help having tenderness for a man after that. Besides, we hunted together in Exmoor, and drank royal nights in Ilfracombe—mad young blades. Fond of him? I’d sooner lose my forefinger than see him go to the dogs.”

“Then, my dear sir,” Frank said, “if you still feel for him despite his faults, how can you doubt God might feel for him too? For my part,” he added, “without believing in Popish Purgatory, I cannot help holding with Plato that heroic souls—those lacking but little of greatness—are disciplined afterward into better minds. Perhaps, as some ancients believed, transmigrated into animals they resembled in spirit. If Sir Thomas Stukely’s soul now animates a lion, I’d say he’d be a valiant, royal lion—and likely grow ashamed and penitent for being nothing more than a lion.”

“What now, Master Frank? I don’t trouble my head with such things. I say Stukely was a good-hearted fellow at heart. If you plague me with your dialectics and college quips, you shall have no more sack, sir. But here come the servants—and I hear the cook knocking for dinner.”

After a few madrigals and an Italian song by Master Frank—both sung sweetly—the ladies rose and withdrew. Then Will Cary drew his chair close to Frank’s and slipped a crumpled letter into his hand.

“This was left for me,” he whispered, “by a countryman this morning. Read it and tell me what to do.”

Frank opened it and read:

“Mister Cary, be you wary
By deer park end to-night.
Yf Irish ffoxe com out of rocks
Grip and hold hym tight.”

“I would have shown it to my father,” Will said, “but—”

Far from the bustling shores of England’s eastern coasts, where the sea meets the land with gentle lullabies, lies a wild and solitary stretch of coastland—where Devon and Cornwall meet in shadowed cliffs and silent coves. Here, under the silver glow of an October moon, the Atlantic breathes its ancient secrets into hidden valleys carved by time and tide.

It was here, in one such secluded glen, that Rose Salterne stood trembling on a shingle beach, her heart pounding like a drum beneath her thin dress. The night was still, the air crisp with autumn chill, and above her, the stars blinked coldly over the dark water. Before her stretched a narrow inlet—a “mouth,” as the locals called it—framed by jagged cliffs that rose like broken teeth from the sea. To the east, the land softened into green pastures and oak woods; to the west, the ocean roared in endless fury, hurling waves against rocks that had stood for centuries.

This particular cove—Marsland—was known only to a few. It bore no harbor, save for a rough stone wall built long ago to shield a single boat from the Atlantic’s wrath. And it was here, under the watchful eye of the White Witch, Lucy Passmore, that Rose had come—not out of duty or love, but from a feverish mix of curiosity, fear, and superstition.

She had kept her appointment, though she could not say why. The words spoken in hushed tones at dusk echoed in her mind: *“If you see nothing, then none are fated to you.”* But what if she did?

Now, standing barefoot on the damp pebbles, Rose hesitated. Behind her, Lucy bustled toward their small boat, muttering about her husband’s laziness. “Oh, the lazy old villain!” she cried, scanning the shore. “He’s been off chasing pollock again and left the boat down by the water! I’ll thrash him when I get home.”

Rose watched her go, then turned back to the sea. The moonlight spilled across the water like molten silver, illuminating the edge of the cove where the tide had receded. A faint ripple stirred the surface, and somewhere in the distance, a gull stirred in its nest.

“You must be alone,” Lucy called from the boat, already settling herself with arms crossed. “No one else may see you. Go now—dip your head three times, speak the words, and look into the mirror. But do not glance toward land or sea until you’ve done it.”

And so Rose stepped forward—naked, shivering, her breath catching in the cool air. She slipped off her clothes and walked slowly into the shallows, the cold water rising to her knees. On either side, cliffs loomed—one black and deep in shadow, the other glowing under the moon, draped with purple seaweed and craggy crevices that seemed to whisper secrets.

She stopped just beyond the tide line, clutched the mirror tightly, and dipped her head once… twice… thrice. Then, raising her face to the glass, she spoke the incantation:

> “A maiden pure, here I stand,
> Neither on sea, nor yet on land;
> Angels watch me on either hand.
> If you be landsman, come down the strand;
> If you be sailor, come up the sand;
> If you be angel, come from the sky,
> Look in my glass, and pass me by;
> Look in my glass, and go from the shore;
> Leave me, but love me for evermore.”

As the final word left her lips, something stirred beneath the waves.

The water flared—golden, blue, crimson—like torches lit by unseen hands. Sea creatures, long asleep, woke and swam in radiant patterns. Starfish glowed, jellyfish pulsed, and great oarweeds waved like banners in the current. The whole sea seemed alive, breathing light.

Rose gasped. She saw every shell, every fish, every hidden cave. She felt the presence of something vast and ancient—something watching from the depths.

Then—rattling down the pebbles—the sound of hooves.

She froze. In terror, she darted into a dark cavern behind the cliff, pulling her clothes on in silence. Outside, voices rose—men shouting, horses snorting. The boat was being dragged into the water.

From the stern, Lucy erupted like a furious storm spirit. “Eh! Ye villains, ye rogues! What do ye want, stealing poor folks’ boats by night?”

The men recoiled. One fled, yelling, “‘Tis a mermaid—sleeping in Willy Passmore’s boat!”

“Oh, dear,” Lucy muttered. “It’s me.” And she swung an oar with such force that Father Parsons yelped and stumbled backward.

Her husband, Will, pleaded, “They promised me two gold nobles!”

“Two?” Lucy shrieked. “And do you call yourself a man? Would you sell your soul for ten?”

“Tu nobles! Tu nobles!” he wailed, hopping sideways.

“Ten,” said Campian, stepping forward. “Give her ten, brother Morgan—just keep your shins clear.”

And so they paid.

But as the boat slid into the sea, another figure appeared—a rider galloping down the beach, his horse rearing as he leapt from the saddle.

“God forgive me,” the servant cried, drawing his sword. “It’s Mr. Eustace! Oh, sir, you’re hurt!”

Eustace staggered, blood dripping from his temple, wrapped in a torn handkerchief. “A scratch,” he moaned. “Help me into the boat.”

Campion frowned. “Not with us, surely, my dear son—vagabonds upon the earth?”

“With you,” Eustace replied, eyes blazing. “All is over here. Whichever way God leads—there I go.”

As he passed Rose’s hiding place, she caught sight of his face—pale, twisted with pain and shame, eyes wide with rage. They met hers.

She shuddered. *He has overlooked me,* she thought. *He sees me.*

“Who wounded you?” Campian asked.

“My cousin—Amyas—and he took the letter.”

“The devil take him!” Parsons roared.

“I dare not curse him,” Eustace whispered. “He saved me. Sent me here.”

Lucy stepped forward. “You must not go with such a wound. Let me bind it.”

“No,” he said, pushing her away. “Better bear it. I deserve it. On board—William Cary is close behind!”

At that, the boat was launched with desperate speed. Just as it cleared the rocks, the thunder of hooves echoed—Cary had arrived.

“Now,” Lucy said, climbing to a ledge above, “I’ll see what happens.”

Below, she watched as Cary charged through the meadow, leapt a wall, and caught the fleeing servant. He struck him hard—then kicked him into the river, mounted his horse, and rode off into the night.

Back in the cave, Rose trembled. “Is he dead?”

“No,” Lucy said. “Just knocked down. But he’ll never forget it.”

Afterward, Lucy returned to Rose. “Come, my dear. You’re freezing. Let’s go home.”

“But I can’t forget that face,” Rose whispered. “I’m sure he saw me.”

“Nonsense,” Lucy said. “Young gentlemen don’t always see what they think they do. Besides—he’s a priest, a Papist. Can’t marry, even if he wanted to.”

“He is not,” Rose insisted. “I know he isn’t.”

And so, without hesitation, she told Lucy everything—the meeting, the letter, the threat, the secret.

Lucy listened, then smiled. “A pretty wooer, indeed. But be brave, child. Maybe he’s meant for you—but not yet. Perhaps he’s far away, across the seas. A duke, or a prince, perhaps. That’s why his spirit came late.”

But Rose could not be comforted. The image of Eustace’s face—bloodied, furious, haunted—burned in her mind.

She reached home unscathed, but fell ill the next day—fevered, weak, haunted by dreams of the sea and the ghostly reflection in the glass.

For two weeks, she lay in bed, recovering slowly. When she finally returned to Bideford, Amyas had already sailed—far across the ocean, bound for Milford Haven, where new adventures awaited.

And so, the tale moves on—leaving behind the shadows of Marsland, the whispers of the sea, and the lingering echo of a promise made beneath the moon.

“The fair breeze blew, the white foam flew;
The furrow follow’d free;
We were the first that ever burst
Into that silent sea.”

—*The Ancient Mariner*

It was too late and too dark the night before to see the old house at Stow. But now, on this bright October morning, we may take a look around while Sir Richard and Amyas Leigh stroll through the terraced garden to the south of the manor, about eleven o’clock. Amyas has slept until luncheon—just an hour ago—but Sir Richard, despite the chaos of the previous night, rose by six, already in the valley with his two terrier dogs, chasing badgers to stir the courage in their own hearts and his.

Old Stow House—once grand, now only memory—stood four miles beyond the Cornish border, perched on the northern slope of the largest and most beautiful of those deep valleys I described in the last chapter. Eighty years after Sir Richard’s time, a massive Palladian mansion arose there, gaudy with every excess of poor taste. Legend says it was built by Charles the Second for Sir Richard’s great-grandson, heir to the famed Sir Bevil, who defeated Parliament’s forces at Stratton and fell bravely at Lansdowne near Bath. But like many things born under the Stuarts, it rose only to fall again. An old man who had watched its foundation stones laid lived long enough to see it torn down, its bricks and timber sold off-site. Since then, the stables became a farmstead, the tennis court a sheep pen, the great quadrangle a hayyard. Civilization, spreading fast elsewhere, retreated from this remote corner of England—though let us hope only for a season.

But I am not writing of that later, showy Stow House, nor of Sir Bevil, the most noble and gallant of his age, whose name Prince honored with a proud epigram:

“Where next shall famous Grenvil’s ashes stand?
Thy grandsire fills the sea, and thou the land.”

I write of an earlier time, a harsher generation, and of the original Stow House—part fortress, part home—standing since ancient times. It was built when the first Sir Richard, son of Hamon Dentatus, Lord of Carboyle, grandson of Duke Robert, son of Rou, settled at Bideford after slaying the Prince of South-Galis and the Lord of Glamorgan. He gave all his conquests in South Wales to the Cistercian monks of Neath. The house itself was a sprawling, half-castle, half-manor, much like Compton Castle near Torquay—the home of Humphrey Gilbert, Walter Raleigh’s half-brother and Richard Grenville’s closest friend, of whom more will come.

On three sides—north, west, and south—the high walls of the old bailey still stood, complete with machicolated turrets, arrow slits, and dark openings for dropping stones and fire upon attackers—remnants of a turbulent past. But the southern courtyard had become a flower garden, adorned with neat terraces, statues, clipped hedges, and intricate floral knots, a testament to the topiary art of the age. To the east, where the valley opened wide, the old walls had vanished, and the ruined Norman keep—shattered in the Wars of the Roses—had been replaced by the rich, elegant architecture of the Tudors. In short, the house reflected the time: caught between eras, embodying the transition from medieval austerity to the blossoming Renaissance, a world awakening to new life across Europe—may it never know autumn or winter.

From the house, the hill sloped steeply downward, and the garden where Sir Richard and Amyas walked offered a truly English view. One moment they could glimpse the blue ocean beyond the western wall, dotted with distant sails; the next, a vast panorama unfolded beneath them—tier upon tier of fertile parkland, stately avenues, golden autumn woods, and purple heather moors rolling up into the valley toward an ancient British earthwork, black and bristling with gorse atop a conical hill. On the eastern ridge, rising against the sky, stood the lofty tower of Kilkhampton Church, adorned with monuments and offerings from five centuries of Grenvilles. A soft yellow haze hung over park and wood, cattle lowed as they brushed flies beside a stream far below, colts played in the horse-park nearby, and their sires answered faintly from the Queen’s Park across the valley. A stag thundered through the woods, challenging another deep in the distance, whose roar echoed back, met in turn by defiance from high, mist-shrouded slopes. And close at hand, on the terrace before the house, sat Lady Grenville herself—the beautiful St. Leger of Annery, the jewel of the place—watching her noble children, then raising her eyes to her noble husband, then sweeping her gaze across this broad paradise of the West, until life seemed too full of joy and heaven too bright.

All the while, Amyas and Sir Richard paced slowly, speaking earnestly and deeply—for both knew this was the turning point in the boy’s life.

“Yes,” said Sir Richard, after Amyas had plainly told him of Rose Salterne and his brother, “yes, lad, you have chosen the better part—and so has your brother. That choice shall not be taken from you. But be strong, and trust in God that He will make a man of you.”

“I do trust,” said Amyas.

“Thank God,” replied Sir Richard, “that you’ve removed from my heart what had troubled me since your father—rest his soul—entrusted you to me. For all good things, Amyas, can become the worst when misused. Love of woman, because it can lift a man’s soul to heaven, can also drag him to hell. But you’ve learned better, Amyas. You know, as our German ancestors did, that *Sera juvenum Venus, ideoque inexhausta pubertas*—the youth’s passion is brief, hence the unspent vigor. And more than that—you understand that the foolish French and Italian fashion of clinging to women’s aprons, where no boy feels manhood unless he can *vagghezziare le donne*, whether maid or wife—this is no less harmful to the soul than open sin. It breeds vanity, waste, envy, bitterness, even hatred and murder. Even if escaped, the true treasure of manly love—meant for one alone—is squandered among many, and the bride receives only the dregs, the caput mortuum, of her husband’s heart, becoming little more than decoration or a means to produce heirs. May God, who saved me from such death-in-life, save you too!”

As he spoke, he looked down toward his wife on the terrace. She, sensing his words, glanced up with a smile so sweet that Sir Richard’s stern face softened into radiant warmth.

Amyas looked at them both and sighed. Then, changing the subject abruptly—

“May I go to Ireland tomorrow?”

“You shall sail in the *Mary* for Milford Haven, carrying these letters to Winter. If the wind favors, the master may lower down the river tonight and set sail. We must not delay.”

“Winter?” asked Amyas. “He’s no friend of mine—he abandoned Drake and us so cowardly at the Straits of Magellan.”

“Duty does not wait for personal quarrels, even just ones, lad. But he won’t be your general. When you reach the marshal or the Lord Deputy, give them this letter—they’ll assign you work—and hard work, I assure you.”

“I want nothing better.”

“Good,” said Sir Richard. “The best reward for doing well is more to do. He who has been faithful over few things will be entrusted with many. That is the true, heroic rest—worthy of gentlemen and sons of God. As for those who seek idleness in this world or the next, hoping God will feed them with a spoon, Amyas, I count them cowards, even if they call themselves saints.”

“I wish you could persuade my cousin of that.”

“He hasn’t yet learned what it means to lose his life to save it. Bad men have taught him—and I fear these Anabaptists and Puritans at home teach nothing else—that the one great task is to save one’s soul after death. Every man for himself. That, not divine self-sacrifice, is the one thing needful—the better part Mary chose.”

“I think men are selfish enough already without being taught it.”

“True, lad. Were I able, I’d hang such a teacher high as an enemy of mankind and corrupter of youth. Is there not enough cowardice and self-interest already in fallen sons of Adam? Must false prophets, with their promises of heaven and threats of hell, elevate our basest vices into heavenly virtues and paths to bliss? Farewell to chivalry, to desperate valor, to patriotism, to loyalty, to England and English manhood—if preachers begin telling every man, as Jesuits do, to care first for the ‘safety of his soul.’ Then no man will dare die at his post, fearing he isn’t fit to die. Do your duty like a man, Amyas—to your country, your queen, your God. Count your life worthless, as the holy men of old did. Do your work, and leave your soul to Him who judges fairly and mercifully. Does God show favoritism? Now come in, take the letters, mount your horse. And if I hear you died at Smerwick fort, wounded frontward, I’ll weep for your mother—but not for you.”

If anyone finds it surprising that a nobleman and warrior like Sir Richard should quote Scripture and speak of faith so freely, they should read the writings of the time. They’ll find how possible it was then for men of the world to be deeply rooted in the Gospel, yet free from superstition or false piety. The religion of that age was one no soldier need have shunned. At least, Sir Richard died as he lived—without fear, without complaint. Fifteen years later, shot through and through, captive among Spanish priests, crucifixes, confession, extreme unction, and all the tools meant to save souls from a loving God, these were his final words:

“Here die I, Richard Grenville, with a joyful and quiet mind; for I have ended my life as a true soldier ought—fighting for my country, queen, religion, and honor. My soul departs willingly from this body, leaving behind the lasting fame of having done what every brave soldier is bound to do.”

These were the last words of Richard Grenville. The pulpits of his day had shaped them.

But let us return. The day was not yet done. When they entered the house, the first person they met was the old steward, searching for his master.

“There’s a kind of rogue at the gate, Sir Richard—a masterless man, bold as brass, insists on speaking with you.”

“A masterless man? Let him speak if he wants gaol or gallows.”

“Well, sir,” said the steward, “I suspect that’s just what he wants. He swears he won’t leave till he sees you.”

“Seen me? By heavens, he’ll see me here and in Launceston too, if he likes. Bring him in.”

“Fegs, Sir Richard, we’re half afraid. With your permission—”

“Hillo, Tony!” called Amyas. “Who ever feared with Sir Richard’s leave?”

“What, does he have horns or a tail?”

“Massy no! But I’m afraid of treason for your honor. The fellow’s covered in strange patterns, brown as a filbert, tall and strong, foreign, and carries a mighty staff. I expect he’s a Jesuit or wild Irishman. The grooms won’t touch him, nor the dogs—otherwise he’d be under the pump already. Those who saw him coming swear fire came out of his mouth!”

“Fire from his mouth?” said Sir Richard. “The men are drunk.”

“Covered in marks? Then he’s a sailor,” said Amyas. “Let me go out and see him. If he needs putting in order—”

“He’s not so big he won’t fit in your pocket. Go, lad, while I finish writing.”

Amyas stepped outside. Leaning on his staff at the back door stood a tall, raw-boned, ragged man—“pinked all over,” as the steward had said.

“Hillo, lad!” said Amyas. “Before we talk, lay down that Plymouth cloak of yours.” He pointed to the cudgel, known among West Country sailors as a “cloak.”

“I’ll warrant,” said the steward, “where he found his cloak, he found a purse not far off.”

“But no hose or doublet—so his staff’s magic didn’t help him much. Put down your staff, man, and speak like a Christian, if you are one.”

“I am a Christian, though I look like a heathen; and no rogue, though masterless. I ask nothing, deserve nothing—only to speak with Sir Richard before I go on my way.”

There was something dignified yet humble in his tone that drew Amyas. He asked gently where he was going and where he came from.

“From Padstow Port to Clovelly town, to see my old mother—if she lives, which God knows.”

“Clovelly man!” cried the grooms in unison. “Why didn’t you say so?” A West Countryman was always kin.

“What’s your mother’s name?” asked the steward.

“Susan Yeo.”

“That one who lived under the archway?” asked a groom.

“Lived?” said the man.

“Aye, sure—she died three days ago, poor soul.”

The man stood silent for a moment. Then quietly, in Spanish, he said: “That which is, is best.”

“You speak Spanish?” asked Amyas, growing more curious.

“I must, young sir. I spent five years in the Spanish Main. Only landed two days ago. If you’ll let me speak with Sir Richard, I’ll tell him something that will make both ears of the listener tingle. If not, I’ll go to Mr. Cary of Clovelly—if he lives—and unburden myself. But I’d rather speak with one who’s a mariner like myself.”

“And you shall,” said Amyas. “Steward, bring this man in—he’s ragged, but a man of sense.” He led him inside.

“I only hope he’s not one of those Popish murderers,” muttered the steward, keeping his distance.

“Popish, old man? There’s little chance of that. Look here!” The man pulled back his rags to reveal a ghastly scar circling his wrist and winding up his forearm.

“I got that on the rack,” he said calmly, “in the Inquisition at Lima.”

“Oh, Father! Why didn’t you tell us you were a poor Christian?” the steward exclaimed.

“Because I’ve had only what I deserved—and barely tasted it, as the Lord knows who delivered me. I wasn’t going to beg or be shown off for it.”

“By heaven, you’re a brave fellow!” said Amyas. “Come straight to Sir Richard’s room.”

They entered the library, where Sir Richard sat amid books, dispatches, state papers, and warrants. Though not yet admiral, general, member of Parliament, or privy councillor—as men would be later—he corresponded with nearly every great man and knew the affairs of the realm.

“Hillo, Amyas—have you tamed the wild man already and brought him in to swear allegiance?”

Before Amyas could answer, the stranger fixed his eyes on him. “Amyas?” he asked. “Is that your name, sir?”

“Amyas Leigh, at your service.”

“Of Burrough by Bideford?”

“Why yes—what do you know of me?”

“Oh, sir, sir! Young minds forget quickly; but old, sorrowful ones remember too long. Do you recall one who served with Mr. Oxenham? A swearing reprobate, God forgive him—yet forgiven, for His Son’s sake—one who gave you a horn, a toy with a chart on it?”

“Soul alive!” cried Amyas, seizing his hand. “You’re him! The horn? I still have it—I’ll keep it till I die. But where is Mr. Oxenham?”

“So, good fellow,” said Sir Richard, rising, “where is Mr. Oxenham? You’re rushing to greet an old acquaintance, Amyas, before we’ve heard if he can give honest account of himself—or of his captain. Many a sailor returns without his captain, as poor Mr. Barker of Bristol discovered. May God spare us any treachery here.”

“Sir Richard Grenville,” said the man, “if I were guilty to my noble captain—as I am to God—I wouldn’t be here today, seeking favor from one who has never welcomed villainy. I know your character well. Trust in the Lord: if you’ll listen, you’ll learn my truth.”

“You’re a well-spoken knave. We shall see.”

“My dear sir,” whispered Amyas, “I’ll wager this man is innocent.”

“I believe him, but this is too serious to rely on guesswork. If he’ll swear—”

The man replied humbly: “If it pleases Sir Richard, I’d rather not be sworn.”

“But it does not please me, rascal! Did I not warn you, Amyas?”

“Sir,” said the man proudly, “God forbid my word should be less than my oath. But it goes against my conscience to swear.”

“What have we here? Some fantastical Anabaptist wiser than his teachers?”

“My conscience, sir—”

“The devil take it and you! I’ve never known a man begin to talk of conscience without planning something cruel or false.”

“Sir,” he answered coolly, “do you sit here to judge me by law, yet break it yourself with profane oaths—fines are levied for such?”

Amyas expected a storm—but Sir Richard merely pulled out a shilling and placed it on the table. “There—my fine to the poor of Kilkhampton. But listen: if you won’t swear, you’ll speak under compulsion. To Launceston gaol you go, accused of Mr. Oxenham’s death and mutiny. We’ve lost too many brave captains to crew treachery. He who refuses to clear himself by oath is guilty in my eyes.”

“My good sir,” said Amyas, unable to abandon his belief, “don’t risk your life, honor, and Mr. Oxenham’s reputation for such scruples. If questioned, you might be forced to lie under torture.”

“Not likely,” the man replied with a grim smile. “I’ve known the rack and strappado. I care little what man can do. I’d gladly swear—if I thought it lawful. But not believing it, I submit to human cruelty—though I hoped for mercy from one who’s seen God’s ways in the sea.”

Sir Richard smiled. “Well, you’re a brave fool, valiant though a fool. See how you’ve sworn a tenfold oath than I’d asked? But this is typical of your kind—hating forms, yet binding yourselves in heavier chains than the law demands. Yet what do they care for the common good, so long as each saves his own soul?”

Still, he nodded. “You’re sworn now—go on. First, who are you, and where from?”

“Well, sir,” said the man, unruffled, “I’m Salvation Yeo, born in Clovelly Street in 1526. My father was a barber-surgeon and preacher—Anabaptist, as they’re called. I thank God for it.”

“Fie!” said Sir Richard. “Thank God your father was an ass?”

“No,” said Yeo, “because he was a barber-surgeon. I learned a bit of that trade—and it saved my life, as I’ll tell you. A good mariner should know all worldly skills—from sewing to shoemaking—so he can adapt to whatever comes.”

“Well said,” said Sir Richard. “Now tell your tale—no comments.”

“Well, sir. I took to the sea young. I sailed with Captain Hawkins on his three voyages to Guinea for slaves, then to the West Indies.”

“Then you went thrice to a bad end,” said Sir Richard. “And the last time, you returned to a worse.”

“No denying the last,” said Yeo. “But the earlier ones? I doubt their unlawfulness. The negroes are of Ham’s line—cursed, reprobate, as Scripture says. Their blackness is Satan’s livery. Among them, none of the elect. So the elect need not treat them as brothers.”

“Ah,” said Sir Richard, “what a pragmatic sea-lawyer we have! And I doubt you’ll preach black robes are Heaven’s livery every Sunday, howling for a parson to wear Geneva fashion. Out upon you! Go on—before you preach at Launceston.”

Yeo continued, undeterred.

“It is virtue, yea, virtue, gentlemen, that maketh gentlemen; that maketh the poor rich, the base-born noble, the subject a sovereign, the deformed beautiful, the sick whole, the weak strong, the most miserable most happy. There are two principal and peculiar gifts in the nature of man—knowledge and reason: the one commandeth, and the other obeyeth. These things neither the whirling wheel of fortune can change, nor the deceitful cavillings of worldlings separate, nor sickness abate, nor age abolish.” —*Lilly’s Euphues*, 1586.

Now it falls to me to tell how the noble brotherhood of the Rose came into being—a fellowship so chivalrous and true that within a few years it earned fame not only in its native Devon but also across distant lands: Ireland, the Netherlands, the Spanish Main, and deep into the heart of South America. If this tale seems to some overly romantic or fanciful, let them remember that the men who spoke and lived thus were no dreamers. They were seasoned soldiers, shrewd politicians, and men of honor. The author of *Arcadia* was at once a diplomat of rare cunning; the poet of *The Faerie Queene* penned *The State of Ireland* with equal insight. And if they mock *Euphues* as mere affectation, I ask only: have they read it? For though it may be long-winded at times, it is still a bold, righteous, and pious work—worthy of any man's attention. That such a book, alongside *Arcadia*, was the favorite reading of Elizabethan youth speaks volumes about the spirit of the age.

Sir Walter Scott, in his clever satire of Sir Piercie Shafton, mocked the Euphuists and their affected manners—yet he placed them in an era too late for truth. His knight speaks of Sidney’s quarrel with Lord Oxford years before it occurred. Worse, Sir Piercie belongs not to Sidney’s time, but to the decadent days after Elizabeth’s death, when folly ran rampant under James. But we need not dwell on such errors. Let those who have never read *Euphues* know this: if a father could raise a son in the manner of Lilly’s Ephoebus, saving both his virtue and his purse, all fathers—even in our modern age—would bless him. Instead of ridiculing these gallants, let us see in them our own ancestors—men of fiery youth, yet deeply principled. In their courage, their wit, their reverence for law, and their sense of national purpose, we find the roots of our own character. Their chivalry was not mere posturing, but a reflection of the tender mercy that has always walked beside English valor. Even their passion for foreign literature and customs reveals a mind open to learning—without losing its soul.

So now, to my story. If it does not please you, turn the page. There is plenty more where this came from.

Amyas could not sail the next day, nor the day after. A southwester blew hard, nearly a gale, straight into the bay. With the *Mary Grenville* safely moored in Appledore pool, waiting only for a shift in wind, Amyas returned home. When his mother set out for Clovelly—riding behind the old serving-man to visit Frank, who lay wounded—he went with her as far as Bideford. There, descending the High Street, they met a procession of horsemen led by Will Cary. He rode cap-a-pie in shining armor, sword at his side, helmet tucked at the saddlebow—such a gallant sight that every woman in the town leaned from door and window to watch.

Behind him followed four or five sturdy servants on country ponies, carrying lances, baggage, longbows, swords, and bucklers. Bringing up the rear, in a horse-litter, was Master Frank himself—much to Mrs. Leigh’s delight. He declared his wounds were nothing more than flesh cuts—the dagger had glanced off his ribs. He insisted on seeing his brother one last time, and asked to stay not at Burrough, but at the Ship Tavern near the bridge, close to Cary. That was settled at once. He took a couch, held court in state, and was mobbed by all the townsfolk—though not without whispered rumors of who had brought him to such a sorry pass.

But while Frank rested, he and Amyas hatched a plan—put into motion the very next day, market-day. First, the innkeeper, under Amyas’s orders, began a feast unlike any seen before at the Ship Tavern—roasting, boiling, frying, and preparing enough food to feed a small army. Then Amyas, stepping into the market, invited each of his old schoolfellows—those Frank had pointed out—to a “merry supper” and a good “rowse” afterward. One by one, the admirers of Rose Salterne arrived, unaware they were walking into a trap. Each found himself seated at the same table with six rivals—none of whom he had spoken to in half a year.

Yet none showed it. They were too well-bred to betray their feelings. The Leighs, knowing full well what was happening, arranged the guests with care—Frank lying on his couch at the head of the table, Amyas at the foot. By filling every mouth with good food, they kept tongues quiet until wine loosened hearts and lips.

Meanwhile, Amyas and Frank ignored the silence with cheerful indifference. They joked, told stories, made themselves so entertaining that even Will Cary, usually grave, soon found himself laughing. He tried to make Mr. Coffin laugh—only to get a stiff nod. He tried Mr. Fortescue—only a frown. Undeterred, he turned his wit on the waiters, driving them from the room in fits of laughter.

So far, so good. When the cloth was drawn and sack and sugar became the order of the day, and “Queen and Bible” had been drunk with due ceremony, Frank made his move.

“I have a toast,” he said. “To the gentlemen of the Irish wars—and may Ireland never lack a St. Leger to stand by a Fortescue, a Fortescue to stand by a St. Leger, and a Chicheстер to stand by both.”

This called for the healths of the three families, and their thanks, and compliments exchanged. The ice cracked a little. Young Fortescue then proposed, “Amyas Leigh and all bold mariners!” To which Amyas replied warmly: “I wish for no better fortune than to sail around the world again with you, fellow adventurers—and give the Spaniards another taste of the men of Devon.”

By now, the wine had done its work. Lips that had been silent began to speak. Conversations sparked. Men talked like rational Englishmen to the man beside them.

“And now, gentlemen,” said Frank, sensing the moment was ripe, “let me offer a toast none of you will refuse—with heart and soul, as well as with lips. A toast to one whose beauty and virtue have raised her above birth, so that her lowly origin is lost in the light she casts. A toast to one so peerless in loveliness that I would name her queen of beauty—were it not that each of you has sisters who might well challenge her for Venus’s girdle. Yet what else can I say, when those very ladies—whose mirrors must judge her better than I—have themselves proclaimed her the fairest? If the goddesses debated the golden apple, even Paris must step down from the throne. Gentlemen, your hearts already echo my words. Drink—‘The Rose of Torridge!’”

If the Rose of Torridge herself had entered the room, she could not have caused greater astonishment. Every man flushed, paled, flushed again—glancing at one another as if to say, *“Who dares claim her? Raise your glass, and I’ll smash it!”*

But Frank, with quiet audacity, drank: “To the Rose of Torridge—and a double health to the worthy gentleman, whoever he may be, whom she chooses to honor with her love!”

“Well said, cunning Frank Leigh!” cried Will Cary. “None of us can argue now, however much we may grumble among ourselves. For each of us believes she loves him best. So we must accept that you’ve drunk our healths all round.”

“And so I have,” Frank replied. “And what better thing can you do, gentlemen, than drink each other’s healths in return? Show yourselves true gentlemen, true Christians, true lovers. For what is love, let me ask freely? It is the very breath of the Deity whose name is Love. The ancients knew this—when they made Eros the eldest of gods, who brought harmony from chaos. How, then, can lovers make him the father of strife? Can Psyche wed Cupid and bear a serpent’s egg? Can the soul be filled with divine love, only to burn with jealousy and distrust? True, the rose has thorns—but the nettle has poison. Cupid has his arrow—but he casts no scorpions. Venus is terrible when scorned, as the daughters of Proetus learned—but her handmaids are the Graces, not the Furies. He who loves rightly will not only find love lovely—but become lovely himself.”

Frank paused, gasping slightly from his wounds. The room grew tense. A murmur rose from several young men—offended that he had presumed to speak for their hearts. Mr. Coffin, who had sat rigidly upright, staring at the wall, rose silently and began to leave.

But Frank’s courage never failed.

“Mr. Coffin!” he called, his voice firm enough to stop him. “My dear sir—if my earnestness has overstepped the bounds of courtesy, I beg your forgiveness. Do not add to my sorrow the grief of losing a friend. Only hear me to the end—I know you will. And then, if you remain angry, I shall ask your pardon again.”

Truth be told, Mr. Coffin had never been to Court. He resented Frank’s polished speech, his fine clothes, his air of superiority. Being the eldest of the group, and only two years younger than Frank, he felt slighted by being grouped with boys barely eighteen. Had Frank shown arrogance, all would have been lost. But instead, he humbled himself—appealing to Coffin’s mercy.

That was enough. Coffin, a man of true heart, and one who had known Frank since childhood, sat back down.

“Believe me,” Frank continued, “I claim no superiority over you—no more than a sailor cast ashore claims to be better than his comrade still battling the waves. For I too—let me confess it, so that by sharing, I may win your trust—have loved, and still love, what you love. Do not start. Is it surprising that the sun which dazzles you dazzles me? That the lodestone which draws you draws me? Do not frown. I have come to love you for loving what I love—for admiring what I admire. Will you not try the same lesson? As easy as it is, and as blissful when mastered?

What binds subjects more closely than loyalty to the same queen? Brothers—than duty to the same father? Devout men—than worship of the same God? Then why should not worship of the same beauty bind lovers together? Why should each see in his rival not a foe, but a fellow-sufferer?

You smile and think: *Only one can possess her. Her joy is my poison.* Be it so—but I deny it. Shall we kill ourselves before death comes? Shall we make ourselves unworthy of her because we long for her? Shall we show ourselves faithful knights by harboring envy—the most unchivalrous sin?

Shall we believe, as Italians and Spaniards do, that we grow more beloved by becoming hateful to God and to each other? Will she love us more if we come to her with hands stained by the blood of him she loves best?

Let us recall ourselves. Our only hope of winning her—*if she is worth winning*—is to will what she wills, honor whom she honors, love whom she loves. If there must be rivalry, let it be in nobleness, in virtue. Let each strive to outdo the other in loyalty to our queen, in valor against her foes, in kindness to the afflicted. Thus, our love will prove its divine origin—by lifting us closer to the gods who gave it.

But I show you a higher way: charity. Why not make our shared love for her—a love I dare not name—into a sacred bond between us? Why not follow the ancient knights, who, having one grief, one desire, one goddess, believed one heart was enough to hold it all? Who united in friendship so closely they became one soul in two bodies—living only for her, vowing to honor her choice, to find their joy in hers, and to serve faithfully the man she chose, as I—though I hope to be surpassed—promise here to be the loyal friend and devoted servant of him who wins the love of the Rose of Torridge.”

He stopped. Silence fell.

At last, young Fortescue spoke.

“I may be paying you a backhanded compliment, sir—but it seems you’re so likely to become your own faithful servant (even if you’ve already won the prize while we slept), that the bargain isn’t fair between a gay Italianist like you and us country swains.”

“You undervalue yourself and your country, sir. But rest assured—I know no more of her mind than you do. Nor shall I. For my peace, I have vowed not to see her, nor hear news of her, for three full years. Dixi?”

Mr. Coffin rose.

“Gentlemen, I may yield to Mr. Leigh in eloquence—but not in generosity. If he leaves these parts for three years, I do likewise.”

“And go in charity with all mankind,” said Cary. “Give me your hand, old fellow. If you’re a Coffin, you were sawn from no weak elm, but solid oak. I’m going too—as Amyas knows—to Ireland, to cool my hot liver in a bog, like a jack-hare in March. Come, give me thy neif, and let us part in peace. I meant to fight thee today—”

“I should have been honored, sir,” said Coffin.

“But now I am all love and charity. May I beg pardon of the world—and thee in particular? Does anyone want to pull my nose? Send me an errand? Borrow five pounds? Buy me a horse—though that’d be like giving ten? Come! Join hands all round, and swear eternal friendship—as brothers of the sacred order of the—of what? Frank Leigh? Open thy mouth, Daniel, and christen us!”

“The Rose!” said Frank quietly—seeing his spell was working, and determined to carry it forward.

“The Rose!” cried Cary, seizing Coffin’s hand in one and Fortescue’s in the other. “Come, Mr. Coffin! Bend, sturdy oak! ‘Woe to the stiffnecked and stout-hearted!’ says Scripture.”

And somehow—whether it was Frank’s words, Cary’s humor, Amyas’s wine, or the nobleness that lies in every young heart when stirred—each man stepped forward, shook hands, and swore upon the hilt of Amyas’s sword to put aside jealousy. They vowed to stand by each other and by their lady-love, to take no offense if she danced with, flirted with, or married whom she pleased. And to spread the honor of their peerless dame and their new brotherhood across the world—equaling the fame of Angelica or Isonde of Brittany—they would each seek their fathers’ leave (easy enough in those bold times) to go abroad wherever “good wars” raged—there to emulate the courage and courtesy of Walter Manny, Gonzalo Fernandes, Bayard, and Gaston de Foix. Why not? Sidney was Europe’s hero at twenty-five. Why not they?

Frank watched, smiling quietly—his eyes sparkling even when his lips were still—and said only: “Gentlemen, be sure you will never regret this day.”

“Regret?” said Cary. “I feel already as angelical as thou lookest, Saint Silvertongue. What was that sneeze?—the cat?”

“The lion, rather, by the roar of it,” said Amyas, diving behind the arras. “Why, there’s a doorway here! And—”

He rushed through, dragging out by the head—Mr. John Brimblecombe.

Who was Mr. John Brimblecombe?

If you’ve forgotten, you’ve done nearly what everyone else in the room had. But you may recall the fat boy, son of the schoolmaster, whom Sir Richard punished three years prior for tattling—by sending him not to Coventry, but to Oxford. That was the man. Now twenty-one, a bachelor of arts, he had studied what Oxford taught—more or less—and now lingered in Bideford, planning to return after Christmas to study divinity, hoping to become a parson and shepherd of souls in his homeland.

Jack Brimblecombe looked, in person, like a pig—but not just any pig. Not like the squat, shapeless Devon pigs of the time—nor the lanky German monsters wallowing in streams, nor the wiry wild boars still roaming Swinley Down and Braunton Woods. No. Jack was a prophecy: in face, figure, and complexion, the forebear of Fisher Hobbs and the triumphs of science. A Fisher Hobbs’ pig of twelve stone, standing on its hind legs—that was Jack, and nothing else.

If you don’t know what a Fisher Hobbs is, reader, you know nothing of pigs—and deserve no bacon for breakfast. But such was Jack. The same plump mulberry skin, dotted with scattered black bristles; the same sleek hide, always seeming ready to burst; the same short, toddling legs; the same dapper curve in the small of the back; the same cracked squeak; the same low forehead and tiny eyes; the same round, self-satisfied jowl; the same sensitive, cocked nose—always searching for a savory scent, yet content with the worst. A pig of serene self-help, as Jack was—fatting fast while others starved.

“Take aim, you noble musqueteers, And shoot you round about; Stand to it, valiant pikemen, And we shall keep them out. There's not a man of all of us A foot will backward flee; I'll be the foremost man in fight, Says brave Lord Willoughby!”

Elizabethan Ballad.

It was Christmas afternoon, and the light was fading fast. The evening service had ended, and the people of Bideford were returning home in cheerful groups—fathers with their children, lovers arm-in-arm—headed for cakes and ale, flapdragons, mummer’s plays, and all the joyful customs of the season. But one woman walked alone, wrapped tightly in a black muffler, her maid close behind. She moved quickly, yet with a heavy heart, toward the long causeway that led to Northam town. Sir Richard Grenville and his wife caught up with her and stopped her courteously.

“You’ll come home with us, Mrs. Leigh,” said Lady Grenville. “We’d be honored to share a merry Christmas night.”

Mrs. Leigh smiled gently, placing one hand on Lady Grenville’s arm and pointing westward with the other.

“I cannot truly celebrate Christmas while that sound lingers in my ears.”

The others turned in the direction she indicated. Above them, the soft blue sky was turning gray, and here and there a misty star blinked through the dusk. But to the west, where the downs of Raleigh met those of Abbotsham, the sky was veiled in delicate white flakes. Iridescent streaks marked the path of the sun’s descent, glowing with the colors of a dying dolphin. Low on the horizon, a band of greenish haze stretched across the land. Yet what was this sound that troubled her? None of them—distracted by the noise and cheer of the town—had noticed it until now. And yet, listen! It was perfectly still. Not a breath stirred the grass. Still, the air thrummed with a deep, low roar—like the rumble of a thousand wheels, the march of endless armies, or, as it truly was, the thunder of waves crashing against the pebble ridge.

“The ridge is noisy tonight,” said Sir Richard. “There must have been wind somewhere.”

“There is wind now, where my boy is,” whispered Mrs. Leigh. “God help him.”
And everyone knew she spoke truth. The spirit of the Atlantic storm had sent its first warning—a smooth swell felt two miles inland. By tomorrow, the pebbles now tumbling back with each retreating wave might leap to the top of the ridge and be hurled like cannonballs onto the marsh, driven by the fury of the western gale.

“God help my boy,” she repeated.

“God is just as near him at sea as he is on land,” said Sir Richard kindly.

“True,” she replied, “but I am a mother alone, and my heart can only rest when I’m home, kneeling in prayer.”

And so Mrs. Leigh continued on her way, spending the entire night listening between prayers to the growl of the surf—until it was drowned, long before dawn, by the full fury of the storm.

Meanwhile, where was Amyas?

Amyas sat bareheaded in the stern of a boat in Smerwick Bay, the spray whistling through his curls as he shouted cheerfully:

“Pull, and with a will, my merry men all, and never mind shipping a sea. Cannon balls are a cargo that don’t spoil by taking salt-water.”

His mother’s fears had proved true. Christmas Eve had been the last of the still, dark, steamy nights of early winter. For twelve hours now, the western gale had raged along the Irish coast.

The short daylight of winter was fading fast. Behind him, waves leapt into the air, shattered into mist by the tempest. Beside him, green foam-fringed columns surged up the black cliffs and crashed down in cascades of snow. Before him lay the deep, sheltered bay—but not far beyond it, four miles out to sea, a thick gray cloud loomed like a sloping roof, stretching over their heads and cutting off the cliffs halfway up, hiding the Kerry mountains, and darkening the distant firths into night. Beneath that swirling veil, the storm howled across the land, sweeping great foaming masses and salt spray, blurring the world into a hazy, dun-gray blur. Let it howl! For the mist was thicker than any spray, the thunder louder than any wave. On the sand-hills, red sparks flashed in the gloom—not from heaven, but from the fort now called Fort Del Oro, where the hated golden flag of Spain flew proudly. Inside, San Josepho and eight hundred Spaniards held out. Three nights earlier, Amyas, Yeo, and Winter’s best men had dragged four culverins from the Admiral’s main deck, floated them ashore, and hauled them up to the battery among the dunes. Now they would see whether Spanish and Italian soldiers could hold their ground against the men of Devon.

No blame to Amyas if his thoughts wandered from his lonely mother at Burrough Court to the bright flashes on the sand-hills and the fort, where Salvation Yeo was hurling eighteen-pound shot with deadly precision, watching with cold triumph as sand flew and gabions crumbled.

Amyas and his crew had just returned to the ship for fresh ammunition—their supply exhausted, and the guns firing stones for the past four hours. They ran the boat ashore through the surf, landed in a cove, and scrambled over the dunes, each man carrying a pair of cannonballs slung across his shoulder. Amyas leapt into the trench and called out to Yeo:

“More food for the bull-dogs, Gunner—and plums for the Spaniards’ Christmas pudding!”

“Don’t talk to a man at work, Master Amyas. I’ve missed five times already—but I’ll bring down that cursed Popish rag, by God!”

“Then bring it down. No one wants crooked shots. Use good iron, not paving stones.”

“I swear, sir, the devil himself is twisting my shot aside. I thought I saw him once. But thank Heaven, here’s real ball again. Ah, sir—if only I could cast a silver one! Now, stand by, men!”

And once more, Yeo’s eighteen-pounder roared. The great yellow flag of Spain—flapping wildly in the wind—was lifted clean from its staff, soared into the air, and then plunged headfirst, far downwind.

A roar of triumph from the sailors echoed across the bay, answered by the soldiers in the opposing camp. But before the cheers faded, a tall officer sprang onto the fort’s parapet, seized the fallen flag, and raised it high upon his lance, holding it firm against the gale as the staff was re-erected within.

At once, a dozen longbows were drawn. But Amyas shouted:

“Shame, lads! Hold your fire—let the gallant gentleman have due courtesy!”

They paused. Amyas bounded onto the battery rampart, doffed his hat, and bowed to the officer. The man, once relieved of his duty, returned the bow with dignity and stepped down.

By now, it was nearly dark, and the firing slowed. Yeo and his gunners covered their weapons with tarpaulins and withdrew for the night. Amyas, having volunteered to take watch until midnight, remained. The rest of the force, after a meager supper of biscuit—provisions running dangerously low—lay down under arms among the dunes, grumbling themselves to sleep.

For an hour or more, Amyas paced back and forth in the gusty darkness, exchanging occasional words with the sentinel, when two figures entered the battery, talking earnestly. One wore full armor; the other, a plain short cloak typical of a scholar or peaceful man. But their conversation was not of war—of siege or sally, catapult or cannon—but of English hexameters.

Do not suppose, gentle reader, that they were trifling while Rome burned. In that critical year, 1580, the very soul of English poetry was in greater peril from these classical imitations than Ireland was from the Spanish threat.

Imitating ancient meters—called “versifying” to distinguish it from rhyme—was becoming fashionable among the learned. Stonyhurst and others had tried their hands at hexameter translations of Latin and Greek epics, which sounded more like doggerel. Young wits broke into iambics, sapphics, elegiacs—damaging the queen’s English and the ears of her subjects.

I know not if Mr. William Webbe had yet published his hints for “Reformation of English Poetry,” but Gabriel Harvey, the Cambridge Malvolio, had already persuaded Spenser, Dyer, Sidney, and perhaps even Sidney’s sister, to follow his model:

“What might I call this tree? A laurel? O bonny laurel! Needes to thy bowes will I bowe this knee, and vail my bonetto;”

after ridiculing the first book of “that Elvish Queene”—then in manuscript—as a base retreat from classicism to romance.

Now Spenser, perhaps in melancholy defiance (he had just been jilted by a Kentish maid), wasted his genius on clumsy lines he imagined antique. Some pirate publisher—Tom Nash swore it was Harvey himself—had just released “Three proper wittie and familiar Letters, lately past between two University men, touching the Earthquake in April last, and our English reformed Versifying,” a correspondence between Spenser and Harvey meant to prove the superiority of lines like:

“For like magnificoes, not a beck but glorious in show, In deede most frivolous, not a looke but Tuscanish always.”

Let them pass—Alma Mater has seen worse since. But then it was serious. There is a story (true or not) that Spenser was half-bullied into rewriting the *Faerie Queene* in hexameters—had not Raleigh, a true romanticist whose verse was “most lofty, insolent, and passionate,” convinced him otherwise.

The great dramatists had not yet risen to form the English school Spenser, unknowingly, was laying the foundation for. It wasn’t until Daniel, twenty years later, demolished Campian’s “eight several kinds of classical numbers” in his defense of rhyme, that the matter was settled. So we may forgive Raleigh’s sharp reply to a quote from the three letters of “Immerito and G. H.”:

“Tut, tut, Colin Clout, much learning has made thee mad. A good old fishwives' ballad jingle is worth all your sapphics and trimeters, and 'riff-raff thurlery bouncing.' Hey? Have I you there, old lad? Do you mind that precious verse?”

“But, dear Wat, Homer and Virgil—”

“But, dear Ned, Petrarch and Ovid—”

“But, Wat, what have we that we do not owe to the ancients?”

“Ancients, quotha? Why, the legend of King Arthur, and Chevy Chase too—of which even your fellow-sinner Sidney cannot deny that every time he hears it, even from a blind fiddler, it stirs his heart like a trumpet blast. Speak well of the bridge that carries you over, man! Did you find your Redcross Knight in Virgil, or such a dame as Una in old Ovid? No more than you did your Pater and Credo, you renegado baptized heathen, you!”

“Yet, surely, our younger and more barbarous taste must bow before divine antiquity, and imitate afar—”

“As dottrels do fowlers. If Homer was blind, lad, why dost not poke out thine eye? Ay, this hexameter is of an ancient house, truly, Ned Spenser, and so is many a rogue: but he cannot make way on our rough English roads. He goes hopping and twitching in our language like a three-legged terrier over a pebble-bank, tumble and up again, rattle and crash.”

“Nay, hear, now—

'See ye the blindfolded pretty god that feathered archer, Of lovers' miseries which maketh his bloody game?' *

True, the accent gapes in places, as I have often confessed to Harvey, but—”

* This distich is Spenser’s own; the others are authentic.

Harvey be hanged for a pedant, and the whole gang of versifiers—from Lord Dorset (though he’s long past hanging) to yourself! Why delude yourselves into playing Procrustes, stretching one word till its joints snap, squeezing the next into a heap like the Inquisitors do heretics in their banca cava? Out upon him and you, and Sidney, and the whole kin. You have not made a verse among you, and never will, which is not as lame a gosling as Harvey’s own—

'Oh thou weathercocke, that stands on the top of Allhallows, Come thy ways down, if thou dar'st for thy crown, and take the wall on us.'

“Hark! There’s our young giant comforting his soul with a ballad. You’ll hear rhyme and reason together now. He won’t miscall ‘blind-folded,’ ‘blind-fold-ed,’ I warrant; nor make an ‘of’ and a ‘which’ and a ‘his’ carry a whole verse on their wretched little backs.”

As he spoke, Amyas, who had been humming a Christmas carol to himself, burst into song:

“As Joseph was a-walking He heard an angel sing— 'This night shall be the birth night Of Christ, our heavenly King.

His birthbed shall be neither In housen nor in hall, Nor in the place of paradise, But in the oxen's stall.

He neither shall be rocked In silver nor in gold, But in the wooden manger That lieth on the mould.

He neither shall be washen With white wine nor with red, But with the fair spring water That on you shall be shed.

He neither shall be clothed In purple nor in pall, But in the fair white linen That usen babies all.'

As Joseph was a-walking Thus did the angel sing, And Mary's Son at midnight Was born to be our King.

Then be you glad, good people, At this time of the year; And light you up your candles, For His star it shineth clear.”

“There, Edmunde Classicaster,” said Raleigh, “does not that simple strain go deeper into the heart of him who wrote *The Shepherd’s Calendar*, than all your artificial, foreign verses—like—

'Wote ye why his mother with a veil hath covered his face?'

Why dost not answer, man?”

But Spenser was silent for a moment, then said:

“Because I was thinking not of the rhyme, but of the rhymer. Good heavens! How that brave lad shames me—singing here the hymns his mother taught him, right before the muzzles of Spanish guns, instead of lamenting unmanly, as I have done, the love he holds, I doubt not, as dear as I did my Rosalind. This is his welcome to the winter storm; while I, who dream of heavenly inspiration, can only see in it a mirror of my own cowardly despair.

'Thou barren ground, whom winter's wrath has wasted, Art made a mirror to behold my plight.'*

Pah! Away with frosts, icicles, tears, and sighs—”

* From *The Shepherd’s Calendar*.

“And with hexameters and trimeters too, I hope,” interrupted Raleigh. “And all the tricks of self-pitying sorrow.”

“—I will set my heart to higher work than barking at the hand that chastens me.”

“Will you put the lad into the *Faerie Queene*, then, by my side? He deserves as good a place as Guyon, or even Arthegall. Let us hail him. Hallo! Young chanticleer of Devon! Are you not afraid of a chance shot, that you crow so lustily on your own mixen?”

“Cocks crow all night long at Christmas, Captain Raleigh, and so do I,” Amyas replied cheerfully. “But who’s there with you?”

“A penitent pupil of yours—Mr. Secretary Spenser.”

“Pupil of mine?” said Amyas. “I wish he’d teach me a little of his art. I could fill my time here with making verses.”

“And who would be your theme, fair sir?” asked Spenser.

“No ‘who’ at all. I don’t want to write sonnets to blue eyes or black ones. But if I could put down some of the things I saw in the Spice Islands—”

“Ah,” said Raleigh, “he would beat you out of Parnassus, Mr. Secretary. Remember—you may write about Fairyland, but he has seen it.”

“And so have others,” said Spenser. “It is not so far off. Wherever there is love and loyalty, great purposes, and lofty souls—even in a hovel or a mine—there is Fairyland.”

“Then Fairyland should be here, friend. You represent love, and Leigh loyalty; as for great purposes and lofty souls—who better than I? Unless my enemies and conscience both lie, I am as ambitious and proud as Lucifer himself.”

“Ah, Walter, Walter, why must you always slander yourself thus?”

“Slander? Tut—I merely give the world a fair challenge: ‘Here—know the worst of me. Come on and try a fall.’ Slander? Ask Leigh here—he’s known me a fortnight—whether I’m not as vain as a peacock, as selfish as a fox, as imperious as a bona roba, ready to use anyone as a cat’s paw if there’s a chestnut in the fire. And yet the poor fool can’t help loving me, running my errands, believing every scheme and dream I utter as gospel. He probably thinks I’ll be the man in the moon someday—and he my big dog.”

“Well,” said Amyas, half-apologetically, “if you’re the cleverest man in the world, what harm in my thinking so?”

“Hearken to him, Edmund! He’ll know better when he grows out of this callow honesty and learns from the goddess Detraction how to appear wiser than the wise, by showing the fool’s motley beneath the philosopher’s cloak. Go to, lad! Slander your equals, envy your betters, pray for an eye that sees spots in every sun, and a vulture’s nose to scent carrion in every rose-bed. If your friend wins a battle, say he wasted his men; if he loses, hint he sold it; if he rises, accuse favor; if he falls, claim divine justice. Believe nothing, hope nothing, endure all—especially kicking—if anything can be gained. Then you’ll wear purple and fine linen, sit in kings’ palaces, and feast sumptuously every day.”

“And wake with Dives in torment,” said Amyas. “Thank you for nothing, captain.”

“Go to, Misanthropos,” said Spenser. “You haven’t tasted the sweets of this world’s comfits yet, and you rail at them?”

“The grapes are sour, lad.”

“And will be forever,” said Amyas, “if they grow on such a devil’s tree. I really think you’re out of your mind, Captain Raleigh, sometimes.”

“I wish I were—for it’s a troublesome, hungry, windy mind, curse it. But come in, lad. We were sent by the lord deputy to invite you to supper. There’s a dainty lump of dead horse waiting for you.”

“Send it out then,” said practical Amyas. “And tell his lordship that, with his leave, I won’t stir from here until morning, if I can stay awake. There’s a stir in the fort—I expect they’ll come out.”

“Tut, man! Their hearts are broken. We know it from their deserters.”

“Seeing’s believing. I never trust runaways. If they’re false to their masters, they’ll be false to us.”

“Well, go thy ways, old honesty. And Mr. Secretary shall give you a book in the *Faerie Queene*—‘Sir Monoculus or the Legend of Common Sense,’ eh, Edmund?”

“Monoculus?”

“Ay, Single-eye, my prince of word-coiners—won’t that fit? And give him the Cyclops head for a device. Heigh-ho! They may laugh that win. I’m sick of this Irish work. Were it not for advancement, I’d rather drive a team of red Devons on Dartside. And now I’m angry with the dear lad because he isn’t sick of it too. What a plague business he has, paddling up and down, contentedly doing his duty like any city watchman! It’s an insult to the mighty aspirations of our nobler hearts—eh, my would-be Ariosto?”

“Ah, Raleigh! You can afford to confess yourself less than some, for you are greater than all. Go on and conquer, noble heart! But as for me, I sow the wind, and I suppose I shall reap the whirlwind.”

“Your harvest seems already come—what a blast that was! Hold on by me, Colin Clout, and I’ll hold on by thee. So! Don’t tread on that pikeman’s stomach, lest he take thee for a marauding Don and slit Cohn’s pipe and Colin’s weasand too.”

And the two stumbled away into the darkness, leaving Amyas to pace as before, wrestling with Raleigh’s wild words and Spenser’s melancholy, until he concluded there was a strange bond between cleverness and unhappiness. Thanking his stars he was neither scholar, courtier, nor poet, he said grace over his lump of horseflesh when it arrived, devoured it as if it were venison, and returned to his patrol—this time in silence, for the night deepened, and no need to alert the Spaniards that he was awake.

So he began to think of his mother—how she might be spending Christmas. Then of Frank, wondering what grand festival he attended amid bright lights, sweet music, and gay ladies—how he dressed, whether he thought of his brother far away on the dark Atlantic shore. Then he said his prayers and his creed. Then he tried not to think of Rose Salterne—and, of course, thought of her all the more.

The hours passed slowly, past eleven o’clock. Lights were out in the battery and the ships. The only sounds were the steady tramp of the sentinels beside him, and now and then a grunt from the men sleeping under arms twenty yards behind.

He paced back and forth, scanning the strip of dune between him and the fort. All was black and blank. Then it began to rain fiercely.

Suddenly, he thought he heard a rustle in the coarse sand-grass. True, the wind whistled through it loudly—but that sound was not quite like the wind. Then a soft sliding noise—something had slipped down a bank, dragging sand with it. Amyas froze, crouched beside a cannon, and pressed his ear to the rampart. Clearly, he heard approaching feet—rabbits or men, he couldn’t tell—but he suspected the latter.

And so he waited.

“And therewith he blent, and cried ha! As though he had been stricken to the harte.” — *Palamon and Arcite*

So it came to pass with Chaucer’s knight in prison; so it came to pass with Don Guzman de Soto. And this is how it happened.

After his release on parole, Don Guzman settled quietly into life at Bideford, lodged in quarters far better than any he’d known in years. He bore himself like a true soldier of fortune—accepting what came without complaint, content to wait for opportunity to present itself. He had been under Sir Richard Grenville’s roof scarcely a month when old Salterne, the Mayor of Bideford, arrived for supper.

At first, Don Guzman found it strange—indeed, almost absurd—that Englishmen should invite merchants and tradesmen to dine at the same table as noblemen, especially above the salt. But he was no fool. He knew that Richard Grenville, though a man of humble origin by Spanish standards, was a gentleman of principle and honor. If he chose to sit beside a ledger-keeper, it was because such customs were right and proper in England. So, though inwardly raising an eyebrow, Don Guzman accepted the arrangement. He ate and drank with dignity, even as the Mayor sat beside him, penning accounts and managing apprentices, speaking plainly but wisely with Grenville.

Before the evening ended, the Spaniard found himself exchanging words with Salterne—not out of obligation, but because the man proved courteous, prudent, and keenly aware of the nobleman’s rank. Yet he did not bow to it. Instead, he spoke with the quiet authority of a seasoned merchant prince, much like those of Genoa or Venice whom Don Guzman had once met in the courts of Europe.

After supper, Salterne asked Grenville to return the next night, and then turned to the Spaniard, saying frankly: “I know well how great a condescension it would be for a nobleman of Spain to grace my humble table. But if you would do me the honor, I promise you the fare shall be worthy of any rank, however low the company may seem.”

Don Guzman, finding little else to occupy his time, graciously accepted. The supper was excellent, and the wine—though he barely touched it—was generous.

Now, Mr. Salterne was not only a merchant, but a man of vision. His mind was ever drawn toward distant lands and ventures beyond the sea—a trait that would later mark him as one of the chief architects of Virginia’s settlement. And so, when a guest who might know something of foreign shores sat before him, he saw an opportunity. First, he offered strong sack in hopes of loosening tongues; second, he began asking shrewd, well-placed questions about the Spanish Main, Peru, the Moluccas, China, and the Indies.

The first plan failed. Don Guzman drank little more than water—so abstemious was he, he might have been mistaken for a monk. The second fared better, yet still fell short. For the Spaniard was cunning as a fox and answered with careful vagueness, spinning tales that pleased without revealing truth.

Then, just as the conversation grew thin, in stepped Rose Salterne—the Rose of Torridge—her beauty as radiant as ever. Hearing her father’s inquiries, she added her own questions, artless and bright. And now, unable to refuse her, Don Guzman found himself speaking freely—without betraying secrets, but with enough charm and detail to make the evening memorable.

And so, the seeds of desire took root.

For though some may call Eros the son of Jove and Venus, others—like Burton in his *Anatomy of Melancholy*—declare him born of idleness and fullness of bread. And indeed, Don Guzman, idle in captivity and well-fed at Sir Richard’s table, had begun to seek amusement where he could find it. Lady Grenville, being nearest, was the first to cross his mind. But he was a man of honor—and Sir Richard was his host. So he cast aside the thought, proud of his self-restraint. Such a chase, though thrilling to both pride and passion, was too dangerous, too unseemly.

As for sin? In that age, such matters were less discussed than lived. Southern Europeans cared little for moral censure, and the English, later under the Stuarts, would prove no different. Still, Don Guzman kept his distance from Lady Grenville. And thus, space opened for another—Rose Salterne.

Not from malice, nor with intent to wound, but simply for amusement—and perhaps because he could not help it—he found himself drawn to her. There was a freshness about her, a natural charm, that stirred him. She loved admiration, yes—but it was innocent, unpretentious. To him, she seemed like “the train of the peacock,” dazzling in appearance, yet possessing “the heart of the dove”—a rare and captivating blend.

Had she consented to love only him, he might have become foolish enough to love her alone. But at that thought, a sudden panic seized him—prudence overcame passion. He resolved to keep away.

Yet days dragged slowly. Lady Grenville, when home, spoke only of her husband. When she went to Stow, leaving the Don alone in the great house, what could he do but wander down to the butt-gardens? There, he showed off his fine black cloak and feathered hat, watched the shooting, played rackets with the young men, bowls with the elders—and inevitably found himself invited back to supper by Mr. Salterne.

And there, he ruled the evening. Not merely by rank, but by wit and experience. For these were the dawn days of English commerce. Every merchant in Bideford, nay, all across England, burned with dreams of discovery, trade routes, patents, and colonies. They spoke of Sir Edward Osborne’s Turkish Company, the Sultan Murad’s new privileges to English traders, Roger Bodenham’s voyages in the *Aucher*, John Fox, Lawrence Aldersey, John Rule—all sailing the Levant. The fall of Famagusta and Elizabeth’s alliance with the Grand Turk had opened the Mediterranean to English ships.

And so, every word Don Guzman spoke of the East or West Indies found fertile ground. Master Edmund Hogan had returned from Morocco. John Hawkins and George Fenner had sailed to Guinea—Walter Wren, a native of Bideford, among them—and traded for musk, civet, gold, and grain. African news was nearly as valuable as news from the Americas.

Just two months prior, Captain Hare had set sail from London in the *Minion*, bound for Brazil, carrying Sheffield hardware, Devonshire kersies, hollands, and Manchester cottons. A man named John Whithall, who had married a Spanish heiress and owned an ingenio in Santos, had opened the door for English goods.

And Drake—Drake had proven not only that the American coasts could be plundered, but that an East Indian trade was possible. Frobisher and Davis had begun probing the Arctic ice, seeking a northern passage to China. Anthony Jenkinson, traveler and agent for the Muscovite Company, had journeyed through Russia, Persia, Armenia, Tartary, Siberia—even the Arctic shores where Sir Hugh Willoughby had perished thirty years before.

Under Elizabeth’s wise rule, English commerce flourished. And as Don Guzman listened, he realized—shrewdly—that these islanders were not mere traders. They were a people determined to defy Spain’s dominion. They hated war, yes—but when provoked, they fought with grim resolve. Their merchant ships sailed armed to the teeth, manned by men trained since childhood to fight, ordered to defend their profits with steel.

One evening, after civilly warning that Englishmen who trespassed might be sent back, Don Guzman heard a volley of defiance:

“We’ll see that, sir.”
“Depends on who says ‘No right.’”
“You found might right when you claimed the Indian seas; we may find right might when we try them.”

He rose, furious. “You are insolent burghers!”

But old Salterne stepped forward. “Sir, we are plain men, and perhaps we’ve spoken rashly. We beg your pardon. It was the strength of my wine. We meant no offense to a noble gentleman and foreigner.”

Don Guzman refused to be appeased. He stormed out, calling himself a fool, a blinkard—how could he have lowered himself to such company? Yet he forgot that he had brought it upon himself.

Next day, Salterne—driven by the great devil Mammon—came again, begging forgiveness. He promised that none who had offended would be invited again, if only the Don would return.

And so, the Spaniard returned—sneering at himself the whole way. “Fool that I am! That girl has bewitched me. Go I must, and eat my share of dirt, for her sake.”

And once inside, he cleverly hinted to Salterne that he had grown so fond of his hospitality that he might reveal things he wouldn’t tell others. “The Spaniards,” he said, “do not fear single traders. But they dread any general effort to strip us of our wealth.” Then he added, with a smile, “Yet there are still many chances for one man here and there to grow rich.”

Salterne, sharp as he was, had a weakness—and the Spaniard struck it. He delighted in learning the secrets of Spain’s monopoly. Often, he urged Rose to question Don Guzman, blind to the danger—believing that no daughter of a merchant would marry a Catholic Spaniard, and no nobleman would marry a burgher’s daughter. He trusted religion and pride to keep them apart.

Love without marriage? Impossible. Rose’s virtue was beyond question—her father had staked his fortune on it. And the Don? Well, he valued his life—though Salterne gave him credit for that, even though Don Guzman cared little for his own neck.

So it came to pass that for weeks and months, the merchant’s house became the Don’s favorite haunt. He saw Rose daily. She heard him speak of distant lands—of vast caverns, deserts, cannibals, men whose heads grew beneath their shoulders. She listened, entranced.

And he, in turn, seasoned his tales with scraps of trade secrets—just enough to keep Salterne eager for more.

His stories were worth hearing. He seemed to have seen everything: born in Peru, sent to Spain at ten; raised in Italy; a soldier in the Levant; an adventurer to the East Indies; back to America, to Mexico, to Spain, Rome, Ireland. Shipwrecked. Captive among savages. Standing at the edge of volcanic craters. Present at courts across Europe. Fighting Turks, Indians, lions, elephants, alligators—every beast and enemy imaginable. By thirty-five, he had lived three lifetimes.

He had endured the siege of Famagusta, escaped the Turks, narrowly avoiding death by flaying or impalement. He had fought at the Battle of the Three Kings, seen Stukely fall beneath a hundred lances, held the dying head of the King of Portugal in his hands.

And now, he told Rose one evening, what did he have left? A heart trampled harder than stone. No one to love. No one who loved him. Only fame remained—and even that was denied him, a prisoner in a foreign land.

“Have you no kin?” asked Rose, pitying.

“My two sisters are in a convent—they had neither money nor beauty. Dead to me. My brother is a Jesuit. Also dead to me. My father died by Indian hands in Mexico. My mother, a penniless widow, serves as companion to some princess in Seville—no better blood than herself. And I—devil! I’ve lost even my sword. Thus ends the house of De Soto.”

He meant to be pitied—and he was.

Then he shifted tone, telling Italian tales—romantic ones when Rose was near, bawdy ones when she was gone. She wept over Juliet and Desdemona long before they graced English stages. The men laughed at jests that would echo through centuries—if only Cinthio and Bandello could rise from their graves to claim them.

And so, time passed.

Before a year had passed, Rose Salterne was deeply in love with Don Guzman—more than he with her. Both sensed the truth, yet neither confessed it. She feared; he, pride.

For he soon realized: to marry her would mean dishonor to his blood. To compromise his lineage for a heretic’s daughter? All his efforts would be wasted.

He had come to admire the English custom—saluting ladies on the cheek at meeting. It was graceful, though it scandalized Romish visitors. He had seen Bideford burghers, reeking of onions, profane that gesture with Rose.

So one day, he offered it—to her alone. Not before her father.

But she refused. Quietly, firmly.

“No, sir. You should know my cheek is not for you.”

“Why?” he asked, anger rising. “It seems free enough to every counter-jumper in town.”

She replied, apologetically: “True, Don Guzman. But they are my equals.”

“And I?”

“You are a nobleman. You should remember that.”

“Ah,” he sneered, “so you prefer the shopkeeper?”

“Prefer?” she laughed. “It is only form. They mean nothing to me.”

“And I—less than nothing?”

She flushed. But answered with courage: “And why should you be anything to me? You have already condescended too much—giving us pleasant evenings. Do not condescend further. You wrong yourself—and me. No, sir. Not a step nearer. I will not! A salute between equals means nothing. But between you and me—it would mean everything. I vow, if you do not leave me now, I will complain to my father.”

“Do so, madam,” he said coldly. “I care as little for his anger as you for my misery.”

“Cruel!” she cried, trembling.
