“I love you, madam!” he cried, throwing himself at her feet. “I adore you! Never mention rank again. I have forgotten it. Forgotten all but you! My light, my lodestar, my goddess! I plead as a suppliant, a beggar—though one who may one day be king! A prince now, a Lucifer of pride to all but you. To you, a wretch who grovels at your feet, crying, ‘Have mercy on me, on my loneliness, my homelessness!’ Ah, Rose—forgive the madness of my passion. You do not know the heart you break. Cold Northerners, you dream not how a Spaniard can love. Love? Worship, rather. I bless the captivity that brought me to you, the ruin that made me rich. Is it possible, saints and Virgin? Are there tears in your eyes too?”

“Go, sir!” she cried, regaining composure. “And let me never see you again.”

And with that, she fled.

“The slave obeys,” said Don Guzman, bowing low. “I kiss your hands and feet forever.” Then, straightening, he walked calmly from the house.

Upstairs, Rose peered from the window, watching him go—his jaunty stride, his careless air. Her heart sank.

How much of that speech was true? How much rehearsed? She began to think it all a script—until she saw he had truly kept his word. He never returned.

And so, she reproached herself. If he died, she would be his murderess. She watched for him at the window, hoping he would look up—then hid in terror when he appeared around the corner.

Such is love. One tale much like another. Since Adam and Eve, it has been so—ready-made, not made.

But while Rose mourned, greater destinies loomed. Elizabeth’s marriage to Anjou hung in balance. Sir Humphrey Gilbert stirred heaven and earth, striving to fulfill his patent—bringing England Newfoundland, Nova Scotia, Canada, the Northern States. And for Gilbert himself—something greater than a world: another world, and a crown that never fades.

“Misguided, rash, intruding fool, farewell! Thou see'st to be too busy is some danger.”

—Hamlet.

It was the spring of 1582–83. The March sky hung low and heavy, a slate-gray vault pressing down upon jagged mountain peaks still capped with winter’s last breath. A biting wind swept across a desolate expanse of bog, where patches of frost lingered in shades of rust and gold. Only one rise broke the flatness—a bare knoll crowned by gnarled oaks, their branches twisted like the limbs of ancient beasts, reaching over a frozen pool that cracked and shivered under the cold. From its edge came a mournful cry, carried on the wind, fading into silence as if swallowed by the bleak air.

Along the bog’s edge, a troop of English soldiers moved swiftly, navigating through wet rocks and spongy moss. Clad head to toe in armor—helmets, quilted jerkins, arquebuses slung over shoulders, pikes dragging behind—they were men hardened by war. Two years earlier, they had manned the guns at Smerwick Fort; since then, they had faced bloodshed again and again, and would face it many more times before their lives ended. At their head rode two captains: one tall and grim, his armor scarred from countless battles; the other resplendent in polished cuirass, plumed helmet, golden hilt, and gaudy sash—an oddly bright figure atop a shaggy pony that seemed ill-suited to such finery.

Beside them walked an Irish kerne, bare-legged, wrapped only in a tattered yellow cloak, his unkempt hair framing wild eyes that darted about with fear and defiance. His wrists were bound to a pikeman’s by a cord—a prisoner, but also guide. He led them in pursuit of Baltinglas, the rebel lord, and if he failed, there would be no mercy.

“A pleasant land indeed, Captain Raleigh,” said the rougher captain, Amyas, glancing at his companion’s gleaming armor. “I wonder how you found the courage to return after escaping to Whitehall, only to ruin your fine clothes in this mud and mire.”

“A very pleasant land,” Raleigh replied, his voice steady. “What you say jestingly, I say earnestly.”

Amyas laughed. “Ah, our tastes have swapped places. I’m already weary of it, just as you predicted. I’d give anything to hear of some adventure Westward-ho!—to feel the sea breeze again, and see those great bones swinging in a hammock. But tell me—what has turned you so suddenly fond of bogs and stone? I thought you’d long since seen through the nakedness of this land.”

“Bogs and rocks?” Raleigh smiled. “Nakedness? What this soil lacks is not nature, but people to work it. Look—this earth could feed thousands if tilled. These stones? Who knows what treasures lie beneath? Gold and jewels have already been found here. My brother Humphrey’s German assayer says these very rocks resemble those in Peru that yield silver. By God’s grace, if Her Majesty would grant me a few square miles of this wilderness, within seven years I’d make it bloom like a rose.”

“Well,” Amyas muttered, “then I might stay.”

“You shall, and serve as my agent—collecting mine rents, corn rents, fishery rents. Could you keep accounts, old knight of the bear’s paw?”

“Good enough for your profit ledger, at least. But no—no more than twelve months in Ireland again. Not among the children of wrath. There’s a curse upon the land, I swear.”

“There is no curse save man’s own sin,” Raleigh answered firmly. “‘Thorns and thistles it shall bring forth to thee.’ But if you root up the thorns, Amyas, who can stop you from planting wheat? If you till the ground like a man, you overturn nature’s curse—and all the foolish fables of the schoolmen besides.” He spoke with a boldness that later earned him accusations of atheism—though none could claim he deserved such a name.

“Sword and bullet are needed here,” Amyas insisted, “before plough and harrow. Until more of these Irish lords follow Desmond into the grave, there’ll be no peace.”

“Perhaps,” Raleigh said slowly, “you’re not far wrong. Yet consider—these rebels may carry purer English blood than we who hunt them. When Yeo slew Desmond recently, he shed no more Irish blood than if he’d killed the Lord Deputy himself.”

“His blood be on his own head,” Yeo interjected. “He looked like a savage, worse than any of them. Shame on him. And the old man nearly cut off his arm before he confessed who he was. Then, sir, with a price on his head and bleeding badly—”

“Enough,” Raleigh said gently. “You’ve done your duty. Strange, isn’t it? Noble Normans reduced to savages—*Hibernis ipsis hiberniores!* Is there some poison in the air?”

“Some venom, at least, that turns Englishmen into traitors,” Amyas replied. “But the Irish themselves are decent enough, if their tyrants let them live. Take Inchiquin—Prince of Themond, they say, rightful king of all Ireland if every man had his due.”

“Don’t speak of rights in a land ruled by wrongs,” Raleigh said. “Yet Inchiquin knows well that the true Irish Esau has no fiercer enemy than the Norman Jacob. Still—would we be better than these men, had we grown up masters over others’ bodies and souls in some distant land where law never reached? Look at Desmond—raised among savages, a Papist among Papists, a despot among slaves. Thousands of maidens saw honor in serving him; thousands of ruffians called piety the fulfillment of his vengeance. Let him who is without sin cast the first stone.”

Raleigh fell into silence. “What would I have become,” he murmured, “had I been born Desmond, whose lands I now desire? What will I become when I possess them? Will my children descend as these noble barons did? Will tyranny and falsehood take root in my heart? What guarantee do I have that I’ll fare better than those who came before? And yet—can I not do justice and love mercy? Can I not build plantations, sow fields, and make desert valleys laugh with grain? Shall I not have Spenser beside me, filling my soul with noble thoughts, raising me to heroic heights? Is this not true knight-errantry—to redeem a generous land and a nobler people to peace, for the glory of our glorious queen? They are trusting, tender-hearted—none more so. Though passionate and fickle, that very softness that makes them easy to lead astray may also make them easy to guide toward good. Yes—here, away from court, among people who might bless me as their benefactor—what golden days might be mine! And yet—is this another mask worn by ambition’s cunning fiend? Will my house truly be God’s house, built on loyalty and righteousness—or merely the house of fame, with walls of soap-bubble and floors of fire-mixed glass? I want to be good and great—but when will I be content simply to be good, like this simple Leigh, walking beside me, doing his duty with no thought beyond tomorrow, like the birds of God? Greatness? I’ve tasted that cup in the past year. I know it’s sweet in the mouth, bitter in the belly. Greatness? Essex was great. John of Austria was great. Desmond was great—his family once stood higher than I could ever dream. Castles, lands, thousands of slaves, five hundred gentlemen sworn to die for him—sworn even to forsake God before they abandoned him. And now—dead in a turf hut, like a rabbit in a burrow!”

“Captain,” Leigh said, “did you hear that sound?”

“I thought it an Irish wail,” Leigh replied. “But it came from the bog. Might be a plover.”

“Something feels wrong,” the ancient soldier said. “They speak of pucks and banshees here. That cry—it sounded like a woman. They say the banshee cried all night before Desmond died.”

“Then perhaps this one mourns Baltinglas,” Raleigh said. “His turn may come next. Though I don’t believe in such tales.”

“Shamus,” Amyas called to the guide, “do you hear it?”

The guide fixed a blank stare. “Shamus hear naught. Perhaps—what you call him?—fishing in ta pool.”

“An otter,” Raleigh said. “And likely right. Wait—did you not hear it, Shamus? It was a woman’s voice.”

“Shamus is shick in his ears since Christmas.”

“Shamus will go after Desmond if he lies,” Amyas said. “Ancient, send a few men to investigate. There may be a poor soul taken by robbers—or starving, as I’ve seen many.”

“I’ve seen them too,” the ancient said. “Not their fault, nor ours. But when lords quarrel, drive each other’s cattle, waste each other’s lands—well, you know—”

“I know,” Amyas snapped. “Why don’t you go?”

“Cry you mercy, noble captain,” the old man said. “I fear nothing born of woman.”

“And what of that?” Amyas asked with a smile.

“But these pucks, sir. The wild Irish say they haunt the pools. They do no harm when you approach—but when you pass, sir, they leap on your back like apes. Who can fight such a fiend?”

“Then by your own logic,” Raleigh said, “you may go safely. And if the puck jumps on you on the way back—just run in with him here. I’ll buy him for a noble. Or keep him caged and show him in London as a monster.”

“Good heavens forefend!” the ancient gasped. “But if I must, Captain Leigh—”

*Where duty calls
To brazen walls,
How base the slave who flinches.*

“Lads, who’ll follow me?”

“You ask for volunteers as if leading a forlorn hope,” Raleigh teased. “Pull out the usquebaugh, man—swallow Dutch courage. Your English is leaking out. Stay—I’ll go.”

“And I with you,” Raleigh declared. “As the queen’s true knight-errant, I cannot lag behind in any adventure. Who knows—we may find a wicked magician about to sever the head of some saffron-robed princess?” And he dismounted.

“Oh, sirs, to endanger your precious—”

“Pooh,” Raleigh said. “I wear an amulet. I have a spell at my tongue’s end—no ghost can see me, nor I them. Come, Yeo, the Desmond-slayer. Let’s shame the devil—or be shamed by him.”

“He may shame me, sir,” Yeo said, “but never frighten me. But the bog, captains?”

“Tut!” Raleigh scoffed. “Devonshire men, born on the heath—don’t you know your way over a peat moor?”

And the three set off.

They splashed and scrambled for half a mile, the cry growing louder. “That’s no ghost or otter,” Yeo said. “A true Irish wail, as Captain Leigh said. And I’ll wager Master Shamus knew it all along.”

Soon they heard clearly the cry—“Ochone, Ochonorie”—of a wild woman. Over the boulders of the knoll, they stumbled upon her.

She was young, unkempt, dressed only in a yellow mantle. She sat on a stone, tearing at her black, tangled hair, lifting her head every now and then to wail—a sound like a beast, not a human. On her lap lay the head of a middle-aged man, wearing the long robe of a Roman priest. One glance told them he was dead.

They paused, struck by awe. Raleigh, always sensitive to poetic imagery, felt the weight of the scene—the harsh sky, the shapeless bog, the stunted trees, the solitary girl mourning over the corpse in utter isolation. As she bent over the lifeless face, calling to one who could not hear, then burst into another mournful cry, a sacred horror gripped them—so deep they almost turned away. But Yeo, tougher in spirit, asked quietly:

“Shall I search him, captain?”

“Better,” Amyas agreed.

Raleigh approached the girl gently, speaking in English. She looked up—wide-eyed, startled by his armor and plume—then shook her head and returned to her lament.

Raleigh placed a hand on her arm and lifted her. Meanwhile, Yeo and Amyas examined the body.

It was a large man, coarse-featured, but wasted—shrunken to a skeleton by hunger. His limbs were curled, his torso bowed—clear signs of starvation or exposure. Yeo pulled back the clothing from his chest while the girl screamed, but made no move to stop him.

“Ask her who he is,” Amyas said. “Yeo, you know some Irish.”

Yeo asked. The girl said nothing. “Stubborn jade won’t speak, of course,” he muttered. “If she were a man, I’d make her talk.”

“Ask who killed him.”

“No one,” she said. And Yeo believed her—for he found no wound. “The man starved,” he said. “God help him, though he’s a priest. Yet he seems full down below. What’s this? A pouch—stuffed with something.”

“Hand it here.”

They opened it—papers, papers, no food. Then a parchment. They unrolled it.

“Latin,” Amyas said. “You must translate, Don Scholar.”

Raleigh read. “Is it possible?” he whispered. “This is Saunders himself!”

Yeo jumped back as if bitten. “Nick Saunders, the Legacy?”

“Nicholas Saunders, the legate.”

“The villain!” Yeo spat. “Why didn’t he wait for me to kill him? Dog!” And he kicked the corpse.

“Quiet!” Amyas warned. “Remember the girl.” She shrieked at the insult.

Raleigh continued, half to himself:

“Yes—Saunders. Misguided fool. This is your end. You brought your plotting, your lies, your banners, your bulls, your holy waters, your saints and angels, your Lady of the Immaculate Conception. You called on heaven to judge between us—and here is their verdict. What’s in his hand, Amyas? Give it to me. A pastoral letter to the Earl of Ormond and all nobles of Ireland—‘To all who groan beneath the loathsome tyranny of an illegitimate adulteress… Nicholas Saunders, by the grace of God, Legate…’ Bah! This was your final thought? Incorrigible pedant! *Victrix causa Diis placuit, sed victa Catoni!*”

He scanned the rest—letters filled with grand promises from the Pope and King Philip, vile slanders against Elizabeth, Burghley, Leicester, Essex, Sidney—any man who upheld the realm. Threats of eternal fire for dissenters. Martyrdom proclaimed for every rebel hanged in the past twenty years. Arguments over the bull *In Caena Domini*, Elizabeth’s excommunication, the nullity of English law, the duty to rebel, the right to kill a heretical prince—madness and treachery alike, recorded in Camden, Fox’s *Martyrs*, or the writings of the conspirators themselves.

With a gesture of disgust, Raleigh shoved the documents back into the pouch. They took it and returned to their company, remounted, and marched on—leaving the girl alone with the dead.

An hour passed. Another Englishman stood beside her, surrounded by a dozen kernes—tawny-haired, javelins in hand, their ragged cloaks fluttering as they wailed alongside her.

It was Eustace Leigh. Still a layman, yet still clinging to his old purpose. For two years, he had labored across Ireland, seeking to satisfy his conscience for rejecting the celibate life—though the fire of hope still burned in his heart. His brow was furrowed, his face harder, the scar on his cheek hidden beneath a thick beard. He never forgot it, nor who gave it to him.

He had been with Desmond, wandering through moor and marsh for months, risking death daily. Now he was heading to James Fitz-Eustace, Lord Baltinglas, to deliver news of Desmond’s death—and with him, a remnant of the clan too defiant or too guilty to surrender.

There stood Eustace, looking down at the remains of the most sacred figure in Ireland—the man who, once, he had hoped, would regenerate his homeland, unite Christendom, and restore the island to the gentle yoke of the Church. Here he was—starved to death in a bog, amid faithful Catholics, valiant soldiers, nobles who had vowed to die for the cause, serfs who worshipped him as a god—yet perished in silence.

It was a cruel verdict on his dreams. But not to Eustace.

It was a failure—yes—but an accident. In a world ruled by the devil, such things were expected. And proof, even, of the cause’s righteousness. For had not the true faith always suffered?

“If one sees with eyes purified from carnal impatience,” he murmured, “this is not defeat—but martyrdom.”

“Blest Saunders,” he whispered. “Let me die as the righteous die. May my end be like yours. *Ora pro me*, noble martyr, while I dig your grave on this lonely moor, waiting for your translation to one of those stately shrines—built with blood like yours—that will rise again, restored to heaven, making this land once more ‘The Isle of Saints.’”

The body was buried. A few prayers were said. Then Eustace left—not for Baltinglas. He knew the English soldiers had passed. They would reach the earl before him. The game was over. So he retraced his steps, hiding, disguising himself, until after a month he found himself back in Devon—while Baltinglas fled to Spain, having gained little from his argument with Ormond: “Had not thy ancestor, blessed Thomas of Canterbury, died for the Church of Rome, thou hadst never been Earl of Ormond.” Sound premises, perhaps—but the conclusion was as weak as the rest.

Now, let us return to Raleigh and Amyas, riding along their weary road. They had much to discuss—only three days since they met.

Amyas was beginning to see Ireland through Raleigh’s eyes. Raleigh, inspired by the possibility of a grant of Desmond’s lands, saw bogs and rocks transformed by hope—like a rainbow transfiguring the landscape. Even thirty years later, when old and broken, he saw the world this way. How fortunate for him if his heart had aged with his mind.

Amyas, unaware of the land grant, puzzled at the change.

“What is this, Raleigh?” he asked. “You’re like children in a market—nothing pleases you. You wanted Court, and got it. You’re lord and master, they say. And now, as soon as fortune fills your mouth with sweets, you turn informant on her?”

Raleigh laughed, but said nothing.

“And how is Mr. Secretary Spenser, who was with us at Smerwick?”

“Spenser? He’s thrived—as I have. He’s learned, as I have, that making one friend at Court earns ten enemies. But *Oderint dum metuant* is no longer my motto—or his. I want to be great—great I already am, they say, if princes’ favor can swell a frog into an ox. But I want to be liked. Loved. I want people to smile when I enter.”

“They do,” Amyas said.

“So do hyenas,” Raleigh replied. “They grin because they’re hungry. I may throw them a bone. I’ll throw you one now—old lad, or rather a good sirloin of beef—for the sake of your smile. That’s honest, at least.”

“Have you heard of my brother Humphrey’s new project?”

“How should I, in this howling waste?”

“Kiss hands to the wilderness,” Raleigh said, “and come with me to Newfoundland!”

“You to Newfoundland?”

“Yes. I to Newfoundland—unless this matter here is settled soon. Gloriana doesn’t know, and won’t until I’m gone. She’d send me to the Tower if she caught me playing truant. I barely got leave to come here. But I must go—try my fortune. I’m neck-deep in debt, sick of courts and courtiers. Humphrey must take possession next spring—or his patent expires. With him I go. And you too, my circumnavigating giant.”

And so Raleigh explained to Amyas the details of the Newfoundland venture—details any reader may find in Hakluyt’s writings.

“Three lords sat drinking late yestreen,
And ere they paid the lawing,
They set a combat them between,
To fight it in the dawing.”—Scotch Ballad.

All who have seen Bideford know its bridge—the very heart of the town, its soul and center. As Edinburgh is defined by its castle, Rome by its Capitol, and Egypt by its pyramids, so Bideford lives and breathes through its ancient stone span. Yet few know the deeper secrets that have shaped this marvel for five centuries: not merely a structure of stone and arch, but a living thing—inspired, spiritual, charitable, learned, sentient, and, above all, generous to the point of feasting. It was said that when the bridge first began to rise half a mile upstream, unseen hands carried each stone downriver every night until Sir Richard Gurney, the parish parson, saw an angel in his dreams commanding him to build where the stones had been brought. There alone, he was told, could the foundation hold against the shifting sands beneath.

Bishop Grandison of Exeter once declared indulgences, blessings, and eternal spiritual benefits to all who aided in bridging the perilous ford—so that men might serve both God and country, and make the best of both worlds. And though many legends surround it—how it trembles at a horse’s step despite its woolen foundation, or how William Alford once bore four bushels of salt on his back across all twenty-three arches—it is most famed for being a noble esquire in its own right. The bridge bears arms—a ship and bridge upon a plain field—and owns lands and tenements across several parishes. From these, it has founded schools, waged lawsuits, and, most notably, hosted annual banquets, maintaining the finest wine cellar in Devon, stocked with vintages from far corners of Christendom.

In the year 1583, one such feast was held, and among those invited were the leading citizens of Bideford, including Mr. St. Leger of Annery, brother to the marshal of Munster and kin to Lady Grenville. A man of great generosity, he gave away his wealth freely during his life, so that his children might never know the temptations of riches. At this gathering, a debate arose between him and one of the bridge’s trustees: whether a salmon caught below the bridge was superior to one taken above. Since no theory could settle it, Mr. St. Leger offered a wager of five pounds—he would catch a fish as firm and flaky as the Appledore salmon they had just eaten from the pool beneath Annery. In good humor, he then invited the entire company to dine with him at Annery three days later, each bringing a wife or daughter. Don Guzman, who happened to be present, was included.

The feast at Annery was grand beyond compare—such a banquet as had not been seen since Judge Hankford entertained King Edward IV. The hall echoed with laughter, music, and clinking goblets. Amidst the revelry, Rose Salterne and Don Guzman sat apart, pretending not to notice each other, yet watching intently. Rose, especially, was cautious—her father sat nearby, and opposite her were William Cary and Arthur St. Leger, lieutenants in Her Majesty’s Irish army, recently returned on leave.

Six months had passed since Rose and the Spaniard had spoken. He avoided her only within her father’s house; elsewhere, he remained deliberately distant, offering nothing more than a formal salute. Yet wherever she was, he made himself radiant—witty, eloquent, romantic—more so than ever before. His silence was not indifference, but strategy. Having failed in his first attempt to win her with open affection, he now sought to awaken desire through absence. For what greater punishment could he inflict than withdrawing his attention? And what better way to stir longing than to appear indifferent?

He knew women well—how their pride often masks vulnerability, how their coyness hides dependence. The more a man seems unattainable, the more they value him. So he let her believe he could live without her. And slowly, Rose began to wonder: *Why does he not care?* What secret strength did he possess? Was there ambition, mystery, hidden power? She found herself drawn to the idea of knowing him—of being the one who unlocked his depths. Could she not be the treasure he guarded? And if so, why had she refused him?

She felt ashamed, confused, and strangely stirred. That day, sitting at table, she could no longer wear her mask of composure. Her eyes dropped, her cheeks flushed. Don Guzman noticed. He smiled inwardly—his plan was working. He glanced at her again, not boldly, but with quiet confidence. She looked up, startled, as if caught in thought. Then, meeting his gaze, she faltered, blushed, and lowered her eyes once more. But after a moment, she raised them again, searching for his—perhaps hoping for forgiveness.

And then, silently, he asked: *Do you forgive me?*

She could not speak. But her face answered. Her lips parted, her neck bent slightly, and in that moment, she whispered with her whole being: *And do you forgive me?*

At once, Don Guzman came alive—jovial, animated, the center of the room. Stories flew, jests sparkled, and he became the most charming knight among them. Rose, meanwhile, felt a pang of guilt. She had caused this joy, and yet she longed to flee. To hide. To dream of love she dared not name.

Outside, the terrace shimmered in midsummer light. Women in rich silks strolled beneath the sunlit trees, admiring one another's gowns, whispering about husbands and servants. Only Rose stood apart, wanting to laugh or cry, unsure which.

“Dear Rose looks sad,” said Lady Grenville, approaching. “Come, child—sing for us.”

Rose obeyed, though her voice trembled. She took the lute and sat beneath the house while others gathered round.

“What shall I sing?” she asked.

“Your old song—‘Earl Haldan’s Daughter.’”

She hesitated. It was a bold, lively ballad—too loud for her mood. Frank had loved it once. Now it seemed ominous. But to refuse would invite suspicion. So she began—

“It was Earl Haldan’s daughter,
She look’d across the sea;
She look’d across the water,
And long and loud laugh’d she:
‘The locks of six princesses
Must be my marriage-fee!
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Who comes a wooing me?’”

“It was Earl Haldan’s daughter,
She walk’d along the sand;
When she was aware of a knight so fair,
Come sailing to the land.
His sails were all of velvet,
His mast of beaten gold,
And ‘hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat,
Who saileth here so bold?’”

“‘The locks of five princesses
I won beyond the sea;
I shore their golden tresses
To fringe a cloak for thee.
One handful yet is wanting,
But one of all the tale;
So hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Furl up thy velvet sail!’”

“He leapt into the water,
That rover young and bold;
He gript Earl Haldan’s daughter,
He shore her locks of gold;
‘Go weep, go weep, proud maiden,
The tale is full to-day.
Now hey bonny boat, and ho bonny boat!
Sail Westward-ho, and away!’”

As the final note faded, a voice cut through the silence—low, foreign, resonant.

“In the East, they say the nightingale sings to the rose. But Devon, more blessed, has both in one.”

Lady Grenville laughed. “We have no nightingales, Don Guzman—but our thrushes sing sweetly enough. Why are you leaving the gentlemen so soon?”

“These letters,” he replied, holding up a sealed missive, “have just arrived. They summon me home to Spain—and thence to the Indies. My ransom has come, and with it a promise of office. I am to be Governor of La Guayra in Caracas. Let me ask your congratulations.”

A hush fell. Then voices rose: “To Spain? And then to the New World?”

“Yes,” he said, smiling. “And I shall need a wife there to complete my paradise.”

Rose’s heart stopped. The news was sudden, cruel. Was this his farewell? Had he never truly cared? Her face burned. She could not look away.

“And where is La Guayra?” she asked, her voice trembling.

“Half around the world, on the Spanish Main. A land of palms and mountains eight thousand feet high. The governor’s house is like a palace in Eden—only lacking a queen.”

“I doubt not some noble lady of Seville will accompany you,” said Lady Grenville.

“Ah, but since I’ve known English ladies,” he replied, “I’ve begun to think none compare. You are the only ones worth wooing.”

“You flatter us kindly,” she said. “But surely none of our free English maidens would submit to a duenna—especially one with a horn on her forehead!”

Rose turned crimson. Lady Grenville understood at once. But before she could speak, another woman murmured something about Spanish jealousy, and another giggled about West Indian masters and their slaves.

Don Guzman grew serious. “Ladies,” he said, “these are lies born of ignorance. We may love deeply—yes, fiercely—but that is because we value honor. If some abroad fall short, it is because they lack a helpmate—like the amethyst, which keeps its wearer pure. I could tell you tales of Spanish husbands’ loyalty in the Indies—stranger than any romance.”

“Then tell us one,” challenged Lady Grenville.

“It would be long,” he said.

“The longer, the better,” she insisted. “While the gentlemen finish their wine.”

Storytelling was a beloved pastime in those times, when books were rare and memory cherished. Knowing the Spaniard’s gift, the ladies gathered close. Servants brought chairs. Don Guzman took his seat, modestly proud, at Lady Grenville’s feet, and began—

“Forty-five years ago, Sebastian Cabota sailed under Emperor Charles the Fifth to find the golden lands of Tarshish, Ophir, and Cipango. Needing supplies, he paused at the mouth of a mighty river—now called Rio de la Plata—and ventured up its course, discovering Paraguay. There, at the mouth of the Rio Terceiro, he built a fort—Cabot’s Tower—where he left a garrison of a hundred and twenty men, commanded by Nuno de Lara, Ruiz Moschera, and Sebastian da Hurtado—old comrades of my grandfather, Don Ferdinando da Soto.

With them went Lucia Miranda, wife of Hurtado—a woman of unmatched beauty, wisdom, and grace. Once a court favorite, she had renounced royal life to marry a poor adventurer and share his perils. Mangora, cacique of the Timbuez Indians, saw her and desired her. He plotted to take her by force, knowing he could never win her by right.

She sensed his intent—wise enough to know when she was loved—and kept from him. Mangora summoned Hurtado to visit, asking him to bring his wife. Hurtado, suspecting a trap, declined politely. The savage, furious, began planning his murder.

Weeks passed. Food ran low. Hurtado and Moschera led fifty men on a foraging mission. Mangora saw his chance.

The tower stands on a knoll where two rivers meet, surrounded on land by marshland—perfect for ambush. He hid four thousand warriors in the reeds, then approached boldly with thirty men bearing grain, fruit, and game.

Smiling, he expressed sorrow for the Spaniards’ hunger, offered gifts, and was welcomed in. The feast lasted into the night, songs and wine flowing freely. Finally, he asked for Miranda.

She had locked herself away, claiming illness.

But when the Spaniards slept, Mangora and his men struck. Fire erupted. The magazine exploded. The hidden warriors surged from the marsh.

Within minutes, the garrison was slaughtered. Only Miranda and four women with infants survived—bound and led into the forest.

Stunned, Miranda traveled all night, arriving at dawn before the Indian king—to find he was not Mangora.

“A ray of hope flashed,” said Don Guzman. “She asked where he was.”

“Slain last night,” said the king. “I, his brother Siripa, am now cacique.”

It was true. Lara, drunk and wounded, had fought wildly, killed Mangora, then fallen pierced by a hundred blows.

Miranda’s hope died. She faced a new tyrant.

“‘You cannot be his queen,’ said the new king. ‘You must be mine.’”

She defied him, calling him a traitor, accusing him of cruelty. She demanded death—hoping to provoke it.

But he smiled. He ordered her freed, gave her a hut, adorned her with jewels, and treated her with courtesy—as if she were a Castilian noblewoman.

For three months, she endured. Then, one day, drawing water by the river, she saw a white man on the opposite bank—clothed in tattered Spanish garb, sword drawn. He shrieked her name, plunged into the current, swam across, and clasped her.

It was Don Sebastian himself—returned with Moschera to find only ruins.

By instinct, he guessed her fate, and vanished into the forest alone. Guided by love, he found her—unharmed, still his.

Their reunion was ecstasy and terror. The Indian women fled. For ten minutes, they kissed—life itself a single embrace.

But escape was impossible. He tried to swim her across, but his strength failed. They sat together, praying.

Then the warriors returned. Don Sebastian, desperate, would have slain them both—but could not raise his hand against her.

He was seized, disarmed, carried off in triumph.

And if you cannot feel for him in that hour,” said Don Guzman, pausing, “then I, a prisoner myself, can.”

He looked up—not at the ladies, but at Rose Salterne. And she, her eyes wide, met his.

“The spirits of your fathers
Shall start from every wave;
For the deck it was their field of fame,
And ocean was their grave.”

“So you see, my dear Mrs. Hawkins,” said Adrian Gilbert, leaning forward in his chair, “with silver already in hand—as your own eyes confirm—alongside lead, manganese, and copper ores, and above all this gossan—the Cornish call it so—I suspect not merely a matrix of metal, but the very crude essence, the *materia prima* of all metals. Mark me well. If my recipes—those I received from Doctor Dee—succeed even half as I expect, then I shall refine out the luna, the silver, and lay it aside. Then I shall transmute the rest into sol, gold. And thus Peru and Mexico become mere footnotes, while England rises to command the globe. Strange, no doubt. Remote, perhaps. But possible, madam—possible!”

“And what good is it to you, Mr. Gilbert?” she replied softly, her voice heavy with sorrow. “If you could find a philosopher’s stone that turned sinners into saints—well, that would be divine work. But only God’s grace can do that. And His grace often comes too late.”

She sighed.

“But truly, my dear sir,” she continued, “what if you succeed? What then?”

“Then the Comb Martin mine becomes a source of endless gold,” he answered, his eyes alight with vision. “It gives me wealth enough to pursue my North-West patent. Meanwhile, my brother Humphrey holds Newfoundland, and builds new ships each year—forests of pine are limitless—for my voyage to China.”

“Sir Humphrey has higher thoughts than gold in his heart, Mr. Adrian,” she said gently. “He has walked closely with God these seven years. I wish Captain John were the same.”

“And how do you know I have nothing better in mind than gold?” he asked. “Or indeed, what could be better? Is not gold the strength of Spain—the very engine of Antichrist? Only through gold, therefore, can we wrest power from them. You shake your head, but say this: which is better—making gold at home without bloodshed, or seizing it abroad by war and violence?”

“Oh, Mr. Gilbert!” she breathed, her hands clasped. “Is it not written that those who rush to riches pierce themselves through with many sorrows? Oh, Mr. Gilbert! God’s blessing cannot rest on such things.”

“Not on you, madam?” he countered. “I swear, when I cast Captain John Hawkins’s nativity, his star told me otherwise. Born under stormy planets, yes—but also under royal and fortunate ones.”

“Ah, Mr. Adrian,” she murmured, “I am a simple woman, and you a great philosopher. But I believe no star for the seaman shines brighter than the Star of Bethlehem. That one brings peace on earth and goodwill to men—not arms like that.” She pointed upward, to the carved oak beam where the crest was displayed: the *Demi-Moor proper, bound*, granted long ago by Clarencieux Hervey to her husband.

“Ah, Mr. Gilbert,” she whispered, “since he first sailed to Guinea after those poor negroes, my heart has known little joy. And the very day that crest was hung in our new house, the parson read the first lesson—and there it was: ‘Woe to him that buildeth his house by iniquity, and his chambers by wrong. Shalt thou live because thou closest thyself in cedar?’ It burned into my ears like fire, into my heart like lead. Then came, ‘Did not thy father eat and drink, and do judgment and justice? Then it was well with him.’ I thought of good old Captain Will. And now—now I carry those negroes on my soul from dawn till dusk. We are grand now, money flows fast—but the Lord will demand their blood at our hands. He will!”

“My dearest madam,” he said, his voice softening, “who could prosper more than you? If your husband once followed the Spaniards too closely in regard to those people—though I do not deny it—he was punished swiftly. He lost ships, men, everything but life at St. Juan d’Ulloa.”

“Ay, yes,” she said. “That gave me some comfort—especially when the queen, God save her tender heart, rebuked him for pitying the poor wretches. But it did not change him. He grows like the rest now—greedy to gain, stingy to spend. Always plotting, always fretting. Envious of Drake, jealous of others who rise higher. Gold, gold—nothing but gold on every tongue. Ah! I remember when Plymouth was a quiet, God-fearing place. But ever since John, Sir Francis, and poor Mr. Oxenham found the way to the Indies, it has been a sorrowful town. Not a sailor’s wife but cries, ‘Give, give!’ like the daughters of the horse-leech. Every woman drives her husband across the seas to bring her back money to waste on hoods and farthingales, to strut with outstretched necks and wanton eyes. Soon they’ll learn worse, for the sake of profit. But the Lord’s hand will strike at their crisping-pins and mufflers, just as it struck the Jews of old. Oh, dear me!”

They sat in a low-ceilinged room in Plymouth, panelled in oak, richly furnished with carvings, gilding, and coats of arms. On the sideboard rested Spanish silver and gold vessels; strange birds, animal skins, and rough maps of coastlines adorned the walls. Above the fireplace, beneath the portrait of old Captain Will Hawkins—favored by Henry VIII—hung the Spanish ensign captured by Captain John at Rio de la Hacha fifteen years prior. With two hundred men, he had seized the town against a thousand Spanish soldiers, and triumphantly watered his ship at the enemy’s wells.

The man was tall and fair, with a broad forehead furrowed by study, eyes dimmed from long hours over crucibles and furnaces. The woman, once comely, was aged and worn—like so many sailors’ wives, shaped by sorrow. Many sad days had passed for her. Though John Hawkins, port-admiral of Plymouth and patriarch of British shipbuilding, was faithful and forgiving, he was stubborn, ruthless, and far from a consistent follower of God. And darker days still awaited her.

Nine years hence, she would be asked to name her son’s new ship. She would christen it *The Repentance*, offering no explanation—only that “Repentance was the best ship in which we could sail to the harbor of heaven.” Queen Elizabeth, displeased with the name, would rename it *The Dainty*, perhaps with a sneer at her son Richard Hawkins, the perfect seaman and Euphuist afloat. And she would watch that gallant vessel sail westward, around the world, as countless others had done before. Then she would wait—like so many mothers before her—for the sail that never returned. Faint news would come: her son fighting three great Armadas, wounded, imprisoned, broken, languishing in Spanish dungeons for years.

And worse was yet to come: a fleet rounding the Ram Head, not with drum and trumpet, but with solemn minute-guns, flags at half-mast, announcing that her terrible husband had died—his heart shattered by failure and fatigue, his body laid beside Drake beneath distant tropical skies.

If, at the end of her long life, a ray of sunlight broke through—when her son Richard returned from prison, knighted for valor, made privy councillor for wisdom—then soon, oh so soon, the shadow would return. Her son would drop dead, some say at the council table, leaving behind shattered fortunes and unfulfilled dreams. The stormy star of that bold line would set forever. Lady Hawkins would bow her weary head and die, the groans of those stolen negroes echoing in her ears—having lived long enough to see her husband’s youthful sin become a national institution, a curse upon generations unborn.

Why, then, did she open her heart so freely to Adrian Gilbert, with a frankness she would never have shown her own family? Perhaps because Adrian, like his brothers Humphrey and Raleigh, was filled with lofty, tender passions—dreams that stirred the hearts of lonely women. So it was. And Adrian, ashamed of his own ambition, sat silent for a moment, staring at her.

“The Lord be with you, dearest lady,” he said at last. “Strange how you women sit at home, loving and suffering, while we men rush forth to break our hearts—and yours—against rocks of our own making. Ah, well—if it were not for Scripture, I might think Adam, not Eve, plucked the forbidden fruit.”

“We women,” she replied softly, “bear the burden of it all our lives.”

“You always remind me,” he said, “of my dear Mrs. Leigh of Burrough, and her wise counsel.”

“Do you see her often? I hear she is one of the Lord’s most precious vessels.”

“I would have done more than see her,” he said, blushing, “had she allowed me. But she lives only for the memory of her husband and the glory of her noble sons.”

As he spoke, the door opened. A man wrapped in a rough sea-gown stepped inside—none other than one of those noble sons.

Adrian paled.

“Amyas Leigh! What brings you here? How fares my brother? Where is the ship?”

“Your brother is well, Mr. Gilbert,” Amyas replied. “The *Golden Hind* has gone on to Dartmouth with Mr. Hayes. I came ashore to go north to Bideford before London. I stopped at Drake’s, but he was away.”

“The *Golden Hind*? Why has she returned so soon?”

“Yet welcome ever, sir,” said Mrs. Hawkins. “This is a great surprise. Captain John expected you not until next year.”

Amyas remained silent.

“Something is wrong!” Adrian exclaimed. “Speak!”

Amyas tried, but could not.

“Will you drive a man mad?” Adrian cried. “Has the adventure failed? You said my brother was well.”

“He is well.”

“Then what—why do you look at me like that?” Adrian sprang up, snatched the candle, and held it close to Amyas’s face.

Amyas’s lip trembled. He placed a hand on Adrian’s shoulder.

“Your great and glorious brother, sir,” he said, “is better off than in settling Newfoundland.”

“Dead?” Adrian shrieked.

“He is with the God whom he served.”

“He was always with Him, like Enoch. Do not tell me parables if you love me!”

“And like Enoch, he was not taken—God took him.”

Adrian clapped his hands over his forehead and leaned against the table.

“Go on, sir. Go on. God will give me strength to hear all.”

Slowly, Amyas told the tragic tale—already recounted too well by Mr. Hayes for me to repeat it fully. Of mutinous men, ruffians gathered haphazardly; conspiracies to seize the ships; plundering of fishing boats; desertions growing daily; licenses granted by the general to the lazy and fearful to return home.

“From him?” Adrian groaned. “Conspired against him? Deserted from him? Dotards, buzzards! Where would they have found another such leader?”

“Your illustrious brother, sir,” Amyas said, “was a great philosopher, but not a general.”

“General? Where was braver man?”

“Not on God’s earth. But bravery alone does not make a general. Cortez was brave—but if he had been only brave, Mexico would still be Mexico. The truth is, Cortez, like my Captain Drake, knew when to hang a man. Your brother did not.”

Amyas was likely right. Gilbert was capable of anger at cowardice or neglect—but too kind to punish it. He could devise the wisest plans, yet lacked the grueling, everyday discipline that great captains mastered.

Then Amyas continued: how they sailed from St. John’s to explore the southern coast; how Sir Humphrey insisted on going in the tiny *Squirrel*, ten tons, “overcharged with nettings, fights, and small ordnance”—not only because she was better suited for exploring creeks, but because he’d heard whispers among the crew that he feared the sea.

Then came disaster. Seven days after leaving Cape Raz, their largest ship, the *Delight*, after a night of revelry—“like the swan that singeth before her death”—struck unknown shoals. The *Golden Hind* and *Squirrel* veered clear just in time. All but fourteen perished, including Frank’s philosopher friend, poor Budaeus. The survivors, starving and half-naked, washed ashore in Newfoundland.

Worn down, the crews of the two remaining ships persuaded Sir Humphrey to turn for England on August 31st. As they turned about, they saw something extraordinary: “a very lion in shape, hair, and color, not swimming, but sliding on the water, whole body visible, turning his head, yawning wide, showing long teeth and glaring eyes. And as he passed, he roared—a horrible voice, like a lion bellowing.”

“What others thought, I leave unsaid. But the general took it as a good omen—joyful that he would fight such an enemy, even if it were the devil.”

“And the devil it was, doubtless,” said Adrian. “The roaring lion who goes about seeking whom he may devour.”

“He has not got your brother, at least,” Amyas said.

“No,” replied Mrs. Hawkins. “He roared in joy at how many souls would remain in heathen darkness without Sir Humphrey. God be with that good knight, and send all mariners where he is now!”

Then Amyas told the final scene: how, near the Azores, storms grew fiercer, with “terrible seas, breaking short and pyramid-wise.” On September 9th, the *Squirrel* nearly sank—then recovered.

“The general,” Amyas said, “sat abaft with a book in hand, calling out to us in the *Hind*: ‘We are as near heaven by sea as by land.’ He repeated it often—fitting words for a soldier resolute in Christ. I can testify he was.”

On Monday, at noon, the *Squirrel* led ahead. Suddenly, her lights went out. The watch shouted: “The general is lost!” And it was true. In that instant, the frigate was swallowed by the sea.

So ended Amyas Leigh’s story.

“Oh, my brother!” Adrian moaned. “The glory of his house, the pride of Devon!”

“What will the queen say?” Mrs. Hawkins asked, weeping.

“Tell me,” Adrian said, “did he wear the jewel when he died?”

“The queen’s jewel? He always wore it—and his own motto: *Mutare vel timere sperno*. He wore it. And he lived it.”

“Ay,” said Adrian. “To the last.”

“Not quite,” said Amyas. “He was a quieter man lately. As he once said, a better refiner than any aboard had followed him across the seas, purifying him in fire. And gold seven times refined—he was, when God, having finished His work, took him home.”

So the talk ended.

There was no doubt: the expedition had failed utterly. Adrian was ruined. Amyas had lost his investment.

Adrian rose, asking leave to retire. He must gather himself.

“Poor gentleman,” said Mrs. Hawkins. “It is little else he has left to collect.”

“Nor I,” said Amyas. “I was going to ask you to lend me one of your son’s shirts—and five pounds to get myself and my men home.”

“Five? Fifty, Mr. Leigh! God forbid John Hawkins’s wife should refuse a penny to a distressed mariner, especially one born a gentleman. But you must eat and drink.”

“It’s more than I’ve had in many a day worth speaking of.”

Amyas sat down in his tattered clothes and ate a generous supper. Mrs. Hawkins told him all she knew of his mother—whom Adrian had seen in London months before—and then naturally moved on to Bideford news.

“And by the by, Captain Leigh,” she said, “I have sad news from your place. I heard it from one who was there. You must know a Spanish captain, a prisoner—”

“The one I sent home from Smerwick?”

“You sent? Mercy on us! Then perhaps you’ve heard—”

“How can I have heard? What?”

“That he’s escaped—the villain!”

“Without paying his ransom?”

“I can’t say that. But a poor innocent young maid has gone with him—one Salterne’s daughter—the Popish serpent!”

“Rose Salterne, the mayor’s daughter, the Rose of Torridge!”

“That’s her. Bless your dear soul, what ails you?”

Amyas sank back in his seat as if struck. But he regained himself before Mrs. Hawkins could rush to fetch cordials.

“Forgive me, madam,” he said. “I’m weak from the sea. And your ale has turned me a bit dizzy, I think.”

“Ay, yes,” she said. “It’s too strong until you’ve been ashore a while. Try the aqua vitae. My Captain John has it excellent—though a bit too fond of it, poor soul, Heaven forgive him!”

She poured strong brandy and water down his throat, despite his protests, and sent him to bed. But he did not sleep. After a restless night, he set off for Bideford, having secured funds from Mrs. Hawkins.

“Ignorance and evil, even in full flight, deal terrible backhanded strokes at their pursuers.”—HELPS.

It was no small peril, in those turbulent times, to journey from Plymouth toward northern Devon. Unless one chose a long detour, the route passed through the domain of a foreign and hostile chieftain—a man who had repeatedly raided English lands and bested royal forces under Queen Elizabeth’s rule. He was known, though never aloud, as the King of the Gubbings. “So now I dare call them,” wrote Fuller, “secured by distance, which one of more valor durst not do to their face, for fear their fury fall upon him. Yet hitherto have I met with none who could render a reason of their name. We call the shavings of fish (which are little worth) gubbings; and sure it is that they are sensible that the word importeth shame and disgrace.”

As for the suggestion of my learned friend Mr. Joseph Maynard—that those dwelling on the gibberous hills were called Gubbings—such a notion would amuse only those who reject truth for wit. I have read of an England beyond Wales, but the land of the Gubbings is a Scythia within England, and its people pure heathens. It lies near Brent. Legend says that two pregnant women fled there two centuries past, seeking hiding. To them came rough men, and thus began their tribe. They are a self-made people, free from bishop, archdeacon, or any lawful authority—civil or ecclesiastical. They live in hovels, scarcely houses, like swine, sharing all things, breeding without marriage into hundreds. Their speech is the dregs of Devonian dialect; the more learned a man, the less he can understand them. During the civil wars, no soldier was quartered among them, for fear of being lost in their midst. Their wealth is stolen goods—sheep taken from the moors. No search of their homes is ever made; such work is beneath a sheriff, beyond a constable. Swift as wind, they outrun horses; hardy as stone, they outlive most men; living in ignorance of luxury, which extinguishes life. They stand together like bees—offend one, and all will avenge him.

But now I am told they begin to change: they baptize their children, return to human ways, and become Christians again. I hope no civilized folk among us shall turn barbarians, while these barbarians grow civilized.

* * *

With this jest against the Anabaptists of his age, Fuller ends his tale. But I leave him to recount how Amyas, fearing these very Scythians and heathens, rode out of Plymouth on a fine horse, clad in full armor, lance and sword at hand, and two great horse-pistols strapped across his saddle. Behind him rode Salvation Yeo and five or six men from north Devon—those who had served with him in Ireland and now returned on furlough. All wore headpieces and quilted jerkins, each armed with pike and sword, Yeo with arquebus and match. Two sumpter ponies carried their baggage.

They pressed forward as fast as possible, through Tavistock, aiming to reach Lydford before nightfall. But delays in buying horses and other matters held them back until noon. By the time they reached the borderlands of the enemy’s territory, darkness had fallen. A bleak place it was, lit only by the dying glow of sunset. A high plateau of heath, bounded on the right by the jagged crags of Dartmooor, sloping southward and westward toward the base of Brent-Tor, a towering cone resembling an extinct volcano (as some say it truly is), crowned with a tiny church—a votive offering from a Plymouth merchant who once vowed to build a chapel to the Blessed Virgin upon the first stretch of English soil he saw after a desperate voyage.

Far down those barren slopes, thin blue wisps of smoke rose from the huts of the Gubbings. More than once the party halted, scanning distant furze-bushes and wandering ponies, wondering if they might be patrols of a hidden army. In our own century, we may laugh at such fears—but then, they were no joke. They soon found that out.

On the open downs stood a roadside inn: a grim, lichen-streaked lump of granite, windows patched with paper, thatch sagging and weighted down with stones and straw. Behind it sprawled a ramshackle courtyard of barns and walls, where pigs and barefoot children wallowed in shared filth. At the door stood the innkeeper—a burly, sullen man, eyes bleary, face swollen with drink, six feet of brutishness. One hand held up his hose, lacking points; the other scratched his matted hair, flecked with feathers. These feathers marked him: either he’d just risen from bed after a night of sheep-stealing, or he’d embraced the philosophy of Fridericus Dedekind and his disciple Dekker, who wrote: “Consider that as cobweb lawn, woven in May mornings, adorns mountain crests and valley bosoms; or as snowy fleece, stolen by briars from innocent sheep, becomes winter livery—so too, feathers scattered through your hair enhance you, set off your crown nobly. None shall accuse you of lying on straw or musty flock, for those feathers will rise to defend you, proving your bed was of softest down.” Thus did the feathers of Rogues’ Harbor Inn proclaim its master well-supplied with geese.

Suddenly he spied Amyas and his party cresting the hill. His ears pricked up. He counted them, saw Amyas’s armor, shook his head, grunted—and then, in his few words, bellowed:

“Mirooi!--Fushing pooale!”

A sturdy girl—clad only in a green bodice and red petticoat, neither wide enough nor long enough to hide her—brought forth his fishing rod and basket. The man tied his hose with bits of string, examined the footlink.

“Don vlies' gone!”

“May be,” said Mary. “Shouldn’t hay’ left mun out to coort. May be old hen’s ate mun off. I see her chocking about a while agone.”

The host cursed, struck her sharply on the head. She dodged, and returned the blow with good effect. Then, accustomed to such exchanges, he shuffled off, howling:

“Tell Patrico!”

Mary darted inside, combed her hair, slipped on stockings and her best gown, and waited for guests. The travelers frowned at the “mucksy sort of a place,” yet preferred it to camping near the enemy.

The old hen who had swallowed the dun fly was killed, plucked, and roasted. Black Dartmoor mutton went onto the gridiron—only to reveal itself, under fiery torment, as venison. Meanwhile, Amyas secured his horse and ponies in a shed with no lock or key. Grumbling, he returned, uneasy for his steed’s safety. Baggage piled in a corner; Amyas stretched his legs before a turf fire. Yeo, suspicious of the place, posted himself at the door. The men, drawn by the cooking, likely because Mary was the cook, gathered round.

Then Yeo returned.

“There’s a gentleman coming up, sir, all alone.”

“Ask him to join us,” said Amyas. “With my compliments.”

Yeo went out, returned in five minutes.

“He’s gone in by the back court, sir.”

“Well, he has odd taste, if he makes himself at home here.”

Yeo left again, returning after another five minutes—excited.

“Come out, sir! For goodness’ sake come out. I’ve got him. Safe as a rat in a trap!”

“Who?”

“A Jesuit, sir.”

“Nonsense, man!”

“I tell you truth, sir. I went round the house—he didn’t look right. I knew him the moment he stepped up. The way he turned his toes inward, placed his feet so still and careful, as if afraid of offending God at every step. So I peered between the wall and gate—saw him knock at the back door, call ‘Mary!’ quiet as a priest. The girl flies out, ready to eat him alive. ‘Go away,’ I heard her say, ‘there’s a dear man.’ Then something about a ‘queer cuffin’—a justice in thieves’ talk. Then he pulled out a thing—I swear it was a Popish Agnus. Gave it her. She kissed it, crossed herself, asked if she did it right. Put it in her bosom. He said, ‘Bless you, my daughter.’ That sealed it. Then he slipped into the stable, peered in. Seeing no one, he went in. I followed, shut the door, blocked it with a cart, sent a man to watch. The girl’s crying like mad.”

“What a fool’s trick, man! How do you know he’s not some honest gentleman?”

“Fool or not, sir—honest gentlemen don’t give maidens Agnuses. I’ve got him. If you want him freed, you’ll have to do it yourself. My conscience won’t let me release the Lord’s enemies. It’s written: ‘If any let one of them go, his life shall be for the life of him.’”

Amyas hurried out with him. He pulled back the cart, opened the door, and began apologizing—to his cousin Eustace.

There he stood, face half foolish, half venomous, like Reynard when the last spadeful of earth falls back and he’s caught sitting disconsolate on his tail, mere yards from the terriers’ snouts.

Neither spoke for a moment. Then Amyas:

“Well, cousin hide-and-seek, how long have you added horse-stealing to your other trades?”

“My dear Amyas,” said Eustace, meekly, “I may surely enter an inn stable without meaning to steal what’s inside.”

“Of course, old fellow,” said Amyas, mollified. “I was jesting. But what brings you here? Not prudence, certainly.”

“I am bound to know no prudence save for the Lord’s work.”

“That’s giving Agnus Dei and deceiving poor heathen girls, I suppose,” said Yeo.

Eustace replied sharply:

“Heathens? Yes, truly. You Protestants leave these wretches heathens, then persecute those who risk their lives to convert them. Mr. Amyas Leigh, you may hang me at Exeter if you wish to disgrace your family. But from this spot, neither you nor all your queen’s soldiers shall drive me while a soul remains unsaved.”

“Come out of the stable, at least,” said Amyas. “You don’t want to make the horses Papists too, do you? Come out, man, and go to the devil your own way. I shan’t inform on you. And Yeo will keep silent if I tell him.”

“It goes sorely against my conscience, sir—but since he’s your cousin…”

“Of course. Now come in and eat. Supper’s ready. Let bygones be bygones.”

How much forgiveness Eustace felt, I cannot say. But he knew he ought to forgive. Eating with Amyas was an act of grace, and a means to further the Church’s cause. Motives and actions correct—what more was needed? So he entered. Yet he never forgot the scar on his cheek. And whenever Amyas looked at him, Eustace feared his gaze lingered on the mark, peering from under his lashes to see if triumph gleamed in those honest eyes.

They talked over the venison, cautiously at first. But Amyas’s warmth thawed Eustace’s frozen heart. Soon they recalled old haunts, boyhood memories—uncles, aunts, cousins. Without malice, Eustace asked why Amyas was going to Bideford, while Frank and his mother were in London.

“To tell you the truth,” said Amyas, “I cannot rest until I hear the whole story about poor Rose Salterne.”

“What about her?” cried Eustace.

“Do you not know?”

“How should I know? For heaven’s sake, what has happened?”

Amyas told him, surprised by his eagerness—for he’d never suspected Eustace’s love.

Eustace shrieked:

“Fool, fool that I have been! Caught in my own trap! Villain, villain that he is! After all he promised me at Lundy!”

He rose, stamped, gnashed his teeth, tossed his head, clawed at empty air—the gesture of despair that seeks blindly for what is lost forever.

Amyas sat stunned. His first impulse was to ask: “Lundy? What did you know of him? What business had you there?” But pity overcame curiosity.

“Oh, Eustace! And you loved her too?”

“Don’t speak to me! Loved her? Yes, sir. I had as good a right to love her as any of your precious Brotherhood of the Rose. Don’t speak to me, or I’ll do you harm!”

So Eustace knew of the brotherhood too! Amyas longed to ask how—but what use? If he knew, he knew. So he said:

“My good cousin, why be angry with me? If you truly love her, now is the time to counsel with me how best we may—”

Eustace cut him short. Conscious of betrayal, he stopped mid-stride, collected himself, and eyed Amyas from beneath his brows with cold disdain.

“How best we shall do what, my valiant cousin?” he asked, voice half-scornful. “What does your noble Brotherhood of the Rose propose in such a case?”

Amyas, slightly nettled, stood firm.

“What the Brotherhood intends, I cannot yet say. What it ought to do, I have a fair guess.”

“So have I. To hunt her down like an outlaw, because she dared love a Catholic. To murder her lover in her arms, drag her home stained with his blood, force her to renounce the Church into whose maternal arms she has doubtless found peace and holiness!”

“If she has found holiness, it matters little to me where she found it, Master Eustace. But that is precisely what I wish to know.”

“And you will go discover it yourself?”

“Have you no desire to discover it too?”

“And if I had, what would that matter to you?”

“Only,” said Amyas, striving to control his temper, “that if we share the same purpose, we might sail in the same ship.”

“You intend to sail, then?”

“I mean simply that we might work together.”

“Our paths lie on different roads, sir!”

“I am afraid you never spoke truer. In the meantime, before we part, please tell me—what did you mean by saying you met this Spaniard at Lundy?”

“I shall refuse to answer.”

“You will remember, Eustace, that however close friends we’ve been this half-hour, you are in my power. I have a right to know the truth—and by heaven, I will know it.”

“In your power? See that you are not in mine! Remember, sir, you are within miles of those who obey me, their Catholic benefactor, but owe no allegiance to Protestant authorities who have left them to the beasts which perish.”

Amyas grew furious. He almost seized Eustace, shook him, handed him over to Yeo. But he knew it would bring death and dishonor. Remembering Frank’s conduct at Clovelly, he held back.

“Take me,” said Eustace, “if you will. You, who complain we Catholics keep no faith with heretics, will recall that you invited me in as your guest—and in your good faith, I trusted you.”

A weak argument in law—Eustace had been a prisoner before a guest, and Amyas guilty of misprision of treason. Still, all he did was walk to the door, open it, bow, and bid his cousin depart, since he seemed resolved to end his days in hell.

And Eustace vanished.

“Pooh!” muttered Amyas. “I can learn enough, and too much, without help from such crooked vermin. I must see Cary. I must see Salterne. And if I’m ready to do my duty, I shall learn what it is. Now to sleep. Tomorrow, up and away—wherever God sends.”

“Come in here, men!” he shouted down the hall. “Sleep here. Haven’t you had enough of this sour cider?”

The men yawned, settled on the floor.

“Where’s Yeo?”

No one knew. He’d gone out to pray and hadn’t returned.

“Never mind,” said Amyas, suspecting a plot. “He’ll take care of himself.”

“No fear of that, sir,” the men replied. Soon they snored in unison around the fire, while Amyas lay on the settle, saddle for a pillow.

* * * * *

At midnight, Amyas leapt—no, fell—backwards, overturning saddle, settle, table, convinced ten thousand dragons burst through the window, howling fiercely. But the dragons were merely terrified geese, flapping and screaming around the house. The noise that startled them did not cease. Another minute proved a sharp fight raged in the courtyard—and Yeo was shouting for help.

The men rushed out, swords drawn, burst the back door, stumbled over buckets, into the yard. There, back against the stable door, Yeo fought bravely with sword and buckler against a dozen men.

Screams echoed—geese, chickens, pigs, donkeys, Mary from an upper window. Even plovers, drawn by the din, wheeled overhead, adding their cries to the chaos.

Yet the fight ended. As Amyas charged into the yard, the ruffians fled over a low hedge.

“Are you hurt, Yeo?”

“Not a scratch, thank Heaven! But I’ve got two of them—the leaders. One’s against the wall. Your horse finished the other.”

The wounded man was lifted. A huge brute, nearly Amyas’s size. Yeo’s sword pierced his body. He groaned, choked.

“Carry him in. Where’s the other?”

“Dead as a herring, in the straw. Have care, men—horses are near mad!”

After a while, the body was brought out. No pulse, no breath.

“Carry him in too. Poor wretch. Now, Yeo—what happened?”

Yeo’s tale was quick. He couldn’t shake the idea—though unfounded—that Eustace meant to steal the horses. He’d seen the innkeeper slip away. Expecting attack, he’d slept in the stable.

As expected, men tried to enter. The door opened—how, he couldn’t guess, for he’d locked it. Two men came in, began untying the horses. Yeo seized the big one, who drew a knife. He broke free. Horses panicked, kicked. One man fell, the other ran, calling for help—Yeo pursued.

“Seeing a dozen more with clubs and bows,” said Yeo, “I thought best to shorten their number. I ran the rascal through and stood on guard. Just in time, too—two arrows in the wall, three in my buckler. I grabbed it as I left—it was hanging by the door, ready.”

Hardly had they entered the back-kitchen when fresh shouts arose. Amyas rushed forward, banging his head on the doorway. Through the flashes in his eyes, he saw an old acquaintance—held by two sailors.

One arm in doublet sleeve, the other in a dirty shirt; holding up his hose with one hand, candle in the other—lighting his own confusion. Foaming with rage stood Mr. Evan Morgans, alias Father Parsons, looking, as Yeo put it, “the very moral of a half-plucked turkey-cock.” Behind him stood Eustace Leigh.

“We found the maid letting these two out by the front door,” said one captor.

“Well, Mr. Parsons,” said Amyas, “and what are you doing here? A fine nest of thieves and Jesuits we’ve uncovered tonight.”

“About my calling, sir,” said Parsons firmly. “By your leave, I shall prepare this wounded lamb for the account to which your man’s cruelty has untimely sent him.”

The wounded man, lying on the floor, heard Parsons’ voice, moaned for the “Patrico.”

“You see, sir,” said Parsons, pompously, “the sheep know their shepherd’s voice.”

“The Kynge of Spayn is a foul paynim,
And lieveth on Mahound;
And pity it were that lady fayre
Should marry a heathen hound.”
—Kyng Estmere

Six weeks had passed since the duel when the miller of Stow arrived at the great house in distress, begging to borrow the bloodhounds. Rose Salterne was gone—vanished in the night, without a trace, and no one knew where she had fled. Sir Richard was away in Bideford, but the old steward took charge, summoning the keepers and sending servants down to the mill with every idle lad from the parish in tow. They saw the search as sport, a grand maiden-hunt, and judged the matter with all the bias they could muster in Rose’s favor. The miller and his wife were accused of cruelty, even of driving their maid to leap from the cliffs or drown herself in the sea.

On the other hand, the women of Stow were unanimous: the girl had “gone off” with some rogue, and pride would surely bring her downfall. Yet the facts told a different tale. Rose’s trinkets remained behind, untouched. Nothing was missing except some of her linen—a detail that led old Anthony, the steward, to suggest quietly that it might soon turn up in someone else’s chest.

The only clue was a faint footprint beneath her bedroom window. The bloodhound, leashed and tense, whined once, then let out a deep, mournful bay. He surged through the garden gate, dragging his keeper along the lane until they reached the downs above, heading straight toward Marslandmouth. There, breathless, the entire posse halted at Lucy Passmore’s door.

Lucy, as we may now recall, was a widow. Her prophecy about her husband had come true—he had never returned after sailing into the night with Eustace and the Jesuits. She shed a few tears, dried them quickly, and decided to act boldly. She rode to Stow and laid before Lady Grenville a tale of woe: her husband had gone fishing for pollock and never came back. But she claimed to have heard horsemen gallop past her window in the dead of night—surely the Jesuits—and believed they had seized her husband by force. After using him, she insisted, they had killed him and salted his body for provisions on their journey back to Rome. She begged protection from these “Popish skulkers” lurking near Chapel, and appealed to Lady Grenville’s sense of justice: should not the queen grant her a pension? For her heart’s love had been turned into a sainted martyr by idolatrous traitors.

Lady Grenville, who valued Lucy’s skill in healing (especially for children suffering from “housty,” or sore throats), pleaded her case before Sir Richard. His influence secured Lucy a better position than ever for the next few years.

But what of Rose? And where was Lucy herself?

Her door was locked. Around it stood her flock of goats, bleating uselessly for milk. From the hillside above, her donkeys wandered freely, answering the bloodhound’s howl with a cacophony of braying.

“They’m laughing at us, keper, they neddies,” muttered one servant. “Sure enough, we’m lost our labor here.”

Yet the bloodhound, after circling the cottage, veered down the glen, never stopping until he reached the edge of the sea.

“They’m taken water. Let’s go back and rout out the old witch’s house.”

“’Tis just like that old Lucy, to lock a poor maid into shame.”

They returned, broke into the cottage, and ransacked it—not only out of anger, but also, if truth be told, in hope of finding something worth taking. But there was nothing. Lucy had fled with all her belongings—except for the enormous black cat, still curled among the embers. At the sound of the dog, it vanished up the chimney, some saying with a strong smell of brimstone. When they looked outside, they chased it into the woods, where it lived many happy years, a terror to the rabbits of the glen.

The goats and donkeys were driven back to Stow. The mob returned, ashamed and uneasy, fearing what Sir Richard might say.

When he returned, he sold the animals and gave the money to the poor, promising to repay if Lucy ever returned and submitted to justice. But she never did. Her cottage, avoided like a haunted place, stood empty, slowly crumbling into four fern-covered walls. A quiet stream murmured past, pool to pool—the only voice for many years in that lonely glen.

A few days later, Sir Richard stopped at Clovelly Court on his way from Bideford to Stow. In passing, he mentioned news that made Will Cary spring from his seat as if struck.

“We know what it is,” he said.

“And no clue?” asked old Cary, his son too stunned to speak.

“Only this—I hear a man seen prowling near the cliffs that night saw a pinnace running for Lundy.”

Will rose without a word and left the room. Within half an hour, he and several armed men were aboard a trawling-skiff, bound for Lundy.

He did not return for three days. When he did, he brought news: an elderly foreigner had been living for months in a section of ruined Moresco Castle, tenanted by John Braund. A younger foreigner had joined him recently, arriving from a ship—likely a Flemish or Easterling vessel. The ship had come and gone more than once, and the young man traveled with it.

Then, a few days ago, a woman and her maid—a stout woman—had come ashore with him. They spoke long and secretly with the elder man, stayed the night, and departed together at dawn.

The fishermen reported hearing the young man call the older man “father.” He was quiet, solemn, much like a mass-priest.

No more was known—or perhaps no one wished to say.

Old Cary and Sir Richard sent Will back to Lundy, this time with the parish constable of Hartland. The island lies ten miles offshore, within the vast parish of Hartland, cursed by its isolation.

They returned with the body of John Braund—farmer, fisherman, smuggler. After endless questioning (during which Braund suffered from deafness and memory loss), he was sent to Exeter gaol on charges of harboring priests, Jesuits, gypsies, and other “suspect and traitorous persons.”

Poor Braund, whose motives were likely not treason but hunger and desperation—seven children, unpaid rent—did not survive the move from Lundy’s fresh air to Exeter’s pestilential prison. Two years later, the infamous “black assizes” would claim dozens, including nobles and judges, felled by sickness or foul air. Braund fell ill within a week, raving in his cell, and died. His secrets perished with him. Only suspicion remained—of Rose Salterne’s fate.

That she had fled with the Spaniard, few doubted. But where? And in what role?

Bideford, for its part, cared little. Many women resented her beauty; country ladies blamed her for drawing undue attention beyond her station. Young girls dreamed of filling the throne she had abandoned. So Bideford moved forward, better or worse, without her. Though she lingered in some hearts as a fair dream, daily life soon swept her memory away. Her place knew her no more.

And Will Cary? He was shattered. He blamed himself—first for bringing Rose into disgrace, then for driving her into the Spaniard’s arms. St. Leger, sensible and kind, tried to reason with him: the attachment must have begun long before the quarrel; it was inevitable; Salterne’s harshness, not Cary’s wrath, had hastened it. And now, he argued, Rose and her fate were beyond their reach—she was gone with a Spaniard, a Papist, and not worth further sorrow.

But Will would not listen. He wrote to Frank at Whitehall, confessing everything, calling himself fool and villain, pleading for forgiveness. Frank replied: Will had no cause to accuse himself. These strange affections were governed by the stars—by fate itself. To resist such bonds was to defy heaven. As a brother of the Rose, Frank declared he must believe, even defend with sword, that the incomparable Rose of Torridge could choose no unworthy man. That man, though Spanish and Catholic, deserved their loyalty, honor, and faith forever.

Honest Will accepted this as gospel, unaware of the agony it caused Francis Leigh.

He showed the letter triumphantly to St. Leger, who said nothing aloud, but inwardly suspected Frank felt differently. Still, he merely nodded and said, “The man is an angel, as his mother is.”

For days, the matter lay quiet—until Jack Brimblecombe stepped forward.

Now curate of Hartland town, and “passing rich” on forty pounds a year, he approached Will.

“I hope no offence, Mr. William—but when are you and the rest going after—after her?”

The name stuck in his throat.

Cary recoiled. “What’s that to thee, Catiline the blood-drinker?” he joked, trying to laugh it off.

“Don’t laugh at me, sir—it’s no laughing matter. I drank red wine that night, nothing worse. But we swore oaths, Mr. Cary—and oaths are oaths, say I.”

“Of course, Jack. But to go seek her—and then cut her lover’s throat? Absurd, even if she were worth finding!”

Jack stared steadily. After a silence: “How far is it to Caracas, then, sir?”

“What’s that to thee?”

“She was made governor there, I hear. That would be where to find her?”

“You don’t mean to go there?”

Shouted Cary, forcing laughter. “That depends on whether I can scrape the money—or get a berth on a ship. God’s will must be done.”

Will stared. Was the man drunk? Mad? But Jack’s eyes were clear, sober, fixed.

Will had no answer. To mock him was easy. To deny his courage, harder. For here was a vulgar, fat curate—out of his mind, perhaps—but determined to do what no gentleman would: to act, to risk all, to fulfill a vow.

“Well,” said Jack, “it’s a bad look-out. But mother’s well-off if father dies. The maidens are stout wenches—they’ll make good servants, please the Lord. You’ll see they come to no harm, Mr. William, if I never come back.”

Cary was silent.

“And, Mr. William—you know me for an honest man. Will you lend me five pounds? Take my books in pawn? Just to help me start?”

“Are you mad? You’ll never find her!”

“That’s no reason not to try. It’s my duty.”

“But even if you reach the Indies, you’ll be clapped into the Inquisition. Burnt alive—just as sure as your name is Jack.”

“I know,” said Jack, voice heavy. “I’ve struggled with it. I’m a coward, Mr. William—a dirty coward. But maybe the Lord will take care of me, as He does little children and drunken men. If not… I’d sooner burn, and have it over, than live like this any longer.”

He burst into sobs.

“What way, my dear old lad?” said Will, softened.

“Not knowing—whether she’s married to him or not. She was an angel to me—pure as daylight. I knew she was too good for a poor pot-head like me. I prayed every night she’d marry a king—if one fit for her existed. And now I don’t know if she’s living in sin or not…”

“It’s more than I can bear.”

“And if she is married, they won’t keep faith with heretics. They can dissolve the marriage—or send her to the Inquisition. Burn her, Mr. Cary—as easily as me. The devils incarnate!”

Cary shuddered. The truth had never struck him so sharply.

“Yes,” Jack went on, “they can force her to deny her God by torture—if she hasn’t already done so for love of him. I know how love makes a man sell his soul. I’ve been in love. Don’t laugh at me, Mr. Will, or I shall go mad!”

“God knows,” said Will, “I’ve never been less inclined to laugh.”

“Is it so? Bless you for that word!” Jack held out his hand.

“But what of my soul, if I don’t seek her? Just to speak to her—warn her, for God’s sake—even if it helps nothing. To tell her the Lord’s curse on idolatry and Antichrist, on those who deny Him for any creature. Though I doubt He’d be hard on her—how could He? But I must speak. The Lord has laid the burden on me. It must be done. God help me!”

“Jack,” said Cary, “if this is your duty, it is others’.”

“No, sir. You’re a layman. I am a deacon, chaplain to you all. Sworn to seek Christ’s sheep scattered across this wicked world—and that innocent lamb first of all.”

“You have sheep at Hartland.”

“There’s plenty better than I to tend them when I’m gone. But none to tend her—because none love her like me. And none will venture. Who will? No shame in that.”

“I wonder what Amyas Leigh would say if he were home.”

“Say? He’d do. He isn’t one for talk. He’d go through fire and water for her. Trust him, Will Cary. Call me an ass if he won’t.”

“Will you wait till he returns?”

“He may not come back for a year or more.”

“Hear reason, Jack. Wait. Be patient. Something may yet be done.”

“You think so?”

“I cannot promise—but—”

“Then promise me one thing. Tell Mr. Frank what I say—or rather, I’ll wager he’s said the same already.”

“You’re wrong, old man. Here’s his own handwriting.”

Jack read the letter and sighed deeply.

“Well, I thought he was a fine gentleman. Still—if my duty isn’t his, it’s mine. I judge no man. But I go, Mr. Cary.”

“You shall not go until Amyas returns. As I live, I’ll tell your father unless you promise. You dare not disobey him.”

“I don’t know,” Jack said, uncertain. “For conscience’ sake.”
