“At least stay and dine here. We’ll settle whether you break the fifth commandment over good brewed sack.”

Now, a good dinner was Jack’s delight—and often denied. He agreed, and Cary thought he had calmed him.

At least he went home and was seen no more for a week.

Then he returned, joyful.

“I’ve settled all, Mr. Will. The parson of Welcombe will serve my church for two Sundays. I’m off to London—to speak to Mr. Frank.”

“To London? How?”

“On Shanks his mare,” he pointed to his legs. “But I expect I can get a lift on a coaster to Bristol. The rest doesn’t matter.”

Cary tried to dissuade him, then pressed a small loan upon him. Jack left, and Cary heard nothing for three weeks.

Then one evening, Jack walked into Clovelly Court just before supper, thin and weary. He sat among the serving-men until Will appeared. Will raised him above the salt, treated him kindly—something the honest fellow needed—and after supper, questioned him privately.

“I’ve learnt a lesson, Mr. William. I’ve learnt that there is one on earth who loves her better than I, if she had the wit to take him.”

“But what says he of seeking her?”

“He says what I say—Go! And what you say—Wait.”

“Go? Impossible! How can that agree with his letter?”

“That’s no concern of mine. Being nearer heaven than I, he sees clearer. Oh, Mr. Will, he’s not a man—he’s an angel of God. But he’s dying.”

“Dying?”

“Yes, of love for her. I can see it in his eyes, hear it in his voice. But I’m of tougher hide. I can’t die, even if I tried. But I’ll obey my betters. I’ll wait.”

And so Jack returned to Hartland that very evening, despite all pleas to stay.

But he left behind thoughts in Cary’s mind—thoughts that tormented him day and night—until a chance encounter crystallized them all, like ice forming on frozen water.

One murky afternoon, Cary lounged on Bideford quay when Mr. Salterne approached. Cary had avoided him lately, partly from delicacy, partly from disapproval of his coldness.

But now they met face to face.

“Well, Mr. Salterne, how goes the shipping trade?”

“Well enough, sir—if some of you young gentlemen would follow Mr. Leigh’s example and go forth to find new markets for us stay-at-homes.”

“You want rid of us, eh?”

“I don’t know why I should. We sha’n’t cross paths again, whatever once was. But if I were you, I’d be in the Indies by now—unless fighting the queen’s battles closer to home.”

“In the Indies? I’d make a poor hand of Drake’s trade.”

The conversation ended, but Cary remembered the hint.

“So, lad,” he told Amyas later, “to make an end of a long story—if you’re minded to take the old man’s offer, so am I. Westward-ho with you, come foul, come fair.”

“It’ll be a wild-goose chase, Will.”

“If she’s with him, we’ll find her at La Guayra. If not, and the villain has cast her off, that’ll only make it more reason to make an example of him.”

“And if neither are there, Will, the Plate fleets will be. We’d be shamed to return empty-handed. But will your father let you run such a risk?”

“My father?” laughed Cary. “He’s so hopeful of a long line of little Carys to fill my place, he’ll have no lack of heir, come what may.”

“Little Carys?”

“I tell you truth. He must have drunk from the fountain of perpetual youth—our friend Don Guzman’s grandfather sought in Florida. Twelve months ago, he married a tenant’s buxom daughter. Mistress Abishag Jewell has already given him one fat baby. So I shall go—back to Ireland, or with you. Somewhere. I can’t stand this squalling, nor seeing Mistress Abishag sitting in my poor dear mother’s place, telling me every other day she’s come of an illustrious house because she’s third cousin seven times removed to Bishop Jewell.”

“I had three-parts of a quarrel with the old man the other day. After one of her peacock-bouts, I couldn’t help saying: since the Bishop wrote an Apology for the people of England, my father ought to conjure his ghost to write one for himself—and title it, ‘Why green heads should grow on gray shoulders.’”

“You impudent villain! What did he say?”

“He laughed till he cried. Told me if I didnI like it, I might leave it—which is just what I intend to do. But mind—if we go, we must take Jack Brimblecombe with us. Or he’ll throw himself over Harty Point, and his ghost will haunt us to our dying day.”

“Jack shall go. None deserves it better.”

After that, they discussed practicalities. It was decided Amyas would go to London to consult Frank and his mother before any action. The other brothers of the Rose were scattered—St. Leger had returned to his uncle. It would be unfair to demand their aid, and delay would be costly.

As Amyas wisely said: “Too many cooks spoil the broth. Half-a-dozen gentlemen on one ship are as bad as two kings of Brentford.”

With that, he departed for London the next morning, leaving Yeo with Cary.

“He is brass within, and steel without,
With beams on his topcastle strong;
And eighteen pieces of ordinance
He carries on either side along.”

—Sir Andrew Barton.

Let us follow Amyas’s path and take a boat at Whitehall stairs, slipping quietly beneath the ancient arches of London Bridge, then drifting down to Deptford Creek. There, preserved as if in time’s own vault, rests the famed ship *Pelican*, the very vessel in which Sir Francis Drake had sailed around the world. She lies high and dry upon the muddy banks of the Thames, like a veteran warrior laid to rest after years of storm and glory. Upon her mainmast are nailed verses and epigrams in honor of both her and her captain—three of them recorded by Camden, the Winchester scholar, in his history. Queen Elizabeth herself once blessed the ship with solemn ceremony, feasting aboard her decks before knighting Drake there and then. “At that moment,” Camden writes, “a plank bridge broke under the crush of people, sending a hundred men plunging into the river below—but not one was harmed. So it seems this ship was built under a lucky star.”

Since that day, the *Pelican* has stood as a monument, a curiosity, and a place of revelry for noble gatherings from the city. Today, she is alive with festivity: flags flutter from her masts, laughter echoes through her old hull, and savory aromas drift from windows that once were portholes. Waiters in white aprons dart between the riverbank and the ship, crossing the same “lucky bridge” that once collapsed under the weight of royal celebration. A grand feast is clearly underway—judging by the city badges worn by the serving men, the presence of civic dignitaries, and the unmistakable air of importance that clings to every guest.

The occasion? The Lord Mayor hosts a dinner for gentlemen connected to the Leicester household, all deeply engaged in dreams of foreign discovery. What better setting than the *Pelican* herself?

Look about you—the company is no ordinary gathering. Few nobler assemblies have ever met beneath a single roof. And if you are an American, look closely at these faces. In their eyes and brows, you see the fathers of your nation—men whose courage, vision, and daring laid the foundation for the lands you now call home.

At the head of the table sits Sir Edward Osborne, Lord Mayor of London—a man whose name still rings in history. He is clothed in the dignity of age and wealth, yet his spirit remains unbroken. Once, he leapt from a window on London Bridge into the roaring tide to save a child—now that child is his wife. That youthful act of chivalry has ripened into the steady resolve of a merchant-adventurer. He sits regally, flanked by the Earl of Cumberland on his right and Sir Walter Raleigh on his left. Together, they speak in low voices of undiscovered lands—vast territories rumored to lie between Florida and the River of Canada. Raleigh, ever the dreamer, cites Doctor Dee, the mystic philosopher, but Osborne listens with quiet skepticism. It is only when he turns to Richard Hakluyt, the Oxford scholar seated beside him, that Raleigh finds a listener who shares his passion. But Hakluyt is deep in conversation with Anthony Jenkinson, the great traveler of Asia, whose beard flows like snow and whose face bears the marks of countless storms. Jenkinson speaks of reindeer sledges across Siberia, of Arctic ice cliffs where Tungoos unearth fossil ivory—proof, he says, of Noah’s flood.

Beside him sits Christopher Carlile, son-in-law to Walsingham and soon to command troops in Drake’s 1585 West Indian campaign. He talks now with Alderman Hart, Sheriff Spencer, Charles Leigh (Amyas’s cousin), Aldworth of Bristol, and William Salterne—another kinsman of Amyas’s friend from Bideford. For two years, Carlile and Walsingham have worked tirelessly to raise funds for Humphrey and Adrian Gilbert’s voyages to the North-West. Carlile himself was meant to sail—but did not. Why? No one knows.

On the opposite side of the table, another circle of minds gathers. Martin Frobisher and John Davis, explorers of the Arctic passage, debate with Alderman Sanderson, the renowned geographer and globe-maker. With them are Mr. Towerson, Sir Gilbert Peckham, Captain John Winter, and lastly, Philip Sidney—whose courteous nature has caused him to give up his rightful seat near the head so he might sit among scholars and adventurers. He brings with him his closest friends: Edward Dyer and Francis Leigh. They too discuss the North-West Passage. Sidney laments his own constraints—bound to court and diplomacy—while envying Frobisher’s life at sea.

“'Tis fine talk here,” Frobisher replies, “sailing on dry land with a glass of wine. But try it among icebergs, your beard frozen to your ruff—especially if you’re faint of stomach.”

“That would be a small trial,” Sidney answers, “if it earned me the honor you received—when the queen herself waved farewell from Greenwich Palace with her ivory hand.”

“Well,” says Frobisher, “they say you’ve no reason to complain of favors. If you can win them ashore, why go to sea?”

Towerson nods gravely, his gray beard having seen many a voyage.

Then a waiter enters.

“My lord mayor, a tall gentleman outside requests to speak with Sir Walter Raleigh.”

“Show him in,” says Osborne. “Sir Walter’s friends are ours.”

Amyas steps into the cabin, hesitating at the door.

“Captain Leigh!” cry several voices.

“Why didn’t you come in?” asks Osborne. “You know your way well enough on these decks.”

“I do, my lords,” Amyas replies. “But Sir Walter—excuse me.” His glance speaks volumes. Raleigh, pale as death, rises and follows him into a private cabin. Five minutes pass. Then Amyas emerges alone.

In brief words, he tells the company what we already know—the tragic news of Sir Humphrey Gilbert’s death. As the tale unfolds, tears glisten on some of the sternest faces.

“The ancient Egyptians,” says Osborne, “placed a corpse at their banquets to remind them of mortality. Have we forgotten God and our frailty in this feast? Has He sent us this message from beyond the grave?”

“No, my lord,” says Sidney. “Not from the dead—but from eternal life.”

“Amen,” Osborne replies. “But our feast ends here. Some may drink on bravely despite personal loss—but none can celebrate while the memory of such a man hangs heavy in the air.”

Indeed. Though many had lost money in the failed expedition, none could forget the shock of Gilbert’s passing. The gathering breaks up quickly, each man whispering to his neighbor, “What will the queen say?”

Raleigh returns shortly, silent, shaking hands with those he passes. He beckons Amyas to follow him to a wherry. Sidney, Cumberland, and Frank join them in another boat. The two brothers travel together, talking in hushed tones.

They disembark at Whitehall stairs. Raleigh, Sidney, and Cumberland go to the palace. Amyas and Frank walk to their mother’s lodgings.

Amyas had prepared a speech for Frank about Rose Salterne—but now, standing before him, he lacks the courage to begin. He hopes Frank will speak first. But Frank, too, dreads what must come—especially since he knows nothing of Amyas’s journey to Bideford or the fate of the *Rose*.

Upstairs, they find their mother away at the Abbey—relief for both. They stand at the bay window, looking out over the river, speaking of trivial things, studying each other’s faces in the fading light. Three years have passed since they last met.

Time has changed them both. Frank looks up at Amyas with affectionate pride. The rollicking sailor-boy is gone. In his place stands a confident, stately warrior—every gesture marked by strength and purpose:

“The reason firm, the temperate will,
Endurance, foresight, strength, and skill.”

Trained by Drake and Grenville, refined by Raleigh and Gilbert, Amyas is a man forged in fire. His long hair is now cropped short. A rich golden beard frames his lips and chin. His face is weathered by sun and storm. A scar crosses his temple—a trophy from an Irish battle. His broad frame has grown even broader. His hand, resting on the sill, is hard and massive like a blacksmith’s. Frank places his own hand upon it—and sighs. Amyas looks down, startled by the contrast: Frank’s fingers are slender, pale, almost translucent—those of a courtier, not a sailor.

Amyas gazes into his brother’s face. It has changed. The flush remains, but the skin is dull, the lips pale, the features sharper. His eyes burn with unnatural fire. When Frank says Amyas looks older, Amyas cannot help thinking the same applies to Frank.

Trying to ignore the truth, he begins asking names of buildings along the river.

“And so this is old Father Thames, with his bank of palaces?”

“Yes,” Frank replies. “His banks are grand—but he does not linger. He rushes toward the sea, the sea into the ocean, and the ocean westward—forever. All things move westward. Perhaps we shall too, Amyas.”

“What do you mean?”

“Only that the ocean mirrors the heavens’ motion—flowing east to west. Is it strange I should think of it, just after drinking to ‘Westward-ho’?”

“And much good came of it! I lost my best friend, the noblest captain, and all my savings—all in that cursed gulf of Westward-ho.”

“Sir Humphrey Gilbert’s star set in the west—why not? Sun, moon, planets all sink westward. Why not the sparks of this world? Why not a will-o’-the-wisp like me, Amyas?”

“God forbid!”

“Why? Is not the west the land of peace, of dreams? Does not every heart leap at the setting sun, longing to float with him on golden clouds? Men are buried facing east. I’d rather face west, Amyas. For I believe the ancients guessed rightly—that Elysium lies beneath the sunset. It is written in the soul of man: the longing for the west. I blame no one who flees thither beyond the sea, as David wished to flee—to be at peace.”

“You blame no one?” Amyas asks.

“No. If I blamed anyone, it would be you—for seeming tired of going Westward-ho.”

“Do you wish me to go?”

“God knows,” Frank says after a pause. “But I must tell you now—once and for all. Something has happened at Bideford…”

“Spare us both, Frank—I know all. I passed through Bideford on my way here. I came not just to see you and Mother—but to ask your advice, and hers.”

“True heart! Noble heart!” Frank exclaims. “I knew you would be loyal!”

“So—Westward-ho it is?”

“Can we escape?”

“We?”

“Amyas—what binds you binds me.”

Amyas steps back, gripping Frank’s shoulders. Through the thin fabric, he feels the bones beneath—skin and bone.

“You? Dearest man—this voyage would kill you in a month!”

Frank smiles, tossing his head.

“I belong to Thales, who said the ocean is the mother of all life. I feel no more repugnance returning to her arms than Humphrey Gilbert did.”

“But—Mother?”

“Mother knows. And she would not have us unworthy of her.”

“Impossible! She’ll never let you go!”

“All things are possible to those who believe in God, my brother—and she believes. Besides, Doctor Dee told her this summer of certain signs concerning me. I am born under a cold, watery planet. To live long, I must seek the sun’s warmth—bask in its heat like a fly on a wall. He warned me to avoid winters here. Return to the sea-breezes, he said, to warm my frozen lungs. He told stories of men given up for dead in Germany or France, who renewed their youth in Italy, Spain, or the Canaries. He has filled Mother’s mind with hope. She may be more willing to let me go than I am to leave her.”

“And yet—you must go. Not only because my heart burns, like Sidney’s, to join your noble band of Argonauts—men who are filling the earth and subduing it for God and the queen. Not only because love calls me—love fierce and unyielding, made stronger by absence, deeper by despair. But honor, Amyas—my oath—”

He stops, gasping, then collapses into a fit of coughing. Amyas supports him.

“Fools—fools we were—fools I was—to take that foolish vow!”

“No,” a gentle voice replies from behind. “You vowed for peace on earth, and goodwill toward men. 'Blessed are the peacemakers,' for they shall be called children of God. My sons—be sure such sacrifice will be rewarded by Him who gave Himself for you.”

“Oh, Mother! Mother!” Amyas cries. “Do you not hate seeing me—coming to take your firstborn?”

“My boy, God takes him—not you. And Doctor Dee said something extraordinary: great honors await you both in the West—each according to your worth.”

“Ah,” says Amyas. “My blessing will be like Esau’s—to live by the sword. While Jacob, the spiritual one, inherits heaven and an angel’s crown.”

“Be it what it may,” she says, “it will be a blessing—if you remain as you are. At least, Frank will be safe from court intrigues and worldly temptations. Would that I could go with you—share your glory! Come now,” she adds, laying her head on Amyas’s chest and smiling up at him, “I’ve heard of heroic mothers who went with their sons to war. Why not with you on a peaceful quest? I could nurse the sick. I could speak to that poor girl—perhaps win her back. Women understand women. Or I could mend and wash. Is it not as easy to be a good housewife on a ship as on land? Come!”

Amyas looks from one to the other.

“God only knows which is less fit to go. Mother—you don’t know what you ask. Frank—you don’t want to come. This is no gentle adventure. It demands sharp shot and cold steel.”

“How?” they cry together, horrified.

“I must pay my men, my partners. I must pay them with Spanish gold. And as a loyal subject, I cannot sail to the Spanish Main with a clear conscience unless I strike at her enemies—by day and night, with every weapon I have.”

“What nobler knight-errantry?” Frank says cheerfully.

Mrs. Leigh shudders.

“Frank too?” she murmurs. Her sons know her fear. Amyas’s life of war, though honorable, she bore as a necessary burden. But Frank—her gentle, poetic son—becoming a man of blood? That was unbearable. She had never known of his duel. Now, imagining him amidst carnage, she recoils. Yet, she whispers: “Is this another idol I must renounce?”

Then, grasping at hope: “Frank must ask the queen’s permission. If she allows it, how can I deny her wisdom?”

So the talk ends—sadly.

But now, a new worry grips Frank—one Amyas at first thinks jest, then realizes is serious. Frank fears the queen’s audience as if facing execution. Days pass before he gathers courage. He spends them building support through Leicester, Hatton, and Sidney—as if pleading for mercy.

“Why,” says Amyas, “can’t she cut your head off for wanting to go to sea?”

“But what axe sharper than her frown?” Frank replies, in deepest despair.

Amyas whistles tunelessly.

“Ah, brother—you cannot know the pain of parting from her.”

“No,” Amyas says. “I’d die for a hair of her royal head. But I could live till Doomsday without ever seeing it.”

“Plato’s Troglodytes regretted not sunlight they’d never seen.”

Amyas, baffled, says nothing.

Finally, Frank gets his audience. After two hours, he returns pale and spent.

“Thank Heaven—it’s over. She was furious at first—what else? She accused me of loving too low. I said my fault was committed before I knew true beauty. Then she charged me with disloyalty—swearing allegiance to another. I confessed with tears. When she threatened punishment, I said the offense had punished itself—exile from her light, into darkness. Then she asked: How could I, her sworn servant, desert her in these perilous times? What if I returned to find her dead by assassin’s hand? I fell to my knees, begging mercy. And she—turning to Hatton—asked, 'What say you, Mouton? Is he humbled enough?' Then she dismissed me.”

“Heigh-ho!” yawns Amyas.
“If the bridge had been stronger,
My tale had been longer.”

“Amyas! Amyas!” Frank says solemnly. “You do not know the power of royalty—its native majesty, magnified by wisdom and womanhood. If I had my way, every realm would have not a Salique law, but an anti-Salique: no kings, only queens. Then weakness, not power, would symbolize divinity. Love, not cunning, would decide every cause. Chivalry, not fear, would inspire obedience.”

“Humph,” says Amyas. “There’s sense in that. I’d run a mile for a woman when I’d not walk a yard for a man. But—who is this Mother brings in? The handsomest fellow I’ve ever seen!”

He was not wrong. Mrs. Leigh’s companion was none other than Mr. Secretary—Amyas’s old acquaintance from Smerwick Fort—also known as Colin Clout, Immerito, Edmund Spenser.

A half-jesting exchange had passed between poet and saint. As they entered, Mrs. Leigh smiled—though somewhat forced—“Well, my dear sons, you are assured of immortality. Mr. Spenser vows to dedicate an entire canto to your adventure in his *Faerie Queene*.”

“And you no less, madam,” Spenser replied. “What value has the story of the Gracchi without Cornelia? If I honor the fruit, I must not forget the stem. Frank, I congratulate you.”

“Then you know the result of my interview, Mother?”

“I know everything—and am content,” she said.

“Mrs. Leigh has reason to be content,” Spenser added, “for it is but her own likeness.”

“Spare your flattery, Mr. Spenser. When did I”—with a loving glance at Frank—“refuse knighthood for duty’s sake?”

“Knighthood?” Amyas cries. “You never told me that, Frank!”

“That may well be, Captain Leigh,” Spenser says, “but I’m told—by Hatton—that the queen informed him today that by taking this voyage, he forfeited the highest earthly honor, one even crowned heads seek from their subjects.”

Spenser spoke truly. Knighthood in that age was the reward of merit, so prized that Elizabeth once said, when asked why she didn’t make a favorite a peer, “I’ve already knighted him—there’s nothing higher to give.” It was not until Essex’s disastrous Irish campaign that the order began to decline—and James I completed its ruin by selling baronetcies, a mockery of nobility that brought shame upon its first holders.

Yet, in that moment, the honor remained sacred—and the dream of Westward-ho, alive.

“The sun's rim dips; the stars rush out;
At one stride comes the dark.”

Land! Land! Land! There it lay, far to the south and west, beside the sinking sun—a long blue line stretching across the crimson sea and golden sky. At last, land: a promise of fresh streams, cool fruit, open space for limbs worn thin by sickness and confinement. And perhaps, too, gold and gems, treasures beyond imagining—wealth from the Indies, whispered in dreams and maps alike. Who could say? Why not? The old world, with its rigid laws and dusty truths, was thousands of miles behind them. Before them stretched the realm of myth and miracle, where every wave might carry wonder, every breeze bring fortune.

Sick men dragged themselves from their stifling hammocks; strong men fell to their knees in gratitude. All eyes strained toward that distant haze, fading as the sun sank, yet growing clearer and broader as the ship surged forward on the steady trade-wind, which murmured softly around sail and spar: *“I am the faithful friend of those who dare.”*

“Blow freshly, freshlier yet, thou good trade-wind,” Frank whispered, his voice barely audible over the wind’s sigh. “It is written that He makes the winds His angels, breathing life into those who are heirs of salvation. Blow freshlier yet—and save, if not me from death, then her from worse than death. Blow on, and land me at her feet, to call the lost lamb home… and die.”

His face was pale, drawn, cheeks hollow, but flushed with feverish color in the dying light. A few minutes passed, and the western skies bled into twilight—rainbows vanished, emeralds and rubies dimmed to silver-gray. Above, the moon rose, bright and cold, joined by Venus, gleaming like a jewel above the darkened sea.

“That should be Barbados, your worship,” said Drew, the master, pointing ahead. “Unless my reckoning is wildly off—which, Heaven knows, it has no right to be after such a passage. God be praised.”

“Barbados?” Amyas replied. “I’ve never heard of it.”

“Very likely, sir,” Drew answered. “But Yeo and I were here with Captain Drake. I came again later, with poor Captain Barlow. There’s safe anchorage to the south and west—I remember well.”

“And no Spaniard, no cannibal, no beast of ill omen,” added Yeo. “A garden of the Lord, hidden in the deeps, meant for those who love Him. I recall Captain Drake speaking of planting it—if ever he had the chance.”

“I remember now,” Amyas said. “There was talk between him and Sir Humphrey about an island here. Would that he had gone there instead of to Newfoundland!”

“Nay,” said Yeo gently, “he rests now with the Lord. You would not have kept him from that?”

“He would have waited as willingly as he went,” Amyas replied, “if he could have served his queen thereby. But what say you, masters? Shall we rest here a few days to heal the sick before we press on to the Main and begin our work?”

All agreed—except Frank, who remained silent.

“Come, fellow-adventurer,” Cary urged. “We must hear your voice too.”

“To my impatience, Will,” Frank murmured aside, “there is but one place on earth, and all day I long for wings to reach it. But the counsel is sound. I approve.”

The decision was made, and the crew cheered. Long before dawn, they sailed along the southern shore, feeling their way into the bay where Bridgetown now stands. Eyes were fixed upon the low hills, wrapped in moonlight and dotted with fireflies, beneath a sky ablaze with stars. Noses drank in the sweet air, thick with floral perfume from the land. Ears welcomed the new sounds—the hum of insects, the snoring of tree-toads, the melancholy calls of shore birds—life pulsing in the night.

At last, the ship stopped. The cable rattled through the hawsehole. And then, heedless of danger—Spaniards or Caribs—an instinctive cheer burst from every throat.

Poor souls! Amyas had to hold them back, reminding them it was still two hours until daylight.

“Never were two such long hours,” muttered a young lad, pacing nervously.

“You never stood before the Inquisition,” Yeo said dryly. “Then you’d know how time can crawl. Stand still, and give thanks you’re where you are.”

“Gunner,” asked another, “is there gold on this island?”

“Never heard of any,” Yeo replied. “And so much the better.”

“But Gunner,” said a scurvy-ridden cripple, licking his cracked lips, “are there oranges and lemons?”

“Not that I’ve seen,” Yeo said, “but plenty of fine fruit down to the beach—thank the Lord. Look—dawn is breaking.”

Up flared the rose-colored sky. The sun rose in full glory, casting sharp rays across the palm trees, painting rainbows on the coral reefs, gilding distant uplands where now stand grand estates and busy factories. Pelicans flew in long lines out to sea. The insect hum faded. Birds erupted into song. A thin blue mist crept upward, then dissolved under the fierce heat. The land-breeze, which had blown all night, died away into silence. The tropic day had begun.

The sick were carried ashore in boats, laid gently on the sand beneath the palms. Within half an hour, the entire crew had scattered across the beach—save a dozen men left aboard to watch the ship until noon.

And now, nature’s first cry echoed: *Fruit! Fruit! Fruit!*

The weak crawled from bush to vine, tearing at violet grapes from creeping vines, staining their mouths with prickly pears, ignoring Yeo’s warnings about the thorns. Some healthy men began chopping down coconut palms, only to dull their hatchets uselessly. Then Yeo and Drew gathered a handful of sensible men and marched inland. An hour later, they returned laden with treasures from the wild orchard—tart junipa apples, juicy guavas, and golden pineapples, crowned queens among fruits, found in abundance on the sun-baked cliffs.

They sat on the warm sand, laughing, eating, unbothered by galliwasps or jackspaniards. Blue crabs scuttled from their burrows, waving claws in defiance. Cranes stood in shallow water, heads tilted, pondering the strange bipeds who had come to disturb their quiet isle.

Frank wandered alone, quiet, more in awe than sorrow. Amyas followed, chewing a junipa apple, playing the part of guide, showing off as though he’d seen all wonders already.

“New, new—everything new!” Frank mused.

“Oh, terrible feeling!” he continued. “All things changed—down to the smallest fly, the tiniest flower—yet we remain the same. Forever unchanged.”

Amyas, unable to grasp such thoughts, pointed. “Look, Frank—that’s a colibri. You’ve heard of colibris?”

Frank watched the tiny creature—alive, radiant, humming loudly—as it hovered over a strange bloom, darting away as if calling its mate, then dancing in dizzy circles, flashing rainbows with every shift of light.

He stared solemnly, then whispered: “*Qualis Natura formatrix, si talis formata?* Oh, my God—how fair must Thy true world be, if even Thy shadows are so beautiful!”

“Phantoms?” Amyas asked, uneasy.

“That’s no ghost, Frank,” he said. “Just a little honey-sucker, with a wife and children no bigger than peas—but solid, greedy little fellows, I’ll wager.”

“Not phantoms in your sense,” Frank replied, “but in the sense of those who see all below as fleeting, false.”

“I’ll tell you what, brother Frank,” Amyas said, “you’re wiser than I, I know. But I can’t abide seeing you look down on God’s good earth. See—God made all these things. No man may have seen them till fifty years ago. Yet they were just as lovely then as now—ever since creation. Why do you think God placed them here? But to please Himself”—and Amyas removed his hat—“with the sight of them. Now, I say, brother Frank, if it’s good enough for God, it’s good enough for us.”

“Your rebuke is just,” Frank said softly. “And God forgive me—though I have learning that brings no profit, longings that bring no peace—when I presume, sinner that I am, to be more refined than the Lord Himself. He walked in Paradise among the trees of the garden, Amyas. So shall we, and be content with what He sends. Why seek the next world before we are fit even for this one?”

“And in the meanwhile,” Amyas said, smiling, “this earth is quite good enough—at least here in Barbados.”

“Do you believe,” Frank asked, trying to shift his thoughts, “those tales the Spaniards tell—of Sirens and Tritons singing in these seas?”

“I can’t say,” Amyas replied. “There’s more fish in the water than ever came out of it. More wonders in the world than we dream of. I’ve never been here before. In the South Seas, I never saw any—but Yeo says he heard sweet music at night, far from land, in the Gulf.”

“The Spaniards say,” Frank continued, “that at certain times, choirs of nymphs gather in the sea, singing songs of watery love. It may be so. For Nature, who has filled the land with rational beings, may not have left the sea entirely empty. Especially when we consider that the ocean is the source of all fertility. Its slime—so the learned say, following Thales of Miletus—is the primal matter from which all things were formed. Thus, the ancients wisely imagined Venus, mother of all life, born from the sea-foam, rising from the deep and floating ashore upon Greek isles.”

“I don’t know what ‘plastic force’ means,” Amyas admitted, “but I wish I’d been there when the pretty poppet came up. The nearest thing I ever saw was maidens swimming alongside us in the South Seas—would have boarded us, too. But Drake sent them off—naughty packs, I suspect. Look at the butterflies now! Don’t you wish you were a boy again, chasing them in your cap?”

And so the two walked together through the glorious woods, returning to find the sick already revived—some who couldn’t move that morning now walking, gaining strength with each step.

“Well done, lads!” Amyas called. “Keep your spirits high. We’ll have music ashore after dinner—no mermaids to sing for us, but those who can dance may.”

And so the four days passed. The men, like boys on holiday, gave themselves to simple joys—washing clothes, filling casks with fresh water, storing fruit that might keep. They searched for gold, hoping to find it in every bush, though Yeo warned them none had ever been found on the island. Soon tired, they lounged about, collecting shells and sea-fans to send home to lovers, smoking agoutis from hollow trees with shouts and laughter, tormenting every creature they could catch—until not a crab dared show its face, nor an armadillo unroll itself.

Then, finally, they set sail again—unaware pioneers of a future rich with wealth, commerce, beauty, and knowledge—setting course westward, leaving behind the island that would one day become the crown jewel of the Caribbean.

They had slipped past the southern tip of Grenada under cover of night, and now found themselves within the enchanted circle of islands where nature had poured out all her splendor and man had heaped up all his sin. If Barbados had seemed a marvel to these Englishmen, how much more so must this sea be—calm almost without end, untouched by the hurricanes that roared far to the north. Sky, sea, and land formed one vast, shimmering rainbow, though perhaps little noticed by the practical sailors whose thoughts were fixed on Spanish gold and pearls. And certainly not by Amyas, who, accustomed to tropical landscapes, was already plotting in secret how best to rid the West Indies of the Spaniards and bring them into the realm of Queen Elizabeth. Yet even their hardened eyes could not help but feel awe at the lands they now approached—lands famed throughout Europe, yet still new, wrapped in legend, greed, and sorrow. To sail here was to enter a world nearly unknown to Englishmen, where failure meant not just death, but the agonies of the Inquisition.

Fewer than five times before had English ships dared these waters. But among those aboard were men who knew them well—men who, under Captain John Hawkins, had first attempted to trade along these coasts, only to be barred by Spanish pride. Denied access to life’s necessities, they had seized what they needed with steel and fire, then traded fairly once peace was won. The old mariners of the *Pelican* and the *Minion* were questioned all day long about every island, every cape, every fish and bird. Frank stood silent beside them, listening intently.

A deep reverence had taken hold of him—not sadness, but wonder. His face seemed to drink in the beauty around him, and often he whispered to himself, “This is the gate of heaven.” He watched the ship’s progress all day, forgetting food and rest, as each new turn revealed another miracle. Islands rose high above the water, their reflections inverted below; sandbars melted and twisted in the heat haze; yellow flowers and towering cacti, like giant torches, flickered and danced. The whole world seemed unreal—a blazing vision where nothing was stable, not even the sun, which sank through crimson mists, warped into strange ovals and pear shapes.

While Frank gazed in awe, Yeo rejoiced. To the south of the setting sun, a cluster of tall peaks rose from the sea. They were, unless his reckoning failed him, the mountains of Macanao, at the western end of Margarita—the Isle of Pearls. A name known across Mediterranean ports and German fairs, second only to the pearl-rich island in Panama’s gulf, where fifteen years earlier John Oxenham had lost his life.

The next day they sailed along the northern shore of the island, passing unseen—so far as they could tell—the Spanish fortress built at the eastern end to guard the pearl fisheries.

At last they entered a deep, quiet bay, thickly wooded to the water’s edge. In the roadstead lay a caravel and three boats moored alongside. At the sight, every man rushed to the deck, voices rising in debate over what should be done. Some urged sailing straight in, the breeze strong toward the shore, as it often was in the afternoon. But seeing waves breaking near the mouth of the bay, they decided instead to pass quietly, then send in the pinnace and boat.

Yeo suggested flying Spanish colors to avoid alarming the caravel. But Amyas refused. “It would be base,” he said, “to lie in such fashion, unless we were in mortal danger or serving some great cause of state.”

So they continued their course until the next headland blocked their view, then launched their boats: Cary in the larger, with twenty men; Amyas in the smaller, with fifteen, including John Brimblecombe, who insisted on coming in full cassock and bands, carrying an old sword passed down from his uncle.

As they neared the bay’s entrance, they saw what they expected—coral reefs, too many to navigate easily. They had to follow the edge for some distance before finding a passage. As they did, those unfamiliar with the region gazed in wonder through the clear water at living gardens beneath the surface—fields of coral, schools of fish, and the hidden realms of Nereus and Amphitrite.

Then, suddenly, below them appeared what Yeo called “a school of sharks”—some nearly as long as the boat itself. Their eyes, dark and cruel, stared upward.

“Jack,” Amyas said, sitting beside him, “look how that big one watches you. I’ll wager he’s eyeing your fat hide and thinking you’d make tender eating.”

Jack turned pale, but said nothing.

Just then, that very shark spotted a parrotfish emerging from a crack in the reef and lunged from below—followed by two others. The fish, cornered, leapt clean out of the water, nearly onto the boat. Where it had surfaced, several broad, shaggy-nosed heads crashed together within two yards of Jack, showing rows of jagged teeth, then sank back into the depths.

At that moment, Jack whispered, “In manus tuas, Domine!” and turned his gaze away, no longer wishing to see the creatures.

Once through the reef, they entered the bay under a fair wind. The caravel was now within musket range. Cary rowed straight for her, boarding before the Spaniards could reach their guns. Standing in the stern, he shouted, “Yield!”

The captain demanded, “In whose name?”

“In the name of common sense, ye dogs!” bellowed Will. “Do you not see? You are fifty against our twenty?”

He scrambled up the side. The captain fired a pistol. Cary knocked him down, unwilling to spill unnecessary blood. The crew surrendered—some falling to their knees, others diving overboard. The prize was theirs.

Meanwhile, Amyas circled behind the ship and boarded the second boat, the nearest being already secured. The Spaniards yielded without resistance, crying “Misericordia!” The negroes jumped into the water and swam ashore like sea-dogs.

But the third boat, barely an oar’s length away, began to pull off. Then came a remarkable scene. John Brimblecombe, eager to prove himself, grabbed a boat-hook and latched onto the stern, shouting, “Stay, ye Papists! Stay, Spanish dogs!”

As expected, outnumbered ten to one, he was pulled overboard, landing hard on his face in the sea. The hook remained fastened to the boat.

Now, something seized him—fear, perhaps, or his imagination running wild. He began roaring like a bull, terrified of the sharks he had just seen. In his confusion, he didn’t think to swim back to his own boat. Instead, he struck out wildly after the fleeing Spanish vessel—either hoping to catch the trailing hook, or driven by a madness of courage. No one could say. He swam forward, his cassock floating behind him like a black monk-fish, screaming and gasping, salt water filling his mouth:

“Stay, ye Spanish dogs! Help, good fellows! See you not I am a dead man? They’re nuzzling my toes! He has hold of my leg! My right thigh is bitten clean off! Oh, that I were preaching in Hartland pulpit! Yield, Papist cowards, lest I make mincemeat of you! Take me aboard! Yield, I say, or my blood be on your heads! I am no Jonah—should he swallow me, he will never cast me up again! Better to fall into the hands of men than into the hands of devils with three rows of teeth each. In manus tuas. Orate pro anima—!”

And so on, more frantic than Panurge ever was in his famous bout of seasickness. The English, fearing he would be torn apart by sharks or beaten by oars, opened fire. The Spaniards, seeing this, jumped overboard. Jack scrambled back into the boat, drew his sword with one hand, wiped water from his eyes with the other, and slashed wildly through the air, yelling, “Yield, idolaters! Yield, Spanish dogs!”

Eventually, regaining his senses and finding no one left to fight, he collapsed in the stern, panting, and began removing his hose.

Amyas, thinking he had been overcome by sunstroke or shock, ordered the boat pulled alongside and asked, “What in heaven’s name are you doing with your breeches?”

Jack, amid laughter that shook the boat, swore his right thigh had been bitten clean through to the bone—and that he felt blood soaking his hose. He nearly fainted from imagined loss of blood, until his friends, after much argument and irritation on his part, convinced him he was unharmed.

They then searched the prize. It carried hides and salt pork—but also, in the captain’s cabin and the boat Brimblecombe had boarded, neat bundles of leaves concealing large pearls. Though somewhat brown (the Spaniards had burned the shells to open them quickly, spoiling the color), they were fine pearls nonetheless. The value was uncertain, but the crew returned satisfied—though one mischievous soul set the ship ablaze. Laden with hides, it burned fiercely.

Amyas was furious at the wanton destruction—Drake himself had never done such a thing. But Cary chuckled. “Ah,” he said, “we’re ‘Lutheran devils,’ you know. So we’re bound to vanish with an evil stench, like other fiends.”

No sooner was Amyas back aboard than he turned on Brimblecombe. “You ought to show more backbone,” he said. “Roaring before you’re hurt, wasting breath—what good does that do? If you wish the crew to heed your words, keep your voice for when it matters.”

Frank, overhearing, stepped in. “Leave him to me,” he said. “Come hither, thou recreant Jack, thou lily-livered Jack, thou hysterical Jack. Tell me—have you read Plato’s Dialogues and Aristotle’s Logic?”

“Yes,” Jack replied humbly.

“Then let us reason like ancient sages. Does the greater contain the less?”

“Yes, sure.”

“And that which exceeds a part, contains that part?”

“Yes, sure.”

“Then is not a priest greater than a layman?”

“Of course.”

“So a priest contains a man, and is a man, and more—his priesthood?”

“I suppose so.”

“Then if a priest shows himself no man, does he not show himself all the more no priest?”

“Master Frank,” Jack said, “you may be right in logic, but sharks don’t understand logic.”

“Nay, but Jack,” Frank replied, “is it a man’s duty to howl like a pig in a gate because he fears something that isn’t there?”

Jack had no answer.

“And if it *were* there, would it not be a brave man’s duty to keep his mouth shut—to keep salt water out, and not choke while being eaten?”

“Oh,” Jack sighed, “that’s all very well. But you know as well as I that it wasn’t the Spaniards I feared. They were Heaven’s work, and I knew how to deal with them. But those fiends’ spawn of sharks—when I saw one take the fish alongside, it upset me clean. That’s the end of it!”

“Ah, Jack, Jack,” said Frank, “behold how one sin breeds another! You were a coward, and now you’ve become a Manichee—blaming an evil creator for creatures made for good ends. Sharks exist to devour useless carcasses like yours. And as a brother of the Rose, you were sworn to leap joyfully into any beast’s mouth for the sake of your vow.”

“Ay, likely,” Jack muttered, “if Mistress Rose were in its stomach. But I wanted to fight Spaniards, not be shark-bitten.”

“Jack,” Frank said, “your answer reeks of self-will. If destiny ordains you to suffer shame or torment—shark-bite, flea-bite, or worse—for the sake of the Brotherhood, you must obey. Not choose your death, as Romans do—that is mere self-murder. We therefore declare you a scandal, a rotten branch, and hereby cut you off.”

“Nay, Master Frank,” Jack protested, “how long have you been Bishop of Exeter?”

Frank smiled. “Your wit is blinded by fear, which is a short madness, raising phantoms—sharks and Spaniards alike. Let the Bishop deal with your Manicheeism. For your howling, offensive even in a cur, but disgraceful in a preacher and brother of the Rose—we hereby strip you of your office as chaplain. Unless within seven days you perform a deed worthy of the Seven Champions, or Ruggiero and Orlando, you shall lose sword and dagger. Henceforth, carry no iron but enough to mend your pen.”

“And now, Jack,” Amyas said, “let me tell you something. No wonder young men refuse to listen to the Gospel, when preached by men they must look down upon—soft-handed, unable to dig, ashamed to beg. Even if Popish priests are vowed not to be men, our English clergy must be both good Christians and good men, good gentlemen, good Englishmen. To show the white feather in need is to undo a lifetime of preaching in a single moment.”

“I tell you,” Amyas continued, “had I not thought you a different sort, I would never have entrusted you with the immortal souls of a hundred brave lads.”

Together, they bombarded him with words—starboard and larboard—until Jack clapped his hands to his ears and fled, leaving Frank laughing so hard that Amyas, though stern, was glad the incident had occurred, for it brought a rare smile to Frank’s solemn face.

The next day was Sunday. After service—Jack reluctant to read, and refusing entirely to preach—they gathered to hear the articles of their agreement, as custom dictated. Then, seeing a sloping beach with a clear stream flowing into the sea, they agreed to land, wash clothes, and refill their water casks—scarce since Barbados.

Jack insisted on going, taking sword and arquebuse. He had dreamed the night before that Spaniards attacked him, and believed it would come true. “Besides,” he said, “I don’t care if I die. Better to die than be mocked, ridiculed, badgered with Ramus and Plato, accused of being a Manichee or a Turk or Jew. I’ll go like a man desperate.”

They landed, Amyas giving strict orders not to fire weapons—fearing alarm. On shore, they washed clothes, stretched legs, and admired the beauty of the place. Then they set up their seine.

“We caught many strange fish,” says the chronicler, “and among them, a sea-cow seven feet long, covered in limpets and barnacles, like driftwood. A gentle creature, yet pious: she guards corpses until decay or burial. The Indians call her *manati*. She carries her young under her arm, suckles it like a woman, and when wounded, cries aloud with a human voice. At certain times, she sings sweetly—perhaps the melody Mr. Frank heard and mistook for Sirens and Tritons. I neither affirm nor deny this, for I have seen wonders enough to know that to reject anything for strangeness is folly. Also, one man brought in two great black birds—like capons, but larger than eagles—the Spaniards call *curassos*. With the sea-cow, they gave us excellent fare—roast and boiled. The cow was delicious, tender and rich, like a four-months-old calf.”

Afterward, they filled casks and barrels, placing the boat near the stream’s outflow. Lucky for them, they were all close together—just as well, for moments later, a cry rang out: “The enemy!”

From the woods, not twenty yards from Jack, emerged fifty soldiers, followed by negroes, led by an officer on horseback, plume in hat, sword drawn.

“Stand, for your lives!” shouted Amyas—just in time. Ten minutes were wasted getting men into order.

But Jack, seeing the Spaniards, as if expecting them, closed his Bible, marked the page, placed it down, picked up his arquebuse, and charged like a mad dog. He shot the captain through the chest, killed him instantly, then hurled the arquebuse at the next man and fell upon them with his sword, swinging like a Colbrand. He broke into their ranks, slashing right and left. The Spaniards, believing him a demon or Luther himself risen, retreated in panic, firing wildly—so badly that Jack received only a glancing wound in the thigh, which would leave him lame for weeks.

Yet he pressed forward. The rest joined him, forty strong, well armed. The Spaniards fled as fast as they came.

Cary murmured, “The dogs tasted the parson. They won’t wait for the clerk and congregation.”

“Come back, Jack! Are you mad?” Amyas shouted.

But Jack, silent all this time, pursued them fiercely—until, striking at an arquebusier, he tripped on a root, fell, and struck his head on the ground, knocking out the last of his breath. He lay still.

Amyas, seeing the Spaniards gone, did not pursue. He lifted Jack, who blinked and cried, “Glory be! Glory be! How many have I killed?”

“Nineteen, at least,” said Cary, “and seven with one backstroke.” He showed Jack the dead captain and two wounded arquebusiers—one the man who had tripped him—along with others limping away.

“There!” said Jack, catching his breath. “Will you laugh at me anymore, Mr. Cary? Say I can’t fight, because I’m a poor parson’s son?”

Cary took his hand and apologized for his mockery. “Today, you fought better than any of us.”

Jack, never holding grudges, laughed in return. “Oh, Mr. Cary, we’ve known your ways since you used to put drumble-drones in my desk at Bideford school.”

They returned to the boats, praising God for their deliverance. The crew cheered Jack, who grew faint—he had bled heavily, though unaware. He made as little of his real wound as he had exaggerated the imaginary one the day before.

That evening, Frank asked him, “How did you show such calm courage in such sudden danger?”

“Great was the crying, the running and riding, Which at that season was made in the place; The beacons were fired, as need then required, To save their great treasure they had little space.”

—Winning of Cales.

The crew would have lingered near Margarita and Cubagua, hoping for another pearl prize. But Amyas, having, as he put it, “fleshed his dogs,” was unwilling to tarry after the alarm had been raised. So they sailed south-west across the mouth of the vast bay stretching from the Peninsula of Paria to Cape Codera, leaving Tortuga to starboard and the low green islands of the Piritoos to port—mere lines of land barely above the tideless sea. Yeo and Drew knew every inch of this coast, having once attempted trade here with Hawkins, long before any Englishman had dared to tread these shores. Ahead, rising abruptly from the ocean like a wall of stone, loomed the towering Caracas mountains—so immense that even the hills most of the men had known seemed mere hillocks beside them. Frank had seen the Alps; Amyas, the Andes; but Cary’s idea of height was bounded by M’Gillicuddy’s Reeks, while Brimblecombe could not fathom such grandeur. He spent an entire day clinging to the rigging, staring upward with his chin higher than his nose, until he developed a stiff neck.

As they neared Cape Codera, the sea grew choppy despite a fair breeze. By the time they passed the cape, they felt the strong eastward current that, during winter months, often thwarted mariners seeking to sail west. All night, they battled through heavy swells, with the sheer cliff of Cape Codera towering a thousand feet above them to the left, and beyond it, layer upon layer of mountain peaks glowing under the yellow moonlight.

At dawn, they sighted a large ship that had passed them during the night, now ten miles to the east. Yeo insisted on returning to take her. It was feasible—the wind blew directly off the land, offering a "soldier's wind" for both ships. But Amyas and Frank hesitated.

“Why, Yeo,” said Amyas, “you said one more day would bring us to La Guayra.”

“Then all the more reason,” replied Yeo, “to do the Lord’s work thoroughly when He has brought us so far.”

“She can pass safely enough,” said Amyas. “No harm done.”

“Ah, sirs,” Yeo urged, “she is delivered into your hands. You will have to give account of her.”

“My good Yeo,” Frank interjected, “I trust we’ll give good account of many a Spaniard before we return. But you know well that La Guayra—and the salvation of one we believe dwells there—was our first purpose in this journey.”

Yeo shook his head sorrowfully. “Ah, sirs, a lady brought Captain Oxenham to ruin.”

“You do not dare compare her with this one?” Frank and Cary said together.

“God forbid, gentlemen,” Yeo replied. “But no venture prospers unless it is guided by a single eye to the Lord’s work—to weaken the Spaniard and exalt Her Majesty the Queen. And I thought nothing dearer to Captain Leigh’s heart than that.”

Amyas stood uncertain. His duty to the queen called him to pursue the Spanish vessel; his vow, to reach La Guayra. It may seem a paradox—but to him, it was real.

Yet Frank’s counsel prevailed. They pressed onward toward La Guayra. Amyas half hoped the Spaniards would spot them and attack. But they did not. Had he turned back, the tale might have ended very differently.

By midday, a canoe approached under a large triangular sail. Two Indians stood aboard.

“Metal floats in these seas,” quipped Cary. “A fresh marvel, Frank.”

“Expound,” said Frank, ever eager for wonders.

“How else could two bronze statues dare set out to sea in such a frail shell? Have I given you the dor now, master courtier?”

“I’m past dors, Will. But what noble creatures! And how boldly they come alongside! Could they know we’re English—avengers of the Indians?”

“I suspect they think we’re Spaniards,” Cary said, “and want to sell coconuts. See—her canoe is full of vegetables.”

“Hail them, Yeo!” Amyas ordered. “You speak Spanish best. I want speech with one.”

Yeo called out. The canoe ran alongside, lowered its sail, and a tall, graceful Indian leapt aboard like a cat.

He stood nearly six feet tall, bold and dignified as Frank or Amyas himself. At first he smiled, showing white teeth. Then his face changed. Springing to the side, he shouted in Spanish:

“Treachery! Not a Spaniard!”

He tried to leap overboard, but a dozen men seized him.

It took some effort to subdue him—his limbs were strong and slippery. Amyas urged the men not to harm him, and the Indian to calm down. After five minutes of chaos, the stranger yielded sullenly.

“Don’t bind him,” Amyas said. “Let him loose. Form a ring around him. Now, my man—here’s a dollar.”

The Indian’s eyes lit up. He took the coin.

“All I ask,” Amyas said, “is first, to know what ships are in La Guayra, and second, to go with me there and show me the governor’s house and the custom-house.”

The Indian placed the coin on deck, crossed himself, and looked Amyas straight in the eye.

“No, senor. I am a freeman, a cavalier, a Christian Guayqueria. My ancestors, the first of all Indians, swore fealty to the King of Spain. He calls us still his most faithful, loyal, noble Guayquerias. God forbid I should betray them to their enemies—my enemies too.”

A growl rose from those who understood. One muttered that a rope round the head or a match between the fingers would soon extract truth.

“God forbid!” Amyas said. “A brave and loyal man—he shall be treated as such. Tell me, noble fellow—how do you know we are the king’s enemies?”

The Indian grinned shrewdly, pointing to various objects: “Not Spanish.”

“Well?” asked Amyas.

“Only Spaniards and free Guayquerias have right to sail these waters.”

Amyas laughed. “Thou art a valiant bit of copper. Take thy dollar and go in peace. Make room for him, men. We can learn what we need without him.”

The Indian paused, stunned. “Overboard with you!” Amyas said. “Don’t you know when you’re well off?”

“Most illustrious senor,” the Indian began, in the slow, deliberate manner of his people, “I have been deceived. I heard you heretics roast and eat true Catholics—as we Guayquerias are—and that your padres have tails.”

“Plague on you, sirrah!” Jack Brimblecombe squeaked. “Have I a tail? Look here!”

“Quien sabe? Who knows?” the Indian sniffed.

“How do you know we’re heretics?” Amyas asked.

“Humph. But in repayment for your kindness, I warn you, illustrious senor—not to go to La Guayra. Warships wait there. And yesterday, Don Guzman sailed east to seek you. I wonder you did not meet him.”

“To seek us? On the watch for us?” Cary scoffed. “Impossible! Lies! Amyas, this is a trick to frighten us away.”

“Don Guzman sailed only yesterday?” Amyas asked sharply.

“As I live, senor. He and another ship—I mistook yours for that.”

Amyas stamped on deck. That must have been the ship they’d passed!

“Fool that I was,” he muttered, “to let my chance slip! If I’d done my duty, all would have gone right!”

But it was too late. Still, the Indian’s tale might be false.

“Off with you!” Amyas said. The Indian leapt back into his canoe, leaving the crew amazed at his nobility and grace.

They sailed on beneath the mighty northern wall—the highest cliff on earth, seven thousand feet high, separated from the sea by a narrow strip of bright green lowland. Sugar cane and coconut palms marked the tropics, but above, the cliffs were bare and savage, like Alpine precipices. Deep clefts allowed sunlight to pour down, revealing strange trees and perpetual mist veiling the summits. Clouds hung motionless in the heat, deepening the sense of vastness and solemn stillness.

“Within those mountains,” Drew said, “three thousand feet above us, lies Saint Yago de Leon—the city the Spaniards founded fifteen years ago.”

“Is it rich?” asked Cary.

“Very, they say.”

“Strong?” asked Amyas.

“No forts, they say. The Spaniards boast Heaven built them walls enough—no man needs to build more.”

“I don’t know,” Amyas mused. “Lads—could you climb those hills?”

“Higher than Harty Point, sir,” came the reply. “But it depends on what’s behind.”

Now the final point rounded. Before them lay La Guayra—the destination of their four-thousand-mile voyage. A black cliff crowned with a wall, batteries at each end. Inside, narrow white streets ran parallel to the sea, on a strip of land less than two hundred yards wide. Behind, the mountain wall cast deep shadow. How one ascended into the interior remained a mystery—until Drew pointed out a narrow path winding up a glen, seemingly perpendicular. That was the road to the capital—if anyone dared.

Though shaded, the town looked dusty and glaring. The air coming off the land was stifling. No wonder—La Guayra, heated by its massive rock, was one of the hottest places on earth.

Where was the harbor? There was none. Only an open roadstead, where five vessels tossed at anchor. Two small merchant caravels sat outside. Behind them, two long, low, ugly galleys. Yeo whistled.

“Galleys, by my soul! And what’s that big one inside? Robert Drew—she’s got more than hawseholes in her black sides, I think.”

“We’ll see her astern of the galleys in a moment,” Amyas said. “Look out, Cary—your eyes are better than mine.”

“Six round portholes on the main deck,” Will observed.

“And I see brass patararoes glittering on her poop,” Amyas added. “We’re in for it.”

“In for it we are, captain.”

“Farewell, farewell, my parents dear. I never shall see you more, I fear.”

“Let’s go in, nevertheless, and pound the Don’s ribs, my old lad of Smerwick. Eh? Three to one is fair odds.”

“Not underneath those fort guns,” Yeo cautioned. “If the Philistines will come out unto us, we’ll make them like Zeba and Zalmunna.”

“Quite true,” Amyas agreed. “Game cocks are game cocks—but reason’s reason.”

“If the Philistines won’t come out,” Cary said, “they’ll send a messenger instead.” He pointed. “Look out, all thin skulls!”

A puff of smoke burst from the eastern fort. A cannonball splashed into the water between fort and ship.

“I don’t altogether like this,” Amyas said. “Why fire without warning? What are these warships doing here? Drew—you said the armadas never came here.”

“They don’t, sir,” Drew replied. “The anchorage is too bad. I’m afraid that rascal told the truth—the Dons have learned of our coming.”

“Run up a white flag, at least,” Amyas said. “If they expect us, they’ve known for days. How else could they have gotten ships here?”

“True,” Drew said. “They must have come from Santa Marta—or Cartagena. That would take a month.”

Amyas suddenly remembered Eustace’s threat in the inn. Could he have betrayed them? Impossible.

“Let’s hold a council,” Amyas said to Frank.

But Frank was lost in thought. Half a mile east of the town, perched three hundred feet up the mountainside, stood a large, low, white house surrounded by trees and gardens. No other house like it existed nearby. And wasn’t that the Spanish royal flag flying before it? It must be the governor’s house. It must be the home of the Rose of Torridge! Frank stared wildly, convinced he saw a woman’s figure on the terrace—her own.

Amyas struggled to pull him away from the view. The council was grim, though not unkind.

The three adventurers—Amyas, Frank, and Cary—gathered with Brimblecombe, Yeo, and Drew on the poop. Each met the others’ eyes.

“What are we to do?” Amyas asked at last. “Months of planning—ruined in an hour.”

“It’s impossible,” he said, “to surprise the town by land while those ships are here. If we land men, our ship is defenseless.”

“As impossible as challenging Don Guzman while he’s not here,” Cary said.

“I wonder why they haven’t opened fire yet,” Drew said.

“Perhaps they respect our flag of truce,” Cary guessed.

“Or perhaps they don’t care,” Yeo said. “Trust them not. They kept no faith with Captain Hawkins at San Juan d’Ulloa. We could have taken the plate fleet—two hundred thousand pounds of gold—and didn’t. We asked only to trade. They granted license, lured us ashore, then attacked five to one—no mercy. No, sir. They’ll attack only when their crews are aboard.”

“They’ll be soon enough,” Amyas said. “I see soldiers descending the landing stairs.”

Indeed, boats filled with armed men pushed off toward the warships.

“We may thank Heaven,” Drew said, “we weren’t here two hours ago. The sun will set before they’re ready. They won’t hunt us at night.”

“So much the worse for us,” Amyas said. “If they stay, we may slip past and try again. I cannot leave without one dash.”

Yeo shook his head. “There are other towns along the coast—more worth trying. But Heaven’s will be done.”

As they spoke, the sun sank into the sea. Darkness fell.

At last, they agreed to anchor and wait till midnight. If the warships left, they would attempt to run in past them, land west of the town, take it in flank, plunder the government storehouses near the shore, fight back to their boats, and escape. Two hours would suffice if the armada cleared.

Amyas called the men forward, explained the plan. It was not welcomed cheerfully—but what else could they do?

They sailed a mile and a half west, anchored.

Night wore on. No movement among the ships. Their lights—easily identified by height—remained fixed. The men fretted, seeing a prize within reach, yet unreachable.

Let Amyas and his men have patience. In five short years, the Great Armada would come and go. Then the storm—of which they, like Oxenham, Hawkins, and Drake, were the vanguard—would break upon every Spanish port, from Corunna to Cadiz, from the Canaries to Havana. La Guayra and St. Yago de Leon would not escape.

Captain Amyas Preston and Captain Sommers, colonist of the Bermudas, would land with a tiny force—larger than Leigh’s, yet where Leigh dared not go. They would seize the fort of Guayra, find, as Leigh had, that their arrival was expected. The Pass of the Venta, three thousand feet high, would be fortified with barricades, abattis, and cannons, making the capital impregnable—except to Englishmen or Zouaves.

Up that seven-thousand-foot precipice, stair upon stair behind the town, those fierce adventurers would climb hand over hand through rain and fog, while men begged their officers to kill them—they could go no farther. Yet they would go, cutting paths through wild plantains and rhododendron thickets, over the saddle of the Silla,* down upon the astonished “Mantuanos” of St. Jago, driving all before them. They would burn the city in default of ransom, return triumphantly by the proper route, and sail along the coast—masters of the sea.

* Humboldt notes a smuggler’s path between the peaks from Caravellada to St. Jago. This may be the “unknowen way of the Indians” Preston used.

I know not if any men still live descended from those two captains. But if they do, let them remember: the history of the English navy tells no greater feat of courage and endurance than that forgotten raid of Amyas Preston and his comrade in 1595.

Yet though the town was beyond reach, another venture stirred in Frank’s heart. A light burned brightly in one window of the governor’s house—the lodestar of his soul. Sitting in the cabin with Amyas, Cary, and Jack, he opened his heart.

“And are we,” he asked mournfully, “to go without doing the very thing we came for?”

Silence. Then John Brimblecombe spoke.

“Show me the way, Mr. Frank, and I’ll go.”

“My dearest man,” Amyas said, “what would you have? Any attempt to see her—though she be here—would mean certain death.”

“And what if it were?” Frank replied. “What if it were, my brother Amyas? Listen. I have long ceased to fear Death. But until I came to these magic lands, I never knew the beauty of his face.”

“Of death?” Cary asked. “I should say of life. God forgive me—but man might wish to live forever in such a world.”

“And do you forget,” Frank said, “that the more beautiful this passing world, the more glorious the eternal one—where all here is but shadow and dream? Where the four mystic beasts stand before His throne, crying, ‘Holy, holy, holy, Lord God of hosts!’ He has made all things—for His pleasure. I have done with shadows. It is better to depart and be with Him, where there is no desire, no anger, no pretense—only eternal truth. One thing remains before I die—God has laid it on me. Let it be done tonight. Then, farewell!”

“Frank! Frank! Remember our mother!”

“I do remember her. I’ve spoken of this with her many times. She sent me forth with my virgin honor, as the Spartan mother sent her son with the shield—‘Return with this, or upon it.’ I must do one or the other—this life or the next. But hear me: my life is God’s. I promise not to cast it away rashly.”

“What would you do?”

“Go to that house, Amyas, and speak with her—if Heaven grants me the chance. And I feel sure it will.”

“And do you call that no rashness?”

“Is any duty rashness? Is it rash to stand amid bullets when your queen sends you? Or to seek Christ’s lost lamb when God and your oath send you? John Brimblecombe answered that long ago.”

“If you go,” the others said in unison, “I go with you!”

“No,” Frank said. “Amyas, you owe duty to our mother and your ship. Cary, you inherit estates—bound to country and tenants. John Brimblecombe—”

“Ay!” Jack squeaked. “And what have you to say, Mr. Frank, against my going? I have neither ship nor estate—only that I am not worthy to travel in such company?”

“Think of your parents, John—your sisters.”

“I thought of them before I started,” Jack said. “I came to keep my vow. I will not turn renegade at the foot of the cross.”

“Someone must go with you, Frank,” Amyas said. “In case—” He faltered.

“In case I fall,” Frank said, smiling. “I’ll finish your sentence. I’m not afraid. But someone must go. Unhappy me—that I cannot risk my worthless life without risking yours!”

“Not so, Mr. Frank!” Jack declared. “Your oath is ours. Your duty is ours! We three will draw lots for the honor of going with you.”

“Lots?” Amyas asked. “I don’t like leaving such matters to chance, friend John.”

“Chance, sir?” Jack replied. “When you’ve used all your wit and it fails, isn’t drawing lots simply handing the matter from your weak hands to God’s strong ones?”

“Full seven long hours in all men's sight
This fight endured sore,
Until our men so feeble grew,
That they could fight no more.
And then upon dead horses
Full savorly they fed,
And drank the puddle water,
They could no better get.

“When they had fed so freely
They kneeled on the ground,
And gave God thanks devoutly for
The favor they had found;
Then beating up their colors,
The fight they did renew;
And turning to the Spaniards,
A thousand more they slew.”

—The Brave Lord Willoughby, 1586.

When the sun burst over the horizon the next morning, drenching the tropic sky in sudden gold, Amyas Leigh stood alone on the deck of the *Rose*, his hair wild and clothes torn, eyes bloodshot from grief and fury, heart shattered by loss. How can I describe what he felt? Let those who have buried a brother imagine it. And let those who have not—thank God you do not know such agony. He paced back and forth like a man possessed, each step heavy with rage, as the ship heaved through the restless sea under close-hauled sails. His mind raced with impossible plans: return to burn the villa, storm Guayra, kill every soul in it for Frank’s sake.

“We can do it, lads!” he roared, voice raw with fire. “If Drake took Nombre de Dios, we can take La Guayra!”

Every man shouted back, “Yes!”

“We’ll have it, Amyas—and we’ll have Frank too,” cried Cary, but Amyas shook his head. A deep certainty settled within him—no port in New Spain would ever bring back that face, that smile, that brother.

“Yes,” he said, voice low but firm, “he shall be avenged.” Then he pointed toward the shore, where three sails loomed on the horizon, barely five miles ahead. “There’s the first taste of vengeance. Look—there they come.”

“The Spanish bloodhounds are after us,” he declared, “the same ships we saw yesterday near Guayra. Back, lads, and welcome them—even if there were a dozen!”

A murmur rose from the crew, sharp with anticipation. Some young hearts faltered at the thought of facing three ships at once—but silence fell quickly, crushed by the thunderous cheer of the older men and Salvation Yeo’s booming voice.

“If there were a dozen,” Yeo bellowed, “the Lord is with us! He said, 'One of you shall chase a thousand.' Clear away, lads, and see the glory of the Lord this day!”

“Amen!” shouted Cary, and the ship leaned closer into the wind.

Amyas had found strength again in the prospect of battle. The pain in his wounds faded. Grief for Frank dimmed beneath the rush of action. Within minutes, he stood straight and steady, commanding as he once had.

“Now, my masters,” he called, voice strong and clear, “let us serve God, then breakfast, and after that—clear for action.”

Jack Brimblecombe began the daily prayers, his voice trembling slightly as he recited the prayer for all conditions of men. Despite Amyas’s despair, he added, “And especially for our dear brother Mr. Francis Leigh, perhaps captive among the idolaters.”

Then they rose.

“To breakfast,” said Amyas. “A Frenchman fights best fasting, a Dutchman drunk, an Englishman full, and a Spaniard when the devil is in him—always.”

“And good beef and a just cause,” said Cary, “are a match for any devil. Come down, captain—you must eat.”

Amyas refused. He took the tiller from the steersman and sent him below to feed. Cary returned in moments with bread and beef, a great tankard of ale, and coaxed it down Amyas’s throat like a nurse feeding a child. Then he fled below, tears streaking his cheeks.

Amyas stood alone at the helm, his face aged by one night’s sorrow. A terrible calm settled over him. Woe to any man who crossed him now.

“There are three of them,” he said, as the crew climbed back on deck. “A big ship ahead, two galleys behind. We’ll let the big ship go—she’s a race ship. If we can regain the wind, we’ll see if our height can match her length. We’ll slip past her and take the galleys first.”

“I thank the Lord,” said Yeo, “who has given such wisdom to a young general—a David and Daniel, though lacking in presence. And if any dare not follow, let them be as the men of Meroz and Succoth. Amen! Silas Staveley, smite that boy over the head—he should be at the powder-room door!”

Yeo moved about the decks with purpose, gunner’s pride and faith burning in his eyes. He knew how to fight—and he meant to do it thoroughly.

All hands worked swiftly. Though the ship had been kept ready all night, there was still much to do: clearing decks, lacing nettings, repairing bulwarks, fitting waist-cloths, arming tops, greasing pikes, slinging yards, doubling sheets and tacks. Even Richard Hawkins himself would have approved.

Amyas took command of the poop, Cary the forecastle, Yeo the main deck, and Drew, the master, stationed himself in the waist. Before the great Spanish ship came within two miles, they were ready—more than ready.

Now, as the English mastiffs and Spanish bloodhounds drew nearer across the churning waves, hungry for blood, let us pause a moment to understand what made these English sailors capable of defying fleets far greater in number and size, and why they could boast that in the entire Spanish war, only one queen’s ship—the *Revenge*—and one private vessel—Sir Richard Hawkins’s *Dainty*—had ever struck their colors.

What enabled Sir Richard Grenville’s *Revenge*, in her final battle off the Azores, to endure twelve hours of attack from eight Spanish armadas—two of which sank beside her—her masts gone, boarded three times without success, yet still defy a fleet of fifty-four ships, “like dogs around the dying forest king”?

What allowed young Richard Hawkins’s *Dainty*, half her guns useless due to the gunner’s negligence or betrayal, to fight for three days against two equal-sized Spanish ships, double her metal, tenfold her men?

How did Sir George Cary’s *Content* fight alone from dawn to dusk against four great galleons and two galleys, her heaviest gun a mere nine-pounder, and for hours with only thirteen men fit to fight?

Why could two London merchantmen—the *Merchant Royal* and the *Tobie*—with three small consorts, cripple an entire fleet of Spanish galleys off Pantellaria and sail triumphantly through the Straits of Gibraltar?

And finally, what allowed the English fleet in 1588 to shatter the Great Armada, capturing, destroying, or scattering every ship, losing only one pinnace and one nobleman?

Many reasons. First, the design of English ships. They were shorter than Spanish vessels, giving them unmatched agility. As Camden wrote of the 1588 battle: “The English ships charged the enemy with marvellous agility, discharged their broadsides, and flew into the deep, leveling their shot directly—without missing—at the heavy, unwieldy Spanish ships.”

Spanish ships in the West Indies were often flush-decked, or “race” ships, built to carry cargo, leaving men exposed. English ships, by contrast, had raised sterns and bows, with stockades and “close fights” on poop and forecastle, offering cover. Below, bulkheads divided the ship into fortified sections armed with swivels and loopholes for muskets and arrows.

But the true edge lay in the men.

The English sailor was amphibious, skilled in everything—from carpentry to gunnery, from needlework to hand-to-hand combat. Every Englishman, from birth, was trained in archery and swordplay, not as privilege, but as law. They fought not for a king alone, but for themselves—for freedom. Each man carried the spirit of a Yankee ranger: self-reliant, proud, trusted to act.

English officers lived side by side with their men—not above them. They shared the same training, the same risks. They consulted, cared, led—not by rank, but by merit. Drake once rebuked gentleman-adventurers: “I’d like to see the gentleman who refuses to set his hand to a rope. I need gentlemen to haul and draw with the mariners.”

Under Elizabeth, bravery and cunning mattered more than birth or age. Knighthood honored courage, not lineage. Merchant captains—many noble-born—were forged in trade, where Spanish traders often silenced rivals with cannon fire.

Thus arose mutual trust. Mutinies were rare in English ships, while in Spanish vessels they broke out constantly. Why? Because Spanish naval life was rigid, hierarchical, cruel. Soldiers watched like guards ashore, gunners were foreigners, mariners were slaves—forced to labor day and night, denied shelter, forbidden to rest beneath the decks.

As one prisoner described: “They divide their crews into soldiers, mariners, and gunners. Soldiers watch like on land. Gunners are foreigners, unskilled. Mariners are slaves—few, poor, never allowed to sleep under deck. In storm or sun, rain or calm, they toil uncovered.”

Now, the great Spanish ship was within two musket shots, her golden flag flying proudly at her stern. Trumpets blared defiance from a dozen brass throats. From the *Rose*, two answered lustily. Her poop bore England’s banner, her foremast the arms of Leigh and Cary side by side, above them the ship and bridge of Bideford.

Then Amyas called:

“Silence, trumpets! Waits, play up! ‘Fortune my foe!’ God and the Queen be with us!”

And so, amid music—cornet, sackbut, fife, drum—rose the old favorite of Queen Bess. Parson Jack, standing with the musicians on the poop, played furiously on his violin, like Volker in the *Nibelungen Lied*.

“Well played, Jack,” said Amyas, forcing a laugh. “Thy elbow flies like a lamb’s tail.”

“It shall fly to a better fiddle-bow soon, sir—if I live.”

“Steady, helm!” Amyas commanded. “What’s he doing now?”

The Spaniard, sailing downwind under full sail, suddenly reefed his light canvas.

“He doesn’t know what to make of us waiting so boldly,” said the helmsman.

“He does,” another shouted. “See—pulling up the foot of his mainsail. He wants to keep the wind of us.”

“Let him try,” said Amyas. “Keep her closer. No fire until we’re near. Man the starboard guns. To starboard—wait, all small arms. Pass the order to the gunner—fire high, aim for rigging.”

Bang! A Spanish bow gun fired—missed. Another. Another. Men checked their musket priming, loosened arrows in sheaths.

“Lie down, men. Sing a psalm. When I call, I’ll need you. Closer still, helmsman—we’ll try a short ship against a long one. We can sail two points nearer the wind than he.”

As Amyas predicted, the Spaniard wanted to cross the *Rose*’s bow—but feared being raked. So he turned sharply into the wind, waiting on the same tack.

Amyas smiled to himself. “Hold on a bit longer. More ways to kill a cat than choking her with cream. Drew, are your men ready?”

“Ay, ay, sir!” On they went, closing fast.

“Ready about!” And the *Rose* spun like an eel, crossing the Spaniard’s stern.

The Spaniard hesitated—then tried to turn, but too late. His massive hull hung in the wind’s eye as the *Rose*’s bowsprit nearly scraped his quarter. She passed slowly, ten yards off.

“Now!” roared Amyas. “Fire! With a will! Archers—have at them! Muskets—fire!”

In an instant, a storm of chain-shot, round shot, canister swept the Spaniard from stem to stern. Muskets and cloth-yard arrows whistled through the smoke. The steersman fell. The poop crew dropped. The mizzen topmast crashed. Stern windows and quarter galleries shattered. The painted image of the Madre Dolorosa—her heart pierced by seven swords—splintered into ruin. And the golden flag, fluttering just seconds before, now trailed in the water.

The ship, tiller shot away, helmsman dead, staggered—then fell up into the wind.
