“Well done, men of Devon!” Amyas shouted, as cheers tore the sky.

“She’s struck!” someone cried.

“No,” said Amyas. “Not yet. Hold on, helmsman. Let her mend her tackle while we finish the galleys.”

On they sailed, swift and merry. Long before the Spaniard could recover, they were two miles ahead—while two galleys surged forward, oars cutting the choppy sea.

They were fearsome vessels—long, low, snout-like, driven by forty oars apiece. Their forecastles bristled with soldiers, cannon muzzles peering from ports forward and sideways. The waist was packed with slaves—five or six per oar—chained to their benches. Down the center, slave-drivers walked, whip in hand. At the stern, armored soldiers stood guard, sunlight flashing on steel and gun barrels.

As they neared, the English heard the cracks of whips, the yells of slaves, the rhythmic “Ha!” of each stroke, the curses of drivers. A foul musky stench—like kennelled hounds—drifted on the breeze. Many a young heart trembled. These were floating hells—cruelties whispered in English taverns, seen by captives, endured by men.

“Must we fire on the slaves?” asked one.

Amyas sighed.

“Spare them if you can. But if they run us down, we must rake them—and God forgive us.”

The two galleys approached abreast, forty yards apart. Outmaneuvering oars was impossible. Running was suicide—trapped between them and the ship.

Amyas decided on the desperate move.

“Lay her head up in the wind, helmsman. We’ll wait for them.”

Within musket range, they opened fire—but their aim was wild in the rolling sea. Amyas held his fire.

Men stood tense, lips tight. Amyas stood motionless on the quarterdeck, issuing orders calmly. The crew saw his confidence—and trusted him.

The Spaniards laughed. Was the Englishman mad? The galleys converged, aiming to strike both bows.

Forty yards. One minute. Impact imminent.

Then—Amyas’s helm turned. Yards creaked. The *Rose* gathered speed and plunged onto the larboard galley.

“A dozen gold nobles to whoever brings down the steersman!” shouted Cary.

Arrows flew from the forecastle—rattling on the galley’s deck.

Whether hit or not, the steersman panicked. His helm swung left. The galley’s beak slid harmlessly along the *Rose*’s bow—then came a long grind, followed by loud crack after crack, as the *Rose* sliced through the bank of oars, hurling slaves into heaps.

Before the other galley could swing around, Amyas unleashed his entire broadside—great and small guns—into the crippled vessel. A scream rent the air.

“Spare the slaves! Fire at the soldiers!” he cried—but the chaos made discrimination impossible.

The larboard galley, wounded but defiant, swung across the *Rose*’s stern and hooked on—desperate, reckless.

It was brave—but foolish. It blocked the second galley’s approach, exposing her to another broadside.

A boarding attempt through the stern-ports failed under a storm of shot and steel. Within minutes, Amyas and twenty men stood on the galley’s poop.

Five minutes of brutal fighting—steel against steel—and the poop was clear. The forecastle soldiers were helpless, exposed to arrows and musketry from the *Rose*’s high stern.

Amyas charged down the central gangway, shouting in Spanish: “Freedom to the slaves! Death to the masters!” He stormed into the forecastle, followed by his men, and showed them how to fight.

Three minutes later, not a Spaniard remained alive or breathing.

“Free the slaves!” he shouted. “Throw us a hammer! Hark—that’s an English voice!”

Indeed it was. From the wreckage, a voice screamed in thick Devon dialect:

“Oh, Robert Drew! Robert Drew! Come down—take me out of hell!”

“Who be you, in God’s name?”

“Don’t you remember William Prust—the man Captain Hawkins left behind in Honduras years ago? Nine of us here—if your shot hasn’t killed us. Come down, if you’ve a Christian heart!”

Forgetting discipline, Drew leapt down with hammer in hand. The two men embraced—old comrades reunited, at last free.

“Follow thee? Follow thee? Who would not follow thee? Long have you led us, and trusted us fairly.”

Amyas might well have fallen to the yellow fever had it not been for one thing—his own unyielding duty. He could not afford to be sick while his men were dying. It was a truth many noble souls in the Crimea would later know: so long as the work is urgent, the body can endure; but once the task ends, the spirit may falter, and the strength collapse. And Amyas, though he felt the sickness creeping through his limbs, refused to yield.

The next morning, he summoned a council—not of war, but of survival. The crew was near mutiny, gripped by fear and fever. Amyas addressed them plainly: “What good will come of killing me—or being killed by me?” Then he descended below deck to consult.

The doctor offered only theory—humors, complexions, animal spirits. Jack Brimblecombe spoke of divine judgment. Cary smiled, but there was no joy in it—only despair masked in wit. Yeo stood stoic, quoting Scripture as if fate were already written. Drew, the master, said nothing. “My job,” he muttered, “is to sail the ship, not cure fevers.”

At that, Amyas clutched his hair—a familiar gesture when frustration seized him—and burst out: “Doctor! A fig for your humors! Can you change a man’s skin? Can an Ethiopian change his hue? Don’t shift your ignorance onto God. I ask what causes this sickness, and you don’t know. Jack, don’t preach God’s visitation—this looks more like the devil’s handiwork to me. We are doing God’s work, Sir John. He won’t hinder us. So down with the devil, I say. Cary, laughter won’t cure a Christian. Yeo, I’ll call it God’s will only when an angel tells me we’re meant to die like dogs in a ditch. And Drew—you say your business is sailing? Then sail her out of this poison-trap this very morning, if you can. But you can’t. The sickness is in the air. I felt it last night, smelled it like rotting sewage. If it weren’t in the air, why were my boatmen struck first?”

No one answered.

Then Amyas explained: “Because the mist rose only five or six feet above the river. We were in it. You were above it. And those taken aboard today? All slept on the main deck. All feared the fever. That tells me two things: stay high, and fear only God. Then we shall survive.”

“But the fog reached our round-tops at dawn,” said Cary.

“I know. But we on the half-deck were exposed less than those below. And we had better air. Besides, I suspect evening heat draws the poison out. When it cools at night, the venom fades.”

How it faded, Amyas could not explain—though he was right in his observations. No one then understood the true nature of such fevers. It took nearly two hundred years of suffering in America before settlers learned the simple rules now taught to children. But common sense was on Amyas’s side. Yeo rose and spoke:

“As I’ve said before, the Lord has sent us a young Daniel to judge. I remember the Spaniards saying these calentures lie only in low ground—never rise more than a few hundred feet above sea.”

“Then let us go up those hundred feet.”

All eyes turned to Amyas, then to each other.

“Gentlemen,” he said, “look the devil straight in the face if you want to strike him where it hurts. We cannot sail the ship as she is. We cannot return empty-handed. And we cannot stay here to die. We must leave the ship and go inland.”

“Inland?” echoed every voice except Yeo’s.

“To those hundred feet Yeo speaks of. Up into the mountains. Stockade a camp. Bring our sick and supplies.”

“And then?”

“And when we are strong again, cross the mountains and take St. Jago de Leon by surprise.”

Cary swore aloud. “Amyas! You’re a reckless man!”

“No,” said Amyas. “It’s plain prudence.”

“So it is, sir,” said old Yeo. “And I follow you.”

“And so do I,” squeaked Jack Brimblecombe.

“Then, Jack, you shall not outrun me,” said Cary. “I say yes too.”

“Mr. Drew?”

“At your service, sir, to live or die. I know nothing of stockades—but I believe Sir Francis himself would have given the same counsel, had he been in your place.”

“Then tell the men we move in an hour. Win over the Pelicans, Yeo and Drew. The rest will follow—like sheep over a hedge.”

The Pelicans and freed galley-slaves joined at once. The others resisted fiercely. Where were the hills? In the thick mangrove swamp, they could see no farther than fifty yards.

“The hills are three miles southwest,” said Amyas. “I marked every ridge as we came in.”

“You meant to take us there?”

That sparked suspicion—angry, whispered doubts. But Amyas cut through it with cold authority.

“Fools! If I hadn’t thought ahead of you, where would you be? Are you mad as well as reckless—rising against your captain because he has two strings to his bow? Go my way—or I swear I’ll blow up the ship and every soul on board. Save you the pain of rotting here slowly.”

They knew Amyas never threatened lightly—even if he didn’t truly intend it. The threat was enough. They agreed. And their spirits lifted when they saw the old Pelican’s crew working with cheerful resolve.

There’s no need to linger on the agony of the march—hours under a broiling sun, stumbling over roots, cutting through thorns, dragging the sick and provisions up steep slopes, plagued by snakes, mosquitoes, false alarms, curses, fatigue, and despair. Suffice it to say: by sunset, they reached a level plateau a thousand feet above the sea. Behind them loomed an impassable cliff—the shoulder of a great mountain. Below, steep wooded slopes formed a natural defense. With just a few trees felled, it could become impregnable.

Amyas set the sick beneath the arching roots of a colossal cottonwood tree. He made a second trip to the ship for hammocks and blankets. Yeo proved invaluable—pioneer, guide, heart. He found the narrow brook they followed upward, urged them past cliffs, promised water and shelter ahead. He encouraged them when they begged to stop, returned again and again to push them forward. Cary brought up the rear, driving stragglers with threats and song, carrying burdens, singing to keep morale high. Both men proved worthy of Amyas’s trust. That night, overwhelmed with relief, he whispered to them both:

“Cortez burned his ships when he landed. Why shouldn’t we?”

Yeo leapt upright, then sat back, whispering: “You say that, captain? It’s from above—certainly. It’s been on my mind all day.”

“There’s no hurry,” said Amyas. “We must clear her out first.” Cary sat silent, deep in thought. Amyas clearly had plans beyond words.

That night, exhaustion claimed the men. But by dawn, Amyas had them hard at work fortifying the site. Nature had already done much: cliffs guarded three sides. The mountain range behind barred any attack from the north. Trees hid them from view. Any assault would come from below—and Amyas prepared for it.

He felled small trees, laid them bough-out over the slope—forming an abatis, a barrier deadly to horse or man. Logs were sawn, laid lengthwise, braced with stakes and earth. Within hours, a sturdy stockade stood—strong against anything but cannon.

Amyas climbed into the branches of the giant ceiba tree, perched high above the world. From there, he surveyed the forest, the sea, and the future. To stay was impossible. To seek vengeance on La Guayra was out of reach. To search for Frank seemed equally hopeless. Yet—could he sacrifice eighty men for his personal quest?

He wept with rage. Then wept again, in prayer. At last, he decided. A hundred chances to one, Frank was dead. Even if alive, he was likely in the hands of the Inquisition. Who could free him? Not Amyas. Crying out, “God help him—for I cannot!” he resolved to move. But where?

Hours passed in silence, high in his leafy nest. Then he descended, calm and determined, pulling Cary and Yeo aside.

“We cannot refit the ship without falling to fever. Even if we did, we’d likely fight the Spaniards again. The Indian’s tale is true—they were warned. And Eustace was the traitor.”

“Then let us try St. Jago,” said Amyas. “Sack it. Strike La Guayra from the rear. Take a ship. Return home.”

“Nay, Will,” said Yeo. “If they strengthened La Guayra, they surely did the same at St. Jago. The town is large, new, and how can we cross these mountains without a guide?”

“Or with one?” asked Cary, gazing at the endless ridges. “Strange to see you, at least, dampening a bold plan.”

“What if I had a bolder one?” said Amyas. “Have you ever heard of the golden city of Manoa?”

Yeo laughed—a grim, joyful sound. “I have, sir. And so have the old hands from the Pelican and the Jesus of Lubec.”

“Then listen,” said Amyas, and told Cary everything he’d learned from the Spaniard. Yeo added rumors of his own. Cary sat stunned, as visions of gold and empires unfolded.

“So that’s why you wanted to burn the ship,” he said. “Well, nobody needs me at home. One less at table won’t be missed. You want to play Cortez, eh?”

“We shall never need to play Cortez—he wasn’t such a bad fellow after all. And I trust we’ll fear God enough not to imitate Pizarro.”

They said no more—but none forgot the talk.

For ten days, they lived in that mountain hideaway. Some sick men died—some from fever compounded by wounds, others perhaps from bloodletting. Most recovered, thanks to herbs Yeo administered—much to the doctor’s disgust. He insisted on bleeding, and nearly mutinied when Amyas stopped him.

Meanwhile, daily trips to the ship kept provisions flowing. Raccoons, monkeys, fruit, vegetables—especially the sweet mountain cabbage of the Areca palm and the fresh milk of the cow-tree—sustained them. They paid well for the hospitality of the land.

All day, watchers kept vigil in the ceiba tree. What a tree it was! Its roots rose twelve feet high, forming walls that housed the entire crew, arms, and stores. The trunk, forty feet in girth, soared like a lighthouse, smooth for a hundred feet, then branched into stately limbs—each a tree in itself. Its topmost twigs touched two hundred and fifty feet above the ground. Lianas hung like ropes, making ascent easy.

Once inside, one entered another world—between heaven and earth. Flowers dangled from every limb—cactuses, orchids, wild pines. One side was lush with leaves; the other, bare, bloomed with brilliant yellow flowers, buzzing with hummingbirds. Parrots peered from crevices. Lizards glimmered like gems. Finches chirped, butterflies danced, insects hummed. At dusk, tree-toads croaked till dawn.

More life swirled around that single tree than in a square mile of England.

Amyas, lounging among the branches, sometimes dreamed of staying forever—then snapped back to reality. The enemy would come soon. He must decide.

One noon, he spotted a large ship along the coast—under easy sail. He recognized it, or thought he did—the vessel they’d passed earlier.

If it was the same ship, it had slipped past them to La Guayra and now returned to search.

She moved slowly. He hoped she’d pass the river mouth—but she didn’t. She lay-to near shore. Then two boats rowed in, vanishing into the mangroves.

Sliding down a liana, Amyas reported what he’d seen. The men, tired of waiting, roared with joy and began preparing.

Four brass swivels were mounted, fixed in logs to cover the path. Musketeers and archers gathered, ready. Six marksmen volunteered for the cottonwood tree—with arquebuses. “A man might have very pretty shooting,” one joked.

Prayers followed. Then dinner. But two hours passed—no sign of the Spaniards.

Then smoke curled from the swamp. A caliver fired. Then the Spanish flag rose above the trees—horrible to see—flying from the masthead of the Rose.

They were signaling for reinforcements. A third boat pushed off into the forest.

Another hour passed. The men grew restless, shouting, pacing. Amyas warned them: “Silence! They may not find us. They may pass unseen. If they miss the track, they’ll return to their ship. Promise me—no noise until I give the word to fire.”

His orders had barely left his lips when a Spanish soldier’s helmet flashed in the path—then another, another.

“Fools!” whispered Amyas to Cary. “They’re coming up single file—running into their own death. Lie low, men!”

The path was narrow—two could hardly walk abreast. The slope was steep. The Spaniards hesitated, unsure. Then, from behind, a commanding voice urged them forward.

At last, a figure emerged—sword drawn—recognized instantly by Amyas and Cary.

“Is it he?”

“Those legs—I’d know them anywhere, even in armor.”

“It’s my turn now, Cary. Remember! Silence, men!”

The Spaniards seemed aware they were marching into danger. Don Guzman—there was no doubt—struggled to lead them.

“They’ve heard how gently we treated the Guayra squadron,” whispered Cary. “They don’t want to be martyrs like the captain of the Madre Dolorosa.”

At last, they reached within forty yards of the stockade—and paused.

Amyas leapt atop the wall, waving a white flag. His heart pounded at the sight of the hated man—but he forced the words out:

“Don Guzman, the quarrel is between you and me. Not your men and mine. I would have challenged you at La Guayra—but you were gone. I challenge you now—to single combat.”

“Lutheran dog! I have a halter for you, not a sword! As you served us at Smerwick, we shall serve you now. Pirate and ravisher—you and yours shall share Oxenham’s fate. Learn what it means to tread unbidden on the king of Spain’s lands.”

“The devil take you and your king!” shouted Amyas, laughing. “This land belongs to no one but Queen Elizabeth. In her name, I claim it—just as you claim Caracas. Fire, men! God defend the right!”

Both sides obeyed. Amyas dropped behind the stockade as a bullet whistled overhead. The Spaniards recoiled as muskets and swivels blazed—raking their line from front to rear.

Front ranks fell in heaps. The rear turned and fled—only to be cut down by bullets and arrows tumbling them down the slope.

“Out, men! Charge! See—the Don is running like the rest!”

Amyas scrambled over the abatis, leading thirty. He hoped to capture a prisoner—perhaps learn Frank’s fate.

But Don Guzman, miraculously, was only wounded. Seeing his men flee, he tried to rally them—but was swept away.

By the time the English reached the path, the Spaniards were lost in thick bushes. Amyas, fearing an ambush, withdrew reluctantly. He found fourteen Spaniards—dead. One wounded man, defiant, had fired as he lay. The English, enraged by their plight and stories of horror from freed slaves, had killed them all before Amyas could stop them.

“Are you mad?” he roared, striking one man’s sword. “Will you kill an Indian?”

And from the bushes, he dragged out a sixteen-year-old Indian boy—slightly wounded, crawling like a snake.

“The black vermin shot me. Poisoned, most likely.”

“God grant not,” said Amyas. “But an Indian is worth his weight in gold now.” He tucked the lad under his arm.

The boy, seeing escape was impossible, accepted his fate with quiet dignity. He was brought in, treated kindly—but refused food.

After questioning, they learned the reason: he believed the English would fatten him and eat him—like the Caribs. Only when they buried the Spaniard corpses did he believe they wouldn’t cook them for supper.

And so, under the great tree, they waited—watchful, weary, yet alive.

Three weeks have passed since the storm of battle and bloodshed on the coast of New Granada. The world has shifted, not in geography alone, but in spirit—darkened by the weight of silence, the cold breath of authority, and the shadow of a faith that brooks no dissent. We now find ourselves in Cartagena, within the grim confines of a long, low corridor lined with cells, each one a tomb waiting to be sealed. One door stands ajar. Inside, two figures stand in the dim glow of a single lamp: one cloaked in black, the other draped in the somber robes of the Holy Office.

The man in black is Eustace Leigh. The other, an agent of the Inquisition, holds the lamp aloft, its flickering light casting long shadows across the straw-strewn bed where a young man lies asleep. His face is pale, his brow high and noble—features we know well. It is Frank, saved from death at the hands of savage men only to be delivered into the hands of those who claim to speak for God.

He was tortured today. The question had been administered; the body broken, though the soul remained unshaken. Now, Eustace has come—not as a friend, nor yet as a foe—but as a servant of duty, compelled by a cause he cannot deny. He must wake Frank and urge him to recant. Perhaps coax him. Perhaps threaten. The Inquisitors call it mercy—"while his heart is still tender from the torture." But Eustace knows better. He knows the cost of such words.

And yet—how calmly the youth sleeps! Is it merely the play of light upon his lips? Or does a faint smile linger there, as if in dreams he sees something beyond this prison?

Eustace leans closer, the lamp trembling in his hand. And then—he hears it. A whisper, soft and clear: “Mother… Mary…” The name rises like a prayer, pure and true. Eustace freezes. He cannot bring himself to wake him. His voice, barely audible, breaks the silence: “Let him rest. I fear my words will do no good.”

The Inquisitor nods. “I fear so too, sir. Never have I seen a more stubborn heretic. He mocked them openly, even in their own court.”

“Ah,” murmurs Eustace, “great indeed is the depravity of the human heart—and the power of Satan.” He turns away. “Let us go—for now.”

They walk down the corridor, passing cell after cell, until the Inquisitor pauses. “Where is she?”

“The elder sorceress—or the younger?”

“The younger—the Senora de Soto?”

“A poor creature,” says the Inquisitor, watching Eustace closely. “One might pity her, were she not a heretic.”

Eustace shudders. “She is with the notary?” he asks, barely able to breathe.

“Yes,” replies the man, pointing to a door ahead. “To the benefit of her soul, I trust.”

Eustace stops short. His knees tremble. His heart pounds against his ribs. A terrible curiosity overcomes him—though he knows full well that any sign of sympathy could be used against him. Still, he presses close to the door, leaning against the wall, hiding his face from his companion.

Inside, voices rise and fall. The notary speaks in measured tones, rehearsing the old charge—witchcraft. A familiar tactic, used not only to justify cruelty but to appease the mob with the illusion of justice.

Then—a woman’s voice, sharp with pain and fury:
“Witchcraft against Don Guzman? What need of that, oh God! What need?”

A pause. Then the notary again: “You deny it, senora? We are sorry for you—but—”

And then—silence, broken only by choking sobs, by muffled cries that might mean nothing or everything.

“She has confessed!” whispers Eustace, half-relieved, half-destroyed. “Saints, I thank you—she—”

But no. A wail—sharp, piercing, echoing through the stones like a cry from hell itself. It tears through Eustace’s mind. He would break the door open, rush in—but his companion drags him back. Another wail. And another. The sound follows him like vengeful spirits, chasing him through the vaulted halls.

He stumbles out into the night air—warm, fragrant, drenched in moonlight. Before him stretches a garden so lush, so perfect, it could be Eden reborn. Yet no peace comes. The wails remain. They ring in his ears, in his bones, in his very soul.

“Oh, misery, misery, misery!” he groans, teeth clenched, fists clenched, body shaking. “I brought her here. I had to. Who else could I have sent? Who dares blame me? And yet—what sin have I committed that demands such punishment? Was there no one else? No one?”

Yet still, a faint hope clings: *It may save her soul. It may bring her to repentance.*

“Indeed it may,” replies the Inquisitor, calm and cold. “She is delicate. She cannot endure much. You should know that, senor. The Holy Office is merciful—when mercy serves the cause.”

“I know it,” Eustace gasps, trembling now—not just for her, but for himself. “All in love… all in love… a paternal chastisement…”

“And the proofs are clear,” continues the Inquisitor. “Heresy, yes. Suspicion of enchantment. And the elder sorceress—well known. You yourself told us she was a notorious witch in England before the senora brought her here.”

“Of course,” Eustace says, voice breaking. “Of course. There was no other way. Though the flesh may be weak… the spirit—” He trails off, unable to finish.

That night, Eustace goes to the Jesuit superior. He pours out his soul—what he can bear to confess. He begs to complete his novitiate, to enter the order, on one condition: that he be sent far away—back to Europe, anywhere—*immediately*. “Otherwise,” he says, “I shall go mad. I am already mad.”

The Jesuit, kind-hearted, takes pity. He speaks with the Inquisitors, presents Eustace’s past service as proof of loyalty, clears his name. His testimony is no longer needed. That same night, Eustace leaves Cartagena. The next week, he sails—no one knows where.

I know not whither. Eustace Leigh vanishes from these pages. He may have risen to lead his order. He may have spent his life in some distant jungle, preaching salvation to Indians—conquering their souls, perhaps their bodies too. He may have returned to England and become the very Ballard hanged and quartered three years later for his part in Babington’s conspiracy.

I know not.

This book is not about legends or saints. It is about men—men caught between duty and conscience, virtue and sin, courage and cowardice. Eustace was once such a man. But now—he is no longer. He has become a thing. A tool. A Jesuit. Sent where he is told, doing what he is bid—good or evil, without choice, without guilt, without soul.

For he has committed moral suicide. He has killed his conscience, believing it would save his soul. He has surrendered his will, his responsibility, his humanity—all to serve “The Society.”

In truth, Eustace is dead. Twice dead, perhaps. Let the dead bury their dead.

We have no further concern with Eustace Leigh.

“My mariners, Souls that have toil'd, and wrought, and thought with me—Death closes all: but something ere the end,
Some work of noble note, may yet be done,
Not unbecoming men that strove with gods!”

—TENNYSON'S *Ulysses*.

Three years had passed since that small band knelt beneath the great tree of Guayra at evensong—years that seemed blank, marked only by scattered names and misspelled records. They had wandered through untouched hills and dense forests across a region eight hundred miles long and four hundred wide, searching for the Golden City—Manoa—and found nothing. They had followed the banks of the Orinoco, crossed the thunderous foam of Maypures, explored the upper reaches of the mighty Amazon, ventured into Peru itself, and walked among the cinchona groves of Loxa, unaware of their healing powers. They had gazed upon the virgin snows of Chimborazo rising above storm clouds, and watched Cotopaxi darken in fury as rivers of fire poured down its slopes. Defeated by the Andes, they turned east again, plunging from alpine cliffs into “the green and misty ocean of the Montana.” Slowly, painfully, they had made their way north along the eastern foot of the inland Cordillera, and now they rested—bivouacked—on one of the many tributaries of the Meta, flowing from the Suma Paz into the forested plains. There they sat, their watchfires flickering on the water, beneath towering trees, Amyas, Cary, Brimblecombe, Yeo, and the loyal Indian boy who had stayed with them through every trial—alive, yes, but no closer to Manoa than before. Its fairy lake, golden palaces, and wonders remained just out of reach. Again and again, rumors had stirred them—faint whispers of the city’s existence—only to lead to fresh disappointment, and hope deferred, which, as Scripture says, sickens the heart.

There they sat—forty-four men left from the eighty-four who had begun their journey under the tree of Guayra. Where were the others?

“Their bones are scatter'd far and wide,
By mount, by stream, and sea.”

Drew, the master, lies on the banks of the Rio Negro, five brave companions beside him, slain by poisoned arrows in a failed attempt to cross the mountain passes of Parima. Two more perished in the Andean valleys, frozen by icy hail falling from the condor’s nest. Four drowned in the rapids of the Orinoco. Five or six wounded men were left behind among friendly Indians, perhaps never to be recovered. Fever, snakes, jaguars, alligators, cannibal fish, electric eels—each month took its toll. No trace remains of their march through the primeval wilds except those lonely graves.

Yet still they sit, beside the quiet river, beneath the tropical moon—sun-baked and lean, but strong and bold as ever. The quiet flame of English courage burns in every eye; the warm smile of English humor lingers on every lip. Danger is mocked, labor turned to sport, as cheerfully as when they first sailed past the bar of Bideford, in days that feel like a life before time. Their beards hang down to their chests; their long hair is tied back like women’s, to shield against the sun. Their leggings are made from the soft hide of the Guazu-puti deer; their shirts patched with cotton from Indian looms. Jaguar pelts, puma skins, and monkey hides drape their shoulders. Their powder is gone, their muskets ruined by the constant steam of the jungle, abandoned in a cave near an Orinoco cataract. But their swords gleam bright and deadly as ever. They carry bows stronger than any Indian could draw, arrows tipped with fragments of armor. Many wield the pocuna—the Indian blowgun—more silent and deadly than the firearms they’ve left behind. So they have wandered, and so they will wander—lords of the forest and its beasts, feared by hostile tribes, yet kind and just to those who deal fairly. Many a Carib, Ature, Solimo, and Guahiba tell with awe of the bearded strangers who called themselves enemies of the treacherous Spaniards and spoke of a great queen across the seas who would send her warriors to free and avenge the oppressed.

The men sleep among the trees—some on the ground, some in grass hammocks strung between trunks. All is still, save the heavy splash of a tapir feeding in the river, tearing up waterweed for his night’s meal. Occasionally, a jaguar climbs from branch to branch after prey, waking monkeys, who in turn rouse the birds. For ten minutes, the forest erupts in unearthly roars, howls, shrieks, and cackles—like hell itself breaking loose—but it fades quickly. Even during the chaos, the sleepers do not stir, nor does it interrupt the council burning beside the watchfire, where Amyas, Cary, Brimblecombe, and Yeo debate their next move. A hundred such councils have come and gone—always without result. This one may be no different. Yet it feels heavier than most. The two-year agreement to search for Manoa has ended. If they stay here much longer, they’ll spend their lives in this green wilderness.

“Well,” said Will Cary, drawing on his cigar, “at least we’ve learned something from those last Indians. It’s a comfort to smoke again after three weeks without tobacco.”

“For me,” Jack Brimblecombe replied, “Heaven forgive me, but when I get that magical leaf between my teeth, I’m tempted to sit still as a chimney and smoke till I die—no movement, no effort.”

“Then I’ll ban tobacco, Master Parson,” Amyas said. “We must rise and go tomorrow. We’ve wasted three full days here—nothing achieved.”

“Will we ever achieve anything?” Brimblecombe asked. “It seems Manoa’s gold is like the rainbow’s touch—always just beyond reach.”

Amyas was silent. So were the others. There was no denying it: their hopes were fading. Across the vast circuit they’d traveled, only disappointment waited.

“There’s one chance left,” Amyas said finally. “The mountains east of the Orinoco—where we failed first. Perhaps the Incas moved there when they fled.”

“Why not?” said Cary. “They’d have the forests, the Llanos, half a dozen great rivers—all between them and those dogs of Spaniards.”

“Shall we try again?” Amyas asked. “This river feeds into the Orinoco. Once there, we’re at the base of the mountains. What say you, Yeo?”

“I cannot forget,” Yeo said, “how the Indians warned us about those mountains—how vast they are, how hard to cross, with cliffs high above and thick forests below. And we lost five good men there already.”

“What matters?” Amyas replied. “No forest can be thicker than the ones we’ve already cut through. If I’d had a monkey’s tail, I could’ve walked a hundred miles along the treetops—far easier than crawling through vines and being eaten alive by creatures from dawn to dusk.”

“But remember,” Jack added, “they warned us about the Amazons.”

“Jack, afraid of women?”

“Why not?” Jack answered. “I wouldn’t run from a man—I know that. But a woman? It’s unnatural. They must be witches or devils. The Caribs feared them. They said there were men without necks, eyes in their chests. How can a Christian fight such beings?”

“He couldn’t chop off their heads, true,” Amyas said, “but a thrust in the ribs should do as much as for any other.”

“Well,” Jack said, “if I fight, let it be honest flesh and blood. Not these monstrous creatures. How do we know they’re not invulnerable by magic?”

“How do you know they are?” Cary countered. “And as for the Amazons—woman is woman everywhere. I’d wager you could charm them with a few compliments, just like burghers’ wives. Pity I don’t have a court suit and a Spanish hat. I’d go alone as ambassador, orange in one hand, handkerchief in the other, and within a week be as close to Queen Blackfacealinda as Raleigh is to the Queen at Whitehall.”

“Gentlemen,” Yeo said, “where you go, I go—and every man of us, I believe. But we’ve lost half our company. Our ammunition is gone. Without guns, we’re no better than the naked savages around us. And yet—do you recall how Cortez and Pizarro conquered? Not by strength alone, but by the terror of their cannons and horses—beasts I have no faith in. Had we brought such weapons, might we not have stunned the people of Manoa?”

“After we found them,” Amyas laughed. “Like the old fable—every craftsman thinks his trade is the pillar of society.”

“Perhaps,” Yeo said, “as a gunner, I overvalue guns. But need I use slate and pencil to see this truth? Are forty men without shot as strong as eighty with?”

“You’re right, old friend,” Amyas said. “I was only jesting—out of sorrow. I laugh because I cannot cry. Our chance is gone, I think—though I won’t admit it to the men.”

“Sir,” Yeo said, “I feel the Lord’s hand against us in this. Whether He means to keep this wealth for nobler hands, or hide the city in His secret place to spare mankind from greed, and England from the sins of gold—as I’ve seen among the Spaniards—I cannot say. But I’ve long heard a voice within: ‘Salvation Yeo, thou shalt never behold the Golden City on earth, where heathens worship sun and moon. Be content, then, to see the Golden City above, where neither sun nor moon shines, but the Lord God and the Lamb are the light.’”

There was a quiet dignity in Yeo when he spoke like this—a reverence that made even Amyas and Cary look upon him as Muslims regard holy fools, touched by divine insight. Brimblecombe, whose pious soul held Yeo in deep respect despite his Anabaptist beliefs, murmured gently:

“Amen. Amen. My masters all. And it’s been on my mind, too, that providence opposes our going east. See how, in the past two years, whenever we’ve pushed east, we’ve met disaster and lost men. But whenever we’ve gone westward—westward-ho—we’ve prospered. And we prosper still.”

“And what’s more,” Yeo added, “if dreams are from the Lord, as Scripture says, I believe mine last night came from Him. As I lay by the fire, I heard my little maid’s voice calling—clearer than ever I’ve heard. She repeated the words she learned from me and William Penberthy: 'Westward-ho! jolly mariners all!'—a reckless song, my masters, from our wild days. But she called it plain as day, again and again, until I answered, 'Coming, my maid, coming!' After that, she called no more—God grant I find her yet—and I woke.”

Cary had long stopped laughing at Yeo’s talk of the “little maid.” Amyas nodded.

“So be it, Yeo—if the rest agree. But what shall we do westward?”

“Do?” Cary said. “Plenty. Gold, and Spaniards too, they say, lie beyond these mountains. Our swords won’t rust for lack of adventure, my gallant knights-errant.”

They talked on. By midnight, a plan was formed—desperate, yes—but what did such hearts care? They would cross the Cordillera to Santa Fe de Bogotá, famed for its riches. They’d seize either the town or a gold convoy, head to the nearest river—said to flow north—and build canoes to reach the northern sea. From there, if fortune smiled, they’d capture a Spanish ship and sail home—not with Manoa’s treasure, but with a fair haul of Spanish gold. A new dream. Wild, yes—but no wilder than Drake’s fulfilled exploits, or Oxenham’s thwarted ambition.

That night, Amyas sat late, heart heavy. To abandon the dream of years was hard. To face his mother—harder. But it must be done—for the men’s sake. The next day, the plan was proposed—and welcomed. They would climb toward the mountains, rest, retrieve their wounded, then begin anew—with new hopes, new dangers. They were used to danger.

They set off the next morning, spirits lifted. For hours, they paddled easily up the glassy, windless river, flanked by walls of forest blooming with flowers, alive with birds and insects. Vines hung from overhanging branches, dipping into the water, reflecting their own radiant blossoms in the clear mirror. River, trees, flowers, birds—everything was a fairyland, but colossal. Yet they barely noticed. These sights were routine now. Trees two hundred feet tall, covered in yellow or purple blooms from top to bottom. Every branch a hanging garden of crimson orchids and orange vanillas. Strange shapes, colors, creatures—more dazzling than any opium-dream—were common. Monkeys raced along the treetops, whistling and grunting, but no longer amused them. The roar of jaguars, the rustle of boas—no longer frightened them. When a brilliant green-and-rose fish, flat-bodied like a bream, flab-finned like a salmon, saw-toothed like a shark, leapt onto the canoe to escape a charging alligator—whose snout nearly rattled against the hull—Jack didn’t flinch. He simply picked it up.

“He’s four pounds!” he said. “If you can catch ‘pirai’ like that, old fellow, stay in our wake. We’ll give you the scraps for wages.”

Yes. The human mind is not infinite, as people suppose. No matter how hungry the appetite for wonder, once satisfied, it grows dull—like a city diner after a lord mayor’s feast. Only the highest minds—Humboldt, Bonpland, Schomburgk—can endure the endless spectacle of the tropics. Even they tire. Words fail. Superlatives become meaningless. What can one say? “Wonderful, wonderful!” Then, “wonderful, past all whooping!” Humboldt himself could not paint it. Let readers fill in the meaning of “South American forest”—for I cannot.

These adventurers could not. Early explorers rarely described the beauty of the tropics—Spanish or English. Only Columbus (because everything was new) and Raleigh (perhaps the most gifted traveler besides Humboldt) hinted at it. But even they fell short. Their souls were overwhelmed. Like Plato’s men dragged from darkness into sunlight, they saw things too vast, too strange, to speak of. Why talk? Just say, “God is great!”

So it was with them. The grandeur around uplifted their spirits, kept them reverent, heroic. But they knew nothing of the flora and fauna in artistic or scientific terms. One tree made good bows; another, canoes; one bore edible fruit; another yielded caoutchouc; one supplied poison for arrows; ashes from palm nuts made salt; another tree gave milk when tapped. They drank it, thanked God, and felt no surprise. God was great—they had known that before the tropics.

Noble, childlike heroes—romantic enough to avoid the hysterical wonder that often comes from modern skepticism. We don’t trust God enough to expect the impossible. When we see a wonder, we shout and praise ourselves for being “refined.” But they saw it, accepted it, and moved on.

Hour after hour, they paddled, sheltering under the southern bank while the sun blazed on the northern wall of mimosas, figs, laurels, bamboo tufts, and parasitic blooms. Layer upon layer of color rose to the sky, where the outline blurred into a quivering rainbow of hues, mingling with the blue mist.

As the sun climbed, silence fell. Jaguars and monkeys hid. Birds fell silent. Butterflies slept on leaves, indistinguishable from flowers. A colibri hummed down to drink, vanished into the dark woods. A parrot screamed from a limb. A monkey slid down a liana, dipped water, chattered back—then froze, seeing an alligator lurking below. Capybaras, rabbit-sized sheep, paddled slowly among blue water-lilies. Water-hens ran on floating leaves. A freshwater dolphin surfaced, spouted spray, arched a rainbow, and sank again. Flamingoes stood knee-deep, dreaming. Cranes pranced. Ibises and egrets probed the water.

By noon, even they had vanished. Silence reigned—so deep it could be heard. Like the quiet of Devon woods or Exmoor’s heather fields. Humboldt wrote: “If we listen closely beyond the silence, we hear a stifled, continuous hum—the swarm of insects in the air, in bark, in soil, in the holes of lizards, millepedes, bees. A voice telling us that all nature breathes, that life swarms in the earth as in water and air.”

At last, a soft murmur grew into a roar. They neared a cataract. Turning a bend, where the soil rose into a low cliff fringed with ferns, they saw a scene that silenced them—not with wonder, but with something like disgust.

“Rapids again!” someone muttered. “I thought we’d had enough on the Orinoco.”

“We’ll have to drag the canoes overland. Three hours lost—in the hottest part of the day.”

“There’s worse behind—see the spray beyond the palms?”

“Stop grumbling,” Amyas said. “Don’t cry out before you’re hurt. Paddle to the largest island. Let’s look.”

“Let us alone. What pleasure can we have
To war with evil? Is there any peace
In always climbing up the climbing wave?
All things have rest, and ripen toward the grave
In silence; ripen, fall, and cease:
Give us long rest or death, dark death, or dreamful ease.”

There is a passage in Humboldt’s writings—curious, melancholy, and telling—where he stands before a group of Indians huddled around their fires, stained with grease and paint, eating ants and clay, and remarks that if it were not for science, which teaches us that such are the raw beginnings of humanity, he might well have thought them the last remnants of a fallen race, doomed to extinction. One wishes he had followed his own instinct, rather than the dogmas of an anthropology that claims no true foundation. For what does science offer but a theory built on silence, denying thousands of facts to uphold its fantasy? His own reason, clear and honest, seemed to align with the Bible’s truth: man, in his natural state, is fallen, broken, and bound for death. That may be a sobering thought—but it is nobler than pretending these poor souls were merely apes who never learned to think.

Our travelers, like most of their age, did not wrestle with such questions. They accepted the story of Genesis as truth, and thus saw in these people not beasts, but immortal souls—lost, yes, but still spirits, captured by the devil at his will, wandering aimlessly through the wilderness, destined to wander eternally unless rescued.

And indeed, the signs were everywhere.

When they landed, they found a scattered village of palm-leaf shelters, hammocks strung between trees, patches of cassava and indigo planted in cleared ground. It was neat, even orderly—a far cry from the wretchedness of the Guahibas they had met before. But something darker stirred beneath the surface.

On either side of the path stood several men, each with a tall drum or a long earthen trumpet, swelling into hollow balls along its length. The moment the English stepped ashore, the instruments erupted into a deafening cacophony—howls, groans, thumps—so violent and discordant that Yeo turned to Amyas and said, “They’re calling upon their devil, sir.”

Cary smiled. “Well, then, they’ll likely get one. None but Sir Urian would come to listen to such noise.”

“And mark this,” said Yeo, “there’s some feast or sacrifice afoot. I’m not yet sure of these folk.”

“Nonsense,” replied Amyas. “We could kill every soul here in half an hour—and they know it as well as we do.”

But plainly, something grander was coming. The villagers stood in two lines—men in front, women behind—all painted with annatto, indigo, and feathers, their bodies adorned with pride and ritual.

Then, with a shriek that split the air, a figure leapt into the center of the clearing. He wore a jaguar skin, complete with tail and grinning teeth, horns on his head, a plume of black and yellow feathers, and a massive rattle. He looked less like a man than a creature born of nightmare.

“There’s the Piache,” said Amyas.

“Ay,” said Yeo. “Satan’s livery, and I’ll wager his deeds match. Trust him for that.”

“Don’t fear, Jack,” said Cary, stepping back from Brimblecombe. “It’s your job to face him. Go boldly—he’ll flee.”

The men laughed. The Piache, who meant to terrify, faltered. But quickly recovering, he struck one musician with his rattle to silence the din, then began a speech—long, loud, and full of gestures—which Amyas listened to calmly, cigar in mouth.

“What’s he saying?” asked Amyas.

“He wants to know if you’ve seen Amalivaca on the other side of the great water.”

Amyas knew the name well—the mythic hero who once carved strange carvings into the cliffs of the region before vanishing beyond the sea. He answered as he always did, praising Queen Elizabeth.

The Piache replied that she must be one of Amalivaca’s seven daughters—some taken with him, others left behind with their legs broken so they couldn’t escape, and forced to populate the forests.

Amyas chuckled. “Her legs are sound enough. She dances better than any woman in England. But tell me—will you give us cassava bread? Will we be safe here while we rest before facing the clothed men—the Spaniards—on the other side of the mountains?”

The Piache danced wildly, capered, rolled over, then beckoned them forward. Seeing no weapons, the English followed, trusting the mood of the crowd.

He crawled to a hut, closed and guarded, and whined at the door.

“Ask what he’s doing,” said Amyas.

The boy spoke to the old cacique, who whispered back: “He is speaking with the Daughter of the Sun.”

“Ah,” said Cary. “Here’s our mare’s nest at last.” And sure enough, the Piache rose from moans to screams, then convulsions—foaming at the mouth, eyes rolling—until he collapsed, seemingly dead.

“As good as a stage play,” muttered someone.

“The devil has played his part,” said Jack. “Now, by all rules, Vice should enter.”

“And a fine Vice she’ll be,” said Cary. “A sweet Iniquity, my lad. Listen.”

From within the hut came a voice—low, clear, rich—not shrill or guttural like an Indian’s, but pure and resonant, like a European soprano. It swelled and soared, leaping and falling with perfect melody, rising in a final, piercing note that rang through the forest. Then silence.

The Piache gasped awake, jumped upright, and resumed his harangue.

“Tell him to make it short,” said Amyas. “That tune won’t hold after that last one.”

The boy returned with news: the Daughter of the Sun had accepted them as friends. Her friends were theirs. Her enemies, theirs.

The Indians roared with joy. Amyas, already rolling another tobacco leaf, said simply, “Then let her give us some cassava,” and lit a fresh cigar.

The door opened. From within stepped the same mysterious girl they had seen before—now adorned in feather robes, plumes of every color, radiant and regal.

She walked slowly, proudly, like a queen among prostrate subjects. With graceful gestures, she pointed to the trees, gardens, huts—signaling that all belonged to them. Then, taking Amyas’s hand, she raised it gently to her forehead.

At that gesture, the crowd erupted in joy. As she retreated into the hut, the villagers swarmed around the English, touching their swords, marveling at their bows and blowguns, pointing at the trophies of beasts they wore. Women hurried off with fruit, flowers, cassava, and calabashes of fermented drink—much to Amyas’s unease.

Under the trees, the English feasted, while drums and trumpets blared, and young dancers performed wild, uncouth rites. Brimblecombe and Yeo were scandalized. “We must leave,” they urged. “Before they lose their minds.”

Amyas agreed. Better to return while the men were still sober. There were farewells, promises to return tomorrow. They paddled back to their island fortress, speculating wildly about the maiden.

Amyas believed her to be of the lost Inca line—perhaps a descendant of the fair daughter of Manco Inca, whom Pizarro had cruelly executed decades before, stripped, scourged, and killed with arrows, all to break the heart of a king whose body he could not reach.

That night, they held their evening service—as they had done nearly every night since leaving England. After prayers, they sang a psalm, then a few catches, their voices ringing out above the thunder of the cataract. Once or twice, they thought they heard an echo.

Then Cary, returning from a brief walk, whispered to Amyas.

“The sweet Iniquity is mimicking us.”

They went to the river’s edge. In the moonlight, they heard it clearly—the same voice, repeating their songs, pure and true, echoing from the darkness of the forest. The deep voices of the men on the island answered with those delicate, soaring notes from the woods. It was eerie, beautiful, haunting. They stood long in silence, gazing across the golden eddies, seeing only the wall of black trees.

Eventually, the voice ceased. They returned to sleep—dreaming of Incas, of nightingales, of forgotten kingdoms.

They visited the village again next day—and every day for a week. But the maiden appeared rarely, and when she did, kept her distance, proud as a queen.

Amyas, eager to learn more, questioned the cacique. The old man made a grimace, shook his head mysteriously. “She is a child of the Sun,” he said. “That is enough.”

“Tell him,” said Cary, “that we are children of the Sun by his first wife. We carry orders from him—to inquire how the Indians have treated his stepdaughter. He cannot see all their tricks down here—the trees are too thick. So let him speak, or all the cassava shall wither.”

“Will, don’t lie!” said Amyas. But the threat worked. The cacique took them in his canoe, a mile downriver, as if afraid the maiden might hear.

Then, in a rhythmic chant, he told their tale:

Long ago, their tribe was mighty, dwelling in Papamene, until the Spaniards drove them out. Wandering northward, near Cotopaxi’s flaming peak, they found a small white girl—about the size of a seven-year-old—wandering alone in the forest. Her skin was pale, her beauty delicate. They took her as a god. When they discovered she was human, their wonder only grew. How had such a fragile being survived the jaguars, the snakes?

She must be protected by the gods. She must be a daughter of the Sun—one of the great Inca line, whose fall had reached even these remote lands. Many Incas, they knew, had fled to the eastern slopes of the Andes, living as exiles near the Ucalayi and Maranon rivers. One day, they believed, they would rise again—when bearded white men came across the seas to restore them.

So they raised her with royal honor, commanded by the tribe’s conjuror, that the Sun might favor them, and the Incas might remember the Omaguas in their hour of glory.

As she grew, she became wise in council, brave in battle, skilled in hunting. Her songs—those strange, sweet melodies—held wisdom about birds, animals, rivers, taught by the Sun and the Good Spirit. She remained unmarried, not because she lacked suitors, but because the conjuror declared it profane to mingle with the race of the Sun. She lived apart, served in state, and gave oracular answers—just as they had seen.

Thus, the cacique explained, she was both prophetess and goddess.

Cary remarked, probably rightly, that “the conjuror made a very good thing of it.” But Amyas was silent, lost in dreams—not of Manoa, but of the remnants of the Inca race. Could they still exist near the southern sources of the Amazon? He had been close. Yet, without a larger force, any search was hopeless. Still, perhaps he could learn more from her. Knowledge gained now might aid a future quest. If he could return home safely, many noble gentlemen—Raleigh, for one—would join him in seeking the Golden City, not by the upper waters, but by the mouth of the Orinoco.

They paddled back, the cacique begging them to tell the Sun how kindly they had treated his daughter—and pleading that they not take her away, lest the Sun forget the Omaguas and let their crops fail.

Amyas had no wish to linger longer than necessary to bring the sick men from the Orinoco. But he knew the journey would take months, fraught with danger.

Cary offered to lead the expedition, if half a dozen men joined him—and if the Indians sent young men to help paddle. But that was difficult. The tribe feared the fierce Guahibas, and all tribes, Amyas knew, viewed outsiders as enemies. This, they believed, was the work of the devil—the divider, the accuser. And perhaps they were right. But Amyas must beware—while he saw the devil in others, he must not allow him to take root in himself. More of that later.

Yet the maiden, though aloof, eventually warmed. Perhaps curiosity, perhaps loneliness in the endless forest, drew her closer. She signaled that though all the English were important, only Amyas was to be her companion.

Within a month, she hunted with him often, leading a train of chosen girls who had left their suitors behind. Such behavior, common among some tribes where women flee male dominance, seemed to the English proof they were near the land of the Amazons—those legendary warrior women spoken of by the Indians.

Amyas saw it differently. As a descendant of the Incas, she preserved the memory of the Virgins of the Sun, the sacred nuns of Peru. Had not George of Spires and Jeronimo Ortal found such convents fifty years before, on these very waters?

So a friendship formed—harmless at first. But soon, it bore fruit. When she learned Amyas needed men for Cary’s mission, she consulted the Piache, gathered the tribe, then withdrew to her hut and sang a song—so powerful, so commanding, that it was said to be a command from a terrible spirit, under penalty of doom. The effect was immediate. Men were chosen, the canoe launched.

Brimblecombe doubted the venture—begun by the devil’s patronage. Amyas, disliking the charade, told Ayacanora it would be better to say the deed pleased the Good Spirit.

“Oh,” she said simply, “they know better. The Good Spirit is big and lazy—he smiles and does nothing. But the little bad spirit—he is busy! Here, there, everywhere.” She waved her hands. “He is the useful one to have as a friend.”

The Piache approved—after all, it was his role. Once, he snapped at Brimblecombe: “You meddle too much, telling them the Good Spirit cares for them. If they start asking Him for cassava and coca, who will feed me?”

But Jack preached on, undeterred. His sermons were strong, righteous, and the Indians, drawn by the singing, came in droves. The Piache’s power dwindled. He vowed to end Jack’s Gospel with a poisoned arrow.

Blinded by Satan, so Jack called it, the Piache confided his plan to Ayacanora—his partner in tithes and offerings.

She responded with a slap and a torrent of abuse. Then she went to Amyas, told him all, and proposed throwing the Piache to the alligators and installing Jack in his place. “Whatever the bearded men say must be true,” she declared. “Whoever plots against them shall die.”

Jack forgave him, and preached on—with renewed zeal. But the Piache had a secret weapon: a holy trumpet, hidden in a cave, forbidden to women, said to possess magical power. It was known throughout the region—tribes paid dearly to hire it each season, for without its blast, the palms would bear no fruit.

One day, the Piache announced he would retire to a more devout tribe, taking the trumpet with him. “Because of the impiety of the Omaguas,” he declared, “their palms will blight, and they will fall into the hands of the evil spirit.”

The village erupted. Wailing filled the air. Jack’s words were good—but what was the Gospel compared to the trumpet?

The Piache saw his chance. He raged against the strangers. His followers raged with him. Suspicion flared like wildfire. Women screamed, men grabbed bows and blowguns. The situation grew dire.

Amyas had fewer than a dozen men. They had only swords. The Indians could muster nearly a hundred. Poisoned arrows were deadly. Several faces paled.

“It is God’s quarrel,” said Brimblecombe. “Let Him defend the right.”

As he spoke, a song rose from Ayacanora’s hut—her magic voice, quivering among the green heights of the forest.

And in that moment, the storm passed.

“God will relent, and quit thee all thy debt,
Who ever more approves, and more accepts
Him who imploring mercy sues for life,
Than who self-rigorous chooses death as due,
Which argues over-just, and self-displeased
For self-offence, more than for God offended.”

—Samson Agonistes.

A fortnight had passed in grueling labor, though no harder than they had endured many times before. Farewell forever to the green expanse of the eastern plains, they had crossed the Cordillera range, casting a long look back at Santa Fe—perched high on its mountain plateau, surrounded by lush gardens. But it was too large, too fortified, for any raid they could hope to carry off. Yet their journey had not been wasted. Their Indian boy had learned that a gold convoy was making its way from Santa Fe toward the Magdalena River. Now, they waited beside the rutted track that served as a road, camped beneath an oak forest so thick and leafy it might have fooled them into thinking they were back in England—had it not been for the tree ferns below, or the deep gorges yawning at their feet. From the cool breezes above, they could glimpse far down through swirling steam the vibrant, tangled world of the tropical jungle.

They had made camp among the ferns, just above a stretch of path winding along a steep hillside, with a sheer cliff plunging hundreds of feet below. Once, perhaps, this had been a proper road—when Cundinamarca was a cultivated kingdom. Now only crumbling steps remained, slipping from their foundations into a muddy ditch. Spanish misrule had left nothing but ruins: aqueducts collapsed, bridges gone, post-houses vanished, llama flocks scattered, gardens choked. Only architecture survived—grand cathedrals rising atop ancient temples of the Sun, built in honor of a gentler pantheon. Or so it seemed—yet if such a pantheon demanded human sacrifice, as the Spaniards did, then it was no milder than the old gods of the Incas.

Now the tropics had reclaimed what man had destroyed. Amyas and his men were utterly alone—within miles of a major Spanish settlement, yet as isolated as if they were lost in the heart of the Orinoco or Amazon.

All attempts to find sulphur and nitre had failed. They were forced to rely once more—much to Yeo’s chagrin—on swords and arrows. So be it. Drake had taken Nombre de Dios and seized a gold train with no better arms. If they could do the same, why not?

They had blocked the path above by felling a massive tree across it. Now they sat among wildflowers, chewing coca instead of eating, and pondering a strange sound that had followed them each night since leaving the Meta River—a deep, mysterious roar. Not a jaguar, nor monkey, nor anything familiar. Why should it follow them? But they were in a land of wonders; and the gold train mattered far more than any noise.

Then came a sharp crack—and a cry.

Not a branch snapping. Not a woodpecker tapping. Not a parrot screeching. Not a monkey howling.

“That was a whip,” said Yeo. “And a woman’s wail. They’re close, lads!”

“A woman?” asked Amyas. “Do they chain women in their gangs?”

“Why not? The brutes!” replied Yeo. “There they are, sir. Did you see their basnets glitter?”

“Men,” whispered Amyas. “I trust you all not to shoot until I give the word. Then one arrow each. Draw your swords. Charge! Pass the word.”

Up they came, slowly, and every heart beat loud.

First came about twenty soldiers—half on foot, half carried in chairs slung on the backs of single Indians. Those marching had handed their heaviest armor and arquebuses to slave attendants, prodded forward by the pikes of the soldiers behind.

“The men are mad to let their weapons out of their hands,” muttered one.

“Oh, sir,” said Yeo, “an Indian will pray to an arquebus not to fire on him. He knows their artillery is safe enough.”

“Look at the proud villains,” whispered another. “Making beasts of men like that.”

Amyas counted: ten muskets, ten pikes. “Will can handle them up above.”

Then came an officer, seated in a chair, moving slowly up the slope. As he passed, he drew his cigar from his lips every few seconds, muttering prayers—or curses—to the Virgin, saints, devils, wooden idols, and even his own body parts. Such piety earned the Spanish of the sixteenth century a reputation both as devout idolaters and shameless swearers.

“The blasphemous dog!” said Yeo, fingers twitching on his bowstring. But Amyas touched his arm just as a second procession emerged—and all forgot the officer.

It was a sight of horror—yet common enough in those remote lands where royal edicts against cruelty were ignored. The Dominican friars had pleaded for mercy; Pedro de la Gasca had enforced laws in Peru. But these were futile efforts against horrors unmatched in Christendom—save perhaps in the conquests of Sennacherib or Genghis Khan. On the frontiers, where enslaved Africans replaced Indians who died under the burden, and tribes accused of cannibalism were hunted down for their souls and bodies, such scenes still occurred. And, according to Humboldt, even in the late eighteenth century, Jesuit missions—though praised—were ruled by military tyranny, preaching the Gospel with fire and sword, much as the first conquistadors had done.

A line of Indians, Negroes, and Zambos marched barefoot, emaciated, scarred by whips and chains, linked wrist to wrist. Each bore a basket suspended by a strap across the forehead, heavy with burden. Yeo’s scorn was justified: there were not only men and youths, but women—slender girls, mothers with children running at their heels. A low murmur rose among the Englishmen, born of righteous anger. In those days, when Raleigh spoke of humanity and freedom, when English hearts still knew manhood and liberty, such injustice stirred outrage. It was a time before the seventeenth century turned them cold with slavery’s legacy.

But the first forty bore something that silenced even that sorrow. Each basket held a square package of tightly bound hide. Amyas recognized it instantly.

“What’s in them, captain?”

“Gold.” At that word, eyes lit with greed. A rustle ran through the ambush. Amyas hushed them.

“Be men. Be men. Or we’ll ruin everything.”

The last twenty carried larger baskets, lighter loads—manioc, maize bread, food for the convoy. Behind them came twenty more soldiers, then the officer again, smirking in his chair, twirling his mustache, unaware that English arrows burned to pierce his ribs. The trap was set. Only when and how?

Amyas hesitated. To draw bow on men so unsuspecting—even while committing cruelty—was hard. Yet it was cruelty indeed. Three drivers armed with whips walked the line, striking every stumble, every lag, with the manati-hide whip that cracked like gunfire against naked skin. The victims endured silently.

Then the moment came.

Near the end of the chain was an old man, gray-haired, followed by a slender girl of eighteen. Amyas’s heart ached for them. Just as they passed, the lead guard rounded the bend above.

“Halt, senors! A tree blocks the path!”

“A tree?” roared the officer, cursing Heaven, Hell, Saint James, and every saint in between. The line halted above, driven forward below. The old man stumbled, fell face-down.

The officer leapt from his chair, rushed up. He found the old man prostrate.

“Sin peccado concebida! Grandfather of Beelzebub—what place is this to worship your demons?” He jabbed the man with his sword.

The old man tried to rise—but the weight crushed him. He fell again, motionless.

The driver lashed him twice with the whip. No response.

“Gastado, Senor Capitan,” he shrugged. “Worn out. He’s failing for three months.”

“What does the intendant think sending me broken cattle like this? Forward! Clear the tree, senors. I’ll clear the chain. Hold it up, Pedrilo!”

The driver lifted the chain from the old man’s wrist. The officer stepped back, brandishing a Toledo blade—so fine it made Amyas forget the commandments.

He raised the sword—once, twice—then struck.

Not the chain.

The wrist.

A shriek. A flash of crimson. The chain broke. The prisoner was severed.

One moment more, and Amyas’s arrow would have pierced the murderer’s throat. But vengeance came from elsewhere.

Like a tiger-cat, the girl sprang—clutched the officer—leapt with him from the ledge into the abyss.

A shout. A rush. All leaned over the edge. She hung by her chained wrist. The officer was gone. A silence fell. Then—crash!—the sound of a body breaking through tree limbs far below.

“Haul her up! Cut her to pieces! Burn the witch!” screamed the driver, pulling the chain. Others leapt from their chairs, gathered at the brink.

Now—now was the time.

Amyas loosed an arrow—ten yards away—it pierced the driver’s chest. Then, roaring like a lion, he charged into the stunned Spaniards.

His first thought: the girl. With strength, he pulled her to safety. The Spaniards recoiled—thinking him a mountain spirit. His cry revealed the truth. “English! Lutheran dogs!” they shouted—but too late.

The Devon men followed. Arrows flew—five dead, a dozen wounded. Salvation Yeo, white hair streaming, leapt down with twenty swords. Death began.

The Spaniards fought fiercely—but no time to load arquebuses. No room to use pikes. The English held the wall. That meant life in their hands.

Five minutes. No Spaniard stood. None lived below.

Two armored men escaped—behind the rest—ran down the hill.

“After them! Michael Evans, Simon Heard—catch them if they run a league.”

The two Clovelly men, swift as deer, raced after. Ten minutes later, they returned—mission complete.

Amyas and his men hurried forward—shouts and gunfire signaled a fight ahead.

Their arrival ended it. Most Spaniards fell. A few scrambled down the cliffs.

“Let none escape! Slay them as Israel slew Amalek!” cried Yeo. Before they reached shelter, arrows found each—bodies rolled down the rocks.

“Now—free the Indians!”

They found tools on a corpse. Chains were cut.

“We are your friends,” said Amyas. “We ask only that you help us carry this gold to the Magdalena. Then you are free.”

Some young ones knelt, kissed his feet, calling him child of the Sun. Most sat silent, indifferent. The iron had entered their souls. They seemed beyond hope, joy, understanding.

But the young girl—last in line—when freed, ran to her father’s body. Without a word, she lifted it, laid it on her knees, kissed his lips, stroked his cheeks, murmured sounds like a dove’s coo—only she understood. Then, suddenly—she rose, holding him.

She leapt into the abyss.

They watched her dark form, wrapped around the corpse, turning over, over—then a crash in the trees, a scream from birds. The green canopy hid her.

“Brave lass!” shouted a sailor.

“The Lord forgive her,” said Yeo. “But, sir, we must take their arms.”

“And their clothes,” added Amyas. “If we’re to pass unchallenged down the Magdalena. Listen, my men. By God’s grace, we’ve won enough gold to last our lives—without losing a man. We may win more—if we stay wise, and He allows. But remember Mr. Oxenham and his crew. Let not this gift become our ruin—by greed, faithlessness, or haste.”

“You’ll find no such thing in us!” several called. “We trust you, sir.”

“Thank God,” said Amyas. “Now—no shame in making the Indians carry the gold. Save the women. God forbid we burden them. But we must pass through Spanish settlements—past Saint Martha itself. So we need their clothes and weapons. How many lie here?”

“Thirteen here. Ten above,” said Cary.

“Twenty missing. Who’ll go down the cliff and bring up the spoils?”

“I—I—I,” said a dozen. Always ready—because they knew Amyas would join them.

“Very well. Follow me. Sir John, take the Indian lad. Comfort these poor souls. Tell them they’re free.”

“Who comes up the road?”

All turned.

And there—wonder of wonders—came Ayacanora herself, blow-gun in hand, bow on back, feathered garments worn from weeks in the woods.

Silence. Then, seeing Amyas, she cried, ran, and fell at his feet, panting.

“I found you!” she gasped. “You ran from me—but you couldn’t escape me!” She circled him like a faithful dog, then sat on the bank, sobbing wildly.

“God help us,” said Amyas, clutching his hair. “What am I to do with her—besides all these poor heathens?”

No time. He scrambled over the cliff. The girl watched from a rock.

Half an hour later, weapons, clothes, armor were hauled up. Corpses thrown over the edge. They moved on—toward the Magdalena. Yeo snorted like a warhorse, thrilled at handling powder again.

“We can face the world now, sir! Why not try Santa Fe?”

But Amyas shook his head. Enough was enough. They pressed onward. The slaves followed—no thanks, but quiet obedience. Some noticed the Englishmen’s calabashes of coca. That woke them. They begged—begged abjectly—for a taste. For coca gave strength, eased breath, and drove away the worst enemy of all—the pain of thought.

As they turned the mountain corner, they paused—one last look.

Vultures streamed from the sky, drawn by the feast. In hours, no trace would remain—only bones amid flowers.

Now Amyas asked Ayacanora why she was there.

He wished she weren’t. But now she was—how could any heart be hard enough to ignore her?

He spoke gently, afraid of hurting her.

She told her tale—passionately. Every night, she had made loud noises, hoping they’d hear, turn back. Now he remembered—the roaring.

“Noises? What made them?”

Ayacanora lifted a finger, then slowly drew from under her cloak an object that made Amyas struggle to keep his composure.

“Look!” she whispered, as if afraid the thing might hear. “I have it—the holy trumpet!”

There it was—the bone of contention. A glazed earthen tube, two feet long, painted with animals and patterns—clearly a relic of a vanished civilization.

Brimblecombe clapped his hands. “Brave maid! You’ve cheated Satan this time!” Yeo insisted the “idolatrous relic” be thrown over the cliff.

“Let be,” said Amyas. “What is this, Ayacanora? And why did you follow us?”

She told her story. The trumpet had tormented her for years—the one thing greater than her, forbidden because she was a woman. She vowed to prove a woman could equal a man. The Piache refused to show it. She feared the tribe’s wrath. But after the English left, the Piache celebrated—leading to a quarrel. She burned his hut, he fled, vowing to take the trumpet to another tribe.

So she took coca, her weapons, her feathers, and chased him—found him just as he revealed the trumpet. Afraid, she almost fled—but remembering the whites laughed at such things, she attacked and stole it.

“I hope you didn’t kill him?”

“I beat him—just a little. I thought you wouldn’t let me kill him.”

Amyas smiled—amused by her belief in his authority.

Then she added: “And I dared not return to the Indians. So I came after you.”

“And is that your only reason?” asked Amyas, stupidly.

He struck a hidden chord. She stiffened, blushing crimson.

“You never lie. Do you think I would?”

Then she fell back—silent, steady—following them, speaking to no one, determined to go wherever they went.

“When captains bold, whom death could not daunt,
Marched to the siege of Gaunt’s proud town,
They mustered soldiers two and three—
But foremost in the fray was Mary Ambree.
When brave Sir John Major fell by her side,
Her true love, joy, and heart’s delight,
Slain by treachery most cruel and vile,
Then vowed she’d avenge him, fair Mary Ambree.”

—Old Ballad, A.D. 1584.

One last glance at the golden sea, the golden evenings, along the shore of New Granada, on the Spanish Main.

The bay of Santa Marta shimmered under the land breeze like a sheet of molten fire. The dense forests sparkled with countless fireflies. A lazy mist curled around the inner hills, glowing gold in the fading light; high above, nineteen thousand feet in the sky, the peak of Horqueta pierced the heavens, rose-red against the deep blue vault. As dusk deepened, the rosy glow faded into dull lead, but only for a moment. Stars blinked into view one by one, and Venus, like a second moon, bathed the eastern snows in gold and cast a long, rippling line of yellow light across the water. Everywhere was splendor, richness, beauty. Was it any wonder that the earth beneath such skies held treasures as dazzling as its surface? That every stream, every bank, glittered with emeralds and rubies, with grains of gold and silver threads?

Such thoughts drifted through the mind of the Bishop of Cartagena as he sat in the state cabin of the great galleon *The City of the True Cross*, gazing toward the shore. He was calm, almost holy in his stillness. His sturdy frame rested in an easy chair, his ankles propped on another, beside a table laden with oranges, limes, guavas, pineapples—the fruits of the Indies.

An Indian girl, adorned with scarves and gold chains, fanned away flies with a feathered fan. Nearby, in a pail of ice from Horqueta (a gift from a devout Spanish lady who had “spent” a few Indians to bring it down), stood several flasks of rich wine from Alicante. But the bishop was not selfish. He had invited Don Pedro, colonel of the shipboard soldiers; Don Alverez, intendant of the royal customs at Santa Marta; and Don Paul, captain of mariners aboard the galleon. Two friars sat at the lower end of the table, helping to ease the bishop’s sorrow over the recent sack and burning of his cathedral city—by none other than Sir Francis Drake.

“We have suffered greatly, senors,” the bishop sighed, quoting Scripture in the manner of the time—fluently, though often too freely, mixing sacred texts with grumbles about mosquitoes and Tita, his servant. “Great sufferers indeed. Yet there shall be a remnant—ah, a remnant like the shaking of the olive tree, or the gleanings after the vintage.” He paused, then added, “Ah! Gold? Yes, I trust Our Lady’s mercy is not closed, nor her arms shortened. Look, senors!” He pointed dramatically out the window. “It looks like gold! It smells of gold, if I may say so poetically. Even the waves sing of gold—gold, gold, gold!”

“It is a rare privilege,” said the intendant, “to receive comfort from both a churchman and a scholar.”

“And a poet,” added Don Pedro. “You’ve no idea what sweet sonnets—”

“Hush, Don Pedro—hush!” the bishop replied, smiling. “If I, a mateless bird, once taught lovers how to sing, what of that? I am a man of God, yes—but also a man. I feel, I sympathize, I understand. Who better knows the frailties of human nature than I, a fallen son of Adam? Tita!”

“Um?” the girl murmured, trembling slightly.

“Fill the intendant’s glass. Will more treasure come down, illustrious senor? May the poor of Mary hope for crumbs from her table?”

“Not a pezo, I fear,” replied the intendant. “The big white cow up there”—he gestured to Horqueta—“has been milked dry for this year.”

“Ah!” the bishop mused, gazing upward. “Only good for cooling wine, eh? And as safe now as Solomon’s birds.”

“Solomon’s birds? Explain your allusion, my lord.”

“Enlighten us, your excellency.”

“Ah, there’s a tale. You know the holy birds that wander the Prado in Seville, among the ladies’ feet—hooked beaks, cinnamon crests? Hoopoes—*Upupa*, as the classics call them. Once, it is said, they wore golden crowns. And what happened? They suffered for it. But one day, all the birds and beasts came to pay homage to King Solomon, and among them were these hoopoes. They made a humble request—can you guess what? That Solomon pray for them to wear any crown but those of gold. For listen, Tita—see the snare of riches—men hunted, shot, trapped, and killed them for those golden crowns. Life became unbearable. So Solomon prayed—and instead of gold, they received crowns of feathers. Since then, they live as cheerfully as crickets in an oven, and bear the name of his most Catholic majesty. Tita—fill the commandant’s glass. Fray Gerundio, what are you whispering?”

Fray Gerundio had simply muttered to his brother:
“O si sic omnia—if only all gold would turn to feathers!”

“Then, friend,” replied the other friar—a Dominican, darker and sterner—“corrupt human nature would find some new bauble within a week, for which to kill and die in vain.”

“What was that, Fray Gerundio?” the bishop asked again.

“I merely said it would be well if all mankind prayed as the hoopoes did.”

“World, sir? What do you know of the world? Convert your Indians, sir, and leave politics to your superiors. Excuse him, senors.” The bishop lowered his voice. “A good man, truly, but born of peasants. And… well—you understand. Too much fasting, too much watching, I fear.” He tapped his temple knowingly.

Fray Gerundio smiled quietly. He was one of those rare souls whose conscience had been stirred by the cruelty of his own people—like Las Casas, Gasca, and others whose names shine in history. He protested against injustice, even before kings. He called dark-skinned heathens brothers. He declared, in words astonishing for the age, that every human being bore the same flesh and blood as Christ. He was a martyr in spirit, bearing witness to truth, often mocked and scorned. Such was Fray Gerundio—a humble, awkward peasant’s son who could barely string sentences together, yet could move crowds of Indians and blacks to tears when preaching with a crucifix in hand. He endured the bishop’s insults without protest, sipped his wine in silence, and let the conversation flow.

“They say,” observed the colonel, “that only a small Plate fleet will sail to Spain this year.”

“What else?” said the intendant. “What can we send, in God’s name, since those cursed English Lutherans have stripped us bare?”

“And if we had anything,” said the sea captain, “what vessel would carry it? That devil incarnate, Drake—”

“Ah!” the bishop interrupted. “Spare my ears! Don Pedro, please refrain from mentioning that man—his name is Tartarean, unfit for polite lips. Draco—a dragon, a serpent—the very emblem of the Devil himself—ah! The guardian of the golden apples of the West, who seeks to devour our new Hercules, His Most Catholic Majesty. He deceived Eve with one of those same apples—a wicked name, senors—a Tartarean name—Tita!”

“Um!”

“Fill my glass.”

“Nay,” the colonel burst out, “this Englishman is of a different breed of serpent altogether.”

“Your reason, senor?”

“Because this one would have drowned Eve in the sea before letting her—or anyone else—taste anything that looked like gold.”

“Ah, ah—very good! But—we laugh, valiant senors, while the Church weeps. Alas for my sheep!”
