“And alas for their fold! It will be four years before Cartagena can be rebuilt. And the blockhouse—when it will rise again, Heaven alone knows, while His Majesty drains the Indies to feed his English Armada. The town is naked now, as an Indian’s back.”

“Baptista Antonio, the surveyor, has sent a report to the king detailing our defenselessness. But reading a report and acting upon it are two very different things, bishop—as every statesman knows. May Heaven grant us orders by the next fleet to fortify, or we’ll be at the mercy of every English pirate!”

“Ah, that blockhouse!” sighed the bishop. “A villainous trick. One hundred and ten thousand ducats for the ransom of the town! After burning half of it—and making me dine with them—ah! Sitting between the serpent and his lieutenant-general—and drinking my health in my own private wine—wine from Xeres nine years old—offered, the shameless heretics, to take me to England, convert me to Lutheranism, find me a wife, make an honest man of me—ah! And then demand fresh ransom for the priory and the fort—perfidious!”

“Well,” said the colonel, “they had the law on their side, the cunning rascals. We forgot to mention anything but the town in the agreement. Who would have guessed such a trick?”

“So I told my dear friend the prior when he came to borrow a thousand crowns. It was Heaven’s will—unexpected as a thunderbolt, to be borne as such. Every man must carry his own burden. How could I lend him anything?”

“Your holiness’s money had already been taken by them,” said the intendant, who knew full well the opposite.

“Exactly—my entire savings! Desolate in my old age. Ah, senors, had we not received warning from my dear friend the Marquess of Santa Cruz—whom I remember daily in prayer—we might have gone down into the pit like the rest. I too might have saved something, had I thought of myself. But in caring for others, I forgot myself—alas!”

“Warning or not,” said the sea captain, “we had no right to be beaten by such a handful. And a shame it is, and will be for many days.”

“Do you mean to cast aspersions on the courage of His Catholic Majesty’s soldiers?” asked the colonel sharply.

“I? No. But we were soundly beaten—behind our barricades, too. That’s the plain truth.”

“Beaten, sir? Do you apply such a term to war’s fortunes? What more could our governor have done? We had poisoned caltrops in the roads, Indian archers guarding them, earth-filled butts blocking the way, culverins and arquebuses firing from every angle. What spirit could have told us they’d come along the beach, not the high road, like Christian men?”

“Ah!” said the bishop. “By diabolical intuition, I suspect. Satan must aid those who serve him. For my part, I believe these men were possessed by every fiend imaginable.”

“Well, your holiness,” said the colonel, “there may have been devilry. How else would men dare run into the mouths of our cannons, fire at our very faces, and tumble harmlessly over those massive earthworks?”

“Doubtless by the power of demons raging within them,” interposed the bishop.

“And then, with their blasphemous cries, leap upon us with sword and pike? I saw Lieutenant-General Carlisle strike down your excellency’s sister’s son’s nephew—the noble ensign—though he was fully armored. Was not art-magic here? And that furious Lutheran Captain Young—I saw him seize our general by the head after Don Alonzo had wounded him, throw him to the ground, and capture him. Was not art-magic here?”

“Well,” said the captain, “if you seek art-magic, what say you to their marching through the flank fire of our galleys—eleven guns, two hundred men shooting at them—like a swarm of mosquitoes? Some said my men fired too high. But that was the English rascals’ doing—they got down on the tide beach. Yet, senor commandant, though Satan may have taught them that trick, did he teach them to carry pikes a foot longer than ours?”

“Ah, well,” said the bishop, “we are sacked. San Domingo, I hear, fares worse. St. Augustine in Florida too. All that remains for a poor priest like me is to return to Spain, and beg His Majesty’s and the Universal Father’s mercy for relief—for the poor of Mary—perhaps—(who knows?)—to transfer me to a quieter post. I am old, senors, weary from many labors—Tita! Fill our glasses. I have saved something—as you may have, senors, from the general ruin. For the flock, when I am gone, Heaven’s mercies are infinite. New cities will rise from ashes, new mines yield treasures to the faithful, new Indians will come to the Cross, embrace the easy yoke and light burden of the Church, and—”

“And where shall I be then? Ah, where? I long to rest, to depart. Tita! Sling my hammock. Senors, forgive my age and weakness. Fray Gerundio, go to bed!”

The Dons rose to leave. The bishop continued muttering:

“Farewell! Life is short. Ah! We shall meet in heaven. And really—no more pearls?”

“Not a one. Nor gold either,” said the intendant.

“Ah, well. Better a dinner of herbs where love is, than—Tita!”

“My breviary—ah! Man’s gratitude is fleeting. Have you seen Senora Bovadilla?”

“No.”

“Ah! She promised—yet no matter. A trifle as a keepsake—a gold cross, an emerald ring—what not? I forget. And what need have I of worldly wealth? Ah! Tita! Bring me the casket.”

When his guests were gone, the old man began mumbling prayers from his breviary, fingers tracing jewels and gold with the dull, greedy eyes of avarice.

“Ah—it may buy the red hat yet! Omnia Romae venalia! Put it by, Tita, and do not gaze at it too much. Enter not into temptation. The love of money is the root of all evil. Heaven, in love for the Indian, has made him poor in this world, that he may be rich in faith. Ah—ugh—so!”

The old miser climbed into his hammock. Tita drew the mosquito net over him, wrapped another around her head, and lay coiled on the floor—seeming to sleep, but watchful. Soon, master and slave snored in harmony, beneath the hum of mosquitoes.

Long past midnight. The moon had set. The sentinels, having challenged until they believed their officers asleep, slipped away from the unhealthy glow of the stars to seek rest—and slept as soundly as the bishop himself.

Two long, silent boats glided from behind the isolated rocks of Morro Grande, five hundred yards astern of the galleon. Nearly invisible on the shimmering water—pure white—they left only faint phosphorescent trails along their sides.

The bishop stirred awake, shifting uneasily. The wine had worn off. His shoulders had slipped down, his heels up, and his head ached. He sat upright in his hammock, stared out at the bay, and called Tita.

“Put another pillow under my head, child! What is that? A fish?”

Tita looked. Not a fish, she thought—but she said nothing. To argue might wake him.

The bishop studied the shape, decided it must be a white whale or shark, crossed himself, prayed for a safe voyage—and snored again.

Then the cabin door opened softly. The intendant’s head appeared.

Tita sat up, then crawled silently across the floor, among chairs and tables, lit by the cabin lamp.

“Is he asleep?”

“Yes. But the casket is under his head.”

“Curse him! How shall we take it?”

“I brought him a fresh pillow half an hour ago. I hung his hammock crooked on purpose, so he’d need one. I tried to slip the box away—but the old ox clutched it in both hands the whole time.”

“What shall we do, in the name of all the devils? She sails tomorrow morning. If we don’t act now, all is lost.”

Tita showed her teeth, touched the dagger at the intendant’s side.

“I dare not!” he shuddered.

“I dare!” she hissed. “He whipped my mother because she refused to give me to him—to be taught in his schools—when she went to the mines. She went, and died in three months. I saw her go, chained. She never returned. Yes—I dare kill him! I will kill him! I will!”

The intendant felt relieved. He had no wish to murder the bishop himself—he was a good Catholic, afraid of damnation. But Tita was an Indian. Her soul was cheap. He answered, “But we’ll be discovered!”

“I’ll leap from the window with the casket and swim ashore. They’ll never suspect you. They’ll think I’m drowned.”

“The sharks may get you, Tita. Give me the casket.”

Tita smiled. “You wouldn’t want to lose that, would you? Though you care little for losing me. And yet you told me you loved me!”

“And I do love you, Tita! Light of my eyes! Life of my heart! I swear by all the saints—I love you. I’ll marry you. I swear it—I’ll swear on the crucifix, if you wish!”

“Swear, then, or I won’t give you the casket.” She held out her crucifix, eyes blazing with wild, tropical passion.

He swore—trembling, pale as death.

“Give me your dagger.”

“No—not mine. It might be found. I’d be suspected. What if my sheath were empty?”

“Your knife will do. His throat is soft enough.”

She crept like a shadow toward the hammock, while he stood frozen at the other end, turned away, unable to watch.

He waited—one minute—two—five? An hour? Cold sweat soaked his limbs. His temples throbbed. Was that a death bell? No—it was his own pulse. Impossible. Where could it come from?

Then—struggle. Ah! She was at it. A stifled cry. He had feared that most—heard the old man scream. Would there be much blood? He hoped not. Another struggle. Tita’s voice, muffled, called for help.

“I cannot help you. Mother of Mercy! I dare not help you!” he hissed. “She-devil! You started it—you finish it!”

A heavy arm seized him from behind. The bishop had broken free and grabbed him! Or was it his ghost? A demon dragging him to hell? In pure terror, he opened his mouth to scream—only for a handkerchief to be thrust in. Then he felt himself bound hand and foot, thrown onto the table by a giant figure.

The cabin filled with armed men. Two tied the bishop in his hammock. Two seized Tita. Others climbed into the stern gallery—wild figures, blades gleaming in the starlight.

“Now, Will,” whispered the giant who had captured him, “forward—shut the fore-hatches tight. Shout ‘Fire!’ with all your might. Girl—murderess—your life is in my hands. Tell me where the commander sleeps, and I’ll spare you.”

Tita looked up at the towering speaker and obeyed in silence.

The intendant heard him enter the colonel’s cabin—then a brief scuffle—silence.

But only briefly. The alarm had sounded. Chaos erupted across every deck. Amyas—none other—had reached the poop. Sentinels were gagged and bound. Every half-naked man who stumbled up the main hatchway—yelling “Fire!” “Wreck!” “Treason!”—was hurled into the scuppers and secured.

“Lower that boat!” Amyas shouted in Spanish to the first prisoners.

Unarmed and naked, they could only obey.

“Now—jump in. Hand them to the gangway as they come.”

Done. Each man was kicked to the scuppers and bundled overboard.

“She’s full. Cast loose—away! If you try to board again, we’ll sink you.”

“Fire! Fire!” cried Cary forward. “Up the main hatchway—save yourselves!”

The ruse succeeded perfectly. Within half an hour, every ship’s boat that could be lowered was filled with Spaniards in their shirts, scrambling ashore.

“A new kind of camisado,” quoth Cary. “The last Spanish one I saw was at Smerwick—but this is far more successful.”

“Raise the main and foresail, Will!” said Amyas. “Cut the cable. We’ll plume our quarry as we fly.”

“Spoken like a true falconer. Heaven grant this great woodcock carries a good trail inside!”

“I’ll warrant it does,” said Jack Brimblecombe. “She floats low.”

“Much like your build, Jack. By the way—where is the commander?”

“All precious things, discover'd late, To them who seek them issue forth; For love in sequel works with fate, And draws the veil from hidden worth.”

—The Sleeping Beauty.

Thus Ayacanora took up her place in Lucy’s cabin, officially welcomed as a member of the crew.

Yet she proved no easy companion. From the moment she stepped into the world of the white man, she entered that perilous transition which has undone so many native souls—when simplicity meets civilization, when wonder overwhelms identity. It was the same crisis that would later hasten the downfall of North American tribes, and had it not been for Amyas’s unwavering sense and steadfast care, Ayacanora might have met a fate even darker than that of Pocahontas, daughter of the Virginia king. Once honored at the court of King James I as a sister sovereign, and later claimed by noble families as an ancestor, Pocahontas ended her days in poverty, shut away in some wretched garret in Wapping.

For the mind of the savage, overwhelmed by the superiority of the white man’s skill, wealth, and knowledge, often collapses under the weight of shame. The body, fed without labor, grows weak and idle. Self-respect fades. Self-reliance vanishes. And with them go the few virtues that once sustained the wilderness-born. Then begins the slide—toward theft, sloth, drunkenness, and despair. Easy, if not certain.

And so, at first, it seemed Ayacanora was slipping down that path fast. The warrior-prophetess of the Omaguas, once fierce and proud, now appeared little more than a willful child. Diana of the Meta, after marveling at the great floating house—wandering decks, peering into cupboards and corners—developed a curious habit: stealing trinkets that caught her fancy. She never asked; she simply took. When Amyas forbade her, she threatened to drown herself and sulked in her cabin all day.

She obeyed him, save in one thing: sweets. Perhaps her blood craved the fruits of her forest home. Whatever the cause, the bishop’s stores of fruit and confections dwindled swiftly. Worse still, the sweet Spanish wine Amyas had reserved for Lucy’s daily comfort vanished too.

Another stern lecture followed. Amyas told her how shameful it was to steal from poor, sick Lucy. She responded, as always, with threats of suicide—and ran toward the rail, intent on fulfilling them. But Amyas caught her just in time, forgave her, and held her close.

Then came a violent fit of weeping, repentance, and promises. A week later, he discovered she had tricked the Portuguese steward into giving her another bottle of wine instead. Lucky for her, the wine affected her as he had warned: she acted foolishly one evening, woke with a splitting headache the next morning. The truth came out. Amyas summoned the steward for punishment—but Ayacanora intervened, begging mercy, even offering to take the flogging herself. She confessed the steward had spoken truth: she had threatened his life, and he had given her the wine only out of fear.

Her own headache and Amyas’s cold gaze taught her enough. After another failed attempt at drowning herself, the stubborn beauty calmed—at least for a while. And better still, she vowed never again to touch fermented liquor, a vow she kept until her last breath.

Yet Amyas still wrestled with worry. How could he guide a girl who, at the slightest provocation, chose death over discipline? Punish her? He dared not. Send her ashore? He felt a deep conviction—no superstition, but a quiet certainty—that her affection for the English was divinely sent, that God had placed her in his care, and that He would demand an account of her soul.

At his wit’s end, he turned to prayer. Again and again, he begged God to show him what to do—before she destroyed herself or someone else. At last, he decided: let Parson Jack teach her the basics of Christianity. That way, she could be baptized upon their return to England.

But here arose a new problem. She refused Jack outright—any teacher but Amyas himself. And Amyas had good reason to decline such a role. So for weeks, Ayacanora remained untaught, except in English, which she mastered with astonishing speed.

Then came yet another trial. She developed a sharp dislike—not only for John Brimblecombe, whose walk and voice she mimicked for the sailors’ amusement—but also for Will Cary, whom she barred from speaking to her or approaching her. Was she jealous of his closeness to Amyas? Or did she, with a woman’s instinct, sense that he was the only other man aboard bold enough to court her—though, to be fair, Will had no such intention, nor did Amyas.

When questioned, her reply was firm: “Cary is a cacique, like Amyas. There should not be two caciques.” One day, she even suggested Amyas kill his rival and claim the ship for himself. She sulked for days when Amyas laughed and repeated her advice to its intended victim.

Even the black crew suffered. Ayacanora regarded them with deep disgust. At first, she avoided them. Later, she hurled anything within reach—bottles, tools, bits of cloth—until the poor men, fearing for their lives, complained to Amyas. He granted them peace—for a time.

Over the rest of the crew, she ruled like a queen. She called them from their work to run errands, make toys, obey her commands—sometimes enforced with a sharp slap across the ear. Yet they loved her, especially old Yeo. Sailors, true to their nature, doted on her, obeyed her, joked with her—as one might with a tame leopard, knowing its claws could strike at any moment. But she entertained them. And Amyas, too. They needed a pet—what finer one could they have? As for Amyas, her presence, though often trying, kept his mind occupied. Her wildness stirred him, distracted him from the heavy thoughts of meeting his mother, from the terrible tale he must tell her. Without her, those memories might have crushed him entirely, making all his victories and escapes seem meaningless.

At last, events reached a breaking point.

For many days, the ship had sailed steadily before a favorable wind. They’d passed the West Indian islands and now drifted safely through the vast weed-choked waters of the Sargasso Sea. Here, for the first time, they relaxed their strict discipline and began to “rummage” their prize—the spoils of war.

What they found—gold, silver, jewels, goods—mattered little to readers. Suffice it to say, there was enough to make Amyas rich beyond measure, after paying all claims: Cary’s share, the crew’s, Mr. Salterne’s third as owner of the vessel.

In the captain’s cabin, however, were two chests. One contained magnificent Mexican feather garments. The other, Spanish and East Indian finery—likely meant for a senora in Caracas, having passed through Havana and Cartagena.

At Cary’s suggestion, the crew voted these two chests to Ayacanora—her due, in recognition of her Amazonian courage and service.

She seized them greedily, carried them to Lucy’s cabin, and spent hours kneeling among them, gazing with delight. The Mexican clothes she dismissed as crude. But the Spanish finery—those were treasures. For days, she appeared on deck, dazzling in Seville mantillas, Madrid hats, brocade farthingales, and every glittering trifle she could wear.

The men tittered. Amyas felt like crying. Nothing is more pitiful than a child’s vanity—except perhaps a grown person pretending to be childish. And nothing more agonizing than a child’s heartbreak when she realizes she’s been mocked instead of admired.

Amyas wanted to speak—but feared her reaction. Yet the cure came naturally. The spectacle lasted three days. Then, unable to endure the laughter any longer, she turned to Amyas: “Ayacanora quite English girl now—is she not?”

A titter echoed behind her. She looked around, saw a dozen faces laughing openly. In an instant, she understood. She darted down the companion-ladder and disappeared.

Amyas, expecting her to leap overboard, rushed after her. But she had locked herself in with Lucy. He heard her sobbing violently, and Lucy’s faint voice asking what had happened.

He knocked. No answer. She refused to come out all day. By evening, they were forced to break the door open to prevent Lucy from starving.

Inside, Ayacanora sat amid torn clothes scattered on the floor. Her finery lay in ruins. She wept as if her heart would burst. Poor Lucy wept too—half from hunger, half from shared sorrow.

Amyas tried to comfort her. “They’ll never laugh at you again,” he promised.

“But then,” he added hesitantly, “you must not be so… so…”

“So what?” she asked, weeping harder.

“So like a wild girl, Ayacanora.”

Her hands fell to her knees. A shudder ran through her chest. She dropped to her knees before him, looking up with pleading eyes.

“Yes—wild girl. Poor, bad wild girl. But I will be English girl now!”

“Fine clothes won’t make you English, my child,” said Amyas.

“No—English clothes. English heart! Good heart, like yours! Yes—I will be good. Sir John shall teach me!”

“There’s my good maid,” said Amyas. “Sir John shall begin tomorrow.”

“No! Now! Now! Ayacanora cannot wait. She will drown herself if she is bad another day! Come, now!”

She made him fetch Brimblecombe. The honest parson spoke patiently for over an hour. That very night, Ayacanora told Lucy everything he had said.

From then on, whenever Jack went to read and pray with Lucy, Ayacanora stayed—no longer fleeing to the deck. She stood quietly, trying to understand. She knelt when he knelt. She tried to pray—so she might have an English heart. And though her prayers were silent, they were not unheard.

Days passed, hopeful and peaceful. Then, one morning, shortly after they left the Sargasso Sea, the ship sailed on quietly. The men worked under the awning—repairing, chatting, tinkering. Brimble7combe catechized Ayacanora in the cabin. Amyas and Cary, cigars in hand, discussed plans for future adventures against the Spaniards. Amyas poured out his bitter hatred—his desire for revenge for his brother and Rose—a darkness settled deep in his soul.

Suddenly, a commotion below. A scuffle. A shout. Both men leapt to their feet.

Jack Brimblecombe burst onto deck, clutching his head.

“Save me! Save me from that she-fiend! She’s possessed by a legion! She broke my nose—ripped out half my hair! I’ve none to spare! Here she comes! Stand by me, gentlemen! Satanas, I defy thee!”

He hid behind Amyas and Cary as Ayacanora stormed up, wild-eyed, like a Maenad. Seeing Amyas, she stopped.

“If you had defied Satan down below,” Cary laughed, “I suspect he wouldn’t have attacked you so boldly, Master Jack.”

“I am innocent!” Jack cried. “As pure as the babe unborn! Oh, Mr. Cary! This is too much!”

“What happened?” Amyas asked.

“He looked at me,” she declared, fiercely.

“Well, a cat may look at a king.”

“But he sha’n’t look at Ayacanora. Only you—no one else—or I’ll kill him!”

Jack protested he hadn’t even glanced at her. But the idea—whether real or imagined—had taken root. She refused to return below. Jack left, grumbling, hairless and sporting a black eye for a week.

“At least,” Cary said, relighting his cigar, “it’s a fault on the right side.”

“God grant me grace,” Amyas replied, “or it may be on the wrong side for me.”

“He will, old heart-of-oak,” Cary said, putting his arm around Amyas’s neck—to Ayacanora’s clear annoyance. She walked to the rail, pulled out a fishing line, and amused herself watching flying fish dart past bonitos.

Eventually, bored, she moved forward to listen to John Squire, the armorer, who sat mending a helmet and humming a tune—altered, of course—about his homeland:

“Oh, Bideford is a pleasant place, it shines where it stands,
And the more I look upon it, the more my heart it warms;
For there are fair young lasses, in rows upon the quay,
To welcome gallant mariners, when they come home from say.”

“It’s Sunderland, John Squire,” said his mate. “Not Bidevor.”

“Well, Bidevor’s as good as Sunderland any day,” Squire replied. “No say-coals to blacken the place, but just as fine harmonies, Tommy Hamblyn—”

“Oh, if I was a herring, to swim the ocean o’er,
Or if I was a say-dove, to fly unto the shoor,
To fly unto my true love, a waiting at the door,
To wed her with a gold ring, and plough the main no moor.”

Then Salvation Yeo interrupted:

“Aren’t you ashamed, John Squire, to your years, singing such carnal vanities after all the providences you’ve seen? Let the songs of Zion be in your mouth, man, if you must caterwaul all day!”

“You sing ‘em yourself then, gunner.”

“Well,” said Yeo, “and why not?” And he pulled out his psalm-book and began:

“Such as in ships and brittle barks
Into the seas descend,
Their merchandise through fearful floods
To compass and to end;
There men are forced to behold
The Lord’s works what they be;
And in the dreadful deep the same,
Most marvellous they see.”

“Humph!” said Squire. “Very good and godly. But still I du like a merry catch now and then. Wouldn’t you let a body sing ‘Rumbelow’—even when he’s heaving the anchor?”

“Well, I don’t know,” said Yeo. “But the Lord’s people had better praise the Lord then too, and pray for a good voyage, instead of howling about—”

“A randy, dandy, dandy O,
A whet of ale and brandy O,
With a rumbelow and a Westward-ho!
And heave, my mariners all, O!”

“Is that fit talk for immortal souls?” Yeo demanded. “How does that child’s-trade sound beside the Psalms, John Squire?”

It was precisely this moment that sealed the discovery.

For Salvation Yeo, wishing to mock the old song, had sung it with its full nasal twang—just as he had done twelve years earlier, hoisting John Oxenham’s anchor in Plymouth Sound.

And Ayacanora, standing beside Amyas, listening to the men, suddenly started. Then, half to herself, she sang the tune back—word for word, note for note, in the very same voice.

Yeo froze. His face turned pale.

“Who sang that?” he asked sharply.

“The little maid here,” Amyas said. “She’s learning English well.”

“The little maid?” Yeo whispered, growing paler still. “Why do you scare an old servant by talking of little maids, Captain Amyas? Well…” he muttered to himself, “as I am a sinful saint, if I hadn’t seen where the voice came from, I could have sworn it was her—just as we taught her by the river, I and William Penberthy of Marazion, my dear comrade. The Lord have mercy on me!”

All grew silent when Yeo spoke of that lost child. Ayacanora, pleased by Amyas’s praise, hummed softly:

“And heave, my mariners all, O!”

Yeo jumped up from his gun.

“I can’t bear it! As I live, I can’t! You, Indian maiden—where did you learn that?”

Ayacanora looked up, startled by his intensity, then at Amyas to see if she had done wrong. Then she turned away, saucy, and hummed on.

“Ask her, for mercy’s sake—ask her, Captain Leigh!”

“My child,” Amyas said, switching to Indian speech, “how is it you sing that so perfectly? Have you ever heard it before?”

Ayacanora looked puzzled, shook her head.

“If you speak Indian to Ayacanora, she is dumb. She must be English girl now, like poor Lucy.”

“Well then,” Amyas said, “do you remember—Ayacanora—anything from when you were a little girl?”

She paused. Then raised her hands above her head.

“Trees—great trees like the Magdalena—always nothing but trees—wild and bad everything. Ayacanora won’t talk about that.”

“Do you remember anything that grew on those trees?” Yeo asked eagerly.

She laughed. “Silly! Flowers and fruit, nuts—monkey-cups too. Ayacanora climbed up after them—when she was wild. I won’t tell any more.”

“But who taught you to call them monkey-cups?” Yeo asked, trembling.

“Monkey’s drink. Mono drink.”

“Mono?” Yeo asked, testing another clue. “How did you know beasts were called monos?”

“She might have heard it coming down with us,” Cary said, joining them.

“Ay, monos,” she replied, defensively. “Faces like little men, tails. And one very dirty black one, with a beard, says Amen in a tree to all the other monkeys—just like Sir John on Sunday.”

This brought laughter from all but Yeo.

“But don’t you remember any Christians? White people?”

She was silent.

“Don’t you remember a white lady?”

“Um?”

“A woman—very pretty—with hair like his?” pointing to Amyas.

“No.”

“What do you remember, besides Indians?” Yeo asked, desperate.

She turned her back, annoyed.

“Try again,” Amyas urged. She focused.

“Ayacanora remembers great monkeys—black, oh, so high,” she said, raising her hand above her head, then making a gesture of disgust.

“Monkeys? With tails?”

“No—like man. Ah! Yes—just like Cooky there—dirty Cooky!”

The unfortunate son of Ham, passing nearby, narrowly escaped a marlingspike thrown by Ayacanora.

“Ayacanora,” Amyas said, “if you throw anything else at Cooky, I must have you whipped.”

“I’ll kill you then,” she answered, flatly.

“She means negurs,” Yeo muttered. “Where could she have seen them? Could it be the Cimaroons?”

“But why would someone who saw whites forget them, yet remember negroes?” Cary asked.

“Let us try again,” Amyas said. “Do you remember any great monkeys but those black ones?”

“Yes,” she said slowly. “Devil.”

“Devil?” all three asked—knowing well that Indians believed the devil appeared to their shamans.

“Ay—him Sir John tells about on Sundays.”

“Save and help us!” Yeo gasped. “And what was he like?”

She made signs—a monkey’s face, a gray beard like Yeo’s. Then came gestures around her neck—confusing them all.

“I know!” Cary burst out, laughing. “Sir Urian had a ruff! Trunk-hose too, my fair dame? Wait—let me test. Was his neck like the senor commandant’s, the Spaniard?”

Ayacanora clapped her hands—understood!

“The ‘devil’ was a monkey, with a gray beard, wearing a ruff—humph!”

“Ay!” she said, in fluent Spanish. “Mono de Panama; viejo diablo de Panama.”

Yeo threw up his hands. “Oh Lord of all mercies! Those were the last words of Mr. John Oxenham! Ay—and the devil is surely none other than Don Francisco Xararte! Oh dear! oh dear! my sweet young lady! My pretty little maid! Don’t you know me? Don’t you know Salvation Yeo, who carried you over the mountains, who climbed for monkey-cups for you, my dear? And William Penberthy, who picked flowers for you? And your poor father—just like Mr. Cary there, only he had a black beard, black curls, and swore terribly, like a Spaniard—my dear young lady?”

And the old man fell to his knees, kissing her hands.

The crew, thinking him mad, crowded aft.

“Steady, men—don’t vex him!” Amyas said. “He thinks he’s found his little maid.”

“And so do I, Amyas,” Cary said.

“Steady, steady, my masters! If this turns out false, his wits will crack. Mr. Yeo—can’t you think of any other token?”

Yeo stamped impatiently. “What need? It’s her! I tell you—it’s her! What a beauty she’s grown! Oh dear! Where were my eyes all this time? Beholding her, and not seeing her? ‘Tis her very mortal self! And don’t you remember me, my dear? Salvation Yeo, who taught you to sing ‘Heave my mariners all, O!’ sitting on a log by the boat on the sand—where red lilies grew in the moss, dear, wasn’t there? And we made posies to put in your hair?”

The old man spoke on, pleading, suggesting, as if he could coax her into being the child he had lost.

Ayacanora watched him—first angry, then amused, then attentive, then utterly absorbed.

Then—suddenly—she flushed crimson. Snatching her hands from his, she covered her face.

“Do you remember any of this, my child?” Amyas asked gently.

“It fell about the Martinmas, When nights were lang and mirk,
That wife's twa sons cam hame again, And their hats were o' the birk.
It did na graw by bush or brae, Nor yet in ony shough;
But by the gates o' paradise That birk grew fair eneugh.”

—The Wife of Usher’s Well.

It was the evening of February 15th, 1587, and Mrs. Leigh—once more drawn back to the familiar scenes and faces of her past—was walking slowly along the terrace at Burrough, her eyes fixed upon the winding river, the misty sandhills, and the vast expanse of the western sea. She had done this every evening for three long years, come rain or shine, without fail. Three years had passed since Frank and Amyas sailed away aboard the *Rose*, a gallant ship carrying brave souls into the unknown. No word had ever reached them from across the ocean. In Bideford, Appledore, Clovelly, and Ilfracombe, families waited with hollowed eyes and broken hearts, just as they had when John Oxenham vanished into the same uncharted West. Like him, the brothers had disappeared into the infinite, leaving only silence behind.

Once, there had been hope. When news came that Drake, Frobisher, and Carlisle had returned from the Spanish Main, Sir Richard—without telling her—sent a rider galloping to Plymouth. He returned with thrilling tales: the sack of Cartagena, the fall of San Domingo, the relief of Raleigh’s colony in Virginia. But no mention of the *Rose*. No trace of her crew. Mrs. Leigh bowed her head in quiet acceptance and whispered, “The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord.”

Her hair had turned silver. Her face was pale, her steps slow. She rarely left home except for church or visits to neighbors. She never spoke the names of her sons. Yet each high tide brought her to the terrace, where she scanned the horizon with desperate longing. The ships still came and went, sails white against the sky, but an instinct deeper than sight told her none of them carried the one she sought. She knew the *Rose*’s shape, her lines, her build—she could have picked her out from a fleet at sea. But she never came. And so, day after day, Mrs. Leigh walked among the poor, who looked upon her with reverence, as one touched by sorrow too profound for words. They saw in her eyes a strength born of fire, a soul that had walked through the furnace and emerged unscathed, standing beside a presence like the Son of God. She herself blamed her own heart for clinging to earthly hopes, confessing each night her weakness, yet always returning to the terrace at high tide beneath the wind-swept trees.

But tonight, Northam was alive.

The pebble ridge thundered below, as it had decades before. Yet the noise came not from the sea, but from the town itself. The bell tower shook with pealing chimes. People poured into the streets, shouting, singing, dancing around bonfires. The pope burned in effigy. Toasts were raised to the queen. “So perish all her enemies!” echoed through the air. Hills glowed red with flames; far off, the bells of Bideford answered those of Northam, just as they had seven years ago when Amyas returned from circumnavigating the globe.

For today, Mary Queen of Scots had been beheaded at Fotheringay. England, as if waking from a nightmare, roared in jubilation. Seventeen years of fear, of plots, of treason—gone at last. The woman who had sown discord, whose blood had stained noble English lives, whose influence had led men to rebellion and murder, was now silenced forever. Elizabeth’s prophecy had come true:

“The daughter of debate,
That discord aye doth sow,
Hath reap’d no gain where former rule
Hath taught still peace to grow.”

And now, she could do no harm. Murder, adultery, a heart that knew no mercy, a tongue that could not speak truth—even for self—had ended. Her face lingered in memory, not as a tyrant, but as a tragic figure, a pitiful dream. “Nothing is left of her now,” some said, “but pure womanly.”

Mrs. Leigh, though a staunch Protestant, offered a prayer: may God have mercy on that soul—“clear as diamond, and as hard,” as she once described herself. She thought of the final moments before the block—could it all have been performance? In seven years, she had learned much. Perhaps Mary Stuart, too, had learned something in seventeen. Mrs. Leigh had been a courtier once, and she knew—though only faintly, as a proper Englishwoman might—the debauchery of French courts, the world of Medicis, Guises, Cardinal Lorraine. She shuddered. “To whom little is given, of them shall little be required.” But still, the bells rang on, relentless.

Then—what was that?

A flash in the twilight, then the thunder of a cannon from the sea.

Mrs. Leigh froze. The flash came from beyond the bar. A ship in distress? Unlikely. The wind was light and westerly. It was a spring tide, high and strong. What could it be? Another flash. Another gun. The crowd fell silent. All rushed to the churchyard overlooking the flats and river.

There, outside the bar, was a large ship—gallant, bold, sails full. Not English rig. A thousand tons, perhaps. But what did it mean? A Spanish cruiser? Not likely. The Don would not risk such reckless action. If he came, he would come with fleet, admirals, transports—by the book. This was different.

Easily, on the rising tide and favorable wind, the vessel slipped up the channel between the sandhills. By now, she was near Appledore. She showed no sign of lowering her topsails—a grave breach of custom for any foreign ship near English shores. For many knew the tale of the Spanish admiral who once sailed into Plymouth Sound without veiling his sails. John Hawkins, port admiral, had risen like a lion from his den, in his famed *Jesus of Lubec*, and fired a shot between the masts. When that failed, he ran alongside and sent a second shot straight through the admiral’s hull. The flag came down. Apologies followed—but not accepted for long. Hawkins defended the queen’s honor with iron resolve.

And if Hawkins could do that in peace, then any old sea-dog in Appledore would do the same in war—with even a pot to burn powder in.

The strange ship vanished behind Appledore hill. Then, from the quiet dusk, came a cheer—like a hundred voices rising together.

Mrs. Leigh stood still. Listened. Then another cannon thundered from the hills. Another cheer.

Twenty minutes passed. Then the ship reappeared around the dark rocks of the Hubbastone, turning up the Bideford river.

On board, music played—drums, fifes, shawms, trumpets—echoing off the woods and slate cliffs. As the ship rounded the bend and came fully into view of Burrough House, another cheer erupted from the crew, rolling across the silver water, a mile away.

Mrs. Leigh moved swiftly toward the house.

“Grace, bring me my hood. Master Amyas is come home!”

“No, surely? O joyful sound! Praised be the Lord!” Grace gasped. “But madam, how did you know?”

“I heard his voice on the river. But I did not hear Mr. Frank’s with him, Grace.”

“Oh, be sure, madam, where one is, the other is. They’d never part. Both come home—or neither. Here’s your hood.”

Mrs. Leigh took it, and with Grace following, hurried toward Bideford.

Was it real? A dream? Had her mother’s intuition truly recognized her son’s voice across the miles, or was her mind breaking under years of grief?

Grace wondered the same as they walked. When they reached the quay, the answer came.

Coming down Bridgeland Street—where later tobacco warehouses would stand, but now only rope-walks and sailmakers’ shops—they saw the ship anchored in the river. Just as they reached the lower end of the street, a great crowd surged around the corner—sailors, women, apprentices, laughing, weeping, shouting.

Mrs. Leigh stopped. And the crowd stopped too.

“Here she is!” someone shouted. “Here’s his mother!”

“His mother? Not *their* mother?” Mrs. Leigh whispered, turning pale. But her heart had long since hardened to pain.

Then, above the throng, rose the giant head and shoulders of Amyas.

“Make way! Make room for Madam Leigh!” he called.

And he fell to his knees at her feet.

She threw her arms around his neck, bending her head over him, while sailors, apprentices, and harbor women fell into hushed silence, forming a ring around mother and son.

She asked no questions. She saw Amyas was alone.

At last, he whispered, “I would have died to save him, mother, if I could.”

“You need not tell me that, Amyas Leigh, my son.”

Silence.

“How did he die?” she murmured.

“He is a martyr. He died in the—”

Amyas could say no more.

“The Inquisition?”

“Yes.”

A shudder ran through Mrs. Leigh. Then she lifted her head.

“Come home, Amyas. I little expected such an honor—such an honor—ha! ha!—and such a fair young martyr, too. A very St. Stephen! God, have mercy on me—and let me not go mad before these people, when I ought to be thanking Thee for Thy mercies! Amyas, who is that?”

She pointed to Ayacanora, standing close behind him, watching everything with sharp eyes.

“She is a poor wild Indian girl—I call her my daughter. I will tell you her story later.”

“Your daughter? Then my granddaughter. Come here, maiden, and be my granddaughter.”

Ayacanora stepped forward, knelt—because she had seen Amyas kneel.

“God forbid, child! Kneel not to me. Come home, and let me know whether I am sane or mazed, alive or dead.”

Drawing her hood over her face, she turned, holding Amyas’s hand in one, Ayacanora’s in the other.

The crowd watched in silence for twenty yards, then burst into cheers that shook the old town.

Mrs. Leigh paused.

“I had forgotten, Amyas. You must not let me stand in the way of your duty. Where are your men?”

“Kissed to death by now—all who are left.”

“Left?”

“We went out a hundred, mother. We came home forty-four—if we are home. Is this a dream? Is this you? Is this Bridgeland Street again? There stands Evans the smith, tankard in hand—just as he did when I was a boy!”

The brawny smith crossed the street, stopped when he saw Amyas, but no Frank.

“Better one than neither, madam,” he said, trying to comfort.

Amyas nodded, shook his hand. Mrs. Leigh saw nothing.

“Mother,” Amyas said, past the causeway, “we are rich for life.”

“Yes,” she replied. “A martyr’s death was the fittest for him.”

“I have brought home treasure untold.”

“What, my boy?”

“Treasure untold. Cary has promised to see to it tonight.”

“Very well. I wish he had slept at our house. He was a kindly lad, and loved Frank. When did he—?”

“Three years ago, and more. Within two months of our sailing.”

“Ah. Yes. He told me so.”

“Told you so?”

“Yes. The dear lad has often come to see me in my sleep. But you never came. I guessed why—as it should be.”

“But I loved you none the less, mother!”

“I know. But you were busy with the men, sweet. Your spirit could not roam home like his, which was free. Yes—just as it should be. My maid—do you not find it cold here, after those hot lands?”

“Ayacanora’s heart is warm. She does not think about cold.”

“Warm? Perhaps you’ll warm my heart, then.”

“Would God I could, mother,” Amyas said, half reproachfully.

Mrs. Leigh looked up at him—then broke into tears.

“Sinful! Sinful that I am!”

“Blessed creature!” Amyas cried. “If you speak so, I shall go mad. Mother, mother—I’ve dreaded this meeting for months. It was a nightmare, a black thundercloud hanging over me. A cliff miles high, hidden in clouds, that I had to climb, and dared not. I longed to leap overboard and flee into the depths of the sea. I feared you might ask—‘Was he not your brother’s keeper?’ Might demand his blood at my hands. And now—now—when it comes—to find you full of love, trust, patience—mother, mother—it’s more than I can bear!” And he wept.

Mrs. Leigh, knowing Amyas’s nature, understood that such emotion meant a deep struggle within. And instantly, she forgot everything else—only wanting to soothe him.

She did. And so the two walked home, arm in arm, Ayacanora clinging to the hem of Mrs. Leigh’s cloak. The fierce independence of the forest girl had melted into timid modesty, suddenly cast into a new world of strangers, fears, and wonders.

“Will your mother love me?” Ayacanora whispered as they entered.

“Yes. But you must do what she tells you.”

Ayacanora pouted.

“She will laugh at me, because I am wild.”

“She never laughs at anyone.”

“Humph!” she muttered. “Well, I won’t be afraid of her. I thought she’d be tall like you—but she’s not even as big as me.”

Not promising obedience, but within twenty-four hours, Mrs. Leigh had won her completely. Ayacanora explained later: “I thought such a great man must have a great mother. I expected a princess with a frown like Juno’s—but found an angel.”

Her story was soon told. Mrs. Leigh, naturally, believed her without question. And the mother pressed no kiss more tender upon her son’s brow than the one she gave in thanks for his vow: that he had kept his soul unspotted—gentleman and Christian alike.

“Then you have forgiven me, mother?”

“Years ago, I said in this very room, ‘What shall I render to the Lord for giving me two such sons?’ And now, I say it again. Tell me all about my other son, that I may honor him as I honor you.”

With quiet strength, she made Amyas recount every detail—Lucy Passmore’s tale, the voyage, the events leading to the fatal night at Guayra. When it was done, she led Ayacanora away and began arranging for her comfort, as calmly as if both boys had merely napped in their cribs.

But barely had she gone upstairs when a loud knock shattered the quiet. The door flew open, and Sir Richard Grenville strode in, ignoring all formality.

Amyas dropped to his knees instinctively. The stern warrior was undone. A tear fell from his iron cheek onto Amyas’s.

“My lad! My glorious lad! Where have you been? Up, and tell me all. The sailors told me a little—but I must hear every word. I knew you’d do something grand. I told your mother—you were too good a workman for God to throw away. Now—tell me the whole. Why, I’m out of breath! To tell truth, I ran three-quarters of the way.”

They sat. Amyas spoke long into the night. Sir Richard, regaining his composure, smiled grimly at each act of courage. When the tale ended, he sighed.

“Would that I had been with you every step. Would that I could show as good a three-year log-book, Amyas, my lad.”

“You can show a better one, I doubt not.”

“Humph. With the exception of one paltry Spanish prize, I don’t know that the queen is the better, nor her enemies the worse, since we parted in Dublin.”

“You are too modest, sir.”

“Would that I were. I got nowhere in Ireland—no better than my neighbors. So I came home to find Raleigh had done a deed with no end. He has found—well, his captains Amadas and Barlow found—between Florida and Newfoundland, a land unlike any on earth. Climate, fertility—unmatched. Gold? I know not. But everything else: furs, timber, rivers, game, sugar-canes, corn, fruit—everything France, Spain, Italy can yield, growing wild. Savages civil enough. A jewel fit for the crown. They call it Wingandacoa. We call it—Virginia.”

“You’ve been there?”

“The year before last. Left Ralf Lane, Amadas, twenty gentlemen, ninety men, and some of my own money, and some of old Will Salterne’s. Never to see it again. The colony quarreled with the Indians—I fear I was too harsh with some who stole—God forgive me!—and couldn’t survive twelve months. Drake, coming back from the West Indies, helped them as best he could, but had to bring them all home. And within two weeks, I was back with three ships, supplies, everything. Found only ruins—already overrun with melons, deer feeding in the huts. A fine sight—but not what I wanted. So I returned, angry, and vented my rage on the Portugals at the Azores. Hard fights, small gain. So things stand—but not for long. Shame it would be if such a paradise, found by Britons, fell to others. We’ll try again this spring—if men and money can be found. Eh, lad?”

“But the prize?”

“Ah—that was no small comfort. Sighted her six days out from America. But dead calm. No boats—lost in a gale. Other ships becalmed two leagues behind. No use pounding her until she sank. So I called the carpenter, gathered old chests, spars—we floated alongside, just in time. The last of us scrambled up as our makeshift ark sank beside us. Could not run away. So I judged valor the better part of discretion—and attacked. Five minutes, and we had her. Fifty thousand pounds’ worth. Restored my purse—and Raleigh’s, though I fear it’s spent again.”

And so ended Sir Richard’s tale.

The next day, Amyas visited Salterne. The old man already knew the outline. But he bid him sit, chin on hand, elbows on knees, listening silently. His face never changed. Only when Amyas mentioned Rose’s marriage did he breathe deeply—like a weight lifted.

“Say that again, sir.”

Amyas repeated it, then hesitated, hinting at her death.

“Go on. Why are you afraid? Nothing to be ashamed of?”

He told the whole, eyes downcast. Then glanced up. No emotion—only a proud smile at the corners of Salterne’s mouth.

“And her husband?” he asked.

“I am ashamed to tell you—he still lives.”

“Still lives?”

“Too true. It was not my fault. My story bears witness.”

“Sir, I never doubted your will to kill him. Still lives? Well, so do rats and adders. And now, Captain Leigh—your worship is minded to recruit on shore with the fair lass you’ve brought home, before another dash at the devil?”

“Do not mention her name with mine, sir. She is no more to me than to you. Spanish blood flows in her veins.”

Salterne smiled grimly.

“But I am minded to do one thing, Mr. Salterne. I will kill Spaniards—fair fight, by land and sea—wheresoever I meet them. And so, I stay not long here. Whithersoever I go next, I go.”

“The daughter of debate,
That discord still doth sow,
Shall reap no gain where former rule
Hath taught still peace to grow.
No foreign banish'd wight
Shall anker in this port;
Our realm it brooks no stranger's force;
Let them elsewhere resort.”

Amyas Leigh had returned home and settled into a quiet life, content for the time being with the peace of his hearth and the company of those he loved. For the next twelve months, little stirred in Northam that merited record—save the slow unfolding of a young soul caught between two worlds.

Yeo, once a rough sea-dog, now served as major domo in name only, though his presence was constant and watchful. He followed Amyas like a silent shadow, a lank gray hound at his heels. In the evenings, he sat by the fire, Bible open on his knee, hands busy fashioning trinkets—useful and useless alike—for every member of the household. Most often, they were for Ayacanora, whom he treated with a mixture of reverence and gentle mockery, offering her toys too childish for her dignity. She would pout, Mrs. Leigh would chide, and yet she would take the gift, tuck it away, and never look at it again. Her heart was not in playthings, but in becoming English.

She ran after Mrs. Leigh all day long, eager to learn the secrets of the kitchen, the still-room, the art of sewing—not just for herself, but for everyone in Northam. First, she wanted to be a good housewife, like Mrs. Leigh. Then came a deeper idea: helping others. The boundless generosity of her own people had been part of her nature, but giving without expectation of return—this was new. She watched Mrs. Leigh spend her free hours mending clothes, visiting the poor, bringing food and comfort to those who had nothing. She saw Amyas, after a public thanksgiving in church for his safe return, distribute money, grain, and provisions across Northam, Appledore, and Bideford. He bought cottages and turned them into almshouses for aged sailors, calling it a thank-offering to God.

Ayacanora was puzzled. Her idea of sacrifice had been shaped by the Aztec rites—blood and bones offered to the gods. But when Mrs. Leigh explained gently that “whoever gives to the poor, gives to the Great Spirit,” because the Great Spirit dwelled in them, Ayacanora listened. And she believed. From that moment, she worked tirelessly for the old women of Northam, sewed for the needy, walked beside Mrs. Leigh with a quiet grace that made her beloved by all. Sometimes, beneath her long lashes, she would glance toward Amyas, hoping to catch a smile. When she did, she was sweet and obedient all day. When she didn’t, she became stubborn, willful, and hard to manage—even for Mrs. Leigh’s patient spirit.

But one thing she could not control: her dress. No amount of pleading could keep her from sweeping into church each Sunday in dazzling Spanish finery—feathers, jewels, silks—so bright and loud that the vicar himself had to speak to Mrs. Leigh about the distraction. Ayacanora replied with fiery pride: “I am not thinking of them, nor should they think of me.” If the Piache—the conjuror, as she called the priest—wanted a present, she said, he might have her Mexican feather dresses. They were wild Indian things, and she was now an English maid. So she darted upstairs, brought down the garments, and insisted on dressing the vicar in them. He fled, but not before taking one of the most magnificent mantles. Instead of selling it, he transformed it into an altar cloth. For years afterward, the communion at Northam was celebrated upon a blaze of emerald, azure, and crimson—once worn by an Aztec prince.

So Ayacanora lived—brilliant, impulsive, half-child, half-queen—while Amyas watched her with amusement and pride. He looked around at the villagers, as if to say, *See what a fine bird I have brought home.*

Another source of trouble was her treatment of the ladies of the neighborhood. They came to visit, drawn by curiosity and admiration—some to congratulate Mrs. Leigh, others to glimpse the "fair savage." But Ayacanora snubbed them all, from the vicar’s wife to Lady Grenville herself. She refused their smiles, their invitations, their kindnesses. Mrs. Leigh remonstrated, and Ayacanora wept: “They come only to stare at a poor wild girl. I was once a queen. Everyone obeyed me. Here, everyone looks down on me.” When asked if she would rather return to the forests, she clung to Mrs. Leigh like a child, begging not to be sent away. “I’d rather be a slave in this kitchen than go back to those bad people.”

Month after month passed—storms and sunsets in equal measure. But always under the calm influence of Mrs. Leigh, whose steady grace tempered the wildness within Ayacanora. The outbursts grew fewer, the moods more even. Though she remained restless, she was growing wiser.

And slowly, details emerged that confirmed her identity—though not to the world, only to those who knew her best. After seeing houses, she remarked that she had seen such before. The red cattle seemed familiar. Sheep confused her at first, until she said, “Too small.” Yeo seized on this: “Ah, madam, she must have known the great camel-sheep—llamas—in Peru.”

But her true delight was horses. The idea of riding one thrilled her beyond words. She begged Amyas for one, but he refused—fearing for her safety. Then she whispered to Yeo about stealing one. When that failed, she went to the vicar and offered all her finery for his broken-kneed pony. The vicar refused, and so Amyas bought her a jennet. Within two weeks, she rode like a Gaucho.

Then came a dream—one night at Lady Grenville’s estate, where the family had gone to visit. Ayacanora slept in the same room as Mrs. Leigh. Suddenly she screamed, then sobbed out a story: the “Old Ape of Panama” had come to her bed, dragged her into the courtyard, and she had mounted a horse and ridden with an Indian across moors and mountains into a dark wood. There, the Indian and the horses vanished—and she woke, transformed again into a small savage child.

The impression was so vivid she could not be convinced it hadn’t happened. Mrs. Leigh, moved, told the company the next morning that God had revealed Ayacanora’s past in a vision: she had been exposed in the forests by her jealous stepfather, Don Xararte, who may have believed her to be Oxenham’s child. He had taken vengeance by abandoning her to die. Yeo added that he had seen Don Xararte aboard the Lima ship at Guayaquil, near Quito—a fact that helped explain how she ended up beyond Cotopaxi, in the Eastern Andes.

More clues surfaced—each tied to animals or natural things. Her memories were fragmented, emotional, tied to sensory impressions. She could not recall her mother, nor any early religious teaching. That changed one day.

Sir Richard Grenville had brought home an Indian from Virginia—possibly Wanchese, mentioned alongside Manteo. This man, named Raleigh, was baptized in Bideford Church, sponsored by Raleigh himself and Sir Richard. The whole town came to witness: mayor, corporation, common folk—all to see the first “Red man” to set foot on British soil receive the Gospel.

Mrs. Leigh attended, and Ayacanora insisted on going too. She was curious about the “Carib.” “Why don’t they make him a Christian?” she asked. “Why not me?”

Because, they told her, she already was. They were certain she had been baptized at birth. But she wasn’t sure. She pouted, resentful that a “ugly red Carib” might be spiritually ahead of her.

At the ceremony, as the service progressed, Ayacanora suddenly gasped, then fainted into Mrs. Leigh’s arms. When she recovered, she told another dream-like memory: the church had grown immense, the priest’s robes richer. The walls were covered with paintings. Above the altar stood a woman in jeweled robes, holding a child. Soft music filled the air. The scent—sweet, heavy, like incense—drifted through the church like mist. She saw not one, but many Indians at the font. A lady held her hand, and she was a little girl again.

Yeo, listening closely, declared: “She is christened, madam—if Popish baptism counts. And she has seen Indians baptized in the Cathedral of Quito. I know that church well. I sat there three hours in a San Benito, listening to a friar preach lies, wondering if I’d burn the next day.”

Thus, Ayacanora returned to Burrough. Raleigh the Indian went to Sir Richard’s house. His baptism is recorded in the Bideford register, written in crooked letters:

“Raleigh, a Winganditoian: March 26, 1587.”

A year later, another entry appears:

“Rawly, a Winganditoian: April 1589.”

But not under baptisms. The forest-born man had missed his homeland—its cedar woods, its lagoons, its magnolias and vines. He died, longing for the old ways. Now he rests in the narrow churchyard of Bideford, buried beneath dingy houses where no one remembers him. Yet the two entries remain—silent witnesses, tragic symbols of the fate of the Native peoples: civilization came too late to save them, but not too late to destroy them.

Meanwhile, though Amyas stayed idle, England stirred. That spring, Raleigh sent another colony to Virginia, led by John White. He had urged Amyas to command it—had he done so, perhaps the United States might have begun twenty years earlier. But Amyas’s mother had sworn him to stay home for at least a year. So instead of leading the fleet, he sent five hundred pounds—money that likely remains in Virginia, lost to history, never to return.

But soon, a deeper trial came. Amyas began to feel restless, bitter. Others fought Spaniards abroad, while he sat idle. His hatred for Don Guzman grew fierce. He was losing his “single eye”—his inner light. Darkness crept over him, the darkness of hatred. And Yeo, ever more fanatical, fed it. Together, they debated whether they had sinned by sparing the Spanish prisoners on the galleon.

Yet the truth must be said: if Englishmen harbored such rage, Spain had earned it. The Spanish were planning more aggression.

We now approach the year 1588—foretold by an astronomer of Konigsberg a century before as “admirable,” and by German chronologers as the climacterical year of the world.

Prophecies may be myths, but events fulfilled them. That year decided the fate of Europe and America.

Some sensed it—a dread feeling that the Day of the Lord was near, that angels and demons were locked in battle for the souls of men. Astrologers dreamed of planetary alignments signaling doom.

But wise men needed no stars to know the hour had come. The long, scattered war between Spain and England would end in one final clash. Until then, conflict had been limited to the Netherlands, the West Indies, Africa—places Spain claimed unjustly. But Spain itself had remained untouched. Trade continued freely—until 1585, when Spain suddenly imposed an embargo on all English ships arriving on its shores. They were to be seized, used to build a vast armada.

Spain claimed it was to crush the rebellious Dutch. But England suspected otherwise. The queen, her ministers, and the people saw it clearly: England was the real target. So instead of negotiating, they fought to force negotiation.

Drake, Frobisher, Carlisle swept the Spanish Main, cutting off Indian supplies. Walsingham, cunning yet honest, blocked Venetian loans that might have saved Spain. No Armada came that year.

Yet the Jesuits, both here and abroad, spoke openly—among their followers—of the true purpose: to destroy the “impious heretics”—the Drakes, Raleighs, Grenvilles, Cavendishes, Hawkinses, Frobishers—who had dared invade the pope’s domain. Their barks would sink beneath Spanish cannon blessed by Rome. England would fall. The proud islanders, who rejected St. Peter and Mary, would kneel again. Elizabeth, illegitimate, excommunicate, would be chained and sent to Rome. Spain would reclaim the throne.

“What nobler cause? What greater glory? Spain was invincible. Heaven itself called her to fulfill her mission. The saints called for a new crusade. The Virgin Mary, wronged and dishonored, demanded justice. Let all valiant knights rise for her!”

So said Parsons, Allen, and others. But their audience was unmoved. The English Roman Catholics, though loyal to Rome, could not betray their country. They preferred survival to martyrdom.

The Jesuits turned to another queen: Mary, Queen of Scots. She was Catholic, beautiful, unfortunate—perfect for their cause. They played her card till it tore. She plotted against Elizabeth, aided by foreign powers, and sought to overthrow the government. Her beauty and charm won hearts, but her schemes doomed her.

And so the people of England answered with one voice—from noble to peasant: “Seventeen years of terror are enough. This woman must die—or England dies.”

We know which choice was made.

And so the stage was set. The fleet that would carry Virginia’s future was halted—not by storm, but by royal command. The queen had spoken. The age of exploration paused, awaiting a greater reckoning.

“Oh, where be these gay Spaniards,
Which make so great a boast O?
Oh, they shall eat the gray-goose feather,
And we shall eat the roast O!”

—Cornish Song.

Imagine, if you will, that those who this summer stood with pride upon the shores of Plymouth—gazing at its grand breakwater, its bustling docks, its towering arsenals, and its mighty screw-driven steamers gliding effortlessly through the crowded harbor—were suddenly transported back three hundred years, to the golden summer of 1588. The modern world vanished in an instant: no railroads, no telegraphs, no engines of steel and fire. Instead, they found themselves in a time when ships were sails and men were made of iron.

Mount Edgecombe still rose above the sea, as green and fair as ever. But where were the vast dry-docks of Devonport? Where the naval yards that now stretch along the shore? Gone. Drake’s Island held only a modest battery, newly built by the man whose name it bore. Mount Wise was little more than a gentleman’s estate surrounded by fields. The citadel, once a fortress, was now a paltry fort, barely strong enough to withstand a cannonade from a single modern steamer. And the ships? The great floating castles of the Hamoaze had shrunk to mere lime-hoys crawling across the water. The Catwater, once filled with merchant vessels, now teemed with warships—ships that would soon set sail into the greatest naval battle the world had yet known.

They lay there, humble in the eyes of today’s men: none larger than a frigate of thirty-six guns, carrying less metal than a modern gunboat, their cannons firing only a fraction of the distance our own can reach. Would the visitors from the nineteenth century—just arrived by train, eager to see the troops embark before returning home for dinner—have looked down on this modest fleet with scorn? Would they have smiled, sneered, and muttered about progress, science, and how foolish their ancestors must have been?

Some surely would have. Those who treat marvels as curiosities, who use inventions without understanding them—like oxen chewing clover without knowing where it came from—would have laughed. But others—the thinkers, the builders, the men who know what effort lies behind every breakthrough—would have seen something deeper. They would not have mocked, but marveled. For in those small, weathered ships, they would have glimpsed the seed of the mighty navy that now ruled the Black Sea, the Atlantic, and beyond. They would have understood that to build such power from such meager means required courage, intellect, and vision no less remarkable than any we claim today.

Yet if these travelers had been students not just of machines, but of men, they might have found few nobler company than gathered on the terrace bowling-green behind the Pelican Inn on the afternoon of July 19th. There, beneath the shade of roses, sat nearly every notable captain of England’s fleet—“England’s forgotten worthies,” as history would later call them. The Spanish Armada had scattered in a storm. Lord Howard had sailed out to hunt for it, as far as the Spanish coast, but the wind had shifted southward. Fearing the Spaniards might slip past him, he had returned to Plymouth, uncertain whether the fleet would come at all.

Now, let us slip into the tavern, like Prince Hal, bearing a tray of long-necked Dutch glasses and a silver tankard of wine. Step through the rose-clad door and look upon the gallant captains waiting—like lions in their den, watching for the herd of deer to pass.

At the center of a silent ring stands a group of five men speaking earnestly. You recognize the soft eyes and pointed chin—Walter Raleigh. Beside him, a young man in a flame-colored doublet, his arm around Raleigh’s neck—Lord Sheffield. Opposite them, standing beside Sir Richard Grenville, is a noble figure: Lord Charles Howard of Effingham, lord high admiral of England. Next to him, his son-in-law, Sir Robert Southwell, captain of the *Elizabeth Jonas*. But who is that short, sturdy man, plainly dressed, hands clasped behind his back, legs slightly apart, gazing intently at each speaker? His cap rests in his hand, revealing a bullet-shaped head of crisp brown hair, a wrinkled brow, high cheekbones, a broad forehead, thick lips—set firm as stone. A rough, plebeian face—but the whole posture speaks of iron will, calm strength, and unshakable resolve. When he finally speaks, even the most seasoned officers turn to listen. His name is Francis Drake.

Then comes a grizzled elder, broad-shouldered and sea-stained, wearing garments soaked in salt and oil, yet adorned with a massive gold chain that seems out of place. He waddles forward as if born to the gale. His face is split between leather-red skin above and coarse fur below. Clapping Drake on the back, he bellows in thick Devon dialect:

“Be you a coming to drink your wine, Francis Drake, or be you not?—saving your presence, my lord!”

Lord Howard laughs, bidding Drake go ahead. For John Hawkins, admiral of the port, is the patriarch of Plymouth seamen. If Drake is their hero, Hawkins is their father. He does as he pleases, says what he likes—and today, the prospect of battle has loosened his usual grim reserve, filling him with boisterous cheer—even toward his rival, Drake.

So they push through the crowd, where many other names linger in legend. Martin Frobisher and John Davis sit on a bench, smoking tobacco from long silver pipes. By them are Fenton and Withrington, both men who had tried to follow Drake’s path around the world and failed—not through fault of their own. The one who raises a toast to them is George Fenner, known among “the seven Portugals” and favored by Leicester, captain of the galleon Elizabeth bought from him. The short, prim man in a huge yellow ruff, sharp chin, tiny mustache, and self-satisfied smile is Richard Hawkins—son of Admiral John, already famed and doomed. Talking with him is his uncle William Hawkins, whose monument still stands—or should stand—in Deptford Church. It reads: “A worshipper of the true religion, an especial benefactor of poor sailors, a most just arbiter in difficult causes, and of singular faith, piety, and prudence.” That, and his service at Porto Rico, is all I know of him—but if any of us could hope for such words after our own lives, we would have no reason to complain.

There is John Drake, brother of Sir Francis, ancestor of the Drakes of today. There is George, his nephew, not overwise, who sailed with Amyas. And there is Amyas himself, talking fiercely with Captain Barker of Bristol—brother of Andrew Barker, who found John Oxenham’s guns and died by Spanish hands in Honduras twelve years prior. Barker now commands the *Victory*, one of the queen’s finest ships, and like Amyas, he has scores to settle with the Spaniards. So they growl together in a corner, while the rest laugh and joke like flies on a summer vine.

But who is the old man seated against the southern wall of the inn? His long white beard flows almost to his waist, his hands rest on his knees, his head moves slowly from side to side, catching fragments of conversation. At his feet, a little girl of six lays her curly head upon his lap. Beside him stands a buxom woman of thirty, tending him—half nurse, half showman, for he draws curious glances from every captain. She whispers again and again: “Bless you, sir, please don’t give him liquor, poor old soul—the doctor says.”

It is old Martin Cockrem, father of the innkeeper, aged beyond reckoning. He remembers the bells of Plymouth ringing for Henry VIII’s coronation. He may well have been the first Englishman to step onto the soil of the New World. He sits there like an ancient Druid stone amid a modern field of wheat and clover. He has seen the death of old Europe and the birth of a new world. Approach him—he is still sharp in mind. Just now, he seems restless, peering through the crowd, listening for a familiar voice.

“There 'a be again! Why don’t 'a come, then?”

“Quiet, gramfer, don’t trouble his worship.”

“Here an hour, and never speak to poor old Martin! I say, sir—” he feebly pulls at Amyas’s cloak. “I say, captain, do ‘e tell young master old Martin’s looking for him.”

“Marcy, gramfer, where’s your manners? Don’t vex him, sir—he’s almost a babe, and tejous at times.”

“Young master who?” asks Amyas, bending down, smiling at the woman to let the old man have his way.

“Master Hawkins; he’m never been near me all day.”

Amyas goes off, finds young Richard Hawkins, but as he reaches to speak, the woman stops him, laughing and blushing.

“No, sir, not Mr. Richard—Admiral John, sir, his father! He always calls him young master, poor old soul!” She points to the grizzled beard and weathered face, scarred by fifty years of storms and battles.

Amyas hurries to the admiral and delivers the message.

“Mercy on me! Where be my wits? Iss, I’m a-coming,” says the old hero in his broadest Devon accent. He waddles over, rummaging in his pockets. “Here, Martin, I’ve got mun, I’ve got mun, man alive! But his Lordship kept me so. Lookee here—why, I do get so lusty of late, Martin, I can’t get to my pockets!”

Out tumble a tarred string, papers, a thimble, a lump of pudding tobacco—and finally, a small paper of Muscovado sugar, a rare delicacy then, as precious as French bonbons today. He thrusts it into the old man’s trembling hand.

Old Martin dips his finger in, rubs it on his toothless gums, smiles, nods thanks. The little girl at his knee takes her share too.

“There, Admiral Leigh—both ends meet—gramfers and babies! You and I shall be like that one day, young Samson!”

“We shall have slain a good many Philistines first, I hope.”

“Amen! So be it. But look to mun! So fine a sailor as ever drank liquor—and now greedy after a hit of sweet trade! Piteous like. But I bring mun a hit whenever I come, and he looks for it. He’s one of my own flesh, is old Martin. He sailed with my father, Captain Will, when they were two little cracks aboard a trawler. My father went up—here I am. He didn’t—there he is. We’m up now, we Hawkinses. We may be down again some day.”

“Never, I trust,” said Amyas.

“‘Tain’t no use trusting, young man—you go and do. I hear too much of that from my lad. Let they ministers preach till they’re black in the face—works is the trade!” He nudges Amyas. “Faith can’t save, nor charity nether. There, you tell with him, while I go play bowls with Drake. He’ll tell you a sight of stories. Ask him about good King Hal, now—”

And off waddles the Port Admiral.

“You’ve seen good King Henry, then, father?” asked Amyas, intrigued.

The old man’s eyes sparkled. He stopped chewing his sugar.

“Seed mun? Iss, I reckon. I was with Captain Will when he went to meet the Frenchman at Calais—the Field, the Field—”

“The Field of the Cloth of Gold, gramfer,” the woman suggested.

“That’s it. Seed mun? Iss, fegs. Oh, he was a king! The face o’ mun like a rising sun, and the back o’ mun so broad as that there” (he spreads his arms). “And the voice o’ mun! Oh, to hear mun swear if he was merry—oh, ‘tas royal! Seed mun? Iss, fegs! And I’ve seed mun do what few has—I’ve seed mun christle like any child.”

“Cry?” asked Amyas. “I shouldn’t have thought there was much cry in him.”

“You think what you like—”

“Gramfer, don’t be rude—”

“Let him go on,” said Amyas.

“I seed mun christle. Oh dear, how he did put hands on mun’s face! ‘Oh, my gentlemen,’ says he, ‘my gentlemen! Oh, my gallant men!’ Them was his very words.”

“But when?”

“Why, Captain Will had just come to the Hard—that’s Portsmouth—to speak with mun. The barge Royal lay against the Hard, and our boat alongside. The king stood above my head on the quay edge. The ship came in near abreast of us, looking most royal to behold, poor dear! And went to cast about. And Captain Will says, ‘Them lower ports is cruel near the water.’ She had not more than sixteen inches to spare. Says he, ‘That won’t do for going to windward in a say, Martin.’ And as the words left his mouth, a bit of a flaw came from the west, sharp like—and overboard goeth my cap, hits the wall, and as I stooped to pick it up, I heard a cry—and it was all over!”

“He’s telling of the Mary Rose, sir.”

“I guessed so.”

“All over—and the cry of mun, and the screech of mun! Oh, sir, up to the heavens! And the king he screeched right out like any maid, ‘Oh my gentlemen, oh my gallant men!’ And as she lay on her beam-ends, just settling, the last souls I seen were that man’s father, and that man’s. I knew mun by their armor.”

He pointed to Sir George Carew and Sir Richard Grenville.

“Iss! Iss! Drowned like rattens. Drowned like rattens!”

“Now, you mustn’t trouble his worship any more.”

“Trouble? Let him tell till midnight—I shall be well pleased,” said Amyas, sitting down beside him. “Drawer! ale—and a parcel of tobacco.”

And Amyas settled in to listen, while the old man purred to himself:

“Iss. They likes to hear old Martin. All the captains look upon old Martin.”

“Hillo, Amyas!” said Cary. “Who’s your friend? Here’s a man been telling me wonders about the River Plate. We should go thither for luck next time.”

“River Plate?” said old Martin. “It’s I knows about the River Plate—none so well. Who’d ever been there, nor heard of it, before Captain Will and me went? And I lived among the savages a whole year. Audacious civil I found ‘em—if they’d had shirts to their backs. And so was the prince o’ mun, that Captain Will brought home to King Henry—leastwise he died on the voyage. But the wild folk took it cruel well, for you see, we was always as civil with them as Christians. If we hadn’t been, I should not be here now.”

“What year was that?”

“In the fifteen thirty. But I was there afore, and learnt the speech o’ mun. That’s why Captain Will left me as a hostage when he took their prince.”

“Before that?” said Cary. “Why, the country was hardly known before that.”

The old man’s eyes flashed.

“Knowed? Iss, and you may well say that! Look ye here! Look to mun!” He waved his hand. “There’s captains! And I’m the father of ‘em all now—poor Captain Will’s in glory. I, Martin Cockrem! . . . Iss, I’ve seen a change. I mind when Tavistock Abbey was full o’ friars and golden idols—such noxious trade as ever was a wheat-rick of rats. I mind the fight off Brest in the French wars—Oh, that was a fight, surely!—when the Regent and the French Carack burned side by side, fast grappled, because of Sir Thomas Knivet. And Captain Will gave him warning as he ran past us, saying—”

“But,” said Amyas, seeing the old man drift, “what do you mind about America?”

“America? I should think so! But I was a-going to tell you of the Regent—and seven hundred Englishmen burnt and drowned in her, nine hundred French in the Brest ship, besides what we picked up. Oh dear! But about America.”

“Yes, about America. How are you the father of all the captains?”

“How? You ask my young master! Why, before the fifteen thirty, I was up the Plate with Cabot—and a cruel fractious ontrustful fellow he was, like all they Portingals—and bid there a year and more, and up the Paraguaio with him, discovering no end. Whereby, gentles, I was the first Englishman, I hold, that ever set foot on the New World!”

“Then here’s your health, sir!” said Amyas and Cary.

“Long life? Iss, fegs, I reckon—long enough already! Why, I mind the beginning of it all. I mind when there wasn’t a master mariner to Plymouth that thought there was aught west of the Land’s End except herrings. They held them, pure wratches, that if you sailed west far enough, you’d fall off the edge. Iss—‘Twas dark parts round here, till Captain Will arose. And the first of it I mind was inside the bar of San Lucar. He and I were boys, ten year old, aboard a Dartmouth ship, going for wine. And there came in over the bar he that was the beginning of it all.”

“Columbus?”

“Iss, fegs, he did—no pistol-shot from us. And I saw mun stand on the poop, so plain as I see you. No great shakes of a man to look at. There’s a sight better here, to please me. And we was disappointed, we lads, for we expected to see mun with a golden crown and sceptre. And the ship o’ mun all over like Solomon’s temple. And I mind that same year, seeing Vasco da Gama, going out over the bar, when he found the Cape of Good Hope and sailed to the Indies. Ah, that was the making of they rascally Portingals! . . . Our crew told what they seen and heerd—but nobody minded sich things. ‘Twas dark parts, and Popish, then. Nobody knowed nothing, nor got no schooling, nor cared for nothing but scrattling up and down like prawns in a pule. Iss, sitting in darkness, we was, and the shadow of death—till the day-spring from on high arose, and shined upon us poor out-o'-the-way folk—The Lord be praised! And now, look to mun!” He swept his hand wide. “Look to the works of the Lord! Look to the captains! Oh blessed sight! One’s been to the Brazils, one to the Indies, the Spanish Main, the North-West, the Rooshias, the Chinas, up the Straits, round the Cape, round the world of God—bless His holy name! And I seed the beginning of it. And I’ll see the end of it too. I was born into the old times—but I’ll see the wondrous works of the new, yet, I will! I’ll see they bloody Spaniards swept off the seas before I die, if my old eyes can reach so far as outside the Sound. I shall, I knows it. I says my prayers for it every night—don’t I, Mary? You’ll bate mun, sure as Judgment, you’ll bate mun! The Lord’ll fight for ye. Nothing’ll stand against ye. I’ve seed it all along—ever since I was with young master to the Honduras. They can’t bide the push of us! You’ll bate mun off the face of the seas, and be masters of the round world, and all that therein is. And then, I’ll just turn my old face to the wall, and depart in peace, according to his word.”

“Deary me, now—while I’ve been telling with you, here’ve this little maid been and ate up all my sugar!”

“I’ll bring you some more,” said Amyas, moved by the childish bathos.

“Will ye, then? There’s a good soul—and come and tell with old Martin. He likes to see the brave young gentlemen, a-going to and fro in their ships, like Leviathan, taking of their pastime therein. We had no such ships to our days. Ah, ‘tis grand times, beautiful times surely—and you’ll bring me a bit sugar?”
