“You were up the Plate with Cabot?” said Cary, after a pause.

“Do you mind the fair lady Miranda, Sebastian de Hurtado’s wife?”

“What! Her that was burnt by the Indians? Mind her? Do you mind the sun in heaven? Oh, the beauty! Oh, the ways of her! Oh, the speech of her! Never was, nor never will be! And she to die by they villains—for the goodness of her! Mind her? I minded naught else when she was on deck.”

“Who was she?” asked Amyas.

“A Spanish angel, Amyas.”

“Humph!” said Amyas. “So much the worse for her, to be born into a nation of devils.”

“They’m not all so bad as that, yer honor. Her husband was a proper gallant gentleman, kind as a maid, couldn’t abide De Solis’s murderous doings.”

“His wife must have taught it him, then,” said Amyas, rising.

“Where did you hear of these black swans, Cary?”

“I have heard of them, and that’s enough,” answered he, unwilling to stir sad memories.

“And little enough,” said Amyas. “Will, don’t talk to me. The devil is not grown white because he has trod in a lime-heap.”

“Or an angel black because she came down a chimney,” said Cary.

And so the talk ended—cut short by a sudden roar from Admiral John Hawkins.

“Fail? Fail? What a murrain do you here, to talk of failing? Who made you a prophet, you scurvy, hang-in-the-wind, croaking, white-livered son of a corby-crow?”

“Heaven help us, Admiral Hawkins, who has put fire to your culverins in this fashion?” said Lord Howard.

“Who? my lord! Croakers! my lord! Here’s a fellow calls himself the captain of a ship, and her majesty’s servant, and talks about failing—as if he were a Barbican loose-kirtle trying to keep her apple-squire ashore! Blurt for him, sneak-up! Say I.”

“Admiral John Hawkins,” quoth the offender, “you shall answer this language with your sword.”

“I’ll answer it with my foot—and buy me a pair of horn-tips to my shoes, like a wraxling man. Fight a croaker? Fight a frog, an owl! I fight those that dare fight, sir!”

“Sir, sir, moderate yourself. I am sure this gentleman will show himself as brave as any when it comes to blows. But who can blame mortal man for trembling before so fearful a chance as this?”

Hawkins turned, his eyes blazing.

“Trembling? Is that all you’ve learned? You think fear is wisdom? Then you’re no soldier, no sailor, no Englishman! I’ll not have such croaking here. When the fleet sails, I’ll not have a man who doubts the cause. This is not a game—we are not playing at soldiers. This is war. And war is not for cowards or prophets of doom. If you cannot stand with courage, then stay ashore. But if you sail with me, you sail with the belief that England shall prevail!”

And with that, he strode away, leaving silence in his wake.

“Britannia needs no bulwarks, No towers along the steep, Her march is o'er the mountain wave, Her home is on the deep.”

CAMPBELL, Ye Mariners of England.

Now began the great sea battle that would decide whether Europe’s future—indeed, the fate of America—would be shaped by faith and freedom or by tyranny and superstition. This was no mere clash of fleets but a twelve-day epic, worthy not of dull prose but of the thunderous verse of Homer. Yet I must tell it as best I can, drawing wherever possible upon the words of those who lived it, rather than my own imagination.

“The Lord High Admiral of England sent ahead a pinnace named the *Defiance*, which fired its guns to declare war. Then, closing in within musket range, he approached with great noise from his own ship, the *Arkroyall* (also known as the *Triumph*), and attacked what he believed to be the Spanish admiral’s vessel—but it was actually Alfonso de Leon’s ship. Shortly after, Drake, Hawkins, and Frobisher opened fire with fierce determination upon the rearmost squadron under Recalde.” The Spaniards quickly realized the superior agility of the English ships. Recalde’s division, unable to match their speed or precision despite his efforts, broke off and hurried forward to join the main fleet. Medina, the Spanish Admiral, seeing his ships scattering, formed them into a half-moon formation. The Armada pressed onward, like a stately herd of buffalo crossing a prairie, heedless of the wolves snapping at their flanks. But these were no wolves—they were swift, skilled hunters, mounted and armed, who would “shamefully shuffle” (in Drake’s own words) that mighty host from the Lizard to Portland, from Portland to Calais Roads. Even in this first two-hour engagement, many a Spaniard began to doubt the legendary invincibility of their fleet.

One of the four great galliasses had already been riddled with shot, its pulpits and chapels shattered, its friars thrown into chaos. The fleet was forced to cluster around her to protect her from Drake and Hawkins, who threatened to sink her. In doing so, the “principal galleon of Seville,” carrying Pedro de Valdez and a company of noble Dons, collided with a neighboring ship, lost her foremast, and was abandoned—despite the chivalry of her crew. This did not look like victory. But courage remained: “Our Lady,” the saints, and the bull *Caena Domini* (inspired by one whose name I dare not speak here) still stood with them. To question divine favor would be sacrilege. Still, if they could not prevail against only a third of the Plymouth fleet, how would they fare when the forty belated ships now whitening the horizon between them and the Mewstone joined the fray?

So ended the first day. Not a single English ship nor man was harmed. The myth of Spain’s unassailable power lay broken in the minds of every English sailor. The strategy adopted by Lord Howard—guided by Raleigh and Drake—had proved wise: maintaining a running fight, avoiding close combat without thought. As Raleigh said, “Had I not been better advised than many malignant fools who criticized my conduct, I would have perished.”

Whatever the truth, the first day passed with honor. Amyas Leigh and the Bideford vessels had spent two hours pounding a massive galleon bearing on her stern a figure of Saint Catherine—a maiden with a wheel—and flying a flag with a coat of arms at her sprit. The arms had been shot away early in the fight, so Amyas could not tell if they belonged to De Soto. But there was time enough for personal vengeance. When finally called off by the admiral’s signal, he retired to his hammock and slept soundly.

Yet barely an hour had passed before Cary descended to awaken him.

“We were supposed to follow Drake’s lantern, Amyas,” he said. “But I cannot see it anywhere—unless he’s been hoisted up among the stars as a new Drakium Sidus.”

Amyas grumbled as he rose. But no lantern appeared. Only a sudden explosion and a great blaze aboard a Spanish ship, which gradually died down. The fleet lay-to all night long, nearly the entire armada anchored in silence.

The next morning found them off Torbay. A pinnace hailed Amyas with a letter from Drake—written in the rough hand of a seaman, spelling haphazard as was common in those days:

“DEAR LAD,—I’ve been wool-gathering all night chasing five great hulks, which the Pixies turned into galleons overnight, then back again into German merchantmen. I let them go with my blessing. Returning, I fell in (God be thanked!) with Valdez’s great galleon—rich spoil, left behind by the Dons, who behaved like faithful comrades, and whom Lord Howard missed, thinking her deserted. I’ve sent to Dartmouth a sight of noblemen and gentlemen—perhaps fifty—and Valdez himself. When I sent my pinnace aboard, he insisted on punctilio, offering terms. I replied I had no time for talk; if he wanted to die, I was the man for it; if he wished to live, then *buena querra*. He returned, boasting he was Don Pedro Valdez, and that it was beneath his honor and that of his companions. I answered: ‘For my part, I am Francis Drake, and my matches are burning.’ At that, he found comfort in my name, came aboard, kissed my fist, and spouted Spanish lies about being fortunate to fall into the hands of lucky Drake—words he might have saved for cooling his porridge. But I learned much from him (he’s a leaky tub). Among other things, your Don Guzman is aboard the *Sta. Catharina*, commander of her soldiers, with his arms flying at her sprit, beside Saint Catherine at the stern—she’s a tall ship of three decks of ordnance. God preserve you, and send you good fortune.

“Your dear Friend and Admiral,

“F. Drake.

“She sails in Recalde’s squadron. The Armada meant to smoke us out of Plymouth—thank God they tried not. But their orders from home are too strict, and so the slaves fight like a bull on a tether, no farther than their rope. They find the devil a hard master, as most do in the end. They cannot match our quick tacking and maneuvering, and take us for witches. So far, so good—and better to come. You and I know the length of their foot well. Time and light will kill any hare, and they’ll find it a long way from Start to Dunkirk.”

“The admiral is in a generous mood, Leigh, to send you such a long letter,” remarked Cary.

“Saint Catherine! Why, that was the galleon we hammered yesterday!” Amyas stamped on deck.

“Of course it was. We’ll find her again, no doubt. That clever old Drake—how he manages to line his pockets even while keeping the whole fleet waiting!”

“He’s given the lord high admiral the slip, at least.”

“Lord Howard is too noble to stop and plunder, Papist though he is, Amys.”

Amyas growled in reply—he adored Drake and held little mercy for Catholics.

They did not meet Lord Howard until dusk. He and Lord Sheffield had pursued the Spanish lanterns through the night with only two ships. Now there could be no doubt of the loyalty of English Roman Catholics. Throughout the battle, the Howards had shown desperate courage—proving themselves more than willing to risk all, even if others had doubted them. Soon, a large Spanish ship drifted past, abandoned and partly burned. Some men urged boarding her, but Amyas refused. “We came to fight, not to loot,” he said. “Let the nearest ship claim her prize without envy.” They passed on. Later, they saw the galleon towed away by a neighbor, taken to Weymouth. It turned out to be Miguel d’Oquenda’s vessel—the vice-admiral’s ship—seen the previous night nearly destroyed by a furious Netherland gunner who, having been mistreated, sought revenge on his tyrants.

Thus ended the second day. The Portland Rock rose higher and clearer each hour. The next morning found them near the island. Would they attempt Portsmouth, despite sparing Plymouth? The wind had shifted northward, blowing cool from the white downs of Weymouth Bay. The Spaniards turned to face the English. They intended to wait until the wind changed, then try for the Needles. At least, they would have work to do before rounding Purbeck Isle.

The English sailed westward—only to return on the opposite tack. Now began a series of maneuvers, each fleet striving to gain the weather gauge. But the struggle was brief. By noon, the English fleet had slipped close-hauled between the Armada and the coast, advancing directly toward them with the wind at their backs.

And then the battle erupted—fierce and deadly. “They fought confusedly, with shifting fortunes. On one side, the English heroically rescued London’s ships, trapped by the Spaniards. On the other, the Spaniards fiercely freed Recalde, who was in danger.” “Never was heard such thunder from ordnance on both sides, yet mostly the Spanish shots flew over the English harmlessly. Only Cock, an Englishman” (whom Prince claims as a son of Devon, and rightly so), “died bravely in the midst of the enemy in a small ship. For the English vessels, smaller and swifter, charged with astonishing agility, discharged their broadsides, then darted away into deeper water, leveling their shots precisely at the massive, unwieldy Spanish ships.” “This was the fiercest and bloodiest skirmish of all,” though it ended only in the capture of a great Venetian vessel and some minor craft. “The Lord Admiral fought amidst the enemy’s fleet and, seeing one of his captains afar off—Fenner by name, the same who fought the seven Portugals at the Azores—cried: ‘O George, what doest thou? Wilt thou now frustrate my hope and opinion conceived of thee? Wilt thou forsake me now?’ Fired by these words, Fenner advanced and fought like a valiant captain”—as did all the rest.

Night fell upon the floating volcano. Morning found them far beyond Purbeck, the white peak of Freshwater ahead. Ship after ship poured past the Needles, joining the gallant chase. From every harbor, nobles and gentlemen flocked forth in privately outfitted vessels—Lords Oxford, Northumberland, Cumberland, Pallavicin, Brooke, Carew, Raleigh, Blunt, and many others—rushing “to a set field where immortal fame and honor were to be won.” Spain had staked its nobility in this great gamble—every noble house of Aragon and Castile had contributed a brother or son—and would mourn their loss. England’s gentlemen would measure their strength once and for all against the Cavaliers of Spain.

Lord Howard had dispatched light vessels to Portsmouth for ammunition, but they would likely not return that night. The wind had died, and all evening the fleets drifted helplessly with the tide, shouting empty defiance through trumpet, fife, and drum.

The sun sank upon a glassy sea, and rose again upon a glassy sea. But what day was this? The twenty-fifth—St. James’s Day, sacred to Spain’s patron saint. Would nothing be attempted in his honor? Had not their ancestors seen him riding white horse, lance aloft, driving infidels before him? He might have sent them a favorable breeze—or perhaps He meant to test their faith. At least, the galleys would attack. Three of the great galliasses (the fourth crippled) cut the waves with three hundred oars each. And lo—no St. James led them, but Lord Howard’s *Triumph*, his brother’s *Lion*, Southwell’s *Elizabeth Jonas*, Lord Sheffield’s *Bear*, Barker’s *Victory*, and George Fenner’s *Leicester*, towed boldly forward to meet them. With salvoes of chain-shot, they shattered oars and severed rigging. Had the wind not risen again by noon, and the Spanish fleet not rushed to rescue them, they would have shared Valdez’s fate.

Now the battle became general. Frobisher brought down the Spanish admiral’s mainmast. Attacked by Mexia and Recalde, he was rescued by Lord Howard—who, himself endangered, was in turn saved. “After that day,” the Spaniards said, “no galliasse dared engage.”

So the fight raged through the livelong afternoon beneath the virgin cliffs of Freshwater. Myriad seabirds rose screaming from every ledge, black wings cutting across the chalk-white wall. A lone shepherd raced down the slopes above, peering over the dizzy edge, forgetting the wheatear caught in his snare, transfixed by glimpses of towering masts and glittering flags piercing the thick veil of sulphurous smoke below.

Thus passed St. James’s Day, as Baal did on Carmel in ancient times: “Either he is talking, or he is pursuing, or he is on a journey; or peradventure he sleepeth, and must be awaked.” Indeed, the only fire offered by the saint was that of English cannon. The Armada, gathering into a tight circle, would fight no more. It made for Calais, hoping the Guises might offer French aid, then to Dunkirk to join Parma and the vast flotilla of the Netherlands.

Onward they glided before a fair Etesian gale, clear and bright from the south-southwest—past Brighton Cliffs, Beachy Head, Hastings, Dungeness. Was it battle or triumph? Lord Howard, instead of fighting, was rewarding. After Lord Thomas Howard, Lord Sheffield, Townsend, and Frobisher received knighthood—then more honorable than peerage—old Admiral Hawkins knelt and rose Sir John, shaking his shoulders after the accolade and saying to the representative of majesty: “My old woman will hardly know herself when folks call her My Lady.”

Meanwhile, the cliffs were lined with pikemen and musketeers, countrymen and grooms bearing arms, led by squires and sheriffs marching eastward as fast as their weapons allowed toward Dover. And not alone. Women and children, elders in wagons, streamed from miles inland to add their feeble shouts and fervent prayers to the great cry of faith and fear rising to God from Britain’s Salamis.

Let them pray. The danger was not yet over. Though Lord Howard had learned from Newhaven that the Guises would not move, and Seymour and Winter had joined the fleet from the Flemish shores, bringing it to 140 ships—slightly larger than the Spanish fleet, but with less than half the tonnage and one-third the men—the Spaniards were battered but unbroken. As they slipped into Calais Roads Saturday evening, all prudent men knew England’s hour had come. The bells that would summon Christendom to church the next morning would either toll the death-knell or ring the triumph of the Reformed faith.

That Sabbath must have been solemn throughout land and town. Many a coward, once scoffing at the Armada’s threat because he feared to believe it, now trembled with dread. And many a brave man, kneeling beside wife and daughters, felt his heart sink at the thought of what they might endure—by fanatical soldiery or the cold cruelty of the Inquisition. The massacre of St. Bartholomew, the fires of Smithfield, the slaughter of Moors, the extermination of West Indians, the horrors of Piedmont—these spectres, not dimmed by time but bleeding fresh in memory, haunted every English soul.

Each knew the fate awaiting him. One misstep, and the doom that later befell the Lutherans of Magdeburg would descend upon every town from London to Carlisle. All understood the peril as they prayed—day after day, throughout England and the Netherlands. None understood it better than she who guided the nation, the target of the invaders’ wrath, and who, inspired by divine vision (as men then wisely believed), devised the bold stroke to strike first.

But where was Amyas Leigh during all this? Day after day he hunted the *Sta. Catharina* through the thickest of the press, unable to find her, unable even to hear of her. One moment he feared she had sunk in the night, denying him his prey. The next, that she had repaired and would escape him after all. He grew moody, restless, irritable—peevish for the first time in his life. He spoke only of Don Guzman. At every spare moment, he drew his sword, handled it, fondled it, even spoke to it, bidding it not fail him in vengeance. At last, he sent for Squire, the armorer, fetched a whetstone, and shamefacedly sharpened and polished the blade in hidden corners, muttering to himself.

A single obsession had seized his mind—vengeance. He forgot England’s need, her triumph, his own safety—everything but his brother’s blood. And yet this was the day he had long awaited since he brought home that magic horn as a fifteen-year-old boy—the day he would stand face to face with an invader, and that invader Antichrist himself. For years, he had believed with Drake, Hawkins, Grenville, and Raleigh that he was born only to fight the Spaniard. And now he fought him—on a cause greater than any he’d ever known, for a stake beyond imagining, within battle lines he would never see again. And yet he was not content.

While crews across the fleet received communion together, rising with joyful faces to shake hands and rejoice in Britain’s Salamis, Amyas turned away from the holy elements.

“I cannot communicate, Sir John. Charity with all men? I hate—more than any man ever hated.”

“You hate only the Lord’s foes, Captain Leigh.”

“No, Jack. I hate my own as well.”

“But no one in the fleet?”

“Don’t try to trick me with the same Jesuit lie Parson Fletcher used for one of Doughty’s men—to poison his conscience while plotting against his admiral. No, Jack. I hate one you know. And that hatred keeps me from loving any human being. I am in love with no man—no man, not even you! Go your ways in God’s name, sir, and leave me and the devil alone. Or you’ll find my words true.”

Jack departed with a sigh. While the crew received communion on deck, Amyas sat below in the cabin, sharpening his sword. Then he summoned a boat and went aboard Drake’s ship to inquire about the *Sta. Catharina*, scowling as he listened to the loud chants and tinkling bells drifting across the water from the Spanish fleet.

At last, Drake was summoned by the Lord Admiral and returned with a secret commission—meant to bear fruit that very night. Amyas, who had gone with him, helped till dusk. Then he returned to his own ship as Sir Amyas Leigh, Knight, to the joy of every soul aboard—except himself.

So there, on that long summer Sabbath, before the little walled town and the yellow sandhills, lay the two mighty fleets—scowling at each other, scarcely out of gunshot. Messenger after messenger sped toward Bruges to the Duke of Parma, begging for faster vessels and demanding he sail immediately with all his forces.

But the duke was not at Dunkirk—he was at the future battlefield of Waterloo, praying before St. Mary of Halle in Hainault, ensuring his place in the Pantheon. Already, he dreamed of the gentle-faced Cardinal Allen placing the crown of England upon his head.

He replied: first, his supplies were not ready; second, his Dutch sailors—held at bay for weeks by the sword—had fled like water; third, and worst, he could not come—because thirty-five Dutch ships, full of stubborn, round-sterned heretics, watched the entrance to Nieuwport and Dunkirk like terriers guarding a rat hole.

Having secured the private favor of St. Mary of Halle, he would return tomorrow to test her power. But only hear the thunder of the fleets across the flats of Dixmude, and the curses echoing from Dunkirk. While he prayed, the English had prayed—and done more. All that remained for the Prince of Parma was to hang a few purveyors as offerings to his angry army—and then, as Drake said, “chafe like a bear robbed of her whelps.”

“Full fathom deep thy father lies; Of his bones are corals made; Those are pearls which were his eyes; Nothing of him that doth fade, But doth suffer a sea-change Into something rich and strange; Fairies hourly ring his knell, Hark! I hear them. Ding dong bell.”

—*The Tempest*

It is over. The great Armada has been defeated. The eternal conflict—the battle between light and darkness, between Michael and Satan, between the children of God and the servants of the devil—has reached its climax. Once every few centuries, this spiritual war becomes flesh and blood, manifesting in mortal men who must choose sides, not with prayers alone, but with cannon fire and steel. This was such a day. Judgment had come, dividing good from evil, sheep from goats, testing every man’s work in the fire. And the devil’s grand design—his empire of fear and falsehood—proved, as always, to be nothing more than a hollow boast, a balloon bursting at the slightest touch.

Byzantine empires, Spanish fleets, papal crowns, Russian thrones—all follow the same path: rise in pride, fall in shame. One bold blow, and the monster vanishes in smoke. The children of Israel, as in ancient times, see the Egyptians dead upon the shore—though they scarcely know how, save that God has done it—and they sing the song of Moses and the Lamb.

From England and the Netherlands, from Geneva and Germany, from the Vaudois shepherds whose bones lie scattered on the cold Alpine slopes—those true seeds of faith, liberty, and future civilization—arose a cry of joy such as the world had not known for generations. It was the birth cry of a new age: North America, Australia, New Zealand, the Pacific Islands, free trade, free colonies across the globe.

Van Meteran records: “By command of Her Majesty, and by order of the States of the United Provinces, a solemn day of thanksgiving was proclaimed. All were required to attend church and give thanks to God. Preachers were instructed to exhort the people accordingly. On November 29th, the day was spent in fasting, prayer, and gratitude.

“The Queen herself, following the example of ancient Rome, rode in triumph into London, attended by all the principal nobles and officers of her realm. She entered the city in a chariot of victory, clothed in robes of state, proceeding from her palace to St. Paul’s Cathedral, where the captured banners of the vanquished Spaniards hung displayed. Citizens lined the streets in their liveries, companies bearing their colors, while blue hangings adorned the thoroughfares, creating a magnificent spectacle. Upon entering the church, the Queen gave thanks to God and listened to a sermon preached before her at Paul’s Cross—whose sole theme was praise and glory to God. With her own voice, she urged the people to join in worship, and they responded with loud acclaim, wishing her long life and prosperity, and the downfall of her enemies.”

Yes—just as the medals declared: *It came, it saw, and it fled!* Where did it go? Northward, like frightened deer, past the Orkneys and Shetlands, picking up hapless fishermen as guides. Along Norway’s coast, where even the descendants of Vikings refused them food and water. Then onward toward the Faroes and the endless dawn circling the Pole.

Their water ran out. Cattle were thrown overboard. The northern seas roared with the screams of drowning horses. They must return—each man as best he could. Let them meet again at Cape Finisterre—if they ever met. Medina Sidonia, with twenty-five of the strongest ships, would lead the way, leaving the rest to fate. He vanished quickly. Forty more, the last remnants of that mighty fleet, drifted wearily southward, hoping to reach Ireland’s southwest coast, where Roman Catholic sympathizers might offer aid—or at least fresh water.

But alas—

“Make Thou their way dark and slippery, And follow them up ever with Thy storm.”

For now, gale after gale rose from the Atlantic. Few of those doomed vessels ever reached Spain.

And where was Amyas Leigh and the *Vengeance* during all this?

At fifty-seven degrees north, the English fleet, low on provisions and entirely out of powder and shot, turned southward, deciding it was wiser to let the Spaniards face the wild northern seas. A few pinnaces remained to watch the fleeing armada. The English fleet, caught in the same storms that shattered the Spaniards, struggled back to Harwich, where they resupplied—just in case the enemy returned. But there was no need. Parma, still clinging to hope, waited weeks on the dunes of Dunkirk, believing the idol at Halle would not betray him. He cast anchors, repaired rigging, and watched the horizon—like a man waiting for a ghost.

“But lang, lang may his ladies sit, With their fans intill their hand, Before they see Sir Patrick Spens Come sailing to the land.”

The Armada was already beyond Scotland. And Amyas was following.

When the Lord High Admiral decided to return, Amyas asked leave to pursue the Spaniard. He approached Sir John Hawkins, who happened to be nearby, and requested ammunition and supplies—promising to repay them, either from plunder if he lived, or from his estate if he died. He left formal bills in Hawkins’s hands, who, being a practical man, tucked them away with his tobacco and string. Then Amyas called his crew together.

He reminded them once more of the Rose of Torridge and Don Guzman de Soto.

“Men of Bideford,” he said, “will you follow me? There will be plunder for those who love it. Revenge for those who seek it. And for all of us—who love honor—the pride of never abandoning the chase so long as one Spanish flag flies in English waters.”

Every man aboard answered yes. They would follow Sir Amyas Leigh to the ends of the earth.

I need not recount each detail of that long, grueling pursuit. How they found the *Santa Catharina*, attacked her, only to be forced off when reinforcements arrived. How, when Medina’s squadron abandoned the crippled ships, the *Vengeance* nearly perished, charging into the heart of the Spanish fleet to prevent escape. How they disabled the galleon, preventing her from beating to windward, forcing her to run south—past the Orkneys, through the Minch, between Cape Wrath and Lewis. How the younger sailors threatened mutiny when Amyas passed by several noble prizes—among them a great galley and two Venetian ships, *La Ratta* and *La Belanzara*, later wrecked on Ireland’s west coast. How they secured fresh water from Skye, where fierce Highlanders first cursed them in an unknown tongue, then fought briefly, then embraced them with wild affection—clothed no better than California’s savages. How Amyas calmed his men by letting them scavenge a wrecked Venetian on Islay, gaining enough loot to cover the cost of the voyage—only to anger them again by refusing to land and plunder two Spanish wrecks on the Mull of Cantire (whose crews James attempted to smuggle into Spain, playing both sides—until the council intervened and allowed them to go, though likely to be hanged elsewhere). How they passed a strange island, half black, half white, which the natives called Raghary—but Cary dubbed “the drowned magpie.” How the *Santa Catharina* nearly foundered on the Isle of Man, where Manxmen killed a boat’s crew with arrows. How they fought her again at Castleton, then again in a full-day battle, destroying her mainyard. How the Spaniard blundered down the Welsh coast, lost and confused. How both ships nearly foundered at Holyhead and Bardsey Island. How they were driven onto a lee shore in Cardigan Bay, caught in a heavy westerly gale, and the *Santa Catharina* ran aground on Sarn David—a submerged pebble ridge said to be the ruins of Gwalior, destroyed by Prince Seithenin the drunkard, whose name still draws spits from loyal Welshmen. How she escaped at high tide and fought Amyas a fourth time. How the wind shifted, allowing her to round St. David’s Head.

These and many other dramatic events I must pass over. The end is near.

It was the sixteenth day of the chase. The previous evening, they had seen St. David’s Head and the Welsh coast around Milford Haven, silhouetted against the lightning of an inland thunderstorm. During the night, flashes lit the sky under a gentle southwest breeze.

They strained their eyes through the darkness, hoping to catch sight of the Spaniard’s masts in the flickering light. At least they knew one thing: with the wind as it was, she couldn’t have gone far westward. To try to pass them and head north would be madness. She was likely lying-to ahead of them, perhaps between them and the shore.

Then, just after midnight, the wind shifted sharply to the west and blew hard until dawn. They were certain—unless she had tried the desperate move of running past them—they had her trapped in the mouth of the Bristol Channel.

Dawn broke slowly and wearily, bringing a dull, drizzly day beneath a low, gray sky—clouds netted and barred with black, a sign the storm was only resting before breaking again. The horizon was blurred with vapor drifting in a cold breeze. As the day wore on, the wind died, the sea settled into a long, glassy roll flecked with foam, while above brooded the inky sky, and around them, mist swallowed both land and chase.

Amyas paced the wet deck, restless and furious. He knew the Spaniard could not escape. Yet every passing moment gnawed at him, delaying the one revenge that consumed his soul. The crew sat sullenly, whistling for wind. Sails flapped listlessly. The ship rolled heavily, her yardarms dipping low.

“Watch those guns,” Amyas growled. “You’ll have something loose next.”

“We’ll watch the guns,” Yeo replied, “if the Lord watches the wind.”

“We’ll have plenty before night,” Cary said. “And thunder too.”

“So much the better,” Amyas said. “Let it roar till it splits the heavens, if it lets me finish my work.”

“He’s not far off,” Cary said. “One break in the clouds, and we’ll see him.”

“To windward, likely,” Amyas snapped. “The devil fights for him. Sixteen days on his heels, and I’ve not sent this through him yet!” He shook his sword violently.

The morning passed without sign of life—not even a gull. The gloom of the sky mirrored the gloom in Amyas’s heart. Was he to lose his prey after all? The thought filled him with rage and despair. Unthinkable. Anything but that.

“No, God!” he cried. “Let me feel this in his cursed heart, and then—strike me dead, if You will!”

“The Lord have mercy on us,” murmured John Brimblecombe. “What have you said?”

“What is that to you?” Amyas snapped. “There—dinner’s piping. Go below. I won’t come.”

Jack descended, whispering in fear about Amyas’s words. Everyone believed they foretold misfortune—except old Yeo.

“Well, Sir John,” he said, “why not? What better can the Lord do for a man than take him home when his work is done? Our captain is stubborn and vengeful—he wants to kill his man himself. But for me, I don’t care how the Don dies, so long as he dies. I owe him no grudge. May the Lord grant him repentance and forgive him all. But if I could see him safely ashore—on Mortestone or Lundy—I’d say, ‘Lord, now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace,’ even if it were lightning that fetched me.”

“But a sudden death?”

“And why not a sudden death, Sir John? Even fools desire a short life and a merry one. Why shouldn’t the Lord’s people pray for a short death and a merry one? Let it come as it may to old Yeo. Hark—there’s the captain’s voice!”

“Here she is!” thundered Amyas from the deck. In an instant, the crew scrambled up the hatchway as fast as the rolling ship would allow.

Yes. There she was. The cloud had lifted suddenly. A ragged patch of blue sky poured sunlight onto her tall masts and proud hull, four or five miles eastward. But no land was visible.

“There she is,” said Cary. “And here we are. But where is here? Where is there? How’s the tide, master?”

“Running up the Channel by now.”

“What matters the tide?” Amyas said, staring at the ship with cold, terrible eyes. “Can’t we get to her?”

“Not unless someone jumps out and shoves behind,” Cary joked. “I’m going down to finish my mackerel—if this roll hasn’t thrown it to the cockroaches under the table.”

“Don’t jest, Will,” Amyas said, his voice trembling. “I can’t stand it.” His whole body shook like a leaf. Cary took his arm and drew him aside.

“Dear old lad,” he said, leaning over the bulwarks, “what’s wrong? You’re not yourself—haven’t been for four days.”

“No,” Amyas said. “I’m not Amyas Leigh. I’m my brother’s avenger. Don’t reason with me, Will. When it’s done, I’ll be merry old Amyas again.” He wiped his brow.

“Do you believe,” he asked after a pause, “that men can be possessed by devils?”

“The Bible says so.”

“If my cause weren’t just, I’d think I had a devil in me. My throat, my heart—burning like hell. God, let it be done. It must be done. Now go.”

Cary left, shuddering. As he descended, he looked back. Amyas had pulled out his hone and was sharpening his sword again—over and over—as if doom demanded endless grinding.

The day dragged on. The blue sky vanished, and all grew dark and oppressive, though the air grew hotter. Distant thunder rumbled to the west.

Nothing could close the gap—every boat except one had been lost, and it was too small to tow them. As the men went below for dinner, Amyas continued sharpening, glancing up every few moments at the Spaniard, as if afraid she’d vanish.

Around two o’clock, Yeo came up.

“She’s ours now, sir. The tide’s been running eastward for two hours.”

“Safe as a fox in a trap. Satan himself can’t take him from us!”

“But God may,” Brimblecombe said simply.

“Who spoke to you, sir? If I thought He—” Thunder cracked from the west, answering his words. It rolled closer, louder, until it crashed overhead, splitting a cloud-cliff far above—and then silence.

All men stared at one another. Amyas did not flinch.

“The storm’s coming,” he said. “And the wind with it. It will be Eastward-ho now, for once, my merry men all!”

“Eastward-ho never brought us luck,” Jack whispered to Cary. But all eyes turned north-west, where a black line along the horizon began to define sea and sky—now clear in the mist.

“There comes the breeze.”

“And there the storm.”

With that strange, accelerating pace some storms possess, the thunder—once distant and slow—now roared in peal after peal directly above.

“Here comes the breeze! Round with the yards, or we’ll be taken aback!”

The yards creaked. The sea crisped. Hot wind swept their faces, tightened ropes, filled sails, bent the ship. A cheer burst from the men as the helm turned and they surged forward—straight toward the Spaniard, still becalmed.

“There’s more behind, Amyas,” Cary said. “Shall we shorten sail?”

“No. Hold every stitch,” Amyas ordered. “Give me the helm. Boatswain, pipe to clear for action.”

It was done. In ten minutes, the crew stood at quarters. Thunder rolled louder. The breeze freshened.

“The dog has it now. There he goes!” Cary said.

“Right before the wind. He doesn’t want to face us.”

“He’s running into destruction,” Yeo said. “An hour more, and he’ll either race up the Channel—or smash into shore.”

“There! He’s put his helm down. Does he see land?”

“He’s like a March hare, beaten from his home,” Cary said. “He doesn’t know where to run next.”

He was right. Ten minutes later, the Spaniard fell off again, sailing straight downwind, while the *Vengeance* gained on her fast. After two hours, the distance shrunk from four miles to one. Lightning flashed closer. From a vast black cloud-arch, a fierce gust blew. Amyas reluctantly yielded to growing murmurs and ordered sail reduced.

On they rushed, barely slower. The black arch followed, veiled by a flat gray sheet of rain, before which the sea boiled in a long white line. Every moment, a blue spark leapt into the water or zigzagged through the rain.

“We’ll have it now, and with a vengeance. This’ll test your tackle, master,” Cary said.

The boatswain shrugged and pulled up his collar as the first drops stung his ears. A minute later, the squall hit—rain like hail, lashing the sea into froth, wind tearing heads off waves, turning the ocean into a seething white waste. Above, lightning danced, blinding and dazzling, while thunder cracked like splintering iron.

“Get arms and ammunition under cover—and below with you all!” Amyas shouted from the helm.

“And heat the pokers in the galley fire,” Yeo called. “In case the rain puts out our linstocks. I hope you’ll let me stay on deck, sir—in case—”

“I need someone. Who better than you? Can you see the chase?”

No. She was lost in the gray whirlwind. She might be half a mile away—or gone.

Now Amyas and Yeo stood alone. Neither spoke. Each knew the other’s thoughts—and knew they were his own. The squall grew fiercer. Rain poured harder. Where was the Spaniard?

“If he’s laid-to, we might overshoot him, sir!”

“If he tried to lay-to, he won’t have a sail left. I know the stubbornness of those Spanish tubs. Hurrah! There he is—right on our larboard bow!”

There she was—two musket shots away, staggering with torn canvas.

“He tried to hull,” Yeo said, rubbing his hands. “Got a buffet. What now?”

“Range alongside, if it blows live imps and witches. Try our luck once more. Pah! This lightning blinds!”

They swept forward, gaining fast. “Call the men up. The rain will be over in ten minutes.”

Yeo ran forward—then leapt back, face pale and wild.

“Land ahead! Port your helm, sir! For God’s sake, port your helm!”

Amyas, with the strength of a bull, slammed the helm down. Yeo shouted to the crew below.

The ship swung. Masts bent like whips. The foresail tore like a cannon blast. What mattered? Within two hundred yards lay the Spaniard. Ahead of her, rising through the hail, a huge dark bank merged with the clouds. At its base, pillars of foam leapt upward.

“What is it? Morte? Hartland?”

It could be anything within thirty miles.

“Lundy!” Yeo shouted. “The south end! I see the head of the Shutter in the breakers! Hard a-port! Get her close-hauled! The Lord may have mercy on us yet! Look at the Spaniard!”

Yes—look at the Spaniard!

On their left, as they broached-to, a granite wall sloped down from the clouds to a lone peak two hundred feet high. Then a hundred yards of roaring breakers over a sunken shelf, where the tide raced like a cataract. Then, amid salt spray, the Shutter—a monstrous black fang—rose, waiting. Between the Shutter and the land, the great galleon loomed dimly through the storm.

She had seen her doom. She tried to broach-to. But her clumsy bulk refused to obey. She struggled—half hidden in foam—fell back, and rushed toward her end.

And Amyas, standing at the helm, watching, felt the weight of destiny. He raised his sword—one final time—then hurled it into the sea.

“It is done,” he whispered. “Now let the Lord decide.”

“Would you hear a Spanish lady,
How she woo'd an Englishman?
Garments gay and rich as may be,
Deck'd with jewels had she on.”
—Elizabethan Ballad.

It was the first of October. The morning broke clear and still, the sky streaked gently from east to west with soft gray clouds, as though nature herself paused in quiet reflection after the tumult of summer storms and battle. The *Vengeance* slipped silently over the bar, her sails low, her flag flying at half-mast—a silent tribute to the man who lay within. Salvation Yeo was dead, and his body rested upon the ship.

A boat pulled away from the vessel, heading for the western edge of the shore. Cary and Brimblecombe helped Amyas Leigh from the skiff and guided him slowly up the hill toward Burrough, his home. A crowd gathered around him—some cheering, some weeping, many moved by pity. All of Appledore and Bideford knew what had happened: the great seaman had returned not in triumph, but in sorrow, blind and broken, carrying with him the weight of loss.

“Spare me, my good friends,” Amyas said, his voice steady despite the pain. “I have come ashore that I might return home quietly, without being paraded through the town like a spectacle. Do not speak of me. Think not of me. But follow me decently, as Christian men, and bear this body to its rest—the body of a better man than I.”

As he spoke, another boat approached, bearing the coffin draped in the English flag. The people obeyed his words. One man was sent ahead to Burrough to warn Mrs. Leigh that her son was coming.

The coffin was carried ashore, and Amyas took his place among the mourners, flanked by Cary and Brimblecombe. Behind them came the crew in solemn order, followed by the growing throng. By the time they reached halfway to Northam, the procession numbered nearly five hundred souls.

They had sent word the day before to the sexton, who had already dug the grave. When they arrived at the churchyard, the parson stood waiting at the gate. Mrs. Leigh remained at home—too overwhelmed to face the gathering. Yet her heart swelled with pride and sorrow. She sat in the bay window, Ayacanora beside her, and when the bell ceased tolling, she opened her Prayer-book and began to read the Burial-service.

“Ayacanora,” she murmured, “they are burying old Master Yeo—the man who loved you, who searched for you across the world, who saved you from the jaws of the crocodile. Are you not sorry for him, child? You look so bright today.”

Ayacanora flushed, her eyes dropping. She thought of nothing but Amyas.

The service ended. The blessing was given. The parson stepped back—but the crowd lingered. They stared at the grave, then at Amyas, the blind giant standing at its head, as if sensing something unspoken yet inevitable.

And then it came.

Amyas straightened himself to his full height, raised his hand, and swept it forward with regal grace. Every eye turned to him. He tried twice to speak—but the words choked in his throat. Then, at last:

“Good people all, and seamen—among whom I was born and bred—and to whom I return today, blind, to dwell until death—Here lies the flower and pattern of bold mariners; the truest of friends, the most terrible of foes; steadfast in purpose, cunning in counsel, swift in action; sober in victory, and in defeat—God knows I’ve known many such days—of endurance beyond mortal measure.

He was the first of Britons to humble the pride of Spain at Rio de la Hacha and Nombre; the first to sail those South Seas which, by God’s grace, shall one day be as free to English ships as this harbor outside our doors.

Having endured strange torments and sufferings, purged of youthful sins, he faced the Popish enemy, and learned from fire and exile to fear God alone. And having served faithfully as a scourge against Spain and a soldier of Christ, he is now lifted up—like Elijah—to his reward, in a chariot of fire unto heaven.

And I pray, I trust, that he has left behind the mantle of his courage and piety—that these shores may never lack brave and godly sailors, who will count their lives cheap in defense of Country, Bible, and Queen. Amen.”

He paused, then reached out for the hands of his companions. Together, they walked from the churchyard, across the village street, and up the lane to Burrough gates. The crowd parted in silence, as though afraid to disturb the sacred moment.

Amyas seemed to know when they reached the gate. He unlocked it himself and stepped forward boldly along the gravel path, while Cary and Brimblecombe followed, trembling—not from fear, but from emotion. They expected either a storm of grief or a sudden collapse. Their hearts fluttered like those of young girls.

Up to the door he went, as if he could see. He felt for the entrance, stepped inside, and called softly: “Mother!”

In an instant, she was in his arms.

Neither spoke for a long while. She wept silently, her eyes dry with sorrow; he stood firm, calm, his great arms wrapped around her.

“Mother,” he said at last, “I am home. You see, I must come. Will you take me in? Look after this useless body? I shan’t be too much trouble, shall I?” He smiled down at her, kissed her brow.

She said nothing, only passed her arm gently around his waist and led him inside.

“Take care of your head, dear child,” she whispered. “The doors are low.”

They entered together.

“Will! Jack!” Amyas called.

But the two men had already hurried off.

“I’m glad we’re gone,” said Cary. “I’d have been a fool in another minute, watching that angel of a woman. How her face trembled—and how she held it in!”

“Ah, well,” said Jack, “there goes a brave servant of the queen, cut down before his work was done. Heigho! I must go home to my father, and then—”

“And then home with me,” said Cary. “You and I have sailed together too long, Jack. You’ll not waste your prize-money on taverns. I’ll watch over you ashore, or we’ll have you treating half the town for a week.”

“Oh, Mr. Cary!” said Jack, scandalized.

“Come home with me,” said Cary. “We’ll poison the parson, and your father can give me the rectory.”

“Oh, Mr. Cary!” said Jack again.

And so the two set off for Clovelly that very day.

Amyas sat alone.

His mother had stepped out briefly to speak with the sailors who had brought his luggage, to feed and shelter them. He remained in the old bay-window—the same spot where, as a boy, he had read *King Arthur*, *Fox’s Martyrs*, and *The Cruelties of the Spaniards*. He reached out and found the books—still there, side by side, untouched for twenty years.

The window was open. A cool breeze carried the scent of four-season roses, rosemary, and autumn gilliflowers—just as it always had.

On the table, he sensed a dish of apples. He knew them by smell—the same apples he used to gather as a boy. He reached out, touched them, rolled them in his palms, played with them—just as he had long ago.

And in that moment, his whole life flashed before him—not in dreams, but in memory: every sea, every storm, every word spoken, every face seen. He saw the fairy island on the Meta, heard the roar of the cataract once more, saw the palm-tops glowing above the spray, stepped across the smooth trunks, leaped down to the gravel beach beside the pool.

Then, rising from the fern-covered rocks, came the vision of Ayacanora.

Where was she?

He had not thought of her until now. How he had wronged her! But punishment had come—justice had been served. Perhaps she no longer cared for him. Who would care for a blind ox, doomed to be fed and tended like a child for the rest of his days?

Tut! How long had his mother been gone?

He returned to playing with the apples, lost in memories of climbing with Frank in the orchard.

Then one slipped from his fingers—fell to the floor.

He bent to find it—but could not.

Frustrated, he turned sharply—his head struck the table.

Was it the pain? The small disappointment? Or was it the cruel absurdity of blindness, made real in such a trivial way—making it all the more humiliating?

Or was it the breaking point of nerves stretched too thin?

He didn’t know. But in that instant, he stamped his foot—pettishly, like a child. Then, catching himself, he burst into violent tears.

A rustle passed by. The apple was placed back in his hand.

And Ayacanora’s voice, trembling, sobbed: “There! There it is! Do not weep! Oh, do not weep! I cannot bear it! I will get you everything! Let me fetch and carry, tend you, feed you, lead you—like your slave, your dog! Say I may be your slave!”

She fell to her knees, seized both his hands, and covered them with kisses.

“Yes!” she cried. “I will be your slave! I must be! You cannot help it! You cannot escape me now! You cannot go to sea! You cannot turn your back on wretched me. I have you safe now! Safe!”

She clutched his hands, triumphant.

“Oh, and what a wretch I am,” she whispered, horrified. “To rejoice in this! To taunt him with his blindness! Oh, forgive me! I am only a wild girl—a savage Indian, you know—but—” And she broke into sobs.

A great shudder ran through Amyas Leigh. He sat silent for a moment. Then, solemnly: “And is this still possible? Then God have mercy upon me, a sinner.”

Ayacanora looked up, uncertain.

But before she could speak, he bent down, lifted her as a lion lifts a lamb, pressed her to his chest, and covered her face with kisses.

The door opened.

A rustle of silk. Ayacanora sprang back, breathless, defiant—half trembling, half claiming her place: “He is mine now. No one shall part us.”

“Who is it?” Amyas asked.

“Your mother.”

“You see,” he said, smiling, “that I am bringing forth fruits meet for repentance.”

He heard her approach.

A kiss passed between the women. A sob. Then he felt Ayacannota sink once more into his arms.

“Amyas, my son,” said Mrs. Leigh, her voice silver and dreamy, like distant chimes from the highest heavens, “fear not to take her to your heart again—for it is your mother who has laid her there.”

“It is true,” Amyas whispered to himself. “What God has joined together, man cannot put asunder.”

* * * * *

From that hour, Ayacanora’s voice returned. Day by day, year by year, her song rose from that happy home, soaring like a skylark into the highest skies—carrying with it the peace of the blind giant, drawing him back to the paradises of the West, in the wake of the heroes who, from that time forth, sailed forth to build a new and greater England, shouting with Heaven’s favor: **Westward Ho!**
